Matthew — Saints Holy Bible cross-references
1061 completed source verses; 1094 retained pairs; 1068 source units read; 7 deferred units.
First unfinished: Matthew 1:14. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: Galatians 1:1.
Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.
Original Saints numbering is retained.

Matthew 1:1
A book of the origin of Jesus Christ, son of David, son of Abraham.
  Romans 1:3 — genealogy: Matthew introduces Jesus Christ as David's son; Romans likewise identifies God's Son as coming from David's seed according to the flesh. The shared claim concerns Davidic descent.
    Romans 1:3 concerning His Son, who came from David’s seed according to the flesh,
  Galatians 3:16 — promise and fulfillment: Matthew's title calls Christ Abraham's son. Galatians interprets the promises to Abraham's seed as concerning Christ, providing an explicit apostolic connection between Abraham's lineage and Christ.
    Galatians 3:16 Now the promises were spoken to Abraham and to his seed. It does not say, “And to seeds,” as concerning many, but as concerning one, “And to your seed,” who is Christ.

Matthew 1:2
Abraham begot Isaac; and Isaac begot Jacob; and Jacob begot Judah and his brothers;
  Genesis 25:19 — genealogy: Genesis explicitly says that Abraham begot Isaac, the first father-son relationship in Matthew's verse.
    Genesis 25:19 And these are the generations of Isaac, Abraham’s son: Abraham begot Isaac.
  Genesis 25:26 — genealogy: Genesis records Jacob's birth to Isaac and Rebekah, supplying the narrative behind Matthew's statement that Isaac begot Jacob.
    Genesis 25:26 And afterward his brother came out, and his hand held Esau’s heel; and he was named Jacob. And Isaac was sixty years old when Rebekah bore them.
  Genesis 29:35 — genealogy: Genesis describes Leah bearing Judah to Jacob, supplying the birth narrative for Matthew's Jacob-Judah link.
    Genesis 29:35 And she conceived again and bore a son, and said, “This time I will give thanks to the Lord.” Therefore she called his name Judah; and she ceased bearing.

Matthew 1:3
Judah begot Perez and Zerah by Tamar; Perez begot Hezron; Hezron begot Ram;
  Genesis 38:27–38:30 — genealogy: Genesis recounts the twins Perez and Zerah and the scarlet-thread incident. In the surrounding Tamar narrative this is the birth episode Matthew compresses into his identification of Judah's sons by Tamar.
    Genesis 38:27 And when she gave birth, behold, there were twins in her womb.
    Genesis 38:28 And as she was giving birth, one put out his hand; and the midwife took it and bound a scarlet thread upon his hand, saying, “This one shall come out first.”
    Genesis 38:29 But as he drew back his hand, immediately his brother came out; and she said, “Why has a barrier been broken through because of you?” And his name was called Perez.
    Genesis 38:30 And afterward his brother came out, upon whose hand was the scarlet thread; and his name was called Zerah.
  Ruth 4:18–4:19 — genealogy: Ruth's closing genealogy follows Perez to Hezron and then Arran; Matthew follows Perez to Hezron and Ram. The sequences are comparable while the edition's different name forms remain visible.
    Ruth 4:18 Now these are the generations of Perez: Perez begot Hezron,
    Ruth 4:19 and Hezron begot Arran, and Arran begot Amminadab,

Matthew 1:4
Ram begot Amminadab; Amminadab begot Nahshon; Nahshon begot Salmon;
  Ruth 4:19–4:20 — genealogy: Ruth follows Arran through Amminadab and Nahshon to Salman; Matthew gives Ram, Amminadab, Nahshon and Salmon. The overlapping succession supports comparison without silently replacing the Saints edition's name forms.
    Ruth 4:19 and Hezron begot Arran, and Arran begot Amminadab,
    Ruth 4:20 and Amminadab begot Nahshon, and Nahshon begot Salman,

Matthew 1:5
Salmon begot Boaz by Rahab; Boaz begot Obed by Ruth; Obed begot Jesse;
  Ruth 4:13–4:17 — genealogy: Ruth narrates Boaz taking Ruth as wife and her bearing the child subsequently named Obed, whose descendants are Jesse and David. Matthew compresses those relationships into the genealogy; Ruth does not identify Rahab as Boaz's mother.
    Ruth 4:13 Boaz took Ruth, and she became his wife. He went in to her, and the Lord gave her conception, and she bore a son.
    Ruth 4:14 The women said to Naomi, “Blessed be the Lord, who has not left you without the near kinsman today; and may he call your name in Israel.
    Ruth 4:15 He shall be a restorer of life to you and a nourisher of your old age, for your daughter-in-law who loved you has borne him, and she is better to you than seven sons.”
    Ruth 4:16 And Naomi took the child and laid him in her bosom, and became his nurse.
    Ruth 4:17 The neighbouring women gave him a name, saying, “A son is born to Naomi.” They called his name Obed. He was the father of Jesse, the father of David.
  Ruth 4:21–4:22 — genealogy: Both genealogies connect Salman/Salmon, Boaz, Obed and Jesse in succession. The Ruth passage also continues to David, but does not independently supply Matthew's statement about Rahab.
    Ruth 4:21 and Salman begot Boaz, and Boaz begot Obed,
    Ruth 4:22 and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David.

Matthew 1:6
Jesse begot David the king. David begot Solomon by the wife of Uriah;
  Ruth 4:22 — genealogy: Ruth's final verse explicitly says Jesse begot David, the first relationship stated in Matthew's verse.
    Ruth 4:22 and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David.
  2 Samuel 12:24 — genealogy: Samuel narrates David and Bathsheba having Solomon, corresponding to Matthew's David-Solomon link. Matthew calls the mother the wife of Uriah, while this Samuel verse names her Bathsheba and calls her David's wife.
    2 Samuel 12:24 David comforted Bathsheba his wife. He went in to her and lay with her. She conceived, bore a son, and named him Solomon. The Lord loved him.

Matthew 1:7
Solomon begot Rehoboam; Rehoboam begot Abijah; Abijah begot Asaph;
  1 Chronicles 3:10 — genealogy: Chronicles gives Solomon, Rehoboam, Abia and Asa in the royal succession. Matthew has Solomon, Rehoboam, Abijah and Asaph: the overlapping sequence is clear, but the Asa/Asaph wording difference is retained and not treated as an exact name match.
    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,

Matthew 1:8
Asaph begot Jehoshaphat; Jehoshaphat begot Joram; Joram begot Uzziah;
  1 Chronicles 3:10–3:12 — genealogy: Chronicles supplies the succession Asa, Jehoshaphat, Joram, Ahaziah, Joash, Amasias, Azariah and Joathan. Compared with Matthew's Asaph, Jehoshaphat, Joram and Uzziah, it exposes both a name-form difference and Matthew's compressed generations; it does not make every Matthew link an immediate father-son relationship.
    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:11 Joram his son, Ahaziah his son, Joash his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,

Matthew 1:9
Uzziah begot Jotham; Jotham begot Ahaz; Ahaz begot Hezekiah;
  1 Chronicles 3:12–3:13 — genealogy: Chronicles names Azariah, Joathan, Ahaz and Hezekiah, the royal succession corresponding to Matthew's Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah. The different name forms are shown rather than harmonized in the source text.
    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,

Matthew 1:10
Hezekiah begot Manasseh; Manasseh begot Amos; Amos begot Josiah;
  1 Chronicles 3:13–3:14 — genealogy: Chronicles continues through Hezekiah, Manasseh, Amon and Josiah. Matthew gives Hezekiah, Manasseh, Amos and Josiah; the Amon/Amos difference remains explicit while the surrounding royal sequence agrees.
    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:14 Amon his son, Josiah his son.

Matthew 1:11
Josiah begot Jechoniah and his brothers at the deportation to Babylon.
  1 Chronicles 3:15–3:16 — genealogy: Chronicles places Joakim among Josiah's sons and Jechonias in Joakim's line. This is a useful comparison with Matthew's compressed Josiah-Jechoniah transition; Chronicles includes the intervening generation and does not corroborate an immediate father-son link between those two names.
    1 Chronicles 3:15 Josiah’s sons: Joanan the firstborn, Joakim the second, Sedekias the third, Salloum the fourth.
    1 Chronicles 3:16 Joakim’s sons: Jechonias his son, Sedekias his son.

Matthew 1:12
After the deportation to Babylon, Jechoniah begot Shealtiel; Shealtiel begot Zerubbabel;
  1 Chronicles 3:17–3:19 — genealogy: The Saints Chronicles record places Salathiel among Jechonia-asir's sons and lists Zerubbabel among Salathiel's sons. This parallels Matthew's Jechoniah, Shealtiel and Zerubbabel sequence while retaining the edition's own spelling and parentage text.
    1 Chronicles 3:17 The sons of Jechonia-asir: Salathiel his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:18 Melchiram, Phadaias, Sanesar, Jekemia, Osamo, and Denethi.
    1 Chronicles 3:19 Salathiel’s sons: Zerubbabel and Semei. Zerubbabel’s sons: Mosollamos and Ananias, and Salomith their sister,
  Luke 3:27 — genealogy: Luke also places Zerubbabel next to Shealtiel, but continues to Neri rather than Matthew's Jechoniah. The link records the shared names and the differing ancestry instead of claiming that the two genealogies are identical.
    Luke 3:27 of Joanan, of Rhesa, of Zerubbabel, of Shealtiel, of Neri,

Matthew 1:13
Zerubbabel begot Abiud; Abiud begot Eliakim; Eliakim begot Azor;
  Luke 3:27 — genealogy: Both genealogies include Zerubbabel. Matthew follows him toward Abiud, Eliakim and Azor, whereas Luke's line toward Jesus goes through Rhesa and Joanan. This is a comparison at the shared ancestor, not independent confirmation of Matthew's three subsequent names.
    Luke 3:27 of Joanan, of Rhesa, of Zerubbabel, of Shealtiel, of Neri,

Matthew 1:16
and Jacob begot Joseph, the husband of Mary, of whom was born Jesus, who is called Christ.
  Luke 1:27 — narrative parallel: Both genealogies connect Jesus with Joseph while qualifying the connection: Matthew identifies Joseph as Mary's husband and says Jesus was born of Mary, while Luke says Jesus was Joseph's son as was supposed. Matthew calls Joseph's father Jacob while Luke names Heli; the difference is not resolved by this cross-reference.
    Luke 1:27 to a virgin betrothed to a man named Joseph, of David’s house. The virgin’s name was Mary.
  Luke 3:23 — genealogy: Both genealogies connect Jesus with Joseph while qualifying the connection: Matthew describes Joseph as Mary's husband, of whom Jesus was born, and Luke says Jesus was Joseph's son as was supposed. Matthew calls Joseph's father Jacob while Luke names Heli; the difference is not resolved by this cross-reference.
    Luke 3:23 Jesus Himself was about thirty years old when He began His ministry. He was the son, as was supposed, of Joseph, of Heli,

Matthew 1:17
So all the generations from Abraham to David were fourteen generations, from David to the deportation to Babylon fourteen generations, and from the deportation to Babylon to the Christ fourteen generations.
  1 Chronicles 3:10–3:19 — genealogy: Chronicles' royal and exilic succession provides a comparison for Matthew's organized fourteen-generation summary. Its inclusion of Ahaziah, Joash, Amasias and Joakim makes Matthew's selectivity visible; this passage does not independently verify Matthew's three numerical groups of fourteen.
    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:11 Joram his son, Ahaziah his son, Joash his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:14 Amon his son, Josiah his son.
    1 Chronicles 3:15 Josiah’s sons: Joanan the firstborn, Joakim the second, Sedekias the third, Salloum the fourth.
    1 Chronicles 3:16 Joakim’s sons: Jechonias his son, Sedekias his son.
    1 Chronicles 3:17 The sons of Jechonia-asir: Salathiel his son,
    1 Chronicles 3:18 Melchiram, Phadaias, Sanesar, Jekemia, Osamo, and Denethi.
    1 Chronicles 3:19 Salathiel’s sons: Zerubbabel and Semei. Zerubbabel’s sons: Mosollamos and Ananias, and Salomith their sister,

Matthew 1:18
The origin of Jesus Christ was like this. When His mother Mary was betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit.
  Luke 1:34–1:35 — narrative parallel: Matthew attributes Mary's pregnancy before marital relations to the Holy Spirit. Luke has Mary ask how conception will occur without knowing a man and has the angel answer that the Holy Spirit will come upon her.
    Luke 1:34 Mary said to the angel, “How will this be, since I do not know a man?”
    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

Matthew 1:19
Joseph her husband, being righteous and unwilling to expose her to public disgrace, decided to divorce her quietly.
  Deuteronomy 24:1 — thematic parallel: Joseph's proposed divorce belongs to the legal world in which Deuteronomy describes a husband giving a wife a divorce certificate and sending her away. The law supplies divorce background; it does not narrate Joseph's case or prescribe his plan to act quietly.
    Deuteronomy 24:1 And if a man takes a wife and dwells with her as husband, and she finds no favour in his sight, because he has found some unseemly thing in her, and he writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house,

Matthew 1:20
As he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said, “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife home, for what has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.
  Luke 1:35 — narrative parallel: The angel's assurance to Joseph that Mary's conception is from the Holy Spirit corresponds to Gabriel's explanation to Mary that the Holy Spirit will come upon her. These are announcements to different recipients about the same conception, not the same reported angelic encounter.
    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

Matthew 1:21
She shall bear a Son, and you shall call His name Jesus, for He shall save His people from their sins.”
  Luke 1:31 — narrative parallel: Both angelic announcements direct that Mary's Son be named Jesus. Matthew addresses Joseph and explains the name through salvation from sins; Luke's instruction is addressed to Mary.
    Luke 1:31 And behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a Son, and you shall name Him Jesus.
  Luke 1:76–1:77 — thematic parallel: Matthew explains Jesus' mission as saving His people from their sins. Zechariah's prophecy describes the child's preparation of the Lord's way as giving His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins; the shared connection is salvation and forgiveness, while the child addressed in Luke is John.
    Luke 1:76 And you also, child, will be called prophet of the Most High, for you will go before the Lord to prepare His ways,
    Luke 1:77 to give His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins,

Matthew 1:22
All this has happened so that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled:
  Isaiah 7:14 — promise and fulfillment: Matthew's fulfillment introduction leads directly into the following virgin-and-Emmanuel quotation. Isaiah supplies that identifiable oracle; the connection depends on reading Matthew's introduction together with its continuation in 1:23.
    Isaiah 7:14 Therefore the Lord Himself will give you a sign: behold, the virgin will conceive and bear a son, and you will call his name Emmanuel.

Matthew 1:23
“Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and they shall call His name Emmanuel”; which means “God with us.”
  Isaiah 7:14 — quotation: Matthew quotes Isaiah's virgin conceiving and bearing a son named Emmanuel and adds the explanation 'God with us.' The Saints Isaiah text uses 'you will call his name' where Matthew has 'they shall call His name'; the supplied wording is preserved.
    Isaiah 7:14 Therefore the Lord Himself will give you a sign: behold, the virgin will conceive and bear a son, and you will call his name Emmanuel.
  Matthew 28:20 — thematic parallel: Within Matthew itself, the opening Emmanuel explanation, 'God with us,' corresponds to Jesus' closing assurance that He is with His disciples all the days. This is an internal literary and theological correspondence, not a second independent birth account.
    Matthew 28:20 teaching them to keep all that I commanded you; and behold, I am with you all the days, until the consummation of the age.”

Matthew 1:24
When Joseph awoke from sleep, he did as the angel of the Lord commanded him and took his wife home.
  Matthew 2:13–2:14 — narrative parallel: Matthew later repeats Joseph's response to an angelic dream: an instruction concerning the Child and His mother is followed by Joseph rising and carrying it out. This same-book narrative parallel connects Joseph's two acts of obedience without treating the dreams as one event.
    Matthew 2:13 After they withdrew, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph in a dream, saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and flee to Egypt. Stay there until I tell you, for Herod is about to seek the Child to destroy Him.”
    Matthew 2:14 He rose, took the Child and His mother by night, and withdrew to Egypt.

Matthew 1:25
He did not have relations with her until she bore a Son; and he called His name Jesus.
  Luke 2:21 — narrative parallel: Both accounts report the actual giving of the name Jesus in accordance with an angel's prior instruction. Luke supplies the naming at circumcision; it does not independently state Matthew's qualification about Joseph's relations with Mary.
    Luke 2:21 When eight days were completed for His circumcision, He was named Jesus, the name given by the angel before He was conceived in the womb.

Matthew 2:1
After Jesus was born in Bethlehem of Judea in the days of King Herod, behold, magi from the east arrived in Jerusalem,
  Luke 2:4–2:7 — narrative parallel: Luke locates Joseph and Mary in Bethlehem when Mary bears her Son, corresponding to Matthew's statement that Jesus was born in Bethlehem of Judea. Luke does not describe the magi in this passage.
    Luke 2:4 Joseph also went up from Galilee, from the city of Nazareth, into Judea, to David’s city, which is called Bethlehem, because he belonged to David’s house and family,
    Luke 2:5 to be registered with Mary, who was betrothed to him and was pregnant.
    Luke 2:6 While they were there, the time came for her to give birth.
    Luke 2:7 She bore her firstborn Son, wrapped Him in swaddling cloths, and laid Him in a manger, because there was no place for them in the lodging place.

Matthew 2:2
saying, “Where is the One born King of the Jews? For we saw His star at its rising and have come to bow before Him.”
  Numbers 24:17 — allusion: Balaam's oracle joins a rising star from Jacob with a ruler arising from Israel. Matthew's star and newborn King invite a royal-star comparison; Matthew does not explicitly quote the oracle, and Numbers does not describe the magi's astronomical observation.
    Numbers 24:17 I will show him, but not now; I pronounce him blessed, but he does not draw near. A star shall rise from Jacob, and a man shall arise from Israel. He shall crush Moab's leaders and plunder all the sons of Seth.

Matthew 2:3
When King Herod heard this, he was troubled, and all Jerusalem with him.
  Matthew 2:16 — narrative parallel: Within this same chapter, Herod's alarm at the report of a newborn king develops into his angry order to kill Bethlehem's boys. This is an internal narrative development of the same threat, not independent confirmation of Herod's reaction.
    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

Matthew 2:4
He gathered all the chief priests and scribes of the people and asked them where the Christ was to be born.
  John 7:41–7:42 — thematic parallel: Herod asks the religious authorities where the Christ is to be born. John's crowd likewise debates the Christ's origin and appeals to Scripture for Davidic descent and Bethlehem; the passages share the specific scriptural birthplace question, not the same conversation.
    John 7:41 Others said, “This is the Christ.” But some said, “Surely the Christ does not come from Galilee?
    John 7:42 Has not the Scripture said that the Christ comes from the offspring of David and from Bethlehem, the village where David was?”

Matthew 2:5
They told him, “In Bethlehem of Judea, for this is what has been written through the prophet:
  Micah 5:1 — quotation: The authorities identify Bethlehem as the birthplace on the basis of a prophet. The following quotation is grounded in Micah's Bethlehem ruler oracle, numbered 5:1 in this Saints edition, rather than silently using another edition's 5:2.
    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.

Matthew 2:6
‘And you, Bethlehem, land of Judah, are by no means least among the rulers of Judah; for out of you will come a ruler who will shepherd My people Israel.’”
  Micah 5:1 — quotation: Both passages identify Bethlehem in Judah as the place from which Israel's ruler comes. Matthew's 'by no means least' reformulates Micah's description of Bethlehem as small, and Matthew's shepherd wording extends beyond this one Micah verse.
    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.
  2 Samuel 5:2 — allusion: Matthew's ruler 'who will shepherd My people Israel' closely parallels the Lord's commission of David to shepherd His people Israel and be ruler over Israel in Samuel. It contributes the Davidic shepherd-ruler language alongside the Bethlehem oracle.
    2 Samuel 5:2 In days past, when Saul was king over us, you were the one who led Israel out and brought them in. The Lord said to you, ‘You will shepherd My people Israel, and you will be ruler over Israel.’”

Matthew 2:7
Then Herod secretly called the magi and learned from them the exact time when the star appeared.
  Matthew 2:16 — narrative parallel: The same chapter explicitly says Herod's age limit for the killings was set according to the exact time learned from the magi. That later statement explains the narrative significance of this secret inquiry; it is an internal causal link.
    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

Matthew 2:8
He sent them to Bethlehem, saying, “Go and inquire carefully about the Child. When you find Him, report to me, so that I too may come and bow before Him.”
  Matthew 2:16 — narrative parallel: Herod says he wishes to bow before the Child, but his subsequent order to kill the boys exposes the hostile purpose of his inquiry. This same-chapter comparison identifies the contrast between his stated pretext and his later action.
    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

Matthew 2:9
After hearing the king, they went on their way. And behold, the star they had seen at its rising went ahead of them until it came and stood over the place where the Child was.
  Matthew 2:2 — narrative parallel: The magi identify the guiding star as the star they had already seen at its rising. The same-chapter reference to their earlier report establishes the recurrence of this particular sign; it supplies no independent account of the star's motion.
    Matthew 2:2 saying, “Where is the One born King of the Jews? For we saw His star at its rising and have come to bow before Him.”

Matthew 2:10
When they saw the star, they rejoiced with exceedingly great joy.
  Luke 2:10–2:11 — thematic parallel: The magi's exceedingly great joy at the sign of the Child corresponds to Luke's announcement of great joy because the Savior, Christ the Lord, has been born. The observers and signs differ, so this is a shared response to Jesus' birth rather than a merged visit.
    Luke 2:10 The angel said to them, “Do not fear, for behold, I announce to you good news of great joy, which will be for all the people:
    Luke 2:11 today in David’s city a Savior has been born for you, who is Christ the Lord.

Matthew 2:11
They entered the house and saw the Child with Mary His mother. Falling down, they bowed before Him; and opening their treasures, they offered Him gifts: gold, frankincense, and myrrh.
  Isaiah 60:5–60:6 — allusion: Isaiah pictures wealth coming from the nations and visitors carrying gold and frankincense. Matthew's visitors offer gold and frankincense to the Child, with myrrh added. The shared gift imagery supports an allusion, while Isaiah's address to Jerusalem is not erased.
    Isaiah 60:5 Then you will see and fear and be astonished in heart, for the wealth of sea, nations, and peoples will turn toward you. There will come to you
    Isaiah 60:6 herds of camels, and camels from Madiam and Gaipha will cover you. They will all come from Saba, bringing gold and carrying frankincense, and they will proclaim the Lord’s salvation as good news.
  Psalms 71:10–71:11 — thematic parallel: The Greek-numbered royal Psalm 71 pictures foreign kings bringing gifts and bowing before the king. Matthew's magi bow and offer treasures to the Child; Matthew calls them magi, and this comparison does not turn them into named or numbered kings.
    Psalms 71:10 kings of Tarshish and the islands will offer gifts; kings of the Arabians and Saba will bring gifts.
    Psalms 71:11 And all kings will bow before him; all nations will serve him.

Matthew 2:12
Having been warned in a dream not to return to Herod, they withdrew to their country by another way.
  Matthew 2:22 — narrative parallel: Matthew twice has a dream warning redirect a journey away from danger associated with Herod's dynasty: the magi avoid Herod here, and Joseph avoids Judea under Archelaus later. This is a same-book repeated protection motif involving different recipients.
    Matthew 2:22 But hearing that Archelaus reigned over Judea in place of his father Herod, he feared to go there. Warned in a dream, he withdrew to the regions of Galilee.

Matthew 2:13
After they withdrew, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph in a dream, saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and flee to Egypt. Stay there until I tell you, for Herod is about to seek the Child to destroy Him.”
  Exodus 1:16–1:22 — narrative parallel: Herod's threat to destroy the Child recalls Pharaoh's orders to kill Hebrew boys. This is a comparison of royal threats against male children, not a claim that the two rulers or historical events are identical.
    Exodus 1:16 And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”
    Exodus 1:17 But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.
    Exodus 1:18 And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”
    Exodus 1:19 The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.
    Exodus 1:20 God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.
    Exodus 1:21 And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.
    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

Matthew 2:14
He rose, took the Child and His mother by night, and withdrew to Egypt.
  Genesis 46:2–46:4 — narrative parallel: Joseph takes his family into Egypt in response to the preceding dream instruction. Genesis similarly places Jacob's descent into Egypt under a divine night vision and a promise of return. The narratives concern different families and circumstances; their shared feature is a divinely directed family descent into Egypt.
    Genesis 46:2 And God spoke to Israel in a vision of the night, saying, “Jacob, Jacob.” And he said, “What is it?”
    Genesis 46:3 And He said, “I am the God of your fathers. Do not fear to go down into Egypt, for I will make you a great nation there.
    Genesis 46:4 “And I will go down with you into Egypt, and I will bring you up at last; and Joseph shall lay his hands upon your eyes.”

Matthew 2:15
He remained there until Herod’s death, so that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled: “Out of Egypt I called My Son.”
  Hosea 11:1 — quotation: Matthew applies the Egypt-call saying to Jesus' return in its fulfillment account. The pinned Saints Hosea text speaks of Israel as an infant and says 'out of Egypt I summoned his children,' whereas Matthew has 'I called My Son.' This is an identifiable quotation relationship with a real wording difference and Matthew's typological application, not an exact English match.
    Hosea 11:1 Because Israel was an infant, I loved him, and out of Egypt I summoned his children.

Matthew 2:16
Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.
  Exodus 1:16–1:22 — narrative parallel: Both rulers command the killing of boys: Herod specifies Bethlehem and its district and the age of two and under, while Pharaoh's commands concern Hebrew newborn boys and, in the later order, drowning. The concrete parallel is targeted royal violence against male children; the details remain distinct.
    Exodus 1:16 And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”
    Exodus 1:17 But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.
    Exodus 1:18 And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”
    Exodus 1:19 The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.
    Exodus 1:20 God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.
    Exodus 1:21 And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.
    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

Matthew 2:17
Then what was spoken through Jeremiah the prophet was fulfilled:
  Jeremiah 38:15 — promise and fulfillment: Matthew explicitly names Jeremiah when introducing the following Rachel lament. Its identifiable source is Jeremiah 38:15 in the Saints Greek-based numbering, and Matthew applies that lament to the aftermath of Herod's killings.
    Jeremiah 38:15 Thus said the Lord: A voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation, weeping, and wailing. Rachel, weeping aloud, would not stop over her sons, because they are no more.

Matthew 2:18
“A voice was heard in Ramah, weeping and great lamentation: Rachel weeping for her children, and she would not be comforted, because they are no more.”
  Jeremiah 38:15 — quotation: Both passages name Ramah and Rachel, describe weeping over her children or sons, and give the reason that they are no more. Matthew reuses Jeremiah's lament in the Bethlehem massacre narrative; the different English phrases about refusing comfort and not stopping are preserved.
    Jeremiah 38:15 Thus said the Lord: A voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation, weeping, and wailing. Rachel, weeping aloud, would not stop over her sons, because they are no more.

Matthew 2:19
After Herod died, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared in a dream to Joseph in Egypt,
  Exodus 4:19 — narrative parallel: In both passages the death of the hostile king precedes a divine message permitting a return. Matthew reports Herod's death before Joseph's dream, while Exodus reports the Egyptian king's death before the Lord addresses Moses.
    Exodus 4:19 And after those many days the king of Egypt died. And the Lord said to Moses in Midian, “Go, depart into Egypt; for all who sought your life are dead.”

Matthew 2:20
saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and go to the land of Israel, for those seeking the Child’s life have died.”
  Exodus 4:19 — allusion: The instruction to return because those seeking the Child's life have died closely echoes the Lord's direction to Moses to depart for Egypt because all who sought his life are dead. The destinations and persons differ, but the life-seekers-have-died wording is specific.
    Exodus 4:19 And after those many days the king of Egypt died. And the Lord said to Moses in Midian, “Go, depart into Egypt; for all who sought your life are dead.”

Matthew 2:21
He rose, took the Child and His mother, and entered the land of Israel.
  Exodus 4:20 — narrative parallel: Joseph takes the Child and His mother and returns to Israel after the divine instruction; Moses likewise takes his wife and children and returns toward Egypt after his instruction. The family-return pattern corresponds while the countries of return are different.
    Exodus 4:20 Moses took his wife and children, set them on the beasts of burden, and returned toward Egypt. Moses took the staff he had from God in his hand.

Matthew 2:22
But hearing that Archelaus reigned over Judea in place of his father Herod, he feared to go there. Warned in a dream, he withdrew to the regions of Galilee.
  Luke 2:39 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place the family in Galilee after the birth narrative, with Luke naming Nazareth as their own city. Luke does not report Archelaus or the dream warning, so the comparison supports the Galilean destination without claiming identical travel chronology.
    Luke 2:39 When they had completed everything according to the Lord’s law, they returned to Galilee, to their own city of Nazareth.

Matthew 2:23
He came and settled in a city called Nazareth, so that what was spoken through the prophets might be fulfilled: “He will be called a Nazarene.”
  John 1:45–1:46 — narrative parallel: Matthew says the family settled in Nazareth and that Jesus would be called a Nazarene. John identifies Jesus as from Nazareth and records Nathanael's reaction to that origin. This supports Jesus' Nazareth identification; it does not identify a direct Old Testament quotation for Matthew's reference to the prophets.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”
  Luke 2:39 — narrative parallel: Both birth narratives conclude with the family living in Nazareth in Galilee. Matthew presents the settlement after flight and return, whereas Luke summarizes the return after completion of the law's requirements; the cross-reference does not resolve the different narrative sequences.
    Luke 2:39 When they had completed everything according to the Lord’s law, they returned to Galilee, to their own city of Nazareth.

Matthew 3:1
In those days John the Baptist appeared, proclaiming in the wilderness of Judea
  Mark 1:4 — narrative parallel: Both accounts introduce John preaching in the wilderness; Mark also describes his baptism as a baptism of repentance.
    Mark 1:4 John appeared, baptizing in the wilderness and proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins.
  Luke 3:2–3:3 — narrative parallel: Luke likewise places John in the wilderness and then describes his proclamation around the Jordan; Matthew specifies the wilderness of Judea.
    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.
    Luke 3:3 He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,

Matthew 3:2
and saying, “Repent, for the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.”
  Matthew 4:17 — thematic parallel: John's announcement here and Jesus' announcement in Matthew 4:17 both call for repentance because the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near. The speakers and occasions differ.
    Matthew 4:17 From then on Jesus began to proclaim, “Repent, for the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.”

Matthew 3:3
For this is the one spoken of through Isaiah the prophet: “A voice of one crying in the wilderness: ‘Prepare the way of the Lord; make His paths straight.’”
  Isaiah 40:3 — quotation: Matthew explicitly identifies Isaiah as the source of the wilderness voice preparing the Lord's way and making His paths straight; Isaiah's wording names the paths of our God.
    Isaiah 40:3 A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the way of the Lord; make straight the paths of our God.
  Luke 3:4 — narrative parallel: Both Evangelists use Isaiah's wilderness-voice passage to explain John's ministry and quote the commands to prepare the Lord's way and straighten His paths.
    Luke 3:4 as it is written in the book of the words of Isaiah the prophet: “A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the Lord’s way; make His paths straight.

Matthew 3:4
John himself wore clothing of camel’s hair and a leather belt around his waist; his food was locusts and wild honey.
  Mark 1:6 — narrative parallel: Both descriptions give John's camel-hair clothing, leather belt, and diet of locusts and wild honey.
    Mark 1:6 John was clothed in camel’s hair, with a leather belt around his waist, and ate locusts and wild honey.
  2 Kings 1:8 — allusion: John's leather belt recalls the description by which the king identifies Elijah. The texts differ in their description of hair: Matthew specifies camel-hair clothing, whereas 2 Kings calls Elijah a hairy man; this is a proposed Elijah association rather than an identical costume quotation.
    2 Kings 1:8 They said to him, ‘A hairy man, with a leather belt fastened around his waist.’ He said, ‘This is Elijah the Tishbite.’

Matthew 3:5
Then Jerusalem, all Judea, and all the region around the Jordan went out to him,
  Mark 1:5 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe people from Jerusalem and Judea going out to John; Matthew additionally names the region around the Jordan.
    Mark 1:5 The whole Judean countryside and all the people of Jerusalem were going out to him. They were being baptized by him in the Jordan River, confessing their sins.

Matthew 3:6
and they were baptized by him in the Jordan River, confessing their sins.
  Mark 1:5 — narrative parallel: Both accounts state that John baptized people in the Jordan River while they confessed their sins.
    Mark 1:5 The whole Judean countryside and all the people of Jerusalem were going out to him. They were being baptized by him in the Jordan River, confessing their sins.

Matthew 3:7
When he saw many of the Pharisees and Sadducees coming to his baptism, he said to them, “Brood of vipers! Who warned you to flee from the coming wrath?
  Luke 3:7 — narrative parallel: Both record John's address 'Brood of vipers' and his question about fleeing the coming wrath. Matthew identifies Pharisees and Sadducees among the arrivals, whereas Luke addresses the crowds.
    Luke 3:7 So he said to the crowds coming out to be baptized by him, “Brood of vipers! Who warned you to flee the coming wrath?

Matthew 3:8
Bear fruit, then, worthy of repentance,
  Luke 3:8 — narrative parallel: Both versions of John's warning require fruit worthy of repentance; Luke continues in the same verse with the warning against relying on Abrahamic ancestry.
    Luke 3:8 Produce fruits worthy of repentance, and do not begin saying among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.

Matthew 3:9
and do not suppose you can say among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.
  Luke 3:8 — narrative parallel: Both reject the boast that Abraham is the hearers' father and state that God can raise children for Abraham from stones.
    Luke 3:8 Produce fruits worthy of repentance, and do not begin saying among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.

Matthew 3:10
Already the axe lies at the root of the trees. Every tree, therefore, that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.
  Luke 3:9 — narrative parallel: Both versions place the axe already at the trees' root and warn that a tree without good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.
    Luke 3:9 Already the axe also lies at the root of the trees. Therefore every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.”

Matthew 3:11
I baptize you in water for repentance, but the One coming after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not fit to carry. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.
  Luke 3:16 — narrative parallel: Both contrast John's water baptism with the stronger coming One's baptism in the Holy Spirit and fire. John's unworthiness is expressed through carrying sandals in Matthew and untying the sandal strap in Luke.
    Luke 3:16 John answered them all, “I baptize you with water, but One stronger than I is coming, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.
  Mark 1:7–1:8 — narrative parallel: Mark also records John's announcement of a stronger successor, his unworthiness concerning the successor's sandals, and the contrast between water baptism and Holy Spirit baptism. Mark's cited passage does not include Matthew's reference to fire.
    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.
    Mark 1:8 I baptized you with water, but He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit.”

Matthew 3:12
His winnowing shovel is in His hand, and He will thoroughly clear His threshing floor and gather His wheat into the storehouse; but He will burn the chaff with unquenchable fire.”
  Luke 3:17 — narrative parallel: Both describe the coming One clearing His threshing floor, gathering wheat into His storehouse, and burning the chaff with unquenchable fire.
    Luke 3:17 His winnowing fork is in His hand, to cleanse His threshing floor thoroughly and gather the wheat into His storehouse; but the chaff He will burn with unquenchable fire.”

Matthew 3:13
Then Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan, to John, to be baptized by him.
  Mark 1:9 — narrative parallel: Both accounts bring Jesus from Galilee to John for baptism in the Jordan; Mark specifies Nazareth as His Galilean point of departure.
    Mark 1:9 In those days Jesus came from Nazareth in Galilee and was baptized in the Jordan by John.
  Luke 3:21 — narrative parallel: Luke also records Jesus' baptism in the context of the people's baptism, adding that He was praying when heaven opened; Matthew describes His arrival to be baptized by John.
    Luke 3:21 When all the people were baptized, and Jesus also had been baptized and was praying, heaven was opened,

Matthew 3:14
John tried to prevent Him, saying, “I need to be baptized by Thee, and dost Thou come to me?”
  John 1:26–1:27 — thematic parallel: Matthew presents John's sense of being subordinate to Jesus through his objection that he needs Jesus' baptism; John presents the baptizer's confessed unworthiness to untie the coming One's sandal. John does not repeat the baptismal objection.
    John 1:26 John answered them, saying, “I baptize in water. Among you stands One whom you do not know,
    John 1:27 the One coming after me, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie.”

Matthew 3:15
Jesus answered him, “Allow it now, for in this way it is fitting for us to fulfill all righteousness.” Then he allowed Him.
  Luke 7:29–7:30 — John's baptism and acknowledgment of divine righteousness: Jesus calls receiving John's baptism fitting for fulfilling all righteousness. Luke describes people baptized by John as acknowledging God's righteousness and contrasts rejection of baptism with rejection of God's purpose. The concrete connection is John's baptism and the appropriate response to divine purpose; Luke does not reproduce Jesus' particular reply or attribute a need for repentance from personal sin to Him.
    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.
    Luke 7:30 But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.

Matthew 3:16
When Jesus was baptized, He immediately came up from the water. And behold, the heavens were opened, and He saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove and coming upon Him.
  Mark 1:10 — narrative parallel: Both baptism accounts describe Jesus coming up from the water, heaven opening, and the Spirit descending like a dove. Matthew says the Spirit came upon Him, whereas Mark says into Him in this edition.
    Mark 1:10 Immediately as He came up out of the water, He saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending like a dove into Him.
  John 1:32–1:33 — narrative parallel: John's testimony describes the Spirit descending from heaven like a dove and remaining upon Jesus; Matthew narrates the corresponding descent at Jesus' baptism. John's testimony also explains the sign's identifying purpose.
    John 1:32 And John testified, saying, “I have beheld the Spirit descending like a dove from heaven, and He remained upon Him.
    John 1:33 And I did not know Him, but the One who sent me to baptize in water said to me, ‘The One upon whom you see the Spirit descending and remaining—this is the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit.’

Matthew 3:17
And behold, a voice from the heavens said, “This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased.”
  Mark 1:11 — narrative parallel: Both accounts record a heavenly voice declaring Jesus the beloved Son in whom God is well pleased. Matthew presents the declaration in the third person and Mark as direct address to Jesus.
    Mark 1:11 A voice came from the heavens: “Thou art My beloved Son; in Thee I am well pleased.”
  Luke 3:22 — narrative parallel: Luke likewise places the heavenly declaration of beloved sonship and divine delight at Jesus' baptism, together with the Spirit's dove-like descent.
    Luke 3:22 and the Holy Spirit descended upon Him in bodily form like a dove. A voice came from heaven: “Thou art My beloved Son; in Thee I have taken delight.”

Matthew 4:1
Then Jesus was led up into the wilderness by the Spirit to be tempted by the devil.
  Luke 4:1–4:2 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place Jesus under the Spirit's leading in the wilderness and identify the devil as the one testing Him; Luke specifies the forty-day duration in the second verse.
    Luke 4:1 Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, returned from the Jordan and was led in the Spirit in the wilderness
    Luke 4:2 for forty days, being tested by the devil. He ate nothing during those days, and when they were completed He was hungry.
  Mark 1:12–1:13 — narrative parallel: Mark likewise joins the Spirit's action in sending Jesus into the wilderness with His temptation by Satan; Mark says the Spirit drove Him out.
    Mark 1:12 Immediately the Spirit drove Him out into the wilderness.
    Mark 1:13 He was in the wilderness forty days, being tempted by Satan. He was with the wild animals, and the angels ministered to Him.

Matthew 4:2
After fasting forty days and forty nights, He became hungry.
  Luke 4:2 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus abstaining from food for forty days and becoming hungry afterward; Matthew additionally specifies forty nights.
    Luke 4:2 for forty days, being tested by the devil. He ate nothing during those days, and when they were completed He was hungry.

Matthew 4:3
The tempter came and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, command these stones to become loaves of bread.”
  Luke 4:3 — narrative parallel: Both record the devil challenging Jesus' divine sonship by asking Him to turn stone into bread; Matthew uses plural stones and loaves, while Luke uses singular stone and bread.
    Luke 4:3 The devil said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, tell this stone to become bread.”

Matthew 4:4
But He answered, “It is written: ‘A human being shall not live on bread alone, but on every word proceeding through the mouth of God.’”
  Deuteronomy 8:3 — quotation: Jesus' answer cites Deuteronomy's contrast between living by bread alone and living by every word from God's mouth. Deuteronomy places the saying in Israel's experience of hunger and manna.
    Deuteronomy 8:3 And He afflicted you, and made you hunger, and fed you with manna, which your fathers did not know, that He might declare unto you that man shall not live by bread alone, but man shall live by every word that proceeds through the mouth of God.
  Luke 4:4 — narrative parallel: Luke also records Jesus answering the bread temptation with the written saying that a human being does not live on bread alone. Luke's quoted wording in this edition ends there, without Matthew's additional clause about every word from God.
    Luke 4:4 Jesus answered him, “It is written: ‘A human being shall not live on bread alone.’”

Matthew 4:5
Then the devil took Him into the holy city and set Him on the pinnacle of the temple,
  Luke 4:9 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place Jesus on the temple's pinnacle at the devil's instigation; Luke names Jerusalem, whereas Matthew calls it the holy city. The temptation appears in a different sequence in Luke.
    Luke 4:9 He brought Him to Jerusalem, placed Him on the temple’s pinnacle, and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, throw Thyself down from here,

Matthew 4:6
and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, throw Thyself down. For it is written: ‘He will command His angels concerning Thee,’ and ‘They will lift Thee on their hands, lest Thou strike Thy foot against a stone.’”
  Psalms 90:11–90:12 — quotation: The tempter quotes the psalm's promise of angels commanded concerning the protected person and of hands preventing the foot from striking a stone. Matthew's citation omits the psalm's clause about guarding in all one's ways. This edition numbers the psalm 90.
    Psalms 90:11 For He will command His angels concerning you, to guard you in all your ways.
    Psalms 90:12 On their hands they will lift you, lest you strike your foot against a stone.
  Luke 4:9–4:11 — narrative parallel: Luke likewise records the challenge to divine sonship, the command to jump from the temple, and the angel-protection citation about being lifted by their hands.
    Luke 4:9 He brought Him to Jerusalem, placed Him on the temple’s pinnacle, and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, throw Thyself down from here,
    Luke 4:10 for it is written: ‘He will command His angels concerning Thee, to guard Thee,’
    Luke 4:11 and, ‘They will lift Thee upon their hands, lest Thou strike Thy foot against a stone.’”

Matthew 4:7
Jesus said to him, “Again it is written: ‘You shall not put the Lord your God to the test.’”
  Deuteronomy 6:16 — quotation: Jesus counters with Deuteronomy's prohibition against putting the Lord God to the test. Deuteronomy continues by recalling Israel's testing at the place called Testing.
    Deuteronomy 6:16 You shall not put the Lord your God to the test, as you tested Him at Testing.
  Luke 4:12 — narrative parallel: Both accounts give Jesus' response to the temple challenge as the prohibition against putting the Lord God to the test.
    Luke 4:12 Jesus answered him, “It has been said: ‘You shall not put the Lord your God to the test.’”

Matthew 4:8
Again the devil took Him to an exceedingly high mountain and showed Him all the kingdoms of the world and their glory.
  Luke 4:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe the devil showing Jesus the world's kingdoms. Matthew specifies a very high mountain and their glory; Luke describes the display as occurring in a moment of time and places it earlier in the temptation sequence.
    Luke 4:5 Taking Him up, the devil showed Him all the kingdoms of the inhabited world in a moment of time.

Matthew 4:9
He said to Him, “I will give Thee all these things if Thou fall down and worship me.”
  Luke 4:6–4:7 — narrative parallel: In both accounts the devil offers the displayed kingdoms or their authority and glory in exchange for Jesus' bowing in worship. Luke additionally reports the devil's claim that their authority was handed over to him.
    Luke 4:6 The devil said to Him, “I will give Thee all this authority and their glory, because it has been handed over to me, and I give it to whomever I wish.
    Luke 4:7 Therefore, if Thou dost bow down before me, it will all be Thine.”

Matthew 4:10
Then Jesus said to him, “Go away, Satan! For it is written: ‘You shall worship the Lord your God, and Him alone shall you serve.’”
  Deuteronomy 6:13 — quotation: Jesus cites the command to worship and serve the Lord God. The Saints Deuteronomy text says to fear, serve, and cleave to Him; Matthew renders the response as worship and adds the explicit exclusivity 'Him alone.' The wordings are related without being identical.
    Deuteronomy 6:13 You shall fear the Lord your God, and you shall serve Him, and you shall cleave unto Him, and you shall swear by His name.
  Luke 4:8 — narrative parallel: Both accounts answer the demand for worship with the written requirement to worship the Lord God and serve Him alone; Matthew also commands Satan to go away.
    Luke 4:8 Jesus answered him, “It is written: ‘You shall worship the Lord your God, and Him alone shall you serve.’”

Matthew 4:11
Then the devil left Him, and behold, angels came and ministered to Him.
  Mark 1:13 — narrative parallel: Both wilderness accounts include angels ministering to Jesus. Matthew places this after the devil leaves, while Mark mentions angelic ministry within his compressed wilderness account.
    Mark 1:13 He was in the wilderness forty days, being tempted by Satan. He was with the wild animals, and the angels ministered to Him.
  Luke 4:13 — narrative parallel: Both end the temptation sequence with the devil departing. Luke specifies departure until an opportune time; the cited Luke verse does not include Matthew's angelic ministry.
    Luke 4:13 Having completed every test, the devil departed from Him until an opportune time.

Matthew 4:12
When He heard that John had been handed over, He withdrew to Galilee.
  Mark 1:14 — narrative parallel: Both relate Jesus' move into Galilee to John having been handed over; Matthew says Jesus heard of it and withdrew, whereas Mark introduces His Galilean proclamation.
    Mark 1:14 And after John was handed over, Jesus came into Galilee, proclaiming the gospel of God,

Matthew 4:13
Leaving Nazareth, He came and settled in Capernaum beside the sea, in the territories of Zebulun and Naphtali,
  Luke 4:31 — narrative parallel: Both locate Jesus' subsequent activity in Capernaum in Galilee. Matthew describes settling there after leaving Nazareth; Luke describes His arrival there and Sabbath teaching rather than a settlement notice.
    Luke 4:31 He went down to Capernaum, a city of Galilee, and was teaching them on the Sabbaths.

Matthew 4:14
so that what was spoken through Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled:
  Isaiah 8:23–9:1 — promise and fulfillment: Matthew introduces the following Isaiah citation as fulfilled through Jesus' move into the Galilean region. The cited Isaiah verses name Zebulun, Naphtali, the sea route and Galilee, then announce light for those in darkness; fulfillment is Matthew's stated interpretation.
    Isaiah 8:23 and the one in confinement will not remain perplexed for a time. “Do this first, do it quickly, country of Zebulun, land of Naphtali, way of the sea, and the rest who dwell by the sea and beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations, the regions of Judea.”
    Isaiah 9:1 You people who walk in darkness, behold a great light; you who dwell in a land and shadow of death, a light will shine upon you.

Matthew 4:15
“Land of Zebulun and land of Naphtali, the way of the sea, beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations:
  Isaiah 8:23 — quotation: Matthew cites Isaiah's geographical sequence of Zebulun, Naphtali, the sea route, beyond the Jordan, and Galilee of the nations. The verse is Isaiah 8:23 in this edition; its longer wording continues beyond Matthew's excerpt.
    Isaiah 8:23 and the one in confinement will not remain perplexed for a time. “Do this first, do it quickly, country of Zebulun, land of Naphtali, way of the sea, and the rest who dwell by the sea and beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations, the regions of Judea.”

Matthew 4:16
the people sitting in darkness saw a great light, and for those sitting in the region and shadow of death a light rose.”
  Isaiah 9:1 — quotation: Both texts announce great light for people in darkness and light for those in the land or shadow of death. Isaiah addresses people walking in darkness, whereas Matthew describes people sitting in darkness. This edition labels the Isaiah verse 9:1.
    Isaiah 9:1 You people who walk in darkness, behold a great light; you who dwell in a land and shadow of death, a light will shine upon you.

Matthew 4:17
From then on Jesus began to proclaim, “Repent, for the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.”
  Mark 1:14–1:15 — narrative parallel: Both summarize the start of Jesus' Galilean proclamation with the nearness of God's kingdom and a call to repent. Mark also calls for belief in the gospel and says the appointed time is fulfilled.
    Mark 1:14 And after John was handed over, Jesus came into Galilee, proclaiming the gospel of God,
    Mark 1:15 and saying, “The appointed time is fulfilled, and the kingdom of God has drawn near; repent, and believe in the gospel.”

Matthew 4:18
As He walked beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw two brothers, Simon called Peter and Andrew his brother, casting a net into the sea, for they were fishermen.
  Mark 1:16 — narrative parallel: Both accounts show Jesus beside the Sea of Galilee seeing the brothers Simon and Andrew casting a net, and both identify them as fishermen. Matthew additionally identifies Simon as Peter.
    Mark 1:16 And passing beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw Simon and Andrew the brother of Simon casting a net in the sea, for they were fishermen.

Matthew 4:19
He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you fishers of people.”
  Mark 1:17 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus calling the fishermen to come after Him and promising to make them fishers of people or men.
    Mark 1:17 And He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you become fishers of men.”
  Luke 5:10 — thematic parallel: Jesus' promise of fishing for people corresponds to His promise to Simon in Luke that he will catch people alive. Luke places the saying after the extraordinary catch of fish, so the comparison does not assume identical narrative circumstances.
    Luke 5:10 and likewise James and John, Zebedee’s sons, who were Simon’s partners. Jesus said to Simon, “Do not fear. From now on you will be catching people alive.”

Matthew 4:20
Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.
  Mark 1:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe the brothers' immediate response as leaving their nets and following Jesus.
    Mark 1:18 Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

Matthew 4:21
Going on from there, He saw two other brothers, James the son of Zebedee and John his brother, in the boat with Zebedee their father, mending their nets; and He called them.
  Mark 1:19–1:20 — narrative parallel: Both identify James and John as Zebedee's sons, place them in a boat attending to their nets, and record Jesus calling them. Matthew places their father in the boat in this verse, while Mark mentions him in the response.
    Mark 1:19 Going a little farther, He saw James son of Zebedee and John his brother, who were also in the boat, putting the nets in order.
    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

Matthew 4:22
Immediately they left the boat and their father and followed Him.
  Mark 1:20 — narrative parallel: Both describe Zebedee's sons leaving their father in response to Jesus' call and going after Him. Mark additionally mentions hired workers in the boat.
    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

Matthew 4:23
He went throughout Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the good news of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity among the people.
  Matthew 9:35 — thematic parallel: The later Matthean summary repeats the combination of synagogue teaching, proclamation of the kingdom's good news, and healing every disease and infirmity; here the region is Galilee, while Matthew 9:35 refers to cities and villages.
    Matthew 9:35 And Jesus went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the gospel of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity.
  Mark 1:39 — narrative parallel: Both summarize Jesus traveling throughout Galilee and proclaiming in the synagogues. Matthew adds broad healing activity; Mark's summary explicitly mentions casting out demons.
    Mark 1:39 He went throughout all Galilee, proclaiming in their synagogues and casting out the demons.

Matthew 4:24
The report about Him spread throughout Syria, and they brought Him all who were ill, afflicted with various diseases and torments, those oppressed by demons, those suffering seizures, and paralytics; and He healed them.
  Luke 4:40–4:41 — thematic parallel: Both describe sick people being brought to Jesus, His healing varied diseases, and His dealing with demons. Luke gives an evening scene and the demons' cries; Matthew gives a wider regional summary including Syria, seizures, and paralysis, so this link compares the activity without equating the settings.
    Luke 4:40 As the sun was setting, everyone who had people sick with various diseases brought them to Him. He laid His hands on each one and healed them.
    Luke 4:41 Demons also came out of many, crying, “Thou art God’s Son!” He rebuked them and would not let them speak, because they knew He was the Christ.

Matthew 4:25
Great crowds followed Him from Galilee, the Decapolis, Jerusalem, Judea, and beyond the Jordan.
  Mark 3:7–3:8 — thematic parallel: Both crowd summaries name Galilee, Judea, Jerusalem, and beyond the Jordan as regions from which people followed or came to Jesus. Matthew additionally names the Decapolis; Mark additionally names Idumea and the Tyre-Sidon region. The overlapping geography does not prove a single occasion.
    Mark 3:7 Jesus withdrew with His disciples to the sea, and a great multitude from Galilee followed; and from Judea,
    Mark 3:8 Jerusalem, Idumea, beyond the Jordan, and the region of Tyre and Sidon, a great multitude heard all He was doing and came to Him.

Matthew 5:1
And seeing the crowds, He went up the mountain; and when He had sat down, His disciples came to Him.
  Luke 6:17–6:20 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus teaching disciples in the presence of a crowd before the beatitudes. The settings differ: Matthew has Jesus ascend and sit on the mountain, whereas Luke has Him descend to a level place; the comparison does not erase that difference.
    Luke 6:17 He came down with them and stood on a level place. There was a large crowd of His disciples and a great multitude of the people from all Judea, Jerusalem, and the coastal region of Tyre and Sidon.
    Luke 6:18 They had come to hear Him and be healed of their diseases; those troubled by unclean spirits were also healed.
    Luke 6:19 The whole crowd sought to touch Him, because power went out from Him and healed everyone.
    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

Matthew 5:2
And He opened His mouth and taught them, saying:
  Luke 6:20 — teaching parallel: Matthew introduces Jesus addressing the disciples and then beginning the beatitudes; Luke likewise directs His gaze and opening blessing toward His disciples.
    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

Matthew 5:3
“Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of the heavens.
  Luke 6:20 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce the poor blessed and promise them the kingdom. Matthew specifies poor in spirit and kingdom of the heavens, while Luke addresses the poor and names God's kingdom.
    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

Matthew 5:4
Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted.
  Luke 6:21 — teaching parallel: Matthew promises comfort to mourners; Luke promises future laughter to those who weep now. Both blessings reverse present sorrow without using identical wording.
    Luke 6:21 Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.

Matthew 5:5
Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth.
  Psalms 36:11 — allusion: The promise that the meek will inherit the earth closely recalls the psalm's promise that the meek will inherit the land and delight in peace. The target is Greek-numbered Psalm 36 in this edition.
    Psalms 36:11 But the meek will inherit the land and delight themselves in abundant peace.

Matthew 5:6
Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be filled.
  Luke 6:21 — teaching parallel: Both bless those who hunger and promise that they will be filled. Matthew explicitly makes righteousness the object of hunger and adds thirst; Luke speaks of those who hunger now.
    Luke 6:21 Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.

Matthew 5:7
Blessed are the merciful, for they shall receive mercy.
  James 2:13 — thematic parallel: Matthew connects showing mercy with receiving mercy; James warns that judgment is without mercy for the person who has shown none and says mercy triumphs over judgment.
    James 2:13 For judgment is without mercy to the one who has shown no mercy; mercy triumphs over judgment.

Matthew 5:8
Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God.
  Psalms 23:3–23:4 — thematic parallel: Matthew associates purity of heart with seeing God; the psalm describes the person with clean hands and a pure heart as qualified to stand in the Lord's holy place. This is a shared association between inward purity and access to God, not an identical promise.
    Psalms 23:3 Who will ascend the Lord’s mountain, and who will stand in His holy place?
    Psalms 23:4 One innocent in hands and pure in heart, who did not take up his soul for emptiness and did not swear deceitfully to his neighbor.

Matthew 5:9
Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God.
  James 3:18 — thematic parallel: Matthew blesses peacemakers and calls them God's sons; James describes a harvest of righteousness sown in peace for those who make peace. The common subject is active peacemaking; James does not repeat the sonship promise.
    James 3:18 And a fruit of righteousness is sown in peace for those who make peace.

Matthew 5:10
Blessed are those who have been persecuted for righteousness’ sake, for theirs is the kingdom of the heavens.
  1 Peter 3:14 — teaching parallel: Both passages pronounce people blessed when they suffer for righteousness. Matthew adds the kingdom promise, while Peter tells the sufferers not to fear.
    1 Peter 3:14 But even if you should suffer for righteousness, you are blessed. Do not fear their fear, and do not be troubled,

Matthew 5:11
Blessed are you when they revile you and persecute you and falsely speak every evil thing against you for My sake.
  Luke 6:22 — teaching parallel: Both bless disciples who are reviled and socially rejected because of their association with Jesus. Matthew emphasizes false evil accusations for His sake; Luke speaks of hatred and exclusion because of the Son of Man.
    Luke 6:22 Blessed are you when people hate you, when they exclude you, reproach you, and cast out your name as evil because of the Son of Man.

Matthew 5:12
Rejoice and be glad, for great is your reward in the heavens; for thus they persecuted the prophets before you.
  Luke 6:23 — teaching parallel: Both command joy amid persecution, promise a great heavenly reward, and compare the disciples' treatment with the persecution of earlier prophets.
    Luke 6:23 Rejoice on that day and leap, for behold, your reward is great in heaven; their fathers treated the prophets in the same way.

Matthew 5:13
You are the salt of the earth. But if the salt becomes tasteless, how shall it be made salty? It is no longer good for anything except to be thrown out and trampled underfoot by people.
  Luke 14:34–14:35 — teaching parallel: Both use salt that has lost its taste to describe something that can no longer serve its purpose and is thrown away. Matthew adds the disciples' identity as the earth's salt and the image of trampling.
    Luke 14:34 Salt, then, is good. But if even the salt becomes tasteless, with what will it be seasoned?
    Luke 14:35 It is fit neither for the soil nor for the manure heap; they throw it outside. Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”

Matthew 5:14
You are the light of the world. A city set upon a mountain cannot be hidden.
  Philippians 2:15 — thematic parallel: Matthew calls the disciples the world's light; Paul describes believers shining as lights in the world amid a crooked generation. Philippians shares the public witness image without repeating Matthew's hilltop city.
    Philippians 2:15 so that you may become blameless and innocent, children of God without blemish in the midst of a crooked and perverted generation, among whom you shine as lights in the world,

Matthew 5:15
Nor do people light a lamp and place it beneath the modius, but upon the lampstand; and it shines for everyone in the house.
  Luke 11:33 — teaching parallel: Both say a lit lamp belongs on a lampstand rather than under a modius so that people may see its light. The household lamp supplies the same illustration in each teaching.
    Luke 11:33 No one who lights a lamp puts it in a hidden place or under the modius basket, but on the lampstand, so that those who enter may see its light.

Matthew 5:16
So let your light shine before people, that they may see your good works and glorify your Father who is in the heavens.
  1 Peter 2:12 — teaching parallel: Both connect visible good works with other people glorifying God. Matthew tells disciples to let their light shine, while Peter applies good conduct to a setting where outsiders slander believers.
    1 Peter 2:12 keeping your conduct good among the Gentiles, so that, in the very matter for which they slander you as evildoers, they may observe your good works and glorify God on the day of visitation.

Matthew 5:17
Do not think that I came to abolish the Law or the Prophets. I did not come to abolish, but to fulfil.
  Romans 3:31 — thematic parallel: Jesus denies abolishing the Law and Prophets and speaks of fulfillment; Paul likewise denies that faith nullifies law and says it establishes law. The comparison concerns refusal to discard the law, without treating the two arguments as identical.
    Romans 3:31 Do we then nullify law through faith? May it never be! Rather, we establish law.

Matthew 5:18
For truly I tell you: until heaven and earth pass away, not one iota nor one stroke shall pass from the Law, until all things come to pass.
  Luke 16:17 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the passing of heaven and earth with the enduring smallest written detail of the Law. Luke's small stroke corresponds to Matthew's iota and stroke.
    Luke 16:17 But it is easier for heaven and earth to pass away than for one small stroke of the Law to fall.

Matthew 5:19
Whoever therefore loosens one of these least commandments and teaches people to do so shall be called least in the kingdom of the heavens; but whoever does and teaches them shall be called great in the kingdom of the heavens.
  James 2:10 — thematic parallel: Matthew warns against loosening even a least commandment; James says stumbling in one point makes a person accountable for the whole law. Both resist treating part of God's law as dispensable.
    James 2:10 For whoever keeps the whole law but stumbles in one point has become accountable for all of it.

Matthew 5:20
For I tell you: unless your righteousness exceeds that of the scribes and Pharisees, you shall by no means enter the kingdom of the heavens.
  Matthew 23:25–23:28 — thematic parallel: The later denunciation explains a concrete defect in the scribes' and Pharisees' righteousness: an outwardly righteous appearance can conceal hypocrisy and lawlessness. It helps illuminate the demand for righteousness exceeding theirs without equating every member of those groups with the criticism.
    Matthew 23:25 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you cleanse the outside of the cup and dish, but inside they are full of plunder and self-indulgence!
    Matthew 23:26 Blind Pharisee, first cleanse the inside of the cup, so that its outside may also become clean.
    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!
    Matthew 23:28 So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.

Matthew 5:21
You have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not murder’; and ‘whoever murders shall be liable to judgment.’
  Exodus 20:15 — quotation: The command not to murder repeats the Decalogue prohibition. In this Saints Greek-based inventory it is Exodus 20:15; the command itself does not include Matthew's following judicial explanation.
    Exodus 20:15 You shall not murder.

Matthew 5:22
But I tell you that everyone who is angry with his brother shall be liable to judgment; and whoever says to his brother, ‘Raca,’ shall be liable to the council; and whoever says, ‘Fool,’ shall be liable to the Gehenna of fire.
  1 John 3:15 — teaching parallel: Matthew brings anger toward a brother under judgment; John calls hatred of a brother murder. Both extend concern beyond the outward act of homicide to the inward hostility behind it.
    1 John 3:15 Everyone who hates his brother is a murderer, and you know that no murderer has eternal life abiding in him.

Matthew 5:23
If therefore you offer your gift upon the altar, and there remember that your brother has something against you,
  Mark 11:25 — thematic parallel: Both bring damaged relationships into the setting of worship. Matthew asks the offerer to remember a brother's grievance, while Mark tells a praying person to forgive his own grievance against another; the directions of the grievance differ.
    Mark 11:25 Whenever you stand praying, forgive if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father in the heavens may also forgive your trespasses.”

Matthew 5:24
leave your gift there before the altar, and go; first be reconciled to your brother, and then come and offer your gift.
  Romans 12:18 — thematic parallel: Matthew requires the worshiper first to seek reconciliation with his brother; Paul tells believers, as far as it depends on them, to live at peace with everyone. Both demand active efforts toward peaceful relationships.
    Romans 12:18 If possible, as far as it depends on you, live at peace with all people.

Matthew 5:25
Come to terms with your adversary quickly, while you are with him on the way, lest your adversary hand you over to the judge, and the judge to the officer, and you be thrown into prison.
  Luke 12:58 — teaching parallel: Both urge reaching a settlement with an adversary while still on the way, before the adversary hands the debtor to a judge and officer and imprisonment follows.
    Luke 12:58 For as you go with your adversary before a ruler, make an effort on the way to be released from him, lest he drag you before the judge, and the judge hand you over to the officer, and the officer throw you into prison.

Matthew 5:26
Truly I tell you: you shall by no means come out of there until you have paid the last quadrans.
  Luke 12:59 — teaching parallel: Both conclude the adversary-and-prison saying by warning that release will wait until the smallest outstanding coin is paid. Matthew names a quadrans and Luke a lepton.
    Luke 12:59 I tell you, you will certainly not come out from there until you have paid even the last lepton.”

Matthew 5:27
You have heard that it was said: ‘You shall not commit adultery.’
  Exodus 20:13 — quotation: The prohibition of adultery repeats the Decalogue command. This Saints Greek-based edition places it at Exodus 20:13, rather than silently adopting another ordering of the commandments.
    Exodus 20:13 You shall not commit adultery.

Matthew 5:28
