But I tell you that everyone who looks at a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her in his heart.
  Job 31:1 — thematic parallel: Matthew condemns a lustful look as inward adultery; Job describes a covenant governing his eyes so that he will not direct his attention toward a virgin. Both connect sexual integrity with disciplined looking, though Job does not formulate Matthew's judgment of the heart.
    Job 31:1 “I established a covenant for my eyes, and I will not direct my attention toward a virgin.

Matthew 5:29
If your right eye causes you to stumble, pluck it out and throw it away from you; for it is better for you that one of your members perish than that your whole body be thrown into Gehenna.
  Mark 9:47–9:48 — teaching parallel: Both use the severe image of discarding an eye that causes stumbling and contrast losing one member with being thrown into Gehenna. Mark continues with the description of undying worm and fire.
    Mark 9:47 If your eye causes you to stumble, cast it out. It is better for you to enter the kingdom of God with one eye than to have two eyes and be thrown into Gehenna,
    Mark 9:48 where their worm does not die and the fire is not extinguished.

Matthew 5:30
And if your right hand causes you to stumble, cut it off and throw it away from you; for it is better for you that one of your members perish than that your whole body go into Gehenna.
  Mark 9:43 — teaching parallel: Both use cutting off a hand that causes stumbling to stress the greater danger of Gehenna. Matthew contrasts the loss of one member with the whole body; Mark contrasts entering life maimed with retaining two hands.
    Mark 9:43 If your hand causes you to stumble, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life maimed than to have two hands and go away into Gehenna, into the unquenchable fire.

Matthew 5:31
And it was said: ‘Whoever divorces his wife, let him give her a certificate of divorce.’
  Deuteronomy 24:1 — quotation: Matthew's saying about giving a certificate of divorce recalls the certificate in Deuteronomy's case law. Deuteronomy describes the written certificate within a legal case; this comparison does not turn that description into an unrestricted command to divorce.
    Deuteronomy 24:1 And if a man takes a wife and dwells with her as husband, and she finds no favour in his sight, because he has found some unseemly thing in her, and he writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house,

Matthew 5:32
But I tell you that everyone who divorces his wife, except on account of sexual immorality, causes her to commit adultery; and whoever marries a divorced woman commits adultery.
  Luke 16:18 — teaching parallel: Both connect divorce and remarriage with adultery and specifically mention marrying a divorced woman. Matthew includes a sexual-immorality qualification and the husband's responsibility for causing adultery; Luke's wording here contains neither qualification.
    Luke 16:18 Everyone who divorces his wife and marries another commits adultery, and the one who marries a woman divorced from a husband commits adultery.

Matthew 5:33
Again, you have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not swear falsely, but shall fulfil your oaths to the Lord.’
  Leviticus 19:12 — allusion: Matthew's prohibition of swearing falsely recalls Leviticus's ban on swearing by God's name for an unjust cause and profaning His name. The shared concern is misuse of an oath invoking God.
    Leviticus 19:12 And you shall not swear by My name for an unjust cause, and you shall not profane the name of your God. I am the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 23:22–23:24 — allusion: Matthew's instruction to fulfill oaths to the Lord corresponds to Deuteronomy's requirement not to delay paying a vow and to do what has proceeded from one's lips. Deuteronomy also distinguishes a voluntarily unmade vow from a vow that binds its maker.
    Deuteronomy 23:22 And if you vow a vow unto the Lord your God, you shall not delay to pay it; for the Lord your God shall surely require it of you, and there shall be sin upon you.
    Deuteronomy 23:23 But if you do not desire to vow, there is no sin upon you.
    Deuteronomy 23:24 That which proceeds through your lips you shall keep and do, as you have vowed a gift unto the Lord your God, which you have spoken with your mouth.

Matthew 5:34
But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,
  Isaiah 66:1 — allusion: Jesus explains that heaven must not be used as an oath object because it is God's throne; Isaiah explicitly calls heaven the Lord's throne. The throne image supplies the stated reason, while Isaiah is not itself an oath prohibition.
    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

Matthew 5:35
nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.
  Isaiah 66:1 — allusion: Matthew's description of earth as the footstool of God's feet closely recalls Isaiah's description of earth as His footstool. Jesus applies the image to the prohibition on swearing by the earth.
    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?
  Psalms 47:3 — allusion: The designation of Jerusalem as the city of the great King recalls the psalm's description of Zion as the city of the great King. The target retains this edition's Greek numbering, Psalm 47:3.
    Psalms 47:3 taking good root for the exultation of all the earth. Zion’s mountains, the sides of the north, the city of the great King.

Matthew 5:36
Nor shall you swear by your head, for you cannot make one hair white or black.
  James 5:12 — teaching parallel: Matthew forbids swearing by one's head and gives human inability to control even one hair's color as a reason; James forbids any other oath in addition to heaven and earth. The general oath prohibition corresponds, though James does not repeat the hair illustration.
    James 5:12 But above all, my brothers, do not swear, either by heaven or by earth or by any other oath. Let your yes be yes, and your no be no, so that you may not fall under judgment.

Matthew 5:37
But let your word be ‘Yes, yes’; ‘No, no.’ Whatever is beyond these is of evil.
  James 5:12 — teaching parallel: Both replace oath-taking with a dependable yes and no. James repeats the paired yes and no and connects the instruction with avoiding judgment.
    James 5:12 But above all, my brothers, do not swear, either by heaven or by earth or by any other oath. Let your yes be yes, and your no be no, so that you may not fall under judgment.

Matthew 5:38
You have heard that it was said: ‘An eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth.’
  Exodus 21:24 — quotation: Matthew's eye-for-eye and tooth-for-tooth formula repeats the matching clauses in Exodus's legal rule of corresponding injury. The target also continues the series with hand and foot.
    Exodus 21:24 eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot,

Matthew 5:39
But I tell you: do not resist the evildoer; but whoever strikes you upon your right cheek, turn the other to him also.
  Luke 6:29 — teaching parallel: Both instruct a person struck on one cheek to offer the other. Matthew places the instruction under not resisting the evildoer and specifies the right cheek.
    Luke 6:29 Offer the other cheek also to the one striking you on the cheek; and from the one taking your cloak, do not withhold your tunic also.

Matthew 5:40
And if anyone would sue you and take your tunic, let him have your cloak also.
  Luke 6:29 — teaching parallel: Both pair tunic and cloak in an instruction to surrender more than an opponent takes. Matthew begins with a lawsuit over the tunic and adds the cloak; Luke begins with the cloak and says not to withhold the tunic.
    Luke 6:29 Offer the other cheek also to the one striking you on the cheek; and from the one taking your cloak, do not withhold your tunic also.

Matthew 5:41
And whoever compels you to go one Roman mile, go with him two.
  Matthew 27:32 — thematic parallel: compulsory service: Both passages explicitly describe being compelled to perform service. The instruction about going a Roman mile is compared with Simon being compelled to carry the cross. The cross-bearing account supplies neither a mile distance nor a second-mile response; this is a concrete thematic comparison, not an identification of commands or occasions.
    Matthew 27:32 As they went out, they found a Cyrenian man named Simon. They compelled him to carry His cross.

Matthew 5:42
Give to the one who asks you, and do not turn away from the one who would borrow from you.
  Luke 6:30 — teaching parallel: Both tell disciples to give to the person who asks. Matthew adds not turning away a prospective borrower; Luke adds not demanding possessions back from someone who takes them.
    Luke 6:30 Give to everyone who asks you, and do not demand back your belongings from the one who takes them.

Matthew 5:43
You have heard that it was said: ‘You shall love your neighbour and hate your enemy.’
  Leviticus 19:18 — quotation: The love-your-neighbor portion recalls Leviticus's command to love the neighbor as oneself. Leviticus also forbids vengeance and lingering anger; it does not supply Matthew's accompanying hate-your-enemy clause.
    Leviticus 19:18 And your hand shall not take vengeance, neither shall you remain angry with the sons of your people; and you shall love your neighbour as yourself; I am the Lord.

Matthew 5:44
But I tell you: love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute you,
  Luke 6:27–6:28 — teaching parallel: Both command love for enemies and prayer for those who mistreat the disciples. Luke also specifies doing good to haters and blessing those who curse.
    Luke 6:27 But I say to you who hear: Love your enemies; do good to those who hate you.
    Luke 6:28 Bless those who curse you; pray for those who mistreat you.

Matthew 5:45
that you may become sons of your Father who is in the heavens; for He makes His sun rise upon the evil and the good, and sends rain upon the righteous and the unrighteous.
  Luke 6:35 — teaching parallel: Both connect loving enemies with becoming children of God and ground that conduct in God's kindness toward evil people. Matthew illustrates indiscriminate beneficence with sunshine and rain; Luke mentions kindness to the ungrateful and evil.
    Luke 6:35 But love your enemies, do good, and lend, expecting nothing back. Your reward will be great, and you will be sons of the Most High, because He is kind to the ungrateful and evil.

Matthew 5:46
For if you love those who love you, what reward do you have? Do not even the tax collectors do the same?
  Luke 6:32 — teaching parallel: Both ask what reward or credit there is in loving only those who return love, since people outside the disciples' community also do that. Matthew uses tax collectors as the comparison; Luke uses sinners.
    Luke 6:32 If you love those who love you, what credit is that to you? For even sinners love those who love them.

Matthew 5:47
And if you greet only your brothers, what are you doing beyond others? Do not even the Gentiles do the same?
  Luke 6:33 — teaching parallel: Both challenge conduct limited to reciprocating ordinary social goodwill. Matthew's example is greeting only brothers, while Luke's is doing good only to those who do good in return; these are corresponding arguments with different examples.
    Luke 6:33 If you do good to those who do good to you, what credit is that to you? Even sinners do the same.

Matthew 5:48
You shall therefore be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
  Luke 6:36 — teaching parallel: Both end the enemy-love teaching by calling disciples to model their conduct on their Father. Matthew uses perfect and Luke uses merciful; the wording and emphasis remain distinct.
    Luke 6:36 Be merciful, just as your Father is merciful.

Matthew 6:1
Take care not to practice your righteousness before people in order to be seen by them; otherwise you have no reward with your Father in the heavens.
  Matthew 23:5 — teaching parallel: The later criticism that religious leaders do their works to be seen by people directly parallels the warning against practicing righteousness for the purpose of being seen. Matthew 6 adds the consequence of losing the Father's reward.
    Matthew 23:5 They do all their works to be seen by people. They broaden their phylacteries and enlarge their fringes.

Matthew 6:2
So when you give alms, do not sound a trumpet before you as the hypocrites do in the synagogues and streets, so that people may glorify them. Truly I tell you, they have received their reward in full.
  Proverbs 19:17 — thematic parallel: Both connect giving to the poor with repayment. Proverbs names God as the one who repays the gift; Matthew warns that public praise can become the ostentatious giver's entire reward.
    Proverbs 19:17 One who has mercy on the poor lends to God, and He will repay him according to his gift.

Matthew 6:3
But when you give alms, do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing,
  Matthew 6:4 — internal purpose, contrast, referent or explanation: The explicit so-that clause states the secrecy purpose of the left/right-hand image and names the Father's seeing and repayment. It explains the image's stated aim without assigning an allegorical identity to either hand.
    Matthew 6:4 so that your almsgiving may be in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.

Matthew 6:4
so that your almsgiving may be in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.
  Proverbs 19:17 — thematic parallel: Matthew promises the Father's repayment for alms given in secret; Proverbs says the giver who has mercy on the poor lends to God and will be repaid by Him. The shared point is divine repayment, while secrecy is specific to Matthew.
    Proverbs 19:17 One who has mercy on the poor lends to God, and He will repay him according to his gift.

Matthew 6:5
And when you pray, you shall not be like the hypocrites, because they love to pray standing in synagogues and at street corners, so that people may see them. Truly I tell you, they have received their reward in full.
  Matthew 23:5–23:6 — thematic parallel: Matthew 6 criticizes prayers staged for public visibility in synagogues and streets; the later passage criticizes works performed to be seen and love of prominent synagogue seats. Both expose religious conduct arranged for public honor.
    Matthew 23:5 They do all their works to be seen by people. They broaden their phylacteries and enlarge their fringes.
    Matthew 23:6 They love the foremost place at dinners and the foremost seats in the synagogues,

Matthew 6:6
But you, when you pray, enter your inner room, shut your door, and pray to your Father who is in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.
  2 Kings 4:33 — thematic parallel: Jesus instructs a person to shut the door and pray privately; Elisha is narrated entering the house, shutting the door behind himself and the child, and praying to the Lord. The shared action illustrates secluded prayer without making Elisha's emergency identical to the command's setting.
    2 Kings 4:33 Elisha entered the house, shut the door behind the two of them, and prayed to the Lord.

Matthew 6:7
When praying, do not babble like the Gentiles, for they think they will be heard through their many words.
  Ecclesiastes 5:1–5:2 — thematic parallel: Matthew rejects the idea that many words secure a hearing from God; Ecclesiastes urges few words before God and associates a fool's voice with many words. Both caution against unrestrained religious speech.
    Ecclesiastes 5:1 Do not be hasty with your mouth, and do not let your heart hurry to bring out a word before God, for God is in heaven and you are on earth. Therefore let your words be few.
    Ecclesiastes 5:2 For a dream comes with much distraction, and a fool’s voice with many words.

Matthew 6:8
So do not become like them, for your Father knows what you need before you ask Him.
  Luke 12:30 — teaching parallel: Both ground trust in the Father in His prior knowledge of human needs. Matthew applies that knowledge to prayer; Luke applies it to seeking food and drink.
    Luke 12:30 for the nations of the world seek after all these things, and your Father knows that you need them.

Matthew 6:9
Pray, then, in this way: ‘Our Father who art in the heavens, hallowed be Thy name.
  Luke 11:2 — teaching parallel: Both model prayer by addressing the Father and asking that His name be hallowed. Matthew expands the address to our Father in the heavens.
    Luke 11:2 He said to them, “When you pray, say: ‘Father, let Thy name be hallowed. Let Thy kingdom come.

Matthew 6:10
Thy kingdom come; Thy will be done, as in heaven, so also upon earth.
  Luke 11:2 — teaching parallel: Both prayers ask for the coming of God's kingdom. Matthew also asks that God's will be done on earth as in heaven; that additional petition is absent from this verse of Luke's supplied text.
    Luke 11:2 He said to them, “When you pray, say: ‘Father, let Thy name be hallowed. Let Thy kingdom come.

Matthew 6:11
Give us this day our daily bread;
  Luke 11:3 — teaching parallel: Both model a petition for daily bread. Matthew says this day, while Luke says day by day.
    Luke 11:3 Give us day by day our daily bread.

Matthew 6:12
and forgive us our debts, as we also have forgiven our debtors;
  Luke 11:4 — teaching parallel: Both connect the petition for divine forgiveness with forgiving others. Matthew speaks of debts and debtors; Luke asks forgiveness of sins while retaining the language of what others owe.
    Luke 11:4 And forgive us our sins, for we ourselves also forgive everyone who owes us. And do not bring us into testing.’”

Matthew 6:13
and bring us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.’
  Luke 11:4 — teaching parallel: Both prayers ask not to be brought into temptation or testing. Matthew continues with deliverance from evil; Luke's supplied wording ends with the testing petition and does not include that additional clause.
    Luke 11:4 And forgive us our sins, for we ourselves also forgive everyone who owes us. And do not bring us into testing.’”

Matthew 6:14
For if you forgive people their trespasses, your heavenly Father will also forgive you.
  Mark 11:25 — teaching parallel: Both connect forgiving other people with the heavenly Father's forgiveness of the worshiper's trespasses. Mark places the instruction within standing prayer.
    Mark 11:25 Whenever you stand praying, forgive if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father in the heavens may also forgive your trespasses.”

Matthew 6:15
But if you do not forgive people, neither will your Father forgive your trespasses.
  Mark 11:25 — teaching parallel: Matthew states negatively the condition that Mark expresses positively: refusing to forgive others is incompatible with receiving the Father's forgiveness of one's own trespasses. Mark does not separately repeat Matthew's negative sentence.
    Mark 11:25 Whenever you stand praying, forgive if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father in the heavens may also forgive your trespasses.”

Matthew 6:16
And when you fast, do not become gloomy like the hypocrites, for they disfigure their faces so that people may see they are fasting. Truly I tell you, they have received their reward in full.
  Isaiah 58:5 — thematic parallel: Matthew rejects conspicuously miserable fasting staged for human recognition; Isaiah rejects the assumption that bowed posture, sackcloth, and ashes by themselves constitute an acceptable fast. Both challenge outward displays of fasting, though Isaiah's passage focuses on the fast God chooses.
    Isaiah 58:5 I did not choose this fast, or a day for a person to humble his life. Even if you bend your neck like a ring and spread sackcloth and ashes beneath you, you will not thereby call it an acceptable fast.

Matthew 6:17
But you, when fasting, anoint your head and wash your face,
  2 Samuel 12:20–12:23 — thematic parallel: Matthew tells a person who is fasting to wash and anoint so the fast is not displayed. David washes and anoints after the death that ends his fast, and the servants question that change. The same grooming acts have different relations to fasting; David is not represented as following the instruction to conceal a continuing fast.
    2 Samuel 12:20 David rose from the ground, washed, anointed himself, and changed his clothes. He entered God’s house and bowed down to Him. Then he entered his own house and asked for bread to eat; they set bread before him, and he ate.
    2 Samuel 12:21 His servants said to him, “What is this that you have done? While the child was still alive, you fasted and wept and kept vigil, but when the child died, you rose and ate bread and drank.”
    2 Samuel 12:22 David said, “While the child was still alive, I fasted and wept, because I said, ‘Who knows whether the Lord will have mercy on me and the child will live?’
    2 Samuel 12:23 But now he has died. Why should I fast? Can I bring him back again? I will go to him, but he will not return to me.”

Matthew 6:18
so that your fasting may be seen by your Father who is in secret, rather than by people; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.
  Matthew 6:4 — teaching parallel: This explicitly same-chapter comparison repeats the promise of repayment by the Father who sees in secret: Matthew 6:4 applies it to almsgiving, while 6:18 applies it to fasting.
    Matthew 6:4 so that your almsgiving may be in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.

Matthew 6:19
Do not store up treasures for yourselves upon earth, where moth and consumption destroy and where thieves break through and steal.
  Luke 12:33 — teaching parallel: Matthew warns against earthly treasure vulnerable to moth and theft; Luke urges an unfailing heavenly treasure where no thief approaches and no moth destroys. Both contrast fragile possessions with secure heavenly treasure.
    Luke 12:33 Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.

Matthew 6:20
But store up treasures for yourselves in heaven, where neither moth nor consumption destroys and where thieves neither break through nor steal.
  Luke 12:33 — teaching parallel: Both command the accumulation of heavenly treasure protected from thieves and moths. Luke connects that instruction with selling possessions and giving alms.
    Luke 12:33 Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.

Matthew 6:21
For where your treasure is, there your heart will also be.
  Luke 12:34 — teaching parallel: Both give the same reason for choosing one's treasure carefully: the heart will be where the treasure is.
    Luke 12:34 For where your treasure is, there your heart will be also.

Matthew 6:22
The lamp of the body is the eye. If therefore your eye is sound, your whole body will be full of light.
  Luke 11:34 — teaching parallel: Both call the eye the lamp of the body and connect a sound or single eye with the whole body being full of light.
    Luke 11:34 Your eye is the lamp of the body. When your eye is single, your whole body is also full of light; but when it is evil, your body is also dark.

Matthew 6:23
But if your eye is evil, your whole body will be dark. So if the light within you is darkness, how great is the darkness!
  Luke 11:34–11:35 — teaching parallel: Both contrast an evil eye and bodily darkness with true light, and warn that what seems to be light within a person can be darkness. Matthew intensifies the warning with the question about how great that darkness is.
    Luke 11:34 Your eye is the lamp of the body. When your eye is single, your whole body is also full of light; but when it is evil, your body is also dark.
    Luke 11:35 Watch, therefore, that the light within you is not darkness.

Matthew 6:24
No one can serve as a slave to two masters: for he will either hate the one and love the other, or hold to one and despise the other. You cannot serve as a slave to God and mammon.
  Luke 16:13 — teaching parallel: Both use slavery to two masters to illustrate incompatible allegiance: love and loyalty to one oppose love and loyalty to the other, so a person cannot serve God and mammon together.
    Luke 16:13 No household servant can serve two masters as a slave. For either he will hate the one and love the other, or he will hold to the one and despise the other. You cannot serve God and mammon.”

Matthew 6:25
Therefore I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you will eat or what you will drink, or about your body, what you will wear. Is life not more than food, and the body more than clothing?
  Luke 12:22–12:23 — teaching parallel: Both tell disciples not to be anxious about food or clothing and reason that life is more than food and the body more than clothing. Matthew includes drinking among the immediate concerns.
    Luke 12:22 And He said to His disciples, “For this reason I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you will eat, or about your body, what you will wear.
    Luke 12:23 For life is more than food, and the body more than clothing.

Matthew 6:26
Look at the birds of the sky: they do not sow or reap or gather into storehouses, yet your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not worth more than they?
  Luke 12:24 — teaching parallel: Both point to birds that neither sow nor reap nor store food, yet are fed by God, and argue that the disciples are worth more. Luke names ravens, while Matthew refers to birds of the sky.
    Luke 12:24 Consider the ravens: they neither sow nor reap, and they have neither storeroom nor storehouse, yet God feeds them. How much more valuable you are than the birds!

Matthew 6:27
Which of you by being anxious can add one cubit to his lifespan?
  Luke 12:25 — teaching parallel: Both ask whether anxiety can add a cubit to a person's span of life. The unusual cubit image is preserved in both supplied texts.
    Luke 12:25 And which of you by being anxious can add a cubit to his span of life?

Matthew 6:28
And why are you anxious about clothing? Observe the lilies of the field, how they grow: they neither toil nor spin.
  Luke 12:27 — teaching parallel: Both direct attention to lilies that do not toil or spin as an answer to anxiety about clothing. Luke also includes the following comparison with Solomon within the same verse.
    Luke 12:27 Consider the lilies, how they grow. They neither toil nor spin. Yet I tell you, not even Solomon in all his glory was clothed like one of these.

Matthew 6:29
Yet I tell you that not even Solomon in all his glory clothed himself like one of these.
  Luke 12:27 — teaching parallel: Both say that even Solomon in all his glory was not clothed like one of the lilies. Matthew places the Solomon comparison in a verse after the instruction to observe their growth.
    Luke 12:27 Consider the lilies, how they grow. They neither toil nor spin. Yet I tell you, not even Solomon in all his glory was clothed like one of these.

Matthew 6:30
If God so clothes the grass of the field, which exists today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, will He not much more clothe you, people of little faith?
  Luke 12:28 — teaching parallel: Both reason from God's clothing of grass that exists today and is thrown into an oven tomorrow to His greater care for people, and both address their lack of faith.
    Luke 12:28 But if God so clothes the grass that is in the field today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, how much more will He clothe you, you of little faith!

Matthew 6:31
So do not be anxious, saying, ‘What shall we eat?’ or ‘What shall we drink?’ or ‘What shall we wear?’
  Luke 12:29 — teaching parallel: Both tell the disciples not to be preoccupied with what they will eat or drink. Matthew includes the anxious question about clothing as well.
    Luke 12:29 And do not seek what you will eat and what you will drink, and do not be unsettled,

Matthew 6:32
For the nations seek all these things, and your heavenly Father knows that you need them all.
  Luke 12:30 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the nations' pursuit of daily necessities with the Father's knowledge that the disciples need those things.
    Luke 12:30 for the nations of the world seek after all these things, and your Father knows that you need them.

Matthew 6:33
But seek first the kingdom of God and His righteousness, and all these things will be added to you.
  Luke 12:31 — teaching parallel: Both redirect the pursuit of necessities toward seeking God's kingdom and promise that these things will be added. Matthew explicitly includes seeking His righteousness.
    Luke 12:31 But seek His kingdom, and these things will be added to you.

Matthew 6:34
So do not be anxious about tomorrow, for tomorrow will be anxious about itself. Sufficient for the day is its own trouble.
  Proverbs 27:1 — thematic parallel: Both warn about one's attitude toward tomorrow. Matthew rejects anxiety and says the present day has sufficient trouble; Proverbs rejects boasting about tomorrow because the coming day's events are unknown. This is a limited thematic comparison between different responses to the future, not an equation of anxiety with boasting.
    Proverbs 27:1 Do not boast of things for tomorrow, for you do not know what the coming day will bear.

Matthew 7:1
Do not judge, so that you may not be judged.
  Luke 6:37 — teaching parallel: Both forbid judging and link refraining from judgment with not being judged. Luke continues with condemnation and release.
    Luke 6:37 Do not judge, and you will certainly not be judged. Do not condemn, and you will certainly not be condemned. Release, and you will be released.

Matthew 7:2
For with the judgment you use, you will be judged, and with the measure you use, it will be measured to you.
  Luke 6:38 — teaching parallel: Both say that the measure people use will be measured back to them. Matthew applies the principle to judgment, while Luke places it in an instruction about giving.
    Luke 6:38 Give, and it will be given to you. They will put into your lap a good measure, pressed down, shaken together, overflowing; for by the measure you measure, it will be measured back to you.”

Matthew 7:3
Why do you see the splinter in your brother’s eye, but do not notice the beam in your own eye?
  Luke 6:41 — teaching parallel: Both ask why someone sees a small splinter or speck in a brother's eye while failing to notice the beam in his own eye.
    Luke 6:41 Why do you look at the speck in your brother’s eye but fail to notice the beam in your own eye?

Matthew 7:4
Or how will you say to your brother, ‘Let me remove the splinter from your eye,’ when behold, the beam is in your own eye?
  Luke 6:42 — teaching parallel: Both expose the absurdity of offering to remove a brother's speck while the helper still has a beam in his own eye. Luke joins the question and the remedy within one verse.
    Luke 6:42 How can you say to your brother, ‘Brother, let me take out the speck in your eye,’ when you yourself do not see the beam in your own eye? Hypocrite! First take the beam out of your eye, and then you will see clearly to take the speck out of your brother’s eye.

Matthew 7:5
Hypocrite! First remove the beam from your own eye, and then you will see clearly to remove the splinter from your brother’s eye.
  Luke 6:42 — teaching parallel: Both address the would-be corrector as a hypocrite and require removal of one's own beam before one can see clearly to remove a brother's speck.
    Luke 6:42 How can you say to your brother, ‘Brother, let me take out the speck in your eye,’ when you yourself do not see the beam in your own eye? Hypocrite! First take the beam out of your eye, and then you will see clearly to take the speck out of your brother’s eye.

Matthew 7:6
Do not give what is holy to dogs or throw your pearls before pigs, lest they trample them with their feet and turn and tear you apart.
  Proverbs 23:9 — thematic parallel: Compare the warning about offering holy and valuable things to recipients who respond destructively with the warning against speaking intelligent words to a fool who will sneer. This is a thematic comparison about discernment, not an identification of Matthew's symbolic animals with every foolish person.
    Proverbs 23:9 Say nothing into a fool’s ears, lest he sneer at your intelligent words.

Matthew 7:7
Ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.
  Luke 11:9 — teaching parallel: Both present the three commands ask, seek, and knock, each joined to its corresponding promise of receiving, finding, and an opened door.
    Luke 11:9 And I tell you: ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.

Matthew 7:8
For everyone who asks receives, the one who seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.
  Luke 11:10 — teaching parallel: Both explain the preceding commands by saying that the one who asks receives, the seeker finds, and the door is opened to the one who knocks.
    Luke 11:10 For everyone who asks receives, and the one who seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.

Matthew 7:9
Or who among you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone?
  Luke 11:11–11:12 — teaching parallel: Both use a father's response to a child's request for food to show the incongruity of giving something harmful instead. Matthew's example is bread and a stone; Luke's supplied text uses fish and snake, then egg and scorpion, and does not repeat the bread example.
    Luke 11:11 What father among you, if his son asks for a fish, will give him a snake instead of a fish?
    Luke 11:12 Or again, if he asks for an egg, will he give him a scorpion?

Matthew 7:10
Or if he asks for a fish, will give him a snake?
  Luke 11:11 — teaching parallel: Both ask whether a father would replace the fish requested by his son with a snake. The shared food-and-danger contrast supports the following argument about the Father.
    Luke 11:11 What father among you, if his son asks for a fish, will give him a snake instead of a fish?

Matthew 7:11
If you, then, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father in the heavens give good things to those who ask Him!
  Luke 11:13 — teaching parallel: Both reason from the good gifts given by imperfect human parents to the heavenly Father's greater generosity. Matthew promises good things to those who ask; Luke specifically names the Holy Spirit.
    Luke 11:13 So if you, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will the Father from heaven give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!”

Matthew 7:12
So in everything, whatever you wish people to do for you, you also do likewise for them; for this is the Law and the Prophets.
  Luke 6:31 — teaching parallel: Both command people to treat others as they themselves wish to be treated. Matthew additionally identifies this instruction with the Law and the Prophets.
    Luke 6:31 As you wish people to do to you, do likewise to them.

Matthew 7:13
Enter through the narrow gate, because the gate is wide and the road broad that leads to destruction, and many enter through it.
  Luke 13:24 — teaching parallel: Both urge entry through a narrow opening. Matthew contrasts that instruction with the broad road to destruction that many enter; Luke warns that many will seek to enter the narrow door without being able. The exhortations correspond, while their descriptions of the many differ.
    Luke 13:24 “Strive to enter through the narrow door, because many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able,

Matthew 7:14
How narrow is the gate and how constricted the road that leads to life, and few find it!
  Luke 13:24 — teaching parallel: Matthew describes the narrow gate and constricted road to life as found by few; Luke urges striving to enter the narrow door because many will seek entry without being able. The narrow-entry warning corresponds without identifying all details of gate, road, and door.
    Luke 13:24 “Strive to enter through the narrow door, because many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able,

Matthew 7:15
Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep’s clothing but inwardly are ravenous wolves.
  Acts 20:29–20:30 — thematic parallel: Both warn the believing community about dangerous teachers pictured as wolves. Matthew describes false prophets disguised as sheep; Paul warns of wolves entering the flock and men using distorted speech to draw disciples after themselves.
    Acts 20:29 I know that after my departure fierce wolves will enter among you, not sparing the flock.
    Acts 20:30 Even from among you yourselves men will arise, speaking distorted things to draw the disciples away after themselves.

Matthew 7:16
You will recognize them by their fruits. Do people gather grapes from thornbushes, or figs from thistles?
  Luke 6:44 — teaching parallel: Both use a tree's fruit as the means of recognizing its character and illustrate the point with the impossibility of harvesting figs or grapes from thorny plants.
    Luke 6:44 Each tree is known by its own fruit. People do not gather figs from thorns, nor harvest grapes from a bramble.

Matthew 7:17
In the same way, every good tree bears good fruit, but the rotten tree bears evil fruit.
  Luke 6:43 — teaching parallel: Both contrast a good tree with a rotten tree by the kind of fruit each produces. Matthew states what each bears; Luke states what each cannot produce.
    Luke 6:43 For no good tree produces rotten fruit, nor again does a rotten tree produce good fruit.

Matthew 7:18
A good tree cannot bear evil fruit, nor can a rotten tree bear good fruit.
  Luke 6:43 — teaching parallel: Both deny that a good tree produces evil or rotten fruit, or that a rotten tree produces good fruit. The fruit image supplies a test of the underlying source.
    Luke 6:43 For no good tree produces rotten fruit, nor again does a rotten tree produce good fruit.

Matthew 7:19
Every tree that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.
  Luke 3:9 — teaching parallel: Matthew repeats the warning found in John's preaching in Luke: every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.
    Luke 3:9 Already the axe also lies at the root of the trees. Therefore every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.”

Matthew 7:20
So then, you will recognize them by their fruits.
  Luke 6:44 — teaching parallel: Matthew concludes that the false prophets will be recognized by their fruits; Luke states the general image that every tree is known by its own fruit. The connection is the same fruit-based criterion of recognition.
    Luke 6:44 Each tree is known by its own fruit. People do not gather figs from thorns, nor harvest grapes from a bramble.

Matthew 7:21
Not everyone who says to Me, ‘Lord, Lord,’ will enter the kingdom of the heavens, but the one who does the will of My Father in the heavens.
  Luke 6:46 — teaching parallel: Both deny that addressing Jesus as Lord is sufficient when obedience is absent. Matthew requires doing the Father's will; Luke asks why people say Lord, Lord while failing to do what He says.
    Luke 6:46 Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?

Matthew 7:22
Many will say to Me on that day, ‘Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in Thy name, cast out demons in Thy name, and do many mighty works in Thy name?’
  1 Corinthians 13:2 — thematic parallel: Matthew depicts people appealing to prophecy and mighty works as evidence of standing with Jesus; Paul says that even prophecy and mountain-moving faith amount to nothing without love. Both challenge reliance on extraordinary religious gifts alone, though Paul does not repeat the judgment scene or the claim about exorcisms.
    1 Corinthians 13:2 And if I have prophecy and know all mysteries and all knowledge, and if I have all faith so as to move mountains, but have no love, I am nothing.

Matthew 7:23
Then I will declare to them, ‘I never knew you. Depart from Me, you who practice lawlessness.’
  Luke 13:27 — teaching parallel: Both portray a decisive refusal of people who claim association with the Lord, followed by a command that workers of wrongdoing depart. Matthew says I never knew you; Luke says I do not know you or where you come from.
    Luke 13:27 And he will say to you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from. Depart from me, all you workers of unrighteousness!’
  Psalms 6:9 — allusion: The command for workers of lawlessness to depart recalls the psalmist's command that all workers of lawlessness stand away from him. Matthew places the declaration on Jesus' lips in the judgment scene; this edition numbers the target Psalm 6:9.
    Psalms 6:9 Stand away from me, all who work lawlessness, for the Lord heard the voice of my weeping.

Matthew 7:24
Therefore everyone who hears these words of Mine and puts them into practice will be likened to a prudent man who built his house upon the rock.
  Luke 6:47–6:48 — teaching parallel: Both compare a person who hears Jesus' words and acts on them to a builder who establishes a house on rock. Luke adds the detail of digging deeply for the foundation.
    Luke 6:47 Everyone who comes to Me, hears My words and does them—I will show you whom he resembles.
    Luke 6:48 He resembles a man building a house, who dug and went deep and laid a foundation on the rock. When a flood came, the river burst against that house but could not shake it, because it had been well built.

Matthew 7:25
The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and fell upon that house, and it did not fall, for it had been founded upon the rock.
  Luke 6:48 — teaching parallel: Both describe a house remaining secure when a flood or river strikes because it has been properly founded. Matthew includes rain and wind and explicitly repeats that the foundation was rock.
    Luke 6:48 He resembles a man building a house, who dug and went deep and laid a foundation on the rock. When a flood came, the river burst against that house but could not shake it, because it had been well built.

Matthew 7:26
And everyone who hears these words of Mine and does not put them into practice will be likened to a foolish man who built his house upon the sand.
  Luke 6:49 — teaching parallel: Both compare hearing without acting to an unsound house. Matthew has a foolish builder on sand; Luke describes a house built on the ground without a foundation.
    Luke 6:49 But the one who heard and did not act resembles a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation. The river burst against it, and immediately it collapsed; the ruin of that house was great.”

Matthew 7:27
The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and struck that house, and it fell; and its fall was great.”
  Luke 6:49 — teaching parallel: Both conclude that the house corresponding to hearing without obedience collapses when the river strikes, and both emphasize how great its ruin is. Matthew also lists rain and wind.
    Luke 6:49 But the one who heard and did not act resembles a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation. The river burst against it, and immediately it collapsed; the ruin of that house was great.”

Matthew 7:28
When Jesus finished these words, the crowds were astonished at His teaching,
  Mark 1:22 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the audience's astonishment at Jesus' teaching. Matthew reports it after the completed sermon; Mark reports it in the Capernaum synagogue setting.
    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

Matthew 7:29
for He taught them as One having authority, and not as their scribes.
  Mark 1:22 — narrative parallel: Both explain astonishment by contrasting Jesus' teaching with that of the scribes: He teaches as one having authority. The common evaluation is retained while the narrative occasions differ.
    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

Matthew 8:1
When He came down from the mountain, great crowds followed Him.
  Luke 6:17–6:19 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place Jesus' descent alongside a large crowd. Matthew places the descent after the preceding discourse; Luke places it after the mountain selection of the Twelve and before the ensuing teaching. The comparison preserves the different sequence and does not identify the mountain, level place or crowds as one historical scene.
    Luke 6:17 He came down with them and stood on a level place. There was a large crowd of His disciples and a great multitude of the people from all Judea, Jerusalem, and the coastal region of Tyre and Sidon.
    Luke 6:18 They had come to hear Him and be healed of their diseases; those troubled by unclean spirits were also healed.
    Luke 6:19 The whole crowd sought to touch Him, because power went out from Him and healed everyone.

Matthew 8:2
And behold, a leper came and bowed before Him, saying, “Lord, if Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”
  Mark 1:40 — narrative parallel: Both narrate a leper approaching Jesus and saying that Jesus can cleanse him if willing. Matthew describes bowing before Him, while Mark describes pleading and kneeling.
    Mark 1:40 A leper came to Him, pleading with Him, kneeling, and saying, “If Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”

Matthew 8:3
He stretched out His hand and touched him, saying, “I am willing; be cleansed.” Immediately his leprosy was cleansed.
  Mark 1:41–1:42 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus touching the leper, declaring His willingness and commanding cleansing, followed immediately by the disappearance of leprosy.
    Mark 1:41 And moved with compassion, He stretched out His hand and touched him, and says to him, “I will; be cleansed.”
    Mark 1:42 Immediately the leprosy departed from him, and he was cleansed.

Matthew 8:4
Jesus said to him, “See that you tell no one, but go, show yourself to the priest, and offer the gift Moses commanded as a testimony to them.”
  Mark 1:43–1:44 — narrative parallel: Both record a strict instruction not to publicize the healing, followed by showing oneself to the priest and making Moses' prescribed offering as testimony.
    Mark 1:43 Sternly warning him, He immediately drove him away
    Mark 1:44 and said to him, “See that you say nothing to anyone. But go, show yourself to the priest, and offer for your cleansing what Moses commanded, as a testimony to them.”

Matthew 8:5
When He entered Capernaum, a centurion came to Him, pleading with Him
  Luke 7:1–7:3 — narrative parallel: Both place a centurion's appeal for his servant in Capernaum. Matthew presents the centurion approaching Jesus directly; Luke narrates the appeal through Jewish elders sent by him. The modes of approach are not silently harmonized.
    Luke 7:1 When He had finished all His sayings in the people’s hearing, He entered Capernaum.
    Luke 7:2 A certain centurion had a slave who was ill and about to die, and who was precious to him.
    Luke 7:3 Hearing about Jesus, he sent elders of the Jews to Him, asking Him to come and save his slave.

Matthew 8:6
and saying, “Lord, my servant lies at home paralyzed, terribly tormented.”
  Luke 7:2 — narrative parallel: Both identify a centurion's seriously ill servant as the reason for the appeal. Matthew specifies paralysis and severe suffering, whereas Luke says the valued slave was ill and about to die.
    Luke 7:2 A certain centurion had a slave who was ill and about to die, and who was precious to him.

Matthew 8:7
He said to him, “I will come and heal him.”
  Luke 7:6 — narrative parallel: Jesus' stated readiness to come in Matthew corresponds to His accompanying the elders toward the centurion's house in Luke. Luke's communication proceeds through messengers rather than Matthew's direct dialogue.
    Luke 7:6 Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.

Matthew 8:8
The centurion answered, “Lord, I am not fit for Thee to enter under my roof. Only speak a word, and my servant will be healed.
  Luke 7:6–7:7 — narrative parallel: Both give the centurion's declaration that he is unworthy to have Jesus enter his roof and that a word will suffice to heal the servant. Luke delivers the declaration through friends and adds his unworthiness to approach personally.
    Luke 7:6 Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.
    Luke 7:7 Therefore I did not even consider myself worthy to come to Thee. But speak a word, and let my servant be healed.

Matthew 8:9
For I too am a man under authority, with soldiers under me. I say to this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; to another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and to my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”
  Luke 7:8 — narrative parallel: Both use the centurion's position under authority and his commands to soldiers and a slave as the concrete analogy for the efficacy of Jesus' command.
    Luke 7:8 For I too am a man placed under authority, with soldiers under me. I tell this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”

Matthew 8:10
When Jesus heard this, He marveled and said to those following, “Truly I tell you, with no one in Israel have I found such faith.
  Luke 7:9 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus marveling at the centurion's faith and declaring to the following crowd that He has not found such faith even in Israel.
    Luke 7:9 When Jesus heard this, He marveled at him. Turning to the crowd following Him, He said, “I tell you, not even in Israel have I found such faith.”

Matthew 8:11
I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,
  Luke 13:28–13:29 — teaching parallel: Both picture people arriving from distant directions to recline in God's kingdom alongside the patriarchs Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Luke also names north and south and includes the prophets.
    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.
    Luke 13:29 And people will come from east and west and from north and south and recline at table in the kingdom of God.

Matthew 8:12
but the sons of the kingdom will be cast into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.”
  Luke 13:28 — teaching parallel: Both contrast inclusion with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob in the kingdom with exclusion accompanied by weeping and grinding of teeth. Matthew calls the excluded sons of the kingdom and adds outer darkness; Luke addresses those cast outside.
    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

Matthew 8:13
Jesus said to the centurion, “Go; as you have believed, let it happen for you.” And his servant was healed at that hour.
  Luke 7:10 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the centurion episode with the servant well. Matthew directly joins healing to Jesus' response to the centurion's faith and specifies that hour; Luke reports the messengers finding the slave healthy on returning home.
    Luke 7:10 Those who had been sent returned to the house and found the slave in good health.

Matthew 8:14
Jesus entered Peter’s house and saw his mother-in-law lying down with a fever.
  Mark 1:29–1:30 — narrative parallel: Both place the healing in Simon Peter's household and identify his mother-in-law as lying ill with a fever. Mark also names Andrew's house and the accompanying James and John.
    Mark 1:29 Immediately after leaving the synagogue, they went into the house of Simon and Andrew, with James and John.
    Mark 1:30 Simon’s mother-in-law was lying down with a fever, and immediately they told Him about her.

Matthew 8:15
He touched her hand, and the fever left her. She arose and served Him.
  Mark 1:31 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus taking or touching the woman's hand, the fever leaving, and her rising to serve. Matthew says she served Him, while Mark says she served them.
    Mark 1:31 He came to her, took her by the hand, and raised her up. The fever left her, and she served them.

Matthew 8:16
When evening came, they brought Him many who were oppressed by demons. He cast out the spirits with a word and healed all who were ill,
  Mark 1:32–1:34 — narrative parallel: Both describe people bringing sick and demon-afflicted persons at evening, followed by healings and expulsions of demons. Matthew says He healed all who were ill; Mark's summary says He healed many and adds the prohibition on demons speaking.
    Mark 1:32 When evening came, after the sun had set, they brought to Him all who were ill and those possessed by demons.
    Mark 1:33 The whole city had gathered at the door.
    Mark 1:34 He healed many who were ill with various diseases and cast out many demons. He would not allow the demons to speak, because they knew Him.

Matthew 8:17
so that what was spoken through Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled: “He took our weaknesses and carried our diseases.”
  Isaiah 53:4 — quotation: Matthew explicitly applies Isaiah's servant passage to Jesus' healings. Its wording about taking weaknesses and carrying diseases differs from this Saints Isaiah text, which says the servant bears our sins and suffers pain for us; the connection is identified without replacing either English wording.
    Isaiah 53:4 He bears our sins, and suffers pain for us; yet we reckoned him to be in toil and affliction and distress.

Matthew 8:18
Seeing a crowd around Him, Jesus gave orders to depart to the other side.
  Mark 4:35–4:36 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the lake crossing by Jesus directing departure to the other side and leaving the crowd. Mark places the direction in the evening and describes taking Him in the boat.
    Mark 4:35 On that day, when evening came, He said to them, “Let us cross to the other side.”
    Mark 4:36 Leaving the crowd, they took Him with them, just as He was, in the boat. Other boats were with Him.

Matthew 8:19
One scribe came and said to Him, “Teacher, I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”
  Luke 9:57 — teaching parallel: Both record a prospective follower promising to follow Jesus wherever He goes. Matthew identifies the speaker as a scribe, whereas Luke leaves him unnamed and places the exchange on the road.
    Luke 9:57 As they traveled on the road, someone said to Him, “I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”

Matthew 8:20
Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have resting places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”
  Luke 9:58 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the shelters of foxes and birds with the Son of Man's having nowhere to lay His head in the response to the prospective follower.
    Luke 9:58 Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have dwelling places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”

Matthew 8:21
Another of His disciples said to Him, “Lord, first permit me to go and bury my father.”
  Luke 9:59 — teaching parallel: Both present a would-be follower asking first to leave and bury his father. Luke explicitly begins with Jesus' call to follow, while Matthew identifies the speaker as another disciple.
    Luke 9:59 He said to another, “Follow Me.” But he said, “Lord, permit me first to go and bury my father.”

Matthew 8:22
But Jesus said to him, “Follow Me, and let the dead bury their own dead.”
  Luke 9:60 — teaching parallel: Both give the striking instruction to let the dead bury their own dead. Matthew joins it to following Jesus; Luke adds the commission to proclaim God's kingdom.
    Luke 9:60 He said to him, “Let the dead bury their own dead; but you go and proclaim God’s kingdom.”

Matthew 8:23
And when He entered the boat, His disciples followed Him.
  Mark 4:36 — narrative parallel: Both put Jesus and His followers in a boat at the start of the storm crossing. Mark describes leaving the crowd and other boats accompanying Him, details absent from Matthew's short boarding notice.
    Mark 4:36 Leaving the crowd, they took Him with them, just as He was, in the boat. Other boats were with Him.

Matthew 8:24
And behold, a great tempest arose in the sea, so that the boat was covered by the waves; but He was sleeping.
  Mark 4:37–4:38 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus asleep in a boat endangered by a severe storm and waves. Mark specifies His place in the stern on a cushion and says water was already filling the boat.
    Mark 4:37 A great windstorm arose, and the waves broke into the boat, so that the boat was already filling.
    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”

Matthew 8:25
And they came to Him and awoke Him, saying, “Lord, save; we are perishing.”
  Mark 4:38 — narrative parallel: Both have the disciples wake Jesus because they are perishing. Matthew addresses Him as Lord and asks salvation; Mark addresses Him as Teacher and asks whether He cares.
    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”

Matthew 8:26
And He says to them, “Why are you fearful, you of little faith?” Then He arose and rebuked the winds and the sea; and there was a great calm.
  Mark 4:39–4:40 — narrative parallel: Both connect Jesus' rebuke of wind and sea with great calm and a rebuke concerning the disciples' fear and faith. Matthew places the question before the calming; Mark places it afterward.
    Mark 4:39 And He awoke, rebuked the wind, and said to the sea, “Be silent; be still.” And the wind ceased, and there was a great calm.
    Mark 4:40 He said to them, “Why are you cowardly? Do you still have no faith?”

Matthew 8:27
And the men marvelled, saying, “What kind of man is this, that even the winds and the sea obey Him?”
  Mark 4:41 — narrative parallel: Both end the storm episode with the question of who Jesus is, prompted by the obedience of wind and sea. Matthew describes amazement; Mark emphasizes great fear.
    Mark 4:41 They were greatly afraid and said to one another, “Who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey Him?”

Matthew 8:28
When He came to the other side, to the country of the Gadarenes, two men oppressed by demons met Him, coming out of the tombs. They were exceedingly fierce, so that no one could pass by that road.
  Mark 5:1–5:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe meeting demon-afflicted people associated with tombs after the crossing. Matthew names the Gadarenes and two men, while Mark names the Gerasenes and focuses on one man; the place-name and participant-count differences remain explicit.
    Mark 5:1 They came to the other side of the sea, to the country of the Gerasenes.
    Mark 5:2 As He stepped out of the boat, a man with an unclean spirit immediately met Him from the tombs.
    Mark 5:3 He made his dwelling among the tombs, and no one could bind him anymore, even with a chain.

Matthew 8:29
And behold, they cried out, “What have we to do with Thee, Son of God? Hast Thou come here to torment us before the appointed time?”
  Mark 5:7 — narrative parallel: Both contain a cry asking what Jesus, identified as God's Son, has to do with the afflicted speaker and an appeal concerning torment. Matthew has plural speakers and adds before the appointed time; Mark reports one man's cry and says Son of the Most High God.
    Mark 5:7 He cried out with a loud voice and said, “What hast Thou to do with me, Jesus, Son of God Most High? I adjure Thee by God: do not torment me!”

Matthew 8:30
Far from them a herd of many pigs was feeding.
  Mark 5:11 — narrative parallel: Both identify a large herd of pigs feeding near the exorcism scene. Matthew describes the herd as far from the men, while Mark places it there near the mountain.
    Mark 5:11 Now a large herd of pigs was feeding there near the mountain.

Matthew 8:31
The demons pleaded with Him, saying, “If Thou castest us out, send us into the herd of pigs.”
  Mark 5:12 — narrative parallel: Both record the demons asking Jesus to send them into the pigs. Mark's narration focuses on one afflicted man, unlike Matthew's two men.
    Mark 5:12 They begged Him, saying, “Send us into the pigs, so that we may enter them.”

Matthew 8:32
He said to them, “Go.” They came out and went into the pigs. And behold, the whole herd rushed down the steep bank into the sea and died in the waters.
  Mark 5:13 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus permitting the departure, the spirits entering the pigs, and the herd rushing down the bank and drowning in the sea. Mark adds an approximate herd count; Matthew gives none.
    Mark 5:13 He allowed them. The unclean spirits came out and entered the pigs, and the herd rushed down the steep bank into the sea—about two thousand—and they drowned in the sea.

Matthew 8:33
The herdsmen fled, and going into the city they reported everything, including what had happened to the men oppressed by demons.
  Mark 5:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe the pig herders fleeing and reporting what happened in the city. Mark also mentions the countryside; Matthew explicitly includes the report about the demon-afflicted men.
    Mark 5:14 Those tending them fled and reported it in the city and in the fields. People came to see what had happened.

Matthew 8:34
And behold, the whole city came out to meet Jesus. When they saw Him, they begged Him to depart from their territory.
  Mark 5:17 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the encounter with local people asking Jesus to leave their territory. Matthew describes the whole city coming to meet Him, whereas the cited Mark verse gives the request without that collective description.
    Mark 5:17 They began begging Him to leave their territory.

Matthew 9:1
He entered a boat, crossed over, and came to His own city.
  Mark 2:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the paralytic-healing setting with Jesus returning to His home setting. Mark explicitly names Capernaum; Matthew calls it His own city after the lake crossing and does not name the city in this verse.
    Mark 2:1 When He entered Capernaum again after some days, it was heard that He was at home.

Matthew 9:2
And behold, they brought Him a paralytic lying on a bed. Seeing their faith, Jesus said to the paralytic, “Take courage, child; your sins are forgiven.”
  Mark 2:3–2:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe a paralytic being brought to Jesus, Jesus seeing their faith, and His declaration that the man's sins are forgiven. Mark adds four bearers and the opening in the roof; Matthew gives a shorter account.
    Mark 2:3 They came, bringing to Him a paralyzed man carried by four men.
    Mark 2:4 Since they could not bring him to Jesus because of the crowd, they removed the roof above the place where He was. After digging through it, they lowered the mat on which the paralyzed man lay.
    Mark 2:5 Jesus saw their faith and said to the paralyzed man, “Child, your sins are forgiven.”

Matthew 9:3
And behold, some of the scribes said among themselves, “This Man blasphemes.”
  Mark 2:6–2:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe scribes responding to Jesus' forgiveness declaration by regarding it as blasphemy. Mark further states their objection that only God can forgive sins.
    Mark 2:6 Some of the scribes were sitting there and reasoning in their hearts,
    Mark 2:7 “Why does this man speak this way? He is blaspheming! Who can forgive sins except the one God?”

Matthew 9:4
Seeing their thoughts, Jesus said, “Why do you think evil things in your hearts?
  Mark 2:8 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus perceive the scribes' inward reasoning and challenge what they are thinking in their hearts. Matthew calls the thoughts evil; Mark explicitly says He perceived them in His spirit.
    Mark 2:8 Immediately Jesus knew in His spirit that they were reasoning this way within themselves. He said to them, “Why are you reasoning these things in your hearts?

Matthew 9:5
For which is easier, to say, ‘Your sins are forgiven,’ or to say, ‘Rise and walk’?
  Mark 2:9 — narrative parallel: Both contrast the ease of saying sins are forgiven with telling the paralytic to rise and walk. Mark includes taking up the bed within the second alternative.
    Mark 2:9 Which is easier: to say to the paralyzed man, ‘Your sins are forgiven,’ or to say, ‘Arise, take your mat, and walk’?

Matthew 9:6
But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—then He said to the paralytic—“rise, take your bed, and go to your house.”
  Mark 2:10–2:11 — narrative parallel: Both connect the healing command with demonstration that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins, then direct the paralytic to rise, take his bed and go home.
    Mark 2:10 But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—He said to the paralyzed man—
    Mark 2:11 “I say to you, arise, take your mat, and go to your house.”

Matthew 9:7
He rose and went to his house.
  Mark 2:12 — narrative parallel: Both report the man rising and leaving after Jesus' command. Mark explicitly mentions his immediately taking the bed and leaving before everyone; Matthew simply reports his rising and going home.
    Mark 2:12 He arose, immediately took up the mat, and went out before everyone. They were all amazed and glorified God, saying, “We have never seen anything like this!”

Matthew 9:8
When the crowds saw this, they feared and glorified God, who had given such authority to human beings.
  Mark 2:12 — narrative parallel: Both show the witnesses responding by glorifying God. Matthew emphasizes fear and authority given to human beings; Mark emphasizes amazement and the declaration that they had never seen such a thing.
    Mark 2:12 He arose, immediately took up the mat, and went out before everyone. They were all amazed and glorified God, saying, “We have never seen anything like this!”

Matthew 9:9
As Jesus passed on from there, He saw a man called Matthew sitting at the tax station, and He said to him, “Follow Me.” He rose and followed Him.
  Mark 2:14 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus calling a man seated at a tax station with Follow Me, and the man rising to follow. Matthew names him Matthew; Mark names him Levi son of Alphaeus. The cross-reference compares the narratives without resolving the different names.
    Mark 2:14 As He passed by, He saw Levi son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax station. He said to him, “Follow Me.” He arose and followed Him.

Matthew 9:10
As He reclined at table in the house, behold, many tax collectors and sinners came and reclined with Jesus and His disciples.
  Mark 2:15 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus and His disciples reclining at table among many tax collectors and sinners. The comparison concerns the shared meal setting without resolving the Matthew/Levi naming difference in the adjoining call accounts.
    Mark 2:15 As He reclined at table in his house, many tax collectors and sinners reclined with Jesus and His disciples, for there were many who followed Him.

Matthew 9:11
When the Pharisees saw this, they said to His disciples, “Why does your Teacher eat with tax collectors and sinners?”
  Mark 2:16 — narrative parallel: Both record religious critics asking the disciples why Jesus eats with tax collectors and sinners. Matthew names Pharisees; Mark specifies the scribes of the Pharisees.
    Mark 2:16 The scribes of the Pharisees saw that He was eating with sinners and tax collectors and said to His disciples, “Why does He eat with tax collectors and sinners?”

Matthew 9:12
Hearing this, He said, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do.
  Mark 2:17 — narrative parallel: Both answer the criticism with the physician analogy: the strong or healthy do not need a physician, but the sick do. Mark continues with the call of sinners within the same verse.
    Mark 2:17 Jesus heard and said to them, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do. I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners.”

Matthew 9:13
Go and learn what this means: ‘I desire mercy and not sacrifice.’ For I did not come to call righteous people, but sinners.”
  Hosea 6:6 — quotation: Jesus explicitly cites the preference for mercy rather than sacrifice. Hosea supplies that wording and continues with knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings; it does not itself contain Matthew's following statement about calling sinners.
    Hosea 6:6 For I desire mercy, and not sacrifice, and knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings.

Matthew 9:14
Then John’s disciples came to Him, saying, “Why do we and the Pharisees fast often, but Thy disciples do not fast?”
  Mark 2:18 — narrative parallel: Both ask why John's disciples and the Pharisees fast while Jesus' disciples do not. Matthew makes John's disciples the questioners; Mark introduces the practice and reports people putting the question.
    Mark 2:18 John’s disciples and the Pharisees were fasting. They came and said to Him, “Why do John’s disciples and the disciples of the Pharisees fast, but Thy disciples do not fast?”

Matthew 9:15
Jesus said to them, “Can the wedding guests mourn as long as the bridegroom is with them? But days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast.
  Mark 2:19–2:20 — narrative parallel: Both use the bridegroom's presence to explain why the wedding party does not fast and predict fasting when he is taken away. Matthew's opening question speaks of mourning; Mark's speaks directly of fasting.
    Mark 2:19 Jesus said to them, “Can the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast.
    Mark 2:20 But days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast on that day.

Matthew 9:16
No one puts a patch of unshrunk cloth on an old garment, for its patch pulls away from the garment, and a worse tear results.
  Mark 2:21 — teaching parallel: Both illustrate incompatible old and new with an unshrunk patch pulling away from an old garment and making the tear worse.
    Mark 2:21 No one sews a patch of unshrunk cloth onto an old garment. Otherwise the new patch pulls away from the old, and a worse tear results.

Matthew 9:17
Nor do people put new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the skins burst, the wine spills out, and the skins are ruined. Instead, they put new wine into fresh wineskins, and both are preserved.”
  Mark 2:22 — teaching parallel: Both explain that new wine bursts old wineskins and destroys wine and skins, then require fresh skins for new wine. Matthew adds that both are preserved.
    Mark 2:22 No one puts new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the wine will burst the skins, and the wine is lost along with the skins. New wine belongs in fresh wineskins.”

Matthew 9:18
While He was saying these things to them, behold, a ruler came and bowed before Him, saying, “My daughter has just died. But come, lay Thy hand upon her, and she will live.”
  Mark 5:22–5:23 — narrative parallel: Both have a ruler approach Jesus and ask Him to lay hands on his daughter so she may live. Mark names Jairus and describes her as near death at this point; Matthew reports her as already just dead. The initial conditions differ rather than being silently merged.
    Mark 5:22 One of the synagogue rulers came, named Jairus. Seeing Him, he fell at His feet
    Mark 5:23 and begged Him earnestly, saying, “My little daughter is at the point of death. Please come and lay Thy hands on her, so that she may be saved and live.”

Matthew 9:19
Jesus rose and followed him, and so did His disciples.
  Mark 5:24 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus going with the ruler after the request concerning his daughter. Matthew mentions His disciples following too; Mark emphasizes the large crowd following and pressing Him.
    Mark 5:24 He went with him. A large crowd followed Him and pressed against Him.

Matthew 9:20
And behold, a woman who had suffered a flow of blood for twelve years came behind Him and touched the fringe of His garment.
  Mark 5:25–5:27 — narrative parallel: Both describe a woman with twelve years of bleeding approaching behind Jesus and touching His garment. Matthew specifies its fringe; Mark adds the unsuccessful treatment history and her hearing about Jesus.
    Mark 5:25 A woman who had had a flow of blood for twelve years,
    Mark 5:26 and had suffered much under many physicians and spent everything she had, without any benefit but instead growing worse,
    Mark 5:27 heard about Jesus. She came behind Him in the crowd and touched His garment.

Matthew 9:21
For she said within herself, “If I only touch His garment, I will be saved.”
  Mark 5:28 — narrative parallel: Both give the woman's own reasoning that touching Jesus' clothing will suffice for her to be saved or made well. Matthew states that she said it within herself.
    Mark 5:28 For she was saying, “If I touch even His clothes, I will be saved.”

Matthew 9:22
Jesus turned, saw her, and said, “Take courage, daughter; your faith has saved you.” And the woman was saved from that hour.
  Mark 5:34 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus address the woman as daughter and say that her faith has saved her. Mark adds a dismissal in peace and freedom from the affliction; Matthew explicitly dates her recovery from that hour.
    Mark 5:34 And He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace, and be well, free from your affliction.”

Matthew 9:23
When Jesus entered the ruler’s house and saw the flute players and the crowd in an uproar,
  Mark 5:38 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus at the ruler's house amid noisy mourning. Matthew specifically names flute players, while Mark describes people weeping and wailing loudly.
    Mark 5:38 They came to the synagogue ruler’s house, and He saw a commotion, with people weeping and wailing loudly.

Matthew 9:24
He said, “Withdraw, for the girl has not died but is sleeping.” And they laughed at Him.
  Mark 5:39–5:40 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus saying the child is not dead but sleeping, followed by the mourners laughing at Him. Mark also narrates putting everyone outside within this target range.
    Mark 5:39 He went in and said to them, “Why are you making a commotion and weeping? The child has not died but is sleeping.”
    Mark 5:40 They laughed at Him. But He put them all outside, took the child’s father and mother and those with Him, and went in where the child was.

Matthew 9:25
When the crowd had been put outside, He entered and took her hand, and the girl arose.
  Mark 5:40–5:42 — narrative parallel: Both have the outsiders removed, Jesus take the girl's hand, and the girl rise. Mark preserves His spoken command and her age and walking; Matthew abbreviates those details.
    Mark 5:40 They laughed at Him. But He put them all outside, took the child’s father and mother and those with Him, and went in where the child was.
    Mark 5:41 Taking the child’s hand, He said to her, “Talitha koum,” which translated means, “Little girl, I say to you, arise!”
    Mark 5:42 Immediately the little girl rose and walked around, for she was twelve years old. Immediately they were overcome with great amazement.

Matthew 9:26
This report went out into all that land.
  Luke 7:14–7:17 — thematic parallel: reports following restoration of the dead: Both narratives describe a report spreading regionally after the restoration of a dead person. Matthew closes the raising of the girl with a report throughout the land; Luke describes the young man being raised and the report spreading through Judea and the surrounding region. These are corresponding narrative effects in different episodes, not the same incident.
    Luke 7:14 He approached and touched the bier; the bearers stood still. He said, “Young man, I tell you, rise.”
    Luke 7:15 The dead man sat up and began speaking, and He gave him to his mother.
    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”
    Luke 7:17 This report about Him spread throughout all Judea and all the surrounding region.

Matthew 9:27
As Jesus passed on from there, two blind men followed Him, crying out, “Have mercy on us, Son of David!”
  Matthew 20:30–20:31 — narrative parallel: A later, distinct healing encounter likewise involves two blind men crying for mercy and addressing Jesus as Son of David. Matthew 9 places its approach before entering a house, whereas Matthew 20 places the men beside the road at Jericho; the events are not identified as one.
    Matthew 20:30 And behold, two blind men sitting beside the road heard that Jesus was passing by and cried out, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”
    Matthew 20:31 The crowd rebuked them to make them silent, but they cried out all the more, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”

Matthew 9:28
When He entered the house, the blind men came to Him, and Jesus said to them, “Do you believe that I can do this?” They said to Him, “Yes, Lord.”
  Mark 9:23–9:24 — thematic parallel: Both healing settings explicitly raise believing in Jesus' ability: Jesus asks the blind men whether they believe He can act, while in Mark He answers a father's if Thou canst and the father professes faith with a plea about unbelief. The sufferers and exchanges are different.
    Mark 9:23 Jesus said to him, “‘If Thou canst’! All things are possible for the one who believes.”
    Mark 9:24 Immediately the father of the child cried out and said, “I believe; help my unbelief!”

Matthew 9:29
Then He touched their eyes, saying, “According to your faith, let it happen for you.”
