  Matthew 20:34 — narrative parallel: Both distinct blind-healing episodes describe Jesus touching the men's eyes. The later Jericho account reports compassion and immediate sight; this verse relates the outcome to faith. Shared action does not make the occasions identical.
    Matthew 20:34 Moved with compassion, Jesus touched their eyes. Immediately they received sight and followed Him.

Matthew 9:30
Their eyes were opened. Jesus sternly warned them, saying, “See that no one knows.”
  Matthew 20:34 — narrative parallel: Both distinct encounters conclude with the blind men's eyes receiving sight. The Jericho verse does not include Matthew 9's command to keep the healing unknown, and no secrecy instruction is inferred for that later event.
    Matthew 20:34 Moved with compassion, Jesus touched their eyes. Immediately they received sight and followed Him.

Matthew 9:31
But they went out and spread the report about Him throughout all that land.
  Mark 1:44–1:45 — narrative parallel: Both describe healed recipients publicizing their experience despite a preceding instruction not to tell. Matthew concerns the blind men spreading the report through the region; Mark concerns the cleansed leper, whose publicity limits Jesus' movements.
    Mark 1:44 and said to him, “See that you say nothing to anyone. But go, show yourself to the priest, and offer for your cleansing what Moses commanded, as a testimony to them.”
    Mark 1:45 But he went out and began proclaiming it widely and spreading the report, so that Jesus could no longer enter a city openly. He remained outside in deserted places, and they came to Him from every direction.

Matthew 9:32
As they were going out, behold, people brought Him a mute man oppressed by a demon.
  Luke 11:14 — narrative parallel: Both introduce an exorcism involving muteness. Luke calls the demon mute and narrates the afflicted person's speech after its departure; Matthew explicitly calls the brought man mute and demon-afflicted. The narratives' settings are not identified as one.
    Luke 11:14 And He was driving out a mute demon. When the demon came out, the mute man spoke, and the crowds marveled.

Matthew 9:33
When the demon was cast out, the mute man spoke. The crowds marveled, saying, “Never has anything like this appeared in Israel.”
  Luke 11:14 — narrative parallel: Both join expulsion of the demon with the formerly mute person's speaking and the crowd's amazement. Matthew adds the crowd's claim about nothing like it having appeared in Israel.
    Luke 11:14 And He was driving out a mute demon. When the demon came out, the mute man spoke, and the crowds marveled.

Matthew 9:34
But the Pharisees said, “By the ruler of the demons He casts out demons.”
  Luke 11:15 — narrative parallel: Both answer the exorcism with an accusation that Jesus acts by the ruler of the demons. Matthew identifies the accusers as Pharisees; Luke says some of them and explicitly names Beelzebul.
    Luke 11:15 But some of them said, “He drives out the demons by Beelzebul, the ruler of the demons.”

Matthew 9:35
And Jesus went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the gospel of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity.
  Matthew 4:23 — thematic parallel: This same-book ministry summary repeats Jesus' teaching in synagogues, proclamation of the kingdom's good news and healing of every disease and infirmity. The earlier summary specifies Galilee, while this verse says all the cities and villages.
    Matthew 4:23 He went throughout Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the good news of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity among the people.

Matthew 9:36
And seeing the crowds, He was moved with compassion for them, because they were harassed and cast down, as sheep having no shepherd.
  Mark 6:34 — thematic parallel: Both explain Jesus' compassion for crowds through the comparison with sheep lacking a shepherd. Mark proceeds to His teaching them; Matthew describes them as harassed and cast down.
    Mark 6:34 As He stepped out, He saw a large crowd and was moved with compassion for them, because they were like sheep without a shepherd. He began teaching them many things.

Matthew 9:37
Then He says to His disciples, “The harvest indeed is plentiful, but the labourers are few.
  Luke 10:2 — teaching parallel: Both describe the harvest as plentiful and the workers as few. Luke places the saying in the commissioning of the larger group, while Matthew addresses His disciples before the Twelve's mission.
    Luke 10:2 He said to them, “The harvest is plentiful, but the workers are few. Therefore petition the Lord of the harvest to send workers out into His harvest.

Matthew 9:38
Pray therefore to the Lord of the harvest, that He send out labourers into His harvest.”
  Luke 10:2 — teaching parallel: Both direct prayer to the Lord of the harvest to send workers into His harvest. The shared saying does not erase the different mission settings.
    Luke 10:2 He said to them, “The harvest is plentiful, but the workers are few. Therefore petition the Lord of the harvest to send workers out into His harvest.

Matthew 10:1
He summoned His twelve disciples and gave them authority over unclean spirits, to cast them out and to heal every disease and every infirmity.
  Luke 9:1–9:2 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus gather the Twelve and give them authority connected with demons and healing disease. Luke also states the commission to proclaim God's kingdom within the cited range.
    Luke 9:1 Calling the Twelve together, He gave them power and authority over all demons and to heal diseases.
    Luke 9:2 He sent them to proclaim God’s kingdom and heal the sick.

Matthew 10:2
These are the names of the twelve apostles: first Simon called Peter, and Andrew his brother; James the son of Zebedee, and John his brother;
  Mark 3:16–3:17 — narrative parallel: Both apostolic lists include Simon named Peter and James and John as Zebedee's sons. Matthew includes Andrew in this verse; Mark places Andrew in the following verse, outside this cited pair.
    Mark 3:16 He appointed the Twelve: Simon, to whom He gave the name Peter;
    Mark 3:17 James son of Zebedee and John, James’s brother, to whom He gave the name Boanerges, that is, Sons of Thunder;

Matthew 10:3
Philip and Bartholomew; Thomas and Matthew the tax collector; James the son of Alphaeus, and Thaddaeus;
  Mark 3:18 — narrative parallel: Both lists name Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas, James son of Alphaeus and Thaddaeus. Matthew calls Matthew a tax collector; Mark's cited verse does not add that description.
    Mark 3:18 Andrew, Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas, James son of Alphaeus, Thaddaeus, Simon the Cananaean,

Matthew 10:4
Simon the Cananaean, and Judas Iscariot, who also handed Him over.
  Mark 3:18–3:19 — narrative parallel: Both apostolic lists name Simon the Cananaean and Judas Iscariot, and identify Judas as the one who handed Jesus over.
    Mark 3:18 Andrew, Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas, James son of Alphaeus, Thaddaeus, Simon the Cananaean,
    Mark 3:19 and Judas Iscariot, who also handed Him over.

Matthew 10:5
Jesus sent these twelve, commanding them, “Do not go along a road of the Gentiles, and do not enter a city of Samaritans.
  Acts 1:8 — thematic parallel: Matthew's instructions restrict this mission away from Gentile routes and Samaritan cities. Acts later explicitly commissions witness in Samaria and to the earth's end. This is a contrast between commissions in different narrative periods, not an assertion that their geographical instructions are identical.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

Matthew 10:6
Go instead to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.
  Matthew 15:24 — teaching parallel: Jesus later describes His own mission as to the lost sheep of Israel's house, the same specifically named group to which He directs the Twelve here. This is a same-book repetition in a different encounter.
    Matthew 15:24 He answered, “I was sent only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.”

Matthew 10:7
As you go, proclaim, ‘The kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.’
  Luke 9:2 — narrative parallel: Both commission the Twelve to proclaim the kingdom. Matthew supplies the message that the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near; Luke says to proclaim God's kingdom and heal.
    Luke 9:2 He sent them to proclaim God’s kingdom and heal the sick.

Matthew 10:8
Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse lepers, cast out demons. Freely you received; freely give.
  Luke 9:1–9:2 — narrative parallel: Both mission instructions include authority over demons and healing the sick. Matthew additionally names raising the dead, cleansing lepers and giving freely; those additional commands are not supplied by this Luke passage.
    Luke 9:1 Calling the Twelve together, He gave them power and authority over all demons and to heal diseases.
    Luke 9:2 He sent them to proclaim God’s kingdom and heal the sick.

Matthew 10:9
Do not acquire gold, silver, or copper for your belts,
  Mark 6:8 — narrative parallel: Both prohibit taking money on the mission journey. Mark's allowance of a staff and its prohibition of bread and a bag belong to its fuller instruction and are not silently transferred to Matthew's different equipment list.
    Mark 6:8 He instructed them to take nothing for the journey except a staff alone: no bread, no bag, no copper money in their belt,

Matthew 10:10
nor a bag for the journey, nor two tunics, nor sandals, nor a staff; for the worker is worthy of his food.
  Mark 6:8–6:9 — narrative parallel: Both restrict mission equipment and disallow two tunics. Matthew also forbids sandals and a staff, whereas Mark permits a staff and instructs wearing sandals; this real equipment difference is retained.
    Mark 6:8 He instructed them to take nothing for the journey except a staff alone: no bread, no bag, no copper money in their belt,
    Mark 6:9 but to wear sandals, and, “Do not put on two tunics.”

Matthew 10:11
Whatever city or village you enter, inquire who in it is worthy, and remain there until you leave.
  Mark 6:10 — narrative parallel: Both tell the messengers to remain in the household entered until leaving the place. Matthew adds inquiry about a worthy host before the stay.
    Mark 6:10 He said to them, “Wherever you enter a house, remain there until you leave that place.

Matthew 10:12
As you enter the house, greet it.
  Luke 10:5 — teaching parallel: Both direct a greeting on entering a house. Luke explicitly gives the peace greeting, while Matthew's next verse explains the peace conveyed by the greeting; the mission groups differ in their narrative settings.
    Luke 10:5 Whatever house you enter, first say, ‘Peace to this house.’

Matthew 10:13
If the house is worthy, let your peace come upon it; but if it is not worthy, let your peace return to you.
  Luke 10:6 — teaching parallel: Both describe peace resting on a receptive household member and otherwise returning to the messenger. Matthew speaks of a worthy house, while Luke calls the recipient a son of peace.
    Luke 10:6 If a son of peace is there, your peace will rest upon him; otherwise it will return to you.

Matthew 10:14
Whoever will not receive you or hear your words, as you leave that house or city, shake the dust off your feet.
  Mark 6:11 — narrative parallel: Both direct messengers leaving a place that refuses reception or hearing to shake dust from their feet. Mark calls it testimony to them; Matthew specifies house or city.
    Mark 6:11 Whatever place does not receive you, and whose people do not listen to you, shake off the dust beneath your feet as you leave there, as a testimony to them.”

Matthew 10:15
Truly I tell you, it will be more bearable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah on the day of judgment than for that city.
  Luke 10:12 — teaching parallel: Both warn that Sodom's treatment on the coming day will be more bearable than that of a city rejecting the messengers. Matthew also names Gomorrah and explicitly says day of judgment.
    Luke 10:12 I tell you, it will be more bearable for Sodom on that day than for that city.

Matthew 10:16
Behold, I send you as sheep in the midst of wolves. So become prudent as snakes and innocent as doves.
  Luke 10:3 — teaching parallel: Both picture the mission as vulnerable animals sent among wolves. Matthew says sheep and adds prudence as snakes and innocence as doves; Luke says lambs without those added comparisons.
    Luke 10:3 Go; behold, I send you as lambs among wolves.

Matthew 10:17
Beware of people, for they will hand you over to councils and flog you in their synagogues.
  Mark 13:9 — teaching parallel: Both warn of disciples being handed to councils and beaten in synagogues. Mark places the warning in a later discourse and also names rulers and kings.
    Mark 13:9 But watch yourselves. They will hand you over to councils; you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for My sake, as a testimony to them.

Matthew 10:18
You will also be brought before governors and kings because of Me, as a testimony to them and to the nations.
  Mark 13:9 — teaching parallel: Both predict appearance before governors and kings because of Jesus, for a testimony. Matthew explicitly adds the nations among the recipients of testimony; the discourse settings differ.
    Mark 13:9 But watch yourselves. They will hand you over to councils; you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for My sake, as a testimony to them.

Matthew 10:19
When they hand you over, do not be anxious about how or what you should speak, for what you should speak will be given to you at that hour.
  Mark 13:11 — teaching parallel: Both tell those handed over not to be anxious about their defense beforehand because the needed speech will be given at that hour.
    Mark 13:11 When they lead you away to hand you over, do not worry beforehand about what to say. Whatever is given you in that hour, say that; for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.

Matthew 10:20
For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you.
  Mark 13:11 — teaching parallel: Both identify the Spirit as the source of the disciples' speech under arrest. Matthew says the Spirit of your Father speaks in you, while Mark says the Holy Spirit rather than you is speaking.
    Mark 13:11 When they lead you away to hand you over, do not worry beforehand about what to say. Whatever is given you in that hour, say that; for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.

Matthew 10:21
Brother will hand brother over to death, and a father his child; children will rise against parents and have them put to death.
  Mark 13:12 — teaching parallel: Both warn of brothers handing brothers to death, fathers handing over children and children rising against parents to have them killed.
    Mark 13:12 Brother will hand brother over to death, and a father his child. Children will rise against parents and have them put to death.

Matthew 10:22
You will be hated by all because of My name, but the one who endures to the end will be saved.
  Mark 13:13 — teaching parallel: Both predict hatred because of Jesus' name and promise salvation to the person who endures to the end. The repeated warning appears in different discourse settings.
    Mark 13:13 You will be hated by all because of My name. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

Matthew 10:23
When they persecute you in this city, flee to the other. For truly I tell you, you will certainly not finish going through the cities of Israel until the Son of Man comes.
  Acts 14:5–14:6 — thematic parallel: Acts supplies a concrete later example of flight to other cities when hostility threatens the messengers: they learn of a plan involving stoning and flee to Lystra and Derbe. It illustrates flight under persecution, not Matthew's further timetable concerning the Son of Man's coming.
    Acts 14:5 When an attempt was made by both Gentiles and Jews, together with their rulers, to mistreat and stone them,
    Acts 14:6 they learned of it and fled to Lystra and Derbe, cities of Lycaonia, and the surrounding region.

Matthew 10:24
A disciple is not above the teacher, nor a slave above his master.
  Luke 6:40 — teaching parallel: Both say a disciple is not above the teacher. Luke adds that a fully trained disciple will be like the teacher, while Matthew also gives the slave-master comparison.
    Luke 6:40 A disciple is not above the teacher, but everyone fully trained will be like his teacher.

Matthew 10:25
It is enough for the disciple to become like his teacher, and the slave like his master. If they called the master of the house Beelzebul, how much more his household members!
  John 15:20 — teaching parallel: Both use the relationship between master and subordinate to explain why disciples can expect treatment like Jesus'. John makes persecution explicit; Matthew's example is the household master's being called Beelzebul, a detail absent from the cited John verse.
    John 15:20 Remember the word that I said to you: ‘A slave is not greater than his master.’ If they persecuted Me, they will also persecute you; if they kept My word, they will also keep yours.

Matthew 10:26
So do not fear them. For nothing is covered that will not be revealed, or hidden that will not become known.
  Luke 12:2 — teaching parallel: Both say that covered things will be revealed and hidden things become known. Matthew uses this assurance to tell disciples not to fear their opponents.
    Luke 12:2 Nothing is covered up that will not be uncovered, or hidden that will not become known.

Matthew 10:27
What I tell you in the darkness, say in the light; and what you hear in the ear, proclaim upon the housetops.
  Luke 12:3 — teaching parallel: Both contrast speech in darkness or private rooms with public hearing in the light and proclamation from housetops. Matthew commands public proclamation of His teaching; Luke predicts the public exposure of private speech.
    Luke 12:3 Therefore whatever you have said in the darkness will be heard in the light, and what you have spoken into the ear in the inner rooms will be proclaimed on the housetops.

Matthew 10:28
Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul; rather fear Him who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna.
  Luke 12:4–12:5 — teaching parallel: Both distinguish people who can kill the body from the one with authority concerning Gehenna and direct fear toward the latter. Matthew explicitly mentions destruction of both soul and body; Luke does not repeat that formulation.
    Luke 12:4 I tell you, My friends, do not fear those who kill the body and after that have nothing more they can do.
    Luke 12:5 But I will show you whom to fear: fear the One who, after killing, has authority to cast into Gehenna. Yes, I tell you, fear Him.

Matthew 10:29
Are not two sparrows sold for an assarion? Yet not one of them will fall to the ground apart from your Father.
  Luke 12:6 — teaching parallel: Both use inexpensive sparrows to illustrate God's care. Matthew prices two at one assarion and says none falls apart from the Father; Luke prices five at two assaria and says none is forgotten before God.
    Luke 12:6 Are not five sparrows sold for two assaria? Yet not one of them is forgotten before God.

Matthew 10:30
Even the hairs of your head are all numbered.
  Luke 12:7 — teaching parallel: Both say even the hairs of the disciples' heads are numbered, as an image of God's detailed care.
    Luke 12:7 Indeed, even the hairs of your head are all numbered. Do not fear; you are worth more than many sparrows.

Matthew 10:31
So do not fear; you are worth more than many sparrows.
  Luke 12:7 — teaching parallel: Both urge freedom from fear because the hearers are more valuable than many sparrows.
    Luke 12:7 Indeed, even the hairs of your head are all numbered. Do not fear; you are worth more than many sparrows.

Matthew 10:32
Therefore everyone who acknowledges Me before people, I too will acknowledge before My Father in the heavens.
  Luke 12:8 — teaching parallel: Both connect acknowledging Jesus before people with a corresponding heavenly acknowledgment. Matthew promises acknowledgment before His Father; Luke describes the Son of Man's acknowledgment before God's angels.
    Luke 12:8 And I tell you, everyone who acknowledges Me before people, the Son of Man will also acknowledge before the angels of God.

Matthew 10:33
But whoever denies Me before people, I too will deny before My Father in the heavens.
  Luke 12:9 — teaching parallel: Both warn that denying Jesus before people leads to heavenly denial. Matthew names His Father as the heavenly audience, while Luke names God's angels.
    Luke 12:9 But whoever denies Me before people will be denied before the angels of God.

Matthew 10:34
Do not suppose that I came to bring peace upon the earth. I did not come to bring peace, but a sword.
  Luke 12:51 — teaching parallel: Both challenge the expectation that Jesus' coming brings uncomplicated earthly peace. Matthew uses the image of a sword; Luke explicitly says division.
    Luke 12:51 Do you think I have come to give peace on earth? No, I tell you, but division.

Matthew 10:35
For I came to divide a man against his father, a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law,
  Luke 12:53 — teaching parallel: Both describe divisions involving father and son, mother and daughter, and mother-in-law and daughter-in-law. Luke gives reciprocal formulations, while Matthew states the divisions in one direction.
    Luke 12:53 They will be divided: father against son and son against father, mother against daughter and daughter against mother, mother-in-law against her daughter-in-law and daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law.”

Matthew 10:36
and a person’s enemies will be the members of his own household.
  Micah 7:6 — allusion: Matthew's statement that a person's enemies will be members of the household recalls Micah's concluding statement that a man's enemies are the men in his house. Micah also lists family divisions matching the preceding Matthew verse.
    Micah 7:6 For a son dishonors his father, a daughter will rise against her mother, a daughter-in-law will rise against her mother-in-law; a man’s enemies are all the men in his house.

Matthew 10:37
Whoever loves father or mother more than Me is not worthy of Me; and whoever loves son or daughter more than Me is not worthy of Me.
  Luke 14:26 — teaching parallel: Both demand allegiance to Jesus over the closest family relationships. Matthew expresses this as not loving parents or children more than Him; Luke uses the stronger language of hating family and one's own life. The wordings are not harmonized into an exact quotation.
    Luke 14:26 “If anyone comes to Me and does not hate his own father and mother and wife and children and brothers and sisters, and even his own life, he cannot be My disciple.

Matthew 10:38
Whoever does not take his cross and follow after Me is not worthy of Me.
  Luke 14:27 — teaching parallel: Both make taking or carrying one's cross and coming after Jesus a requirement of discipleship. Matthew describes the person as not worthy of Him, while Luke says the person cannot be His disciple.
    Luke 14:27 Whoever does not carry his own cross and come after Me cannot be My disciple.

Matthew 10:39
Whoever finds his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life because of Me will find it.
  Mark 8:35 — teaching parallel: Both reverse the attempt to preserve one's life: losing it because of Jesus leads to gaining or saving it. Mark explicitly adds the gospel as a cause and uses save where Matthew uses find.
    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

Matthew 10:40
Whoever receives you receives Me, and whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me.
  John 13:20 — teaching parallel: Both describe reception of Jesus' messenger as reception of Jesus and reception of Jesus as reception of the One who sent Him.
    John 13:20 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who receives anyone I send receives Me, and the one who receives Me receives the One who sent Me.”

Matthew 10:41
Whoever receives a prophet in the name of a prophet will receive a prophet’s reward, and whoever receives a righteous person in the name of a righteous person will receive a righteous person’s reward.
  2 Kings 4:8–4:10 — thematic parallel: The Shunammite woman's hospitality to Elisha because she recognizes him as a holy man of God illustrates receiving a prophet in that capacity. These verses narrate bread and lodging; they do not themselves state Matthew's general promise of a prophet's reward.
    2 Kings 4:8 One day Elisha passed through Shunem. A prominent woman was there, and she pressed him to eat bread. As he continued coming there over time, he would turn aside to eat there.
    2 Kings 4:9 The woman said to her husband, ‘Behold, I know that this is a holy man of God who continually passes by us.’
    2 Kings 4:10 ‘Please let us make a small upstairs place for him and put a bed, a table, a stool, and a lampstand there for him. When he comes to us, he can turn aside there.’

Matthew 10:42
And whoever gives one of these little ones even a cup of cold water in the name of a disciple, truly I tell you, will certainly not lose his reward.”
  Mark 9:41 — teaching parallel: Both promise that giving a cup of water on account of the recipient's belonging to Jesus will not lose its reward. Matthew says cold water to one of these little ones in a disciple's name; Mark speaks of giving to the disciples because they are Christ's.
    Mark 9:41 Whoever gives you a cup of water to drink in recognition that you belong to Christ, truly I tell you, will certainly not lose his reward.

Matthew 11:1
When Jesus finished directing His twelve disciples, He moved on from there to teach and proclaim in their cities.
  Matthew 13:53 — same-book structural parallel: completed teaching and departure: Both notices use the completion of Jesus' teaching to introduce His moving on. One follows the directions to the Twelve and leads to teaching and proclaiming in their cities; the other follows the parables. This compares an explicit internal transition pattern without identifying the journeys as one occasion.
    Matthew 13:53 When Jesus finished these parables, He departed from there.

Matthew 11:2
John, hearing in prison about the works of the Christ, sent through his disciples
  Luke 7:18–7:19 — narrative parallel: Both describe John sending disciples to Jesus after hearing reports about Him. Matthew specifies John's imprisonment; Luke explicitly mentions two disciples without naming the prison in this passage.
    Luke 7:18 John’s disciples reported all these things to him. John called two of his disciples
    Luke 7:19 and sent them to the Lord, saying, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”

Matthew 11:3
and said to Him, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”
  Luke 7:19–7:20 — narrative parallel: Both preserve John's question whether Jesus is the coming One or another should be expected. Luke records both the sending instruction and the messengers' delivery of the question.
    Luke 7:19 and sent them to the Lord, saying, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”
    Luke 7:20 When the men came to Him, they said, “John the Baptist sent us to Thee, saying, ‘Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?’”

Matthew 11:4
Jesus answered them, “Go and report to John what you hear and see:
  Luke 7:22 — narrative parallel: Both direct the messengers to report to John what they have seen and heard, introducing the list of healing and proclamation signs.
    Luke 7:22 He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.

Matthew 11:5
the blind receive sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, the dead are raised and the poor have good news proclaimed to them.
  Luke 7:22 — narrative parallel: Both list blind people seeing, lame people walking, lepers cleansed, deaf people hearing, dead people raised and poor people receiving good news as the reply to John's question.
    Luke 7:22 He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.

Matthew 11:6
And blessed is whoever does not stumble because of Me.”
  Luke 7:23 — teaching parallel: Both conclude the reply to John by pronouncing blessed the person who does not stumble because of Jesus.
    Luke 7:23 And blessed is whoever does not stumble because of Me.”

Matthew 11:7
As they went away, Jesus began to speak to the crowds about John: “What did you go out into the wilderness to behold? A reed shaken by the wind?
  Luke 7:24 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus beginning to address the crowd about John after the messengers leave and asking whether they went into the wilderness to see a reed shaken by wind.
    Luke 7:24 When John’s messengers had departed, He began to speak to the crowds about John: “What did you go out into the wilderness to behold? A reed shaken by the wind?

Matthew 11:8
But what did you go out to see? A man dressed in soft clothing? Behold, those wearing soft clothing are in kings’ houses.
  Luke 7:25 — teaching parallel: Both reject the image of John as a softly clothed court figure. Matthew says such people are in kings' houses, while Luke describes splendid clothing and luxury in royal palaces.
    Luke 7:25 But what did you go out to see? A man clothed in soft garments? Behold, those who live in splendid clothing and luxury are in royal palaces.

Matthew 11:9
But why did you go out? To see a prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet.
  Luke 7:26 — teaching parallel: Both ask whether the crowds went to see a prophet and affirm that John is more than a prophet.
    Luke 7:26 But what did you go out to see? A prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet.

Matthew 11:10
This is the one about whom it is written: ‘Behold, I send My messenger before Thy face, who will prepare Thy way before Thee.’
  Malachi 3:1 — quotation: Matthew's messenger preparing the way is grounded in Malachi's announced messenger. The Saints Malachi wording has the way before My face and goes on to the Lord's coming to His sanctuary, whereas Matthew addresses Thy face and Thy way; the differences remain visible.
    Malachi 3:1 “Look, I am sending out My messenger, and he will look to a way before My face. Suddenly the Lord whom you seek will come to His own sanctuary, and the messenger of the covenant whom you desire. Look, he is coming,” says the Lord Almighty.

Matthew 11:11
Truly I tell you, among those born of women none greater than John the Baptist has arisen; yet the least in the kingdom of the heavens is greater than he.
  Luke 7:28 — teaching parallel: Both compare John's greatness among those born of women with the greater standing of the least in God's kingdom. Matthew calls him John the Baptist and says kingdom of the heavens.
    Luke 7:28 I tell you, among those born of women no one is greater than John; yet the lesser one in God’s kingdom is greater than he.”

Matthew 11:12
From the days of John the Baptist until now, the kingdom of the heavens suffers violence, and violent men seize it.
  Luke 16:16 — teaching parallel: Both link John with a new phase of the kingdom and use language of force or pressing. Matthew says the kingdom suffers violence and violent men seize it; Luke says the kingdom is proclaimed and everyone presses into it. The comparison does not resolve these distinct expressions into one interpretation.
    Luke 16:16 The Law and the Prophets were until John. From that time the kingdom of God is proclaimed as good news, and everyone presses into it.

Matthew 11:13
For all the Prophets and the Law prophesied until John.
  Luke 16:16 — teaching parallel: Both place the Law and Prophets up to John. Luke continues with the proclamation of God's kingdom from then onward, while Matthew explicitly says the Law and Prophets prophesied until John.
    Luke 16:16 The Law and the Prophets were until John. From that time the kingdom of God is proclaimed as good news, and everyone presses into it.

Matthew 11:14
And if you are willing to receive it, he is Elijah who is to come.
  Luke 1:17 — thematic parallel: Matthew identifies John with the Elijah who is to come; Luke describes John's going before the Lord in Elijah's spirit and power. This connects the Elijah role without claiming John and the historical Elijah are literally the same person.
    Luke 1:17 He himself will go before Him in Elijah’s spirit and power, to turn fathers’ hearts toward children and the disobedient to the understanding of the righteous, to prepare for the Lord a people made ready.”

Matthew 11:15
Let the one who has ears hear.
  Mark 4:9 — teaching parallel: Both use the summons for the one with ears to hear. Mark places the formula after the sower parable, while Matthew places it in the discourse about John; the occasions differ.
    Mark 4:9 He said, “Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”

Matthew 11:16
To what shall I compare this generation? It is like children sitting in the marketplaces who call to the others,
  Luke 7:31–7:32 — teaching parallel: Both compare the present generation to children sitting in a marketplace and calling to one another, introducing the complaint about unresponsive playmates.
    Luke 7:31 “To what, then, shall I compare the people of this generation, and what are they like?
    Luke 7:32 They are like children sitting in the marketplace and calling to one another, saying, ‘We played the flute for you, and you did not dance; we lamented, and you did not weep.’

Matthew 11:17
‘We played the flute for you, and you did not dance; we sang a lament, and you did not beat your breasts.’
  Luke 7:32 — teaching parallel: Both give the children's complaint that flute playing produced no dancing and lament produced no mourning response. Matthew says beating the breast, while Luke says weeping.
    Luke 7:32 They are like children sitting in the marketplace and calling to one another, saying, ‘We played the flute for you, and you did not dance; we lamented, and you did not weep.’

Matthew 11:18
For John came neither eating nor drinking, and they say, ‘He has a demon.’
  Luke 7:33 — teaching parallel: Both report the accusation that John has a demon in response to his abstinent way of life. Luke specifies neither eating bread nor drinking wine; Matthew says neither eating nor drinking.
    Luke 7:33 For John the Baptist has come neither eating bread nor drinking wine, and you say, ‘He has a demon.’

Matthew 11:19
The Son of Man came eating and drinking, and they say, ‘Behold, a gluttonous man and a wine drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners!’ Yet wisdom was vindicated by her works.”
  Luke 7:34–7:35 — teaching parallel: Both report Jesus being called a glutton, wine drinker and friend of tax collectors and sinners, then speak of wisdom's vindication. Matthew says vindicated by her works, while Luke says by all her children; the ending differs.
    Luke 7:34 The Son of Man has come eating and drinking, and you say, ‘Behold, a glutton and wine-drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners.’
    Luke 7:35 Yet wisdom was justified by all her children.”

Matthew 11:20
Then He began to reproach the cities in which most of His mighty works had occurred, because they did not repent:
  Luke 10:13 — thematic parallel: Luke's woe over Chorazin and Bethsaida gives the same contrast between mighty works and the repentance that would have occurred elsewhere. Matthew's verse introduces the reproach of unrepentant cities, with those names in its following verse; the discourse settings differ.
    Luke 10:13 Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty deeds performed among you had happened in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented, sitting in sackcloth and ashes.

Matthew 11:21
“Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty works that occurred in you had occurred in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented in sackcloth and ashes.
  Luke 10:13 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce woe on Chorazin and Bethsaida and say Tyre and Sidon would have repented in sackcloth and ashes if they had experienced the same mighty works.
    Luke 10:13 Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty deeds performed among you had happened in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented, sitting in sackcloth and ashes.

Matthew 11:22
Nevertheless I tell you, it will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon on the day of judgment than for you.
  Luke 10:14 — teaching parallel: Both warn that judgment will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon than for the reproached towns.
    Luke 10:14 Nevertheless, it will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon at the judgment than for you.

Matthew 11:23
And you, Capernaum, will you be exalted to heaven? You will descend to Hades! For if the mighty works that occurred in you had occurred in Sodom, it would have remained until today.
  Luke 10:15 — teaching parallel: Both address Capernaum with the contrast between supposed exaltation to heaven and descent to Hades. Luke's verse does not include Matthew's additional comparison with Sodom's survival.
    Luke 10:15 And you, Capernaum, will you be exalted as far as heaven? You will go down as far as Hades.

Matthew 11:24
Nevertheless I tell you, it will be more bearable for the land of Sodom on the day of judgment than for you.”
  Matthew 10:15 — teaching parallel: Matthew repeats the warning of more bearable judgment for Sodom, here in the reproach of Capernaum and earlier in instructions about towns rejecting the mission. This is a same-book recurring judgment comparison in different settings; the earlier verse also names Gomorrah.
    Matthew 10:15 Truly I tell you, it will be more bearable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah on the day of judgment than for that city.

Matthew 11:25
At that time Jesus answered, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants.
  Luke 10:21 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' acknowledgment of the Father as Lord of heaven and earth for hiding these things from the wise and understanding and revealing them to infants. Luke additionally locates His joy in the Holy Spirit.
    Luke 10:21 At that very hour He rejoiced in the Holy Spirit and said, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants. Yes, Father, for such was Thy good pleasure.

Matthew 11:26
Yes, Father, because this was pleasing before Thee.
  Luke 10:21 — teaching parallel: Both complete the address with Yes, Father and an appeal to what was pleasing before Him. Luke includes it in the same verse as the preceding thanksgiving.
    Luke 10:21 At that very hour He rejoiced in the Holy Spirit and said, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants. Yes, Father, for such was Thy good pleasure.

Matthew 11:27
All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.
  Luke 10:22 — teaching parallel: Both declare all things handed over by the Father and the unique mutual knowledge of Father and Son, with the Son revealing the Father to those He chooses.
    Luke 10:22 Everything has been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows who the Son is except the Father, or who the Father is except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.”

Matthew 11:28
Come to Me, all you who labour and are burdened, and I will give you rest.
  Sirach 51:23–51:27 — thematic parallel: Both present an invitation associated with a teacher, instruction and rest. Sirach invites the uninstructed near, speaks of a yoke and reports finding much rest after little labor; Jesus invites the burdened to Himself and promises to give rest. This is a wisdom-teaching comparison, not a claim that Sirach names Jesus.
    Sirach 51:23 Draw near to me, you who are uninstructed, and lodge in a house of instruction.
    Sirach 51:24 Why do you say you are lacking in these things, while your souls thirst exceedingly?
    Sirach 51:25 I opened my mouth and said, “Acquire for yourselves without silver.
    Sirach 51:26 “Put your neck beneath the yoke, and let your soul receive instruction. She is near at hand to be found.
    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

Matthew 11:29
Take My yoke upon you, and learn from Me; for I am meek and lowly in heart, and you shall find rest for your souls.
  Sirach 51:26–51:27 — allusion: The combination of taking a yoke, receiving instruction and finding rest recalls Sirach's invitation to put the neck beneath the yoke and receive instruction, followed by much rest. Matthew centers the invitation on learning from Jesus and adds His meekness and humility.
    Sirach 51:26 “Put your neck beneath the yoke, and let your soul receive instruction. She is near at hand to be found.
    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

Matthew 11:30
For My yoke is easy, and My burden is light.”
  Sirach 51:27 — thematic parallel: Jesus describes His yoke as easy and burden as light, while Sirach describes little labor yielding much rest. The comparison concerns the rewarding lightness of instruction; Sirach does not repeat Matthew's statement about Jesus' own yoke.
    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

Matthew 12:1
At that time Jesus went through the grainfields on the Sabbath; and His disciples grew hungry, and began to pluck heads of grain and eat.
  Mark 2:23 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus and His disciples passing through grainfields on the Sabbath and the disciples plucking heads of grain. Matthew explicitly mentions hunger and eating in this verse.
    Mark 2:23 He was passing through the grainfields on the Sabbath, and His disciples began to make their way, plucking the heads of grain.

Matthew 12:2
And the Pharisees, seeing it, said to Him, “Behold, Thy disciples do what is not lawful on the Sabbath.”
  Mark 2:24 — narrative parallel: Both have Pharisees challenge Jesus about the disciples' doing what is not lawful on the Sabbath in the grainfield setting.
    Mark 2:24 The Pharisees said to Him, “Look! Why are they doing what is not lawful on the Sabbath?”

Matthew 12:3
He said to them, “Have you not read what David did when he and those with him became hungry:
  Mark 2:25 — narrative parallel: Both answer the Sabbath challenge by asking whether the critics have read what David did when he and his companions were hungry.
    Mark 2:25 He said to them, “Have you never read what David did when he was in need and hungry, he and those with him:

Matthew 12:4
how he entered the house of God, and they ate the bread of the Presence, which it was not lawful for him or those with him to eat, but only for the priests?
  1 Samuel 21:5–21:7 — allusion: Samuel narrates the priest giving David holy loaves of presentation, the bread of the Presence, after discussion about the young men's purity. This supplies the bread episode Jesus recalls; these cited verses do not themselves state Matthew's full legal restriction to priests.
    1 Samuel 21:5 The priest answered David and said, “There are no common loaves at hand, only holy loaves. If the young men have kept themselves from a woman, then he may eat.”
    1 Samuel 21:6 David answered the priest and said to him, “But we have abstained from a woman yesterday and the third day. When I went out on the road, all the young men had been purified. The road itself is common, for it will be sanctified today through my equipment.”
    1 Samuel 21:7 Abimelech the priest gave him the loaves of presentation, for there was no bread there except the loaves of the Presence, removed from before the Lord so that hot bread could be set out on the day he took them.

Matthew 12:5
Or have you not read in the Law that on Sabbaths the priests in the temple profane the Sabbath and are guiltless?
  Numbers 28:9–28:10 — allusion: The law prescribes additional burnt offerings on each Sabbath, supplying the commanded temple-service background for Jesus' argument about priests working on Sabbaths without guilt. Numbers states the offering requirement; it does not itself call the service profaning the Sabbath.
    Numbers 28:9 And on the Sabbath day you shall bring two unblemished year-old lambs, and two tenths of fine flour mixed with oil for a sacrifice, and a drink offering,
    Numbers 28:10 a Sabbath whole burnt offering on each Sabbath, besides the continual whole burning and its drink offering.

Matthew 12:6
But I tell you that something greater than the temple is here.
  John 2:19–2:21 — thematic parallel: Matthew claims something greater than the temple is present. John explains Jesus' temple saying as referring to the sanctuary of His body. These are distinct ways of relating Jesus to the sanctuary; John does not repeat Matthew's greater-than comparison.
    John 2:19 Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”
    John 2:20 So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”
    John 2:21 But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.

Matthew 12:7
If you had known what ‘I desire mercy and not sacrifice’ means, you would not have condemned the guiltless.
  Hosea 6:6 — quotation: Jesus' mercy-not-sacrifice quotation matches Hosea's statement of the Lord's desire for mercy rather than sacrifice. Matthew applies it to the condemnation of the guiltless in the Sabbath dispute; Hosea continues with knowledge of God over burnt offerings.
    Hosea 6:6 For I desire mercy, and not sacrifice, and knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings.

Matthew 12:8
For the Son of Man is Lord of the Sabbath.”
  Mark 2:28 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the grainfield controversy with the Son of Man's lordship over the Sabbath.
    Mark 2:28 So the Son of Man is Lord even of the Sabbath.”

Matthew 12:9
Moving on from there, He entered their synagogue.
  Mark 3:1 — narrative parallel: Both move into a synagogue scene that introduces a man with a withered hand. Matthew's verse gives the entry, while Mark includes the man's presence in the same verse.
    Mark 3:1 He entered a synagogue again, and a man with a withered hand was there.

Matthew 12:10
And behold, a man had a withered hand. They asked Him, “Is it lawful to heal on Sabbaths?” so that they might accuse Him.
  Luke 6:7 — narrative parallel: Both place Sabbath healing under hostile scrutiny aimed at finding an accusation. Matthew reports a direct question about lawfulness, while Luke describes the scribes and Pharisees watching whether Jesus would heal.
    Luke 6:7 The scribes and Pharisees watched closely to see whether He would heal on the Sabbath, so that they might find a way to accuse Him.

Matthew 12:11
He said to them, “Who among you, if he has one sheep and it falls into a pit on a Sabbath, will not grasp it and lift it out?
  Luke 14:5 — teaching parallel: Both appeal to rescuing a vulnerable creature from a pit or well on the Sabbath as an answer to healing objections. Matthew uses a sheep and its being grasped and lifted; Luke's supplied saying uses a son or an ox, so the examples and narrative occasions differ.
    Luke 14:5 And He said to them, “Which of you, if his son or ox falls into a well, will not pull him out at once on the Sabbath day?”

Matthew 12:12
How much more valuable, then, is a human being than a sheep! So it is lawful to do good on Sabbaths.”
  Luke 6:9 — teaching parallel: Both argue the Sabbath question in terms of doing good. Matthew concludes it is lawful to do good and compares a human's value with a sheep's; Luke asks whether doing good or evil, saving life or destroying it, is lawful.
    Luke 6:9 Jesus said to them, “I ask you: Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or do evil, to save a life or destroy it?”

Matthew 12:13
Then He said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and it was restored, healthy like the other.
  Mark 3:5 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' command to stretch out the hand, the man's action and its restoration. Matthew explicitly says it became healthy like the other; Mark describes Jesus' anger and grief before the command.
    Mark 3:5 He looked around at them with anger, grieving at the hardness of their hearts, and said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and his hand was restored.

Matthew 12:14
The Pharisees went out and took counsel against Him, how they might destroy Him.
  Mark 3:6 — narrative parallel: Both have the Pharisees go out and take counsel about destroying Jesus after the healing. Mark additionally names the Herodians as partners in the plot.
    Mark 3:6 The Pharisees went out and immediately took counsel with the Herodians against Him, as to how they might destroy Him.

Matthew 12:15
But Jesus, knowing this, withdrew from there. Great crowds followed Him, and He healed them all.
  Mark 3:7–3:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe withdrawal, crowds following and healing activity. Matthew says He healed them all and links withdrawal to knowledge of the plot; Mark lists the crowds' regions and says He healed many, so scope and wording are not made identical.
    Mark 3:7 Jesus withdrew with His disciples to the sea, and a great multitude from Galilee followed; and from Judea,
    Mark 3:8 Jerusalem, Idumea, beyond the Jordan, and the region of Tyre and Sidon, a great multitude heard all He was doing and came to Him.
    Mark 3:9 He told His disciples to have a small boat ready for Him because of the crowd, so that they would not crush Him.
    Mark 3:10 For He healed many, so that all who had afflictions pressed upon Him to touch Him.

Matthew 12:16
He sternly warned them not to make Him known,
  Mark 3:12 — narrative parallel: Both report a stern command not to make Jesus known. Matthew addresses the healed crowd in the surrounding narrative; Mark's immediate recipients are the unclean spirits, a difference in audience retained here.
    Mark 3:12 He sternly warned them not to make Him known.

Matthew 12:17
so that what was spoken through Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled:
  Isaiah 42:1–42:4 — quotation: Matthew introduces the following servant passage as fulfillment of Isaiah. The cited Saints Isaiah unit supplies the chosen servant, Spirit, restrained public conduct, bruised reed and nations' hope, though its naming and some wording differ from Matthew's quotation.
    Isaiah 42:1 “Jacob is My servant; I will uphold him. Israel is My chosen one; My soul accepted him. I put My spirit upon him; he will bring judgment to the nations.
    Isaiah 42:2 He will not cry out or raise his voice, nor will his voice be heard outside.
    Isaiah 42:3 He will not crush a bruised reed or extinguish a smoking flax wick; rather, he will bring judgment in truth.
    Isaiah 42:4 He will shine forth and will not be broken until he sets judgment upon the earth, and nations will hope in his name.”

Matthew 12:18
“Behold, My Servant whom I chose, My beloved in whom My soul was well pleased. I will put My Spirit upon Him, and He will proclaim justice to the nations.
  Isaiah 42:1 — quotation: Both describe God's chosen servant, divine approval, the Spirit placed upon him and judgment proclaimed to the nations. Saints Isaiah explicitly names Jacob as servant and Israel as chosen one, whereas Matthew has My Servant and My beloved; this difference and Matthew's application to Jesus remain explicit.
    Isaiah 42:1 “Jacob is My servant; I will uphold him. Israel is My chosen one; My soul accepted him. I put My spirit upon him; he will bring judgment to the nations.

Matthew 12:19
He will not quarrel or cry out, nor will anyone hear His voice in the streets.
  Isaiah 42:2 — quotation: Both describe the servant's refusal to cry out or raise a public voice. Matthew says no quarreling and no voice heard in the streets; Isaiah says no crying or raising the voice and no voice heard outside.
    Isaiah 42:2 He will not cry out or raise his voice, nor will his voice be heard outside.

Matthew 12:20
He will not break a bruised reed or extinguish a smoldering wick, until He brings justice forth to victory.
  Isaiah 42:3–42:4 — quotation: Both preserve the bruised reed and smoking wick images and join the servant's gentleness to the establishment of judgment. Matthew speaks of judgment brought to victory, while Saints Isaiah says judgment in truth and judgment set upon the earth.
    Isaiah 42:3 He will not crush a bruised reed or extinguish a smoking flax wick; rather, he will bring judgment in truth.
    Isaiah 42:4 He will shine forth and will not be broken until he sets judgment upon the earth, and nations will hope in his name.”

Matthew 12:21
And nations will hope in His name.”
  Isaiah 42:4 — quotation: Matthew's promise that nations will hope in His name matches the final clause of the Saints Isaiah servant passage, retaining this Greek-based English wording.
    Isaiah 42:4 He will shine forth and will not be broken until he sets judgment upon the earth, and nations will hope in his name.”

Matthew 12:22
Then a man oppressed by a demon, blind and mute, was brought to Him. He healed him, so that the mute man spoke and saw.
  Luke 11:14 — narrative parallel: Both link expulsion of a demon with a mute man's speaking. Matthew additionally identifies blindness and restored sight; Luke's cited account does not mention blindness, and the settings are not asserted to be identical.
    Luke 11:14 And He was driving out a mute demon. When the demon came out, the mute man spoke, and the crowds marveled.

Matthew 12:23
All the crowds were amazed and said, “Could this be the Son of David?”
  Matthew 20:30 — thematic parallel: Both place the designation Son of David in a blind-healing context. Here the crowds ask whether Jesus could be that figure after a healing; in the separate Jericho episode two blind men directly address Him that way. The speakers and occasions differ.
    Matthew 20:30 And behold, two blind men sitting beside the road heard that Jesus was passing by and cried out, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”

Matthew 12:24
But when the Pharisees heard it, they said, “This Man casts out demons only by Beelzebul, the ruler of the demons.”
  Luke 11:15 — narrative parallel: Both accuse Jesus of casting out demons by Beelzebul, the ruler of demons. Matthew identifies the speakers as Pharisees; Luke refers to some among the observers.
    Luke 11:15 But some of them said, “He drives out the demons by Beelzebul, the ruler of the demons.”

Matthew 12:25
Knowing their thoughts, He said to them, “Every kingdom divided against itself is laid waste, and every city or house divided against itself will not stand.
  Luke 11:17 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus perceive their thoughts and answer that a kingdom divided against itself is devastated. Matthew adds divided city or house not standing; Luke gives the house-collapse formulation.
    Luke 11:17 But He, knowing their thoughts, said to them, “Every kingdom divided against itself is laid waste, and house falls upon house.

Matthew 12:26
If Satan casts out Satan, he is divided against himself. How then will his kingdom stand?
  Luke 11:18 — teaching parallel: Both ask how Satan's kingdom can stand if Satan is divided against himself, in answer to the accusation that Jesus expels demons through Satan's power.
    Luke 11:18 And if Satan also is divided against himself, how will his kingdom stand? For you say that I drive out the demons by Beelzebul.

Matthew 12:27
And if I cast out demons by Beelzebul, by whom do your sons cast them out? Therefore they will be your judges.
  Luke 11:19 — teaching parallel: Both challenge the accusation by asking through whom the hearers' own sons cast out demons and conclude that those sons will be their judges.
    Luke 11:19 But if I drive out the demons by Beelzebul, by whom do your sons drive them out? Therefore they will be your judges.

Matthew 12:28
But if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you.
  Luke 11:20 — teaching parallel: Both interpret Jesus' expulsions of demons as evidence that God's kingdom has come upon the hearers. Matthew says by God's Spirit; Luke says by God's finger. The distinct imagery is preserved.
    Luke 11:20 But if I drive out the demons by the finger of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you.

Matthew 12:29
Or how can someone enter the strong man’s house and seize his belongings unless he first binds the strong man? Then he will plunder his house.
  Mark 3:27 — teaching parallel: Both use the image of entering a strong man's house and first binding him before plundering his belongings.
    Mark 3:27 No one can enter the strong man’s house and plunder his belongings unless he first binds the strong man. Then he will plunder his house.

Matthew 12:30
Whoever is not with Me is against Me, and whoever does not gather with Me scatters.
  Luke 11:23 — teaching parallel: Both declare that the person not with Jesus is against Him and the person not gathering with Him scatters.
    Luke 11:23 Whoever is not with Me is against Me, and whoever does not gather with Me scatters.

Matthew 12:31
Therefore I tell you, every sin and blasphemy will be forgiven people, but blasphemy against the Spirit will not be forgiven.
  Mark 3:28–3:29 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the forgiveness available for sins and blasphemies with the exception of blasphemy against the Holy Spirit. Mark states the exclusion in terms of never having forgiveness and liability for an eternal sin; Matthew's cited verse does not use that final phrase.
    Mark 3:28 Truly I tell you, people will be forgiven everything—their sins and whatever blasphemies they may utter.
    Mark 3:29 But whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit has no forgiveness for ever, but is liable for an eternal sin.”

Matthew 12:32
Whoever speaks a word against the Son of Man will be forgiven; but whoever speaks against the Holy Spirit will not be forgiven, either in this age or in the coming one.
  Luke 12:10 — teaching parallel: Both distinguish speech against the Son of Man, which can be forgiven, from blasphemy against the Holy Spirit, which will not. Matthew adds neither in this age nor in the coming one.
    Luke 12:10 And everyone who speaks a word against the Son of Man will be forgiven, but whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit will not be forgiven.

Matthew 12:33
Either make the tree good and its fruit good, or make the tree rotten and its fruit rotten; for the tree is known by the fruit.
  Luke 6:43–6:44 — teaching parallel: Both connect a tree's character with its fruit and treat fruit as the means of knowing the tree. Matthew frames a choice between making tree and fruit good or rotten; Luke gives the good-tree and rotten-tree contrasts and examples.
    Luke 6:43 For no good tree produces rotten fruit, nor again does a rotten tree produce good fruit.
    Luke 6:44 Each tree is known by its own fruit. People do not gather figs from thorns, nor harvest grapes from a bramble.

Matthew 12:34
Brood of vipers! How can you speak good things when you are evil? For out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks.
  Luke 6:45 — teaching parallel: Both connect speech with what fills the heart and say the mouth speaks from that abundance. Matthew addresses hostile hearers as vipers and evil; Luke places the statement in a general contrast between good and evil persons.
    Luke 6:45 The good person brings out good from the good treasure of his heart, and the evil person brings out evil from the evil treasure; for his mouth speaks from the heart’s abundance.

Matthew 12:35
The good person brings forth good things from the good treasure, and the evil person brings forth evil things from the evil treasure.
  Luke 6:45 — teaching parallel: Both describe the good person producing good from good treasure and the evil person producing evil from evil treasure. Luke explicitly names the heart as the treasure's location.
    Luke 6:45 The good person brings out good from the good treasure of his heart, and the evil person brings out evil from the evil treasure; for his mouth speaks from the heart’s abundance.

Matthew 12:36
But I tell you that people will give account on the day of judgment for every idle word they speak.
  Romans 14:12 — thematic parallel: Both state personal accountability before God or at judgment. Matthew specifically includes every idle word spoken; Romans speaks more generally of each person's giving an account of himself to God and does not repeat the idle-word criterion.
    Romans 14:12 So then, each of us will give an account of himself to God.

Matthew 12:37
For by your words you will be justified, and by your words you will be condemned.”
  Luke 19:22 — thematic parallel: Both make a person's own speech a basis of judgment. Matthew gives a general saying about justification or condemnation by words; Luke's parable has the master judge the servant from his own mouth. The parable does not repeat Matthew's whole general rule.
    Luke 19:22 He said to him, ‘From your own mouth I will judge you, evil slave. You knew that I am a severe man, taking up what I did not lay down and reaping what I did not sow?

Matthew 12:38
Then some of the scribes and Pharisees answered Him, saying, “Teacher, we wish to see a sign from Thee.”
  Mark 8:11 — narrative parallel: Both report religious opponents asking Jesus for a sign. Matthew includes scribes and Pharisees and addresses Him as Teacher; Mark names Pharisees, specifies a sign from heaven and describes their testing purpose.
    Mark 8:11 The Pharisees came out and began disputing with Him, seeking from Him a sign from heaven, to test Him.

Matthew 12:39
He answered them, “An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, but no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah the prophet.
  Luke 11:29 — teaching parallel: Both call the sign-seeking generation evil and allow no sign except Jonah's. Matthew adds adulterous and identifies Jonah as prophet.
    Luke 11:29 As the crowds were gathering in greater numbers, He began to say, “This generation is an evil generation. It seeks a sign, and no sign will be given to it except the sign of Jonah.

Matthew 12:40
For just as Jonah was in the sea creature’s belly three days and three nights, so the Son of Man will be in the heart of the earth three days and three nights.
  Jonah 2:1 — allusion: Jonah explicitly spends three days and three nights in the sea creature's belly, the duration and setting Jesus names as the comparison for the Son of Man in the heart of the earth. This Saints edition numbers the Jonah verse 2:1; the comparison does not independently fix a chronology of Jesus' burial.
    Jonah 2:1 The Lord commanded a great sea creature to swallow Jonah, and Jonah was in the sea creature’s belly three days and three nights.

Matthew 12:41
Men of Nineveh will rise at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because they repented at Jonah’s proclamation; and behold, something greater than Jonah is here.
  Luke 11:32 — teaching parallel: Both say the men of Nineveh will rise in judgment and condemn this generation because they repented at Jonah's preaching, while something greater than Jonah is present.
    Luke 11:32 The men of Nineveh will rise at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because they repented at the proclamation of Jonah, and behold, something greater than Jonah is here.

Matthew 12:42
The queen of the south will be raised at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear Solomon’s wisdom; and behold, something greater than Solomon is here.
  Luke 11:31 — teaching parallel: Both invoke the queen of the south's journey to hear Solomon's wisdom as grounds for condemnation of this generation, with something greater than Solomon now present.
    Luke 11:31 The queen of the south will be raised at the judgment with the men of this generation and will condemn them, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon, and behold, something greater than Solomon is here.

Matthew 12:43
When the unclean spirit leaves a person, it passes through waterless places seeking rest, and finds none.
  Luke 11:24 — teaching parallel: Both describe a departed unclean spirit passing through waterless places in search of rest and finding none. Luke includes its decision to return in the same verse.
    Luke 11:24 When the unclean spirit comes out of a person, it passes through waterless places seeking rest. Finding none, it then says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’

Matthew 12:44
Then it says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’ When it comes, it finds it vacant, swept, and put in order.
  Luke 11:24–11:25 — teaching parallel: Both have the spirit decide to return to its former house and find it swept and arranged. Matthew additionally calls the house vacant; the Luke passage does not explicitly add that adjective.
    Luke 11:24 When the unclean spirit comes out of a person, it passes through waterless places seeking rest. Finding none, it then says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’
    Luke 11:25 And when it comes, it finds it swept and put in order.

Matthew 12:45
Then it goes and takes with itself seven other spirits more evil than itself; they enter and dwell there, and that person’s last condition becomes worse than the first. So it will also be with this evil generation.”
  Luke 11:26 — teaching parallel: Both narrate the returning spirit bringing seven worse spirits, their inhabiting the person and a final condition worse than the first. Matthew additionally applies the picture to the evil generation.
    Luke 11:26 Then it goes and takes seven other spirits more evil than itself, and they enter and dwell there. And the last state of that person becomes worse than the first.”

Matthew 12:46
While He was still speaking to the crowds, behold, His mother and brothers stood outside, seeking to speak with Him.
  Mark 3:31 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' mother and brothers outside seeking access to Him while He is teaching. Mark says they send and call Him; Matthew says they seek to speak with Him.
    Mark 3:31 His mother and His brothers came. Standing outside, they sent to Him and called Him.

Matthew 12:47
Someone said to Him, “Behold, Thy mother and Thy brothers stand outside, seeking to speak with Thee.”
  Mark 3:32 — narrative parallel: Both report someone informing Jesus that His family is outside seeking Him. Matthew names mother and brothers; Mark also names His sisters. The family terms remain distinct rather than being harmonized.
    Mark 3:32 A crowd was sitting around Him. They said to Him, “Look! Thy mother and Thy brothers and Thy sisters are outside looking for Thee.”

Matthew 12:48
He answered the one speaking to Him, “Who is My mother, and who are My brothers?”
  Mark 3:33 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' answering question about who His mother and brothers are.
    Mark 3:33 He answered them, “Who is My mother, or who are My brothers?”

Matthew 12:49
Stretching out His hand toward His disciples, He said, “Behold, My mother and My brothers!
  Mark 3:34 — narrative parallel: Both identify those present with Jesus as His mother and brothers. Matthew describes His stretching out a hand toward His disciples; Mark describes His looking around at those seated around Him.
    Mark 3:34 He looked around at those sitting in a circle about Him and said, “Look—My mother and My brothers!

Matthew 12:50
For whoever does the will of My Father in the heavens is My brother and sister and mother.”
  Mark 3:35 — teaching parallel: Both define Jesus' brother, sister and mother through doing God's will. Matthew explicitly says the will of His Father in the heavens; Mark says God's will.
    Mark 3:35 For whoever does the will of God is My brother and sister and mother.”

Matthew 13:1
On that day Jesus went out of the house and sat beside the sea.
  Mark 4:1 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place Jesus beside the sea before the parable teaching; Mark also describes the crowd and boat in this verse.
    Mark 4:1 Again He began teaching beside the sea. A very great crowd gathered around Him, so that He got into a boat on the sea and sat down, while the whole crowd was on the land beside the sea.

Matthew 13:2
Great crowds gathered to Him, so that He entered a boat and sat down, while all the crowd stood on the shore.
  Mark 4:1 — narrative parallel: Both describe the large crowd, Jesus sitting in a boat, and the crowd remaining on the land beside the sea.
    Mark 4:1 Again He began teaching beside the sea. A very great crowd gathered around Him, so that He got into a boat on the sea and sat down, while the whole crowd was on the land beside the sea.

Matthew 13:3
He spoke many things to them in parables, saying, “Behold, the sower went out to sow.
  Mark 4:2–4:3 — narrative parallel: Both introduce teaching in parables and begin with the sower going out to sow.
    Mark 4:2 He taught them many things in parables, and in His teaching He said to them:
    Mark 4:3 “Listen! Look, the sower went out to sow.

Matthew 13:4
As he sowed, some seeds fell beside the road, and the birds came and devoured them.
  Mark 4:4 — narrative parallel: Both versions describe seed beside the road being eaten by birds.
    Mark 4:4 As he sowed, some fell beside the road, and the birds came and ate it up.

Matthew 13:5
Others fell on rocky places, where they did not have much soil, and immediately sprang up because they had no depth of soil.
  Mark 4:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe seed springing up quickly on rocky ground because the soil is shallow.
    Mark 4:5 Other seed fell on rocky ground, where it had little soil, and immediately sprang up because the soil had no depth.

Matthew 13:6
But when the sun rose, they were scorched, and because they had no root they withered.
  Mark 4:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe the sun scorching the plants and their withering because they have no root.
    Mark 4:6 When the sun rose, it was scorched, and because it had no root, it withered.

Matthew 13:7
Others fell among thorns; the thorns grew up and choked them.
  Mark 4:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe growing thorns choking the seed; Mark also explicitly states that it produced no fruit.
    Mark 4:7 Other seed fell among the thorns. The thorns grew up and choked it, and it gave no fruit.

Matthew 13:8
Others fell on the good soil and yielded fruit: some a hundredfold, some sixtyfold, some thirtyfold.
  Mark 4:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe good soil yielding thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold, although the order of the yields is reversed.
    Mark 4:8 Other seeds fell into the good soil and gave fruit, growing up and increasing; they yielded thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold.”

Matthew 13:9
Let the one who has ears hear.”
  Mark 4:9 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the sower parable with the summons for the person with ears to hear.
    Mark 4:9 He said, “Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”

Matthew 13:10
The disciples came and said to Him, “Why dost Thou speak to them in parables?”
  Mark 4:10 — narrative parallel: Both place a question about the parables among Jesus' followers. Matthew gives the question why He speaks in parables; Mark describes their asking about the parables when He was alone.
    Mark 4:10 When He was alone, those around Him, together with the Twelve, asked Him about the parables.

Matthew 13:11
He answered them, “To you it has been given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of the heavens, but to them it has not been given.
  Mark 4:11 — narrative parallel: Both contrast the disciples' received knowledge of the kingdom's mystery with the use of parables for those outside.
    Mark 4:11 He said to them, “To you the mystery of the kingdom of God has been given. But for those outside, everything comes in parables,

Matthew 13:12
For whoever has, to him more will be given, and he will have abundance; but whoever does not have, even what he has will be taken from him.
  Mark 4:25 — teaching parallel: Both state that whoever has will receive more, while whoever does not have will lose even what he has; Matthew adds abundance.
    Mark 4:25 For whoever has will be given more; and whoever does not have, even what he has will be taken from him.”

Matthew 13:13
Therefore I speak to them in parables, because seeing they do not see, and hearing they do not hear or understand.
  Mark 4:12 — narrative parallel: Both connect parable teaching with seeing without seeing and hearing without understanding. Matthew gives this as the reason He speaks in parables, whereas Mark phrases it as so that; the causal wording is not harmonized.
    Mark 4:12 so that, looking, they may look and not see, and hearing, they may hear and not understand, lest they turn back and be forgiven.”

Matthew 13:14
In them is fulfilled Isaiah’s prophecy, which says: ‘You will hear and hear but certainly not understand; you will look and look but certainly not see.
  Isaiah 6:9 — quotation: Matthew explicitly calls this Isaiah's prophecy and quotes the warning about repeated hearing without understanding and repeated seeing without perception.
    Isaiah 6:9 And He said, Go, and say to this people: Hearing, you will hear and certainly not understand; and seeing, you will see and certainly not perceive.

Matthew 13:15
For the heart of this people has grown fat, and with their ears they heard with difficulty, and they closed their eyes, lest they see with their eyes, hear with their ears, understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.’
  Isaiah 6:10 — quotation: Both describe the people's dull or fattened heart, difficult hearing, closed eyes, and the possibility of turning and being healed. The vocabulary differs slightly in this supplied English edition.
    Isaiah 6:10 For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.

Matthew 13:16
But blessed are your eyes, because they see, and your ears, because they hear.
  Luke 10:23 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce the disciples' seeing eyes blessed. Matthew also mentions their hearing ears; Luke's cited verse mentions the eyes.
    Luke 10:23 Turning privately to the disciples, He said, “Blessed are the eyes that see what you see.

Matthew 13:17
For truly I tell you, many prophets and righteous people desired to see what you see and did not see it, and to hear what you hear and did not hear it.
  Luke 10:24 — teaching parallel: Both describe earlier people longing to see and hear what the disciples now experience. Matthew names prophets and righteous people, whereas Luke names prophets and kings.
    Luke 10:24 For I tell you that many prophets and kings wished to see what you see, and did not see it, and to hear what you hear, and did not hear it.”

Matthew 13:18
You, then, hear the parable of the sower.
  Mark 4:13–4:14 — narrative parallel: Matthew introduces the sower explanation by calling the disciples to hear it; Mark introduces the explanation with a question about their understanding and then identifies the sowing as sowing the word.
    Mark 4:13 He said to them, “Do you not understand this parable? Then how will you understand all the parables?
    Mark 4:14 The sower sows the word.

Matthew 13:19
When anyone hears the word of the kingdom and does not understand, the evil one comes and snatches what was sown in his heart. This is the one sown beside the road.
  Mark 4:15 — narrative parallel: Both interpret the roadside seed as hearers from whom the evil one or Satan takes away the sown word; Matthew additionally stresses lack of understanding.
    Mark 4:15 These are the ones beside the road where the word is sown: whenever they hear, Satan immediately comes and takes away the word sown into them.

Matthew 13:20
The one sown on rocky places is the one who hears the word and immediately receives it with joy,
  Mark 4:16 — narrative parallel: Both identify the rocky-ground hearers as those who immediately receive the word with joy.
    Mark 4:16 Likewise, these are the ones sown on the rocky places: whenever they hear the word, they immediately receive it with joy,

Matthew 13:21
yet has no root in himself, but lasts only a short time. When affliction or persecution arises because of the word, he immediately stumbles.
  Mark 4:17 — narrative parallel: Both describe rootless hearers lasting only briefly and stumbling when distress or persecution arises because of the word.
    Mark 4:17 but they have no root in themselves and last only for a time. Then, when distress or persecution comes because of the word, they immediately stumble.

Matthew 13:22
The one sown among the thorns is the one who hears the word, but the anxiety of this age and the deceit of riches choke the word, and it becomes unfruitful.
  Mark 4:18–4:19 — narrative parallel: Both interpret the thorns as cares and the deceitfulness of riches choking the heard word until it is unfruitful. Mark additionally mentions desires for other things.
    Mark 4:18 Others are the ones sown among the thorns. These are the ones who have heard the word,
    Mark 4:19 but the cares of the age, the deceitfulness of riches, and desires for other things enter and choke the word, and it becomes unfruitful.

Matthew 13:23
The one sown upon the good soil is the one who hears the word and understands; he indeed bears fruit and produces, some a hundredfold, some sixtyfold, some thirtyfold.”
