  Mark 4:20 — narrative parallel: Both identify the good-soil hearers by their fruitful reception of the word and the thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and hundredfold yields. Matthew says understanding and Mark says accepting; the yield order also differs.
    Mark 4:20 Those sown on the good soil are the ones who hear the word, accept it, and bear fruit: thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold.”

Matthew 13:24
He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens was likened to a man who sowed good seed in his field.
  Matthew 13:37 — parable and explanation: The later explanation identifies the sower of the good seed in this same chapter's parable as the Son of Man. This is an explicit link between the parable and its interpretation.
    Matthew 13:37 He answered, “The One sowing the good seed is the Son of Man.

Matthew 13:25
While people slept, his enemy came and sowed darnel among the wheat and went away.
  Matthew 13:38–13:39 — parable and explanation: The same chapter's explanation identifies the darnel as sons of the evil one and the enemy who sowed it as the devil; it explains the hostile sowing described here.
    Matthew 13:38 The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;
    Matthew 13:39 the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.

Matthew 13:26
When the crop sprouted and bore fruit, then the darnel appeared also.
  Matthew 13:38 — parable and explanation: The later explanation distinguishes the good seed as sons of the kingdom and the darnel as sons of the evil one, explaining the two growths that become visible in this parable.
    Matthew 13:38 The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;

Matthew 13:27
The householder’s slaves came and said to him, ‘Master, did you not sow good seed in your field? From where, then, does it have darnel?’
  Matthew 13:37–13:39 — parable and explanation: The householder's slaves ask how darnel arose despite good sowing; the later explanation identifies the good sower, good seed, darnel, and hostile devil who sowed it. It supplies the parable's interpretive identities.
    Matthew 13:37 He answered, “The One sowing the good seed is the Son of Man.
    Matthew 13:38 The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;
    Matthew 13:39 the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.

Matthew 13:28
He said to them, ‘An enemy did this.’ The slaves said to him, ‘Do you want us, then, to go and gather it?’
  Matthew 13:39 — parable and explanation: The explanation identifies the enemy blamed here as the devil and relates the harvest and reapers to the consummation and angels; the slaves' proposal to gather the darnel is not itself repeated there.
    Matthew 13:39 the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.

Matthew 13:29
He said, ‘No, lest while gathering the darnel you uproot the wheat with it.
  Matthew 13:38–13:40 — internal parable interpretation: The later explanation identifies the good seed as the kingdom's sons, the darnel as the evil one's sons, and the harvest as the age's completion with angelic reapers. These are the parable's own referents for the wheat-and-darnel warning, without adding a botanical procedure or an unstated policy for human punishment.
    Matthew 13:38 The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;
    Matthew 13:39 the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.
    Matthew 13:40 Just as the darnel is gathered and burned with fire, so it will be at the consummation of the age.

Matthew 13:30
Let both grow together until the harvest. At harvest time I will tell the reapers: Gather the darnel first and bind it into bundles to burn it, but gather the wheat into my storehouse.’”
  Matthew 13:39–13:42 — parable and explanation: The later explanation interprets the harvest as the age's consummation, the reapers as angels, and the burning of gathered darnel as judgment. This is the same parable's explanation rather than an independent event.
    Matthew 13:39 the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.
    Matthew 13:40 Just as the darnel is gathered and burned with fire, so it will be at the consummation of the age.
    Matthew 13:41 The Son of Man will send His angels, and they will gather from His kingdom all causes of stumbling and those who practice lawlessness.
    Matthew 13:42 They will cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.

Matthew 13:31
He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens is like a mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field.
  Mark 4:30–4:31 — narrative parallel: Both compare the kingdom with a mustard seed being sown; Matthew says the kingdom of the heavens and Mark the kingdom of God.
    Mark 4:30 He said, “How shall we compare the kingdom of God, or in what parable shall we set it out?
    Mark 4:31 It is like a mustard seed, which, when sown on the ground, is smaller than all the seeds on the earth.

Matthew 13:32
It is smaller than all the seeds, but when it grows it is greater than the garden plants and becomes a tree, so that the birds of the sky come and rest in its branches.”
  Mark 4:31–4:32 — narrative parallel: Both describe the small mustard seed growing beyond the garden plants and providing a place for birds; Matthew describes a tree and resting in branches, whereas Mark describes great branches and shelter under their shade.
    Mark 4:31 It is like a mustard seed, which, when sown on the ground, is smaller than all the seeds on the earth.
    Mark 4:32 Yet when sown, it grows up and becomes larger than all the garden plants, and makes great branches, so that the birds of the sky can shelter under its shade.”

Matthew 13:33
He spoke another parable to them: “The kingdom of the heavens is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three sata of flour until the whole was leavened.”
  Luke 13:20–13:21 — teaching parallel: Both compare the kingdom to a woman hiding leaven in three sata of flour until the whole is leavened.
    Luke 13:20 And again He said, “To what shall I compare the kingdom of God?
    Luke 13:21 It is like leaven that a woman took and hid in three sata of flour, until the whole was leavened.”

Matthew 13:34
Jesus spoke all these things to the crowds in parables, and He spoke nothing to them without a parable,
  Mark 4:33–4:34 — narrative parallel: Both summarize Jesus' public use of parables and state that He did not speak to them without a parable. Mark additionally describes private explanation to the disciples.
    Mark 4:33 With many such parables He spoke the word to them, as they were able to hear.
    Mark 4:34 He did not speak to them without a parable, but privately He explained everything to His own disciples.

Matthew 13:35
so that what was spoken through the prophet might be fulfilled: “I will open My mouth in parables; I will utter things hidden from the foundation of the world.”
  Psalms 77:2 — quotation: Matthew's fulfillment citation shares the psalm's opening of the mouth in parables and utterance of ancient hidden material. The psalm says riddles from the beginning, while Matthew says things hidden from the foundation of the world; this edition numbers the psalm 77.
    Psalms 77:2 I will open my mouth in parables; I will utter riddles from the beginning,

Matthew 13:36
Then He left the crowds and entered the house. His disciples came to Him, saying, “Explain to us the parable of the darnel in the field.”
  Mark 4:34 — narrative parallel: Matthew describes disciples privately requesting an explanation after Jesus leaves the crowds; Mark summarizes His private explanation of parables to His own disciples. Mark does not name the darnel parable or the house here.
    Mark 4:34 He did not speak to them without a parable, but privately He explained everything to His own disciples.

Matthew 13:37
He answered, “The One sowing the good seed is the Son of Man.
  Matthew 13:24 — parable and explanation: Jesus identifies the Son of Man as the good sower from the parable introduced earlier in this chapter; the source gives the interpretive identity for that earlier image.
    Matthew 13:24 He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens was likened to a man who sowed good seed in his field.

Matthew 13:38
The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;
  Matthew 13:24–13:26 — parable and explanation: This explanation identifies the field, good seed, and darnel from the preceding parable as the world, sons of the kingdom, and sons of the evil one.
    Matthew 13:24 He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens was likened to a man who sowed good seed in his field.
    Matthew 13:25 While people slept, his enemy came and sowed darnel among the wheat and went away.
    Matthew 13:26 When the crop sprouted and bore fruit, then the darnel appeared also.

Matthew 13:39
the enemy who sowed them is the devil; the harvest is the consummation of the age; and the reapers are angels.
  Matthew 13:28–13:30 — parable and explanation: This explanation identifies the enemy, harvest, and reapers in the earlier parable as the devil, the consummation of the age, and angels. The earlier command to wait for harvest supplies its narrative setting.
    Matthew 13:28 He said to them, ‘An enemy did this.’ The slaves said to him, ‘Do you want us, then, to go and gather it?’
    Matthew 13:29 He said, ‘No, lest while gathering the darnel you uproot the wheat with it.
    Matthew 13:30 Let both grow together until the harvest. At harvest time I will tell the reapers: Gather the darnel first and bind it into bundles to burn it, but gather the wheat into my storehouse.’”

Matthew 13:40
Just as the darnel is gathered and burned with fire, so it will be at the consummation of the age.
  Matthew 13:30 — parable and explanation: The source explicitly interprets the earlier gathering and burning of the darnel as an image of the consummation of the age.
    Matthew 13:30 Let both grow together until the harvest. At harvest time I will tell the reapers: Gather the darnel first and bind it into bundles to burn it, but gather the wheat into my storehouse.’”

Matthew 13:41
The Son of Man will send His angels, and they will gather from His kingdom all causes of stumbling and those who practice lawlessness.
  Matthew 13:49 — thematic parallel: Both explanations of judgment parables describe angels separating or gathering the wicked from among those belonging to the kingdom; the darnel and dragnet remain different images.
    Matthew 13:49 So it will be at the consummation of the age: the angels will go out and separate the evil from the midst of the righteous

Matthew 13:42
They will cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.
  Matthew 13:50 — thematic parallel: The two parable explanations repeat the furnace of fire and the weeping and grinding of teeth as the fate of the wicked.
    Matthew 13:50 and cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.

Matthew 13:43
Then the righteous will shine like the sun in the kingdom of their Father. Let the one who has ears hear.
  Daniel 12:3 — allusion: Matthew's righteous shining like the sun recalls Daniel's understanding people appearing like heaven's lights and those strengthening his words like stars. This is an illuminating-light comparison, with Daniel's supplied Greek-based wording retained rather than replaced by another edition.
    Daniel 12:3 Those who understand will appear like heaven’s lights, and those who strengthen my words like the stars of heaven for the age of the age.

Matthew 13:44
The kingdom of the heavens is like treasure hidden in a field, which a man found and hid. In his joy he goes and sells everything he has and buys that field.
  Matthew 19:21 — thematic parallel: The parable's man sells his possessions for the treasure-bearing field; Jesus elsewhere calls the rich man to sell his belongings, give to the poor, receive heavenly treasure, and follow Him. The connection is costly pursuit of kingdom treasure, without equating the two transactions.
    Matthew 19:21 Jesus said to him, “If you wish to be perfect, go, sell your belongings, and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in the heavens. Then come, follow Me.”

Matthew 13:45
Again, the kingdom of the heavens is like a merchant seeking fine pearls.
  Matthew 13:44 — thematic parallel: These neighboring kingdom parables use valued material objects, fine pearls and hidden treasure, to portray the kingdom's worth. The merchant's search differs from the other man's discovery of a field's treasure.
    Matthew 13:44 The kingdom of the heavens is like treasure hidden in a field, which a man found and hid. In his joy he goes and sells everything he has and buys that field.

Matthew 13:46
Finding one pearl of great value, he went and sold everything he had and bought it.
  Matthew 13:44 — thematic parallel: Both kingdom parables describe selling everything to obtain the discovered object of great value, a pearl in this verse and a treasure-bearing field in the other.
    Matthew 13:44 The kingdom of the heavens is like treasure hidden in a field, which a man found and hid. In his joy he goes and sells everything he has and buys that field.

Matthew 13:47
Again, the kingdom of the heavens is like a dragnet cast into the sea and gathering fish of every kind.
  Matthew 13:38 — thematic parallel: The dragnet's gathering of every kind of fish can be compared with the field parable's mixed world of sons of the kingdom and sons of the evil one. These are distinct parable images whose subsequent explanations describe separation at the consummation.
    Matthew 13:38 The field is the world; the good seed are the sons of the kingdom; the darnel are the sons of the evil one;

Matthew 13:48
When it was full, they drew it onto the shore, sat down, and gathered the good into containers, but threw the rotten away.
  Matthew 13:30 — thematic parallel: Both parables distinguish a retained good yield from a rejected bad yield: good fish are gathered while rotten fish are discarded, and wheat is stored while darnel is burned. The disposal imagery differs.
    Matthew 13:30 Let both grow together until the harvest. At harvest time I will tell the reapers: Gather the darnel first and bind it into bundles to burn it, but gather the wheat into my storehouse.’”

Matthew 13:49
So it will be at the consummation of the age: the angels will go out and separate the evil from the midst of the righteous
  Matthew 13:41 — thematic parallel: Both explanations describe angels carrying out the separation or gathering of evil at the consummation; the source belongs to the dragnet explanation and the target to the darnel explanation.
    Matthew 13:41 The Son of Man will send His angels, and they will gather from His kingdom all causes of stumbling and those who practice lawlessness.

Matthew 13:50
and cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.
  Matthew 13:42 — thematic parallel: The dragnet explanation repeats the darnel explanation's furnace of fire, weeping, and grinding of teeth.
    Matthew 13:42 They will cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.

Matthew 13:51
Have you understood all these things?” They said to Him, “Yes.”
  Matthew 13:11 — thematic parallel: The disciples' affirmation that they understand these teachings can be read alongside Jesus' earlier statement that knowledge of the kingdom's mysteries has been given to them. The link concerns this chapter's contrast in understanding, not a second report of this question.
    Matthew 13:11 He answered them, “To you it has been given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of the heavens, but to them it has not been given.

Matthew 13:52
He said to them, “Therefore every scribe made a disciple for the kingdom of the heavens is like a householder who brings out of his treasure things new and old.”
  Song of Songs 7:14 — bounded image comparison: stored things new and old: Matthew compares a kingdom-trained scribe to a householder bringing new and old things from his treasure; the Song describes new together with old fruits kept for the kinsman. Both combine stored goods with the explicit new-and-old contrast. The Song concerns fruits and the speaker's kinsman, not scribal teaching, and is not made to identify or predict Matthew's scribe.
    Song of Songs 7:14 The mandrakes have given fragrance, and at our doors are all tree fruits, new together with old, which I have kept for you, my kinsman.

Matthew 13:53
When Jesus finished these parables, He departed from there.
  Matthew 19:1 — same-book structural parallel: completed teaching and departure: Both sentences say that Jesus finished a block of teaching and then departed. Matthew 13 closes the parables; Matthew 19 closes the preceding words and names the move from Galilee toward Judea beyond the Jordan. The shared discourse-closing and departure pattern is an internal literary connection, not independent testimony or an identical journey.
    Matthew 19:1 When Jesus finished these words, He departed from Galilee and came into the territories of Judea beyond the Jordan.

Matthew 13:54
Coming to His hometown, He taught them in their synagogue, so that they were astonished and said, “Where did this Man get this wisdom and these mighty works?
  Mark 6:2 — narrative parallel: Both hometown accounts describe synagogue teaching and astonished questions about Jesus' wisdom and mighty works.
    Mark 6:2 When the Sabbath came, He began teaching in the synagogue. The many who heard Him were astonished, saying, “Where did this Man get these things? What is this wisdom given to Him? And such mighty works taking place through His hands!

Matthew 13:55
Is He not the craftsman’s son? Is His mother not called Mary, and His brothers James, Joseph, Simon, and Judas?
  Mark 6:3 — narrative parallel: Both hometown questions name Mary and Jesus' brothers. Matthew calls Him the craftsman's son; Mark calls Him the carpenter. Matthew's Joseph and Mark's Joses are retained as each edition's wording rather than silently harmonized.
    Mark 6:3 Is this not the carpenter, the Son of Mary and brother of James, Joses, Judas, and Simon? Are not His sisters here with us?” They took offense at Him.

Matthew 13:56
And are not all His sisters with us? Where, then, did this Man get all these things?”
  Mark 6:3 — narrative parallel: Both hometown objections refer to Jesus' sisters living among the townspeople; Matthew also repeats their question about the source of His abilities.
    Mark 6:3 Is this not the carpenter, the Son of Mary and brother of James, Joses, Judas, and Simon? Are not His sisters here with us?” They took offense at Him.

Matthew 13:57
They stumbled because of Him. But Jesus said to them, “A prophet is not without honor except in his hometown and his own house.”
  Mark 6:3–6:4 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe offense at Jesus and His saying that a prophet lacks honor in his hometown and household; Mark additionally mentions relatives.
    Mark 6:3 Is this not the carpenter, the Son of Mary and brother of James, Joses, Judas, and Simon? Are not His sisters here with us?” They took offense at Him.
    Mark 6:4 Jesus said to them, “A prophet is not without honor except in his hometown, among his relatives, and in his own house.”

Matthew 13:58
He did not do many mighty works there because of their unbelief.
  Mark 6:5–6:6 — narrative parallel: Both connect the restricted mighty works in Jesus' hometown with unbelief. Matthew says He did not do many; Mark says He could do no mighty work except healing a few sick people and then marvels at their unbelief.
    Mark 6:5 He could do no mighty work there, except that He laid His hands on a few sick people and healed them.
    Mark 6:6 He marveled at their unbelief. Then He went around the surrounding villages, teaching.

Matthew 14:1
At that time Herod the tetrarch heard the report about Jesus
  Mark 6:14 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Herod hearing the report about Jesus as His name becomes known; Matthew calls him tetrarch and Mark king.
    Mark 6:14 King Herod heard of it, for His name had become known. People were saying, “John the baptizer has been raised from the dead, and that is why mighty powers are at work in Him.”

Matthew 14:2
and said to his attendants, “This is John the Baptist. He has been raised from the dead, and therefore the powers are at work in him.”
  Mark 6:14–6:16 — narrative parallel: Both associate reports of Jesus' powers with the claim that John has risen. Matthew puts the claim about resurrection and powers directly in Herod's speech; Mark first reports it as what people said and later records Herod identifying the risen John whom he beheaded.
    Mark 6:14 King Herod heard of it, for His name had become known. People were saying, “John the baptizer has been raised from the dead, and that is why mighty powers are at work in Him.”
    Mark 6:15 Others said, “He is Elijah.” Still others said, “He is a prophet, like one of the prophets.”
    Mark 6:16 But when Herod heard, he said, “John, whom I beheaded—he has been raised!”

Matthew 14:3
For Herod had seized John, bound him, and put him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip.
  Mark 6:17 — narrative parallel: Both explain John's arrest, binding, and imprisonment through Herod's relationship with Herodias, identified as his brother Philip's wife.
    Mark 6:17 For Herod himself had sent men to seize John and bind him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip, because he had married her.

Matthew 14:4
For John kept saying to him, “It is not lawful for you to have her.”
  Mark 6:18 — narrative parallel: Both record John's repeated objection that Herod's possession of his brother's wife is unlawful.
    Mark 6:18 For John had been saying to Herod, “It is not lawful for you to have your brother’s wife.”

Matthew 14:5
Although he wanted to kill him, he feared the crowd, because they regarded him as a prophet.
  Matthew 21:26 — thematic parallel: Both passages describe fear of the crowd because the people regard John as a prophet. Here the fear restrains Herod; in the later passage it shapes the authorities' response to Jesus about John's baptism.
    Matthew 21:26 But if we say, ‘From human beings,’ we fear the crowd, for all regard John as a prophet.”

Matthew 14:6
When Herod’s birthday celebration came, Herodias’s daughter danced in their midst and pleased Herod.
  Mark 6:21–6:22 — narrative parallel: Both place the pleasing dance at Herod's birthday celebration. Matthew calls the dancer Herodias's daughter, whereas this supplied Mark text calls her his daughter Herodias; that difference is preserved without resolving her parentage.
    Mark 6:21 An opportune day came when Herod gave a dinner on his birthday for his dignitaries, commanders of thousands, and the leading men of Galilee.
    Mark 6:22 His daughter Herodias came in and danced, pleasing Herod and those reclining with him. The king said to the girl, “Ask me for whatever you want, and I will give it to you.”

Matthew 14:7
Therefore he promised with an oath to give her whatever she asked.
  Mark 6:23 — narrative parallel: Both describe Herod swearing to give the girl what she asks; Mark additionally states the promise extends up to half his kingdom.
    Mark 6:23 He swore to her repeatedly, “Whatever you ask of me, I will give you, up to half my kingdom.”

Matthew 14:8
Prompted by her mother, she said, “Give me here on a platter the head of John the Baptist.”
  Mark 6:24–6:25 — narrative parallel: Both connect the mother's instruction with the girl's request for John the Baptist's head on a platter; Mark narrates the consultation and request separately.
    Mark 6:24 She went out and said to her mother, “What should I ask for?” She said, “The head of John the baptizer.”
    Mark 6:25 Immediately she hurried in to the king and made her request, saying, “I want you to give me at once, on a platter, the head of John the Baptist.”

Matthew 14:9
Although the king was grieved, because of his oaths and those reclining with him, he ordered it to be given.
  Mark 6:26 — narrative parallel: Both describe the king's grief and his unwillingness to refuse because of his oaths and the guests reclining with him.
    Mark 6:26 The king became deeply grieved, but because of his oaths and those reclining at table, he did not want to refuse her.

Matthew 14:10
He sent and had John beheaded in the prison.
  Mark 6:27 — narrative parallel: Both report Herod ordering John's beheading in prison; Mark identifies the messenger as an executioner.
    Mark 6:27 Immediately the king sent an executioner and ordered him to bring John’s head. He went and beheaded him in prison,

Matthew 14:11
His head was brought on a platter and given to the girl, and she brought it to her mother.
  Mark 6:28 — narrative parallel: Both narrate John's head being brought on a platter, given to the girl, and passed by her to her mother.
    Mark 6:28 brought his head on a platter, and gave it to the girl; the girl gave it to her mother.

Matthew 14:12
His disciples came, took the corpse, and buried him; then they went and reported to Jesus.
  Mark 6:29 — narrative parallel: Both describe John's disciples taking his corpse for burial. Matthew additionally reports their subsequent visit to tell Jesus.
    Mark 6:29 When his disciples heard, they came and took his corpse and laid it in a tomb.

Matthew 14:13
When Jesus heard this, He withdrew from there by boat to a deserted place privately. Hearing of it, the crowds followed Him on foot from the cities.
  Mark 6:32–6:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe the private boat withdrawal to a deserted place and crowds traveling there on foot from the cities. Matthew links the withdrawal to news of John's death, whereas Mark's preceding context concerns the apostles' return and rest.
    Mark 6:32 They went away privately in the boat to a deserted place.
    Mark 6:33 People saw them leaving, and many recognized them. They ran there together on foot from all the cities and arrived ahead of them.

Matthew 14:14
Coming out, He saw a great crowd, was moved with compassion for them, and healed their sick.
  Mark 6:34 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus emerging, seeing the great crowd, and being moved with compassion. Matthew follows with healing; Mark explains the compassion through sheep without a shepherd and follows with teaching.
    Mark 6:34 As He stepped out, He saw a large crowd and was moved with compassion for them, because they were like sheep without a shepherd. He began teaching them many things.

Matthew 14:15
When evening came, the disciples approached Him, saying, “The place is deserted, and the hour has already passed. Dismiss the crowds so that they may go to the villages and buy food for themselves.”
  Mark 6:35–6:36 — narrative parallel: Both disciples point to the deserted location and late hour and ask Jesus to dismiss the crowds to buy food in nearby settlements.
    Mark 6:35 When the hour was already late, His disciples came to Him and said, “The place is deserted, and the hour is already late.
    Mark 6:36 Dismiss them, so that they can go into the surrounding fields and villages and buy themselves something to eat.”

Matthew 14:16
But Jesus said to them, “They have no need to go away. You give them something to eat.”
  Mark 6:37 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' instruction that the disciples themselves provide food; Mark also records their question about the cost of buying bread.
    Mark 6:37 He answered them, “You give them something to eat.” They said to Him, “Should we go and buy two hundred denarii worth of bread and give them something to eat?”

Matthew 14:17
They said to Him, “We have here only five loaves and two fish.”
  Mark 6:38 — narrative parallel: Both identify the available food as five loaves and two fish, although Mark places the answer after Jesus tells the disciples to check the supply.
    Mark 6:38 He said to them, “How many loaves do you have? Go and see.” When they found out, they said, “Five, and two fish.”

Matthew 14:18
He said, “Bring them here to Me.”
  John 21:10 — narrative parallel: Jesus requests that food be brought before the ensuing meal in both scenes. Matthew refers back to the five loaves and two fish; John requests some newly caught fish. The crowd meal and shore breakfast, amounts and food origins remain distinct, and neither command is independently attested by the other.
    John 21:10 Jesus says to them, “Bring some of the fish that you have just caught.”

Matthew 14:19
He ordered the crowds to recline on the grass. Taking the five loaves and the two fish, He looked up to heaven, blessed, broke the loaves, and gave them to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds.
  Mark 6:39–6:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe reclining on grass and Jesus taking the five loaves and two fish, looking heavenward, blessing, breaking, and distributing through the disciples. Mark additionally describes the seated groups.
    Mark 6:39 He ordered them to have everyone recline in dining groups on the green grass.
    Mark 6:40 They reclined in rows, by hundreds and by fifties.
    Mark 6:41 He took the five loaves and the two fish, looked up to heaven, pronounced a blessing, and broke the loaves. He kept giving them to His disciples to set before the people, and He divided the two fish among them all.

Matthew 14:20
They all ate and were filled, and they took up the surplus fragments: twelve baskets full.
  Mark 6:42–6:43 — narrative parallel: Both state that everyone ate and was satisfied and that twelve baskets of fragments were collected.
    Mark 6:42 They all ate and were satisfied.
    Mark 6:43 They took up broken pieces, twelve baskets full, and some of the fish.

Matthew 14:21
Those eating were about five thousand men, besides women and children.
  Mark 6:44 — narrative parallel: Both count the eaters as about five thousand men; Matthew explicitly adds that women and children are outside that count.
    Mark 6:44 Those who had eaten the loaves were five thousand men.

Matthew 14:22
Immediately He compelled the disciples to enter a boat and go ahead of Him to the other side, while He dismissed the crowds.
  Mark 6:45 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus compelling the disciples to go ahead by boat while He dismisses the crowd; Mark additionally names Bethsaida as their direction.
    Mark 6:45 Immediately He compelled His disciples to board the boat and go ahead to the other side, toward Bethsaida, while He Himself dismissed the crowd.

Matthew 14:23
After dismissing the crowds, He went up the mountain privately to pray. When evening came, He was there alone.
  Mark 6:46–6:47 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus withdrawing to the mountain to pray and being alone when evening comes.
    Mark 6:46 After saying farewell to them, He went away to the mountain to pray.
    Mark 6:47 When evening came, the boat was in the middle of the sea, and He was alone on the land.

Matthew 14:24
The boat was already many stadia from land, battered by the waves, for the wind was against them.
  Mark 6:48 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples' boat being hindered by an opposing wind; Matthew emphasizes waves and distance from land, whereas Mark describes their strained rowing.
    Mark 6:48 Seeing them straining at the rowing, for the wind was against them, He came toward them about the fourth watch of the night, walking on the sea. He intended to pass by them.

Matthew 14:25
In the fourth watch of the night He came toward them, walking upon the sea.
  Mark 6:48 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' approach while walking on the sea around the fourth watch of the night.
    Mark 6:48 Seeing them straining at the rowing, for the wind was against them, He came toward them about the fourth watch of the night, walking on the sea. He intended to pass by them.

Matthew 14:26
When the disciples saw Him walking upon the sea, they were troubled and said, “It is an apparition!” They cried out in fear.
  Mark 6:49–6:50 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples mistaking Jesus' sea-walking appearance for a phantom or apparition and crying out in fear.
    Mark 6:49 When they saw Him walking on the sea, they thought it was a phantom and cried out,
    Mark 6:50 for they all saw Him and were troubled. Immediately He spoke with them and said to them, “Take courage! It is I; do not fear.”

Matthew 14:27
Immediately Jesus spoke to them, saying, “Take courage; it is I. Do not fear.”
  Mark 6:50 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus immediately reassuring them with the commands to take courage and not fear and the words it is I.
    Mark 6:50 for they all saw Him and were troubled. Immediately He spoke with them and said to them, “Take courage! It is I; do not fear.”

Matthew 14:28
Peter answered Him, “Lord, if it is Thou, command me to come to Thee upon the waters.”
  Matthew 14:29 — internal request and response: Peter requests a command to approach upon the water; Jesus' Come answers that request and precedes Peter's narrated walk. The conditional identity wording remains Peter's request, and the ensuing action is the same account's response rather than an independent witness or a claim that Peter has divine identity.
    Matthew 14:29 He said, “Come.” Peter got down from the boat, walked upon the waters, and came toward Jesus.

Matthew 14:29
He said, “Come.” Peter got down from the boat, walked upon the waters, and came toward Jesus.
  Job 9:8 — thematic parallel: Peter walks upon the waters after Jesus commands him to come; Job describes God alone walking upon the sea as on ground. The particular sea-walking image is shared, while divine sovereignty and the dependent disciple remain distinct. Job does not independently narrate Peter or make him the sole divine walker.
    Job 9:8 He alone stretched out heaven and walks on the sea as on ground.

Matthew 14:30
But seeing the strong wind, he became afraid; beginning to sink, he cried out, “Lord, save me!”
  Matthew 8:25 — thematic parallel: Peter's cry for the Lord to save him in danger on the water resembles the disciples' earlier plea for the Lord to save them while they feared perishing at sea. These are different episodes, and the earlier passage does not describe Peter walking.
    Matthew 8:25 And they came to Him and awoke Him, saying, “Lord, save; we are perishing.”

Matthew 14:31
Immediately Jesus stretched out His hand, grasped him, and said to him, “You of little faith, why did you doubt?”
  Matthew 8:26 — thematic parallel: Both sea episodes connect fear with Jesus' rebuke of little faith. Here He grasps Peter and asks why he doubted; in the earlier episode He questions the disciples' fear and stills the storm.
    Matthew 8:26 And He says to them, “Why are you fearful, you of little faith?” Then He arose and rebuked the winds and the sea; and there was a great calm.

Matthew 14:32
When they climbed into the boat, the wind ceased.
  Mark 6:51 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe the wind ceasing when Jesus enters the boat; Matthew also includes Peter in the plural they.
    Mark 6:51 He climbed into the boat with them, and the wind ceased. They were utterly astounded within themselves,

Matthew 14:33
Those in the boat worshiped Him, saying, “Truly Thou art God’s Son.”
  Matthew 16:16 — thematic parallel: The boat occupants' confession that Jesus is God's Son corresponds to Peter's later confession of Him as the Christ and Son of the living God. The occasions and speakers differ.
    Matthew 16:16 Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”

Matthew 14:34
Having crossed over, they came to land at Gennesaret.
  Mark 6:53 — narrative parallel: Both describe the crossing ending with arrival at Gennesaret; Mark additionally records mooring the boat.
    Mark 6:53 When they crossed over to land, they came to Gennesaret and moored there.

Matthew 14:35
The men of that place recognized Him and sent into all the surrounding region. They brought Him all who were ill
  Mark 6:54–6:55 — narrative parallel: Both connect recognition of Jesus with people carrying the sick to Him throughout the surrounding region.
    Mark 6:54 As they stepped out of the boat, people immediately recognized Him
    Mark 6:55 and ran through that whole country. They began carrying the sick around on their mats to wherever they heard He was.

Matthew 14:36
and begged Him that they might only touch the fringe of His garment. As many as touched it were made well.
  Mark 6:56 — narrative parallel: Both describe requests to touch the fringe of Jesus' garment and the healing or saving of those who touched Him.
    Mark 6:56 Wherever He entered villages, cities, or fields, they laid the sick in the marketplaces and begged Him to let them touch even the fringe of His garment. As many as touched Him were saved.

Matthew 15:1
Then Pharisees and scribes from Jerusalem came to Jesus, saying,
  Mark 7:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Pharisees and scribes who came from Jerusalem to Jesus.
    Mark 7:1 The Pharisees and some of the scribes who had come from Jerusalem gathered around Him.

Matthew 15:2
“Why do Thy disciples transgress the tradition of the elders? For they do not wash their hands when they eat bread.”
  Mark 7:5 — narrative parallel: Both record the objection that Jesus' disciples eat without following the elders' handwashing tradition.
    Mark 7:5 The Pharisees and the scribes asked Him, “Why do Thy disciples not walk according to the tradition of the elders, but eat bread with common hands?”

Matthew 15:3
He answered them, “Why do you also transgress God’s commandment because of your tradition?
  Mark 7:8–7:9 — narrative parallel: Both contrast God's commandment with the opponents' adherence to or establishment of human tradition.
    Mark 7:8 You abandon God’s commandment and hold to human tradition.”
    Mark 7:9 He said to them, “You set aside God’s commandment very well indeed, so that you may establish your tradition!

Matthew 15:4
For God said, ‘Honor father and mother,’ and ‘Whoever reviles father or mother must surely die.’
  Mark 7:10 — narrative parallel: Both cite the commandments to honor father and mother and the death sanction for reviling a parent. Matthew introduces them as what God said; Mark as what Moses said.
    Mark 7:10 For Moses said, ‘Honor your father and your mother,’ and, ‘Let whoever speaks evil of father or mother surely die.’

Matthew 15:5
But you say, ‘Whoever tells his father or mother, “Whatever benefit you might receive from me is a gift,”
  Mark 7:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe declaring resources that could benefit a parent to be a gift. Mark additionally supplies and translates the term Corban.
    Mark 7:11 But you say, ‘If a person says to his father or mother, “Whatever benefit you might receive from me is Corban”—that is, a gift—’

Matthew 15:6
will certainly not honor his father.’ You have thus nullified the word of God because of your tradition.
  Mark 7:12–7:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe the resulting failure to help or honor parents as nullifying God's word through tradition.
    Mark 7:12 you no longer allow him to do anything for his father or mother,
    Mark 7:13 making void the word of God by your tradition that you have handed down. You do many similar things.”

Matthew 15:7
Hypocrites! Isaiah prophesied well about you, saying,
  Mark 7:6 — narrative parallel: Both call the opponents hypocrites and introduce Isaiah's prophecy as apt to them; Mark also begins the citation in the same verse.
    Mark 7:6 He said to them, “Isaiah prophesied well about you hypocrites, as it is written: ‘This people honors Me with its lips, but their heart is far from Me.

Matthew 15:8
‘This people honors Me with their lips, but their heart is far from Me.
  Isaiah 29:13 — quotation: Matthew quotes Isaiah's contrast between honoring God with the lips and keeping the heart far away; Isaiah's fuller verse also describes drawing near and vain reverence.
    Isaiah 29:13 The Lord said, “This people draws near Me; with their lips they honor Me, but their heart is far from Me. They revere Me in vain, teaching human commands and teachings.

Matthew 15:9
In vain they worship Me, teaching human commandments as teachings.’”
  Isaiah 29:13 — quotation: Both describe vain worship or reverence associated with teaching human commands; the supplied Isaiah text says human commands and teachings.
    Isaiah 29:13 The Lord said, “This people draws near Me; with their lips they honor Me, but their heart is far from Me. They revere Me in vain, teaching human commands and teachings.

Matthew 15:10
He summoned the crowd and said to them, “Hear and understand:
  Mark 7:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus summoning the crowd and telling them to listen and understand.
    Mark 7:14 Calling the crowd to Him again, He said to them, “Listen to Me, all of you, and understand.

Matthew 15:11
what enters the mouth does not defile a person, but what comes out of the mouth—this defiles a person.”
  Mark 7:15 — narrative parallel: Both distinguish what enters a person from what comes out, locating defilement in what comes out; Matthew explicitly speaks of the mouth.
    Mark 7:15 There is nothing outside a person that, by entering him, can defile him. Rather, the things coming out of a person are what defile the person.”

Matthew 15:12
Then the disciples came and said to Him, “Dost Thou know that the Pharisees stumbled when they heard this saying?”
  Luke 11:45 — bounded narrative comparison: reporting offense at purity-related correction: Both report religious interlocutors taking offense at Jesus' correction in a dispute whose surrounding scene involves purity and washing. Matthew's disciples report the Pharisees stumbling at His saying; Luke's expert in the Law tells Jesus that He insults his group also. The groups, reporters, sayings and occasions differ, so the link compares adverse reception without making them the same objection or independent accounts of one exchange.
    Luke 11:45 One of the experts in the Law answered Him, “Teacher, by saying these things Thou dost insult us also.”

Matthew 15:13
He answered, “Every plant that My heavenly Father did not plant will be uprooted.
  John 15:1–15:2 — thematic parallel: Matthew uses uprooting for plants not planted by the heavenly Father; John identifies the Father as vinegrower and describes Him removing fruitless branches. Both portray the Father's discriminating action through plant imagery, without identifying plants and branches as the same parable.
    John 15:1 “I am the true vine, and My Father is the vinegrower.
    John 15:2 Every branch in Me that does not bear fruit He takes away; and every one that bears fruit He prunes, so that it may bear more fruit.

Matthew 15:14
Leave them; they are blind guides of the blind. If a blind person guides a blind person, both will fall into a pit.”
  Luke 6:39 — teaching parallel: Both use a blind guide leading another blind person into a pit; Matthew applies the image to the guides being discussed, while Luke frames it as a question.
    Luke 6:39 He also told them a parable: “Can a blind person guide a blind person? Will they not both fall into a pit?

Matthew 15:15
Peter answered Him, “Explain this parable to us.”
  Mark 7:17 — narrative parallel: Both record a private request to explain the defilement parable. Matthew names Peter as the requester; Mark describes the disciples asking after entering a house.
    Mark 7:17 When He entered a house away from the crowd, His disciples asked Him about the parable.

Matthew 15:16
He said, “Are you also still without understanding?
  Mark 7:18 — narrative parallel: Both open Jesus' explanation by questioning the disciples' continued lack of understanding.
    Mark 7:18 He said to them, “Are you also so lacking in understanding? Do you not grasp that everything entering a person from outside cannot defile him,

Matthew 15:17
Do you not understand that everything entering the mouth goes into the stomach and is expelled into the latrine?
  Mark 7:19 — narrative parallel: Both explain the physical route of food through the stomach into the latrine; Mark additionally contrasts the heart and appends a statement about cleansing all foods.
    Mark 7:19 because it does not enter his heart but his stomach, and goes out into the latrine?”—thus cleansing all foods.

Matthew 15:18
But the things coming out of the mouth come from the heart, and those defile a person.
  Mark 7:20–7:21 — narrative parallel: Both identify defilement with what comes from within a person, specifically the heart, rather than with ingested food.
    Mark 7:20 He said, “What comes out of a person is what defiles the person.
    Mark 7:21 For from within, out of people’s hearts, come evil reasonings, acts of sexual immorality, thefts, murders,

Matthew 15:19
For out of the heart come evil reasonings, murders, adulteries, sexual immoralities, thefts, false testimonies, and blasphemies.
  Mark 7:21–7:22 — narrative parallel: Both list evils originating in the heart, including murders, adulteries, sexual immorality, and thefts. The lists differ in length and some terminology, so their individual items are not forced into exact equivalence.
    Mark 7:21 For from within, out of people’s hearts, come evil reasonings, acts of sexual immorality, thefts, murders,
    Mark 7:22 adulteries, acts of greed, wickedness, deceit, licentiousness, an evil eye, slander, arrogance, foolishness.

Matthew 15:20
These are the things that defile a person, but eating with unwashed hands does not defile a person.”
  Mark 7:23 — narrative parallel: Both conclude that evil things originating within defile the person; Matthew additionally returns explicitly to the issue of eating with unwashed hands.
    Mark 7:23 All these evil things come out from within and defile the person.”

Matthew 15:21
Jesus went out from there and withdrew to the regions of Tyre and Sidon.
  Mark 7:24 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' withdrawal in the territory of Tyre; Matthew also names Sidon, whereas Mark adds His desire to remain unnoticed in a house.
    Mark 7:24 He rose from there and went away into the territory of Tyre. He entered a house and wanted no one to know, but He could not remain hidden.

Matthew 15:22
And behold, a Canaanite woman from those territories came out and cried, “Have mercy on me, Lord, Son of David! My daughter is terribly oppressed by a demon.”
  Mark 7:25–7:26 — narrative parallel: Both describe a woman petitioning Jesus for a demon-afflicted daughter. Matthew calls her Canaanite and records her address Lord, Son of David; Mark calls her Greek and Syrophoenician by birth.
    Mark 7:25 Immediately a woman whose little daughter had an unclean spirit heard about Him, came, and fell at His feet.
    Mark 7:26 The woman was Greek, Syrophoenician by birth. She asked Him to cast the demon out of her daughter.

Matthew 15:24
He answered, “I was sent only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.”
  Matthew 10:6 — thematic parallel: Jesus' statement that He was sent to Israel's lost sheep corresponds to His earlier instruction sending the Twelve to those same lost sheep. The statements concern His mission and the disciples' mission respectively.
    Matthew 10:6 Go instead to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.

Matthew 15:25
She came and bowed before Him, saying, “Lord, help me.”
  Mark 7:25 — narrative parallel: Both describe the woman approaching and bowing or falling at Jesus' feet; Matthew records the words Lord, help me, while Mark's cited verse does not reproduce that plea.
    Mark 7:25 Immediately a woman whose little daughter had an unclean spirit heard about Him, came, and fell at His feet.

Matthew 15:26
He answered, “It is not good to take the children’s bread and throw it to the little dogs.”
  Mark 7:27 — narrative parallel: Both use the contrast between children's bread and little dogs in Jesus' answer; Mark additionally says the children should first be satisfied.
    Mark 7:27 He said to her, “Let the children first be satisfied, for it is not good to take the children’s bread and throw it to the little dogs.”

Matthew 15:27
She said, “Yes, Lord, for even the little dogs eat from the crumbs falling from their masters’ table.”
  Mark 7:28 — narrative parallel: Both replies appeal to the little dogs eating crumbs under or from the table. Matthew describes crumbs falling from their masters' table; Mark calls them the children's crumbs.
    Mark 7:28 She answered and said to Him, “Lord, even the little dogs under the table eat some of the children’s crumbs.”

Matthew 15:28
Then Jesus answered her, “Woman, great is your faith! Let it happen for you as you wish.” Her daughter was healed from that hour.
  Mark 7:29–7:30 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus granting the woman's request and her daughter being delivered. Matthew expressly praises her great faith and states healing from that hour; Mark connects the response to her words and describes her finding the child delivered at home.
    Mark 7:29 He said to her, “Because of this word, go; the demon has gone out of your daughter.”
    Mark 7:30 She went home and found the child lying on the bed, and the demon gone.

Matthew 15:29
Moving on from there, Jesus came beside the Sea of Galilee. He went up the mountain and sat there.
  Mark 7:31 — narrative parallel: Both travel notices move from the Tyre-Sidon region to the Sea of Galilee. Mark includes passage through the Decapolis; Matthew additionally describes ascent of a mountain and sitting there.
    Mark 7:31 Leaving the territory of Tyre again, He came through Sidon to the Sea of Galilee, through the middle of the territory of the Decapolis.

Matthew 15:30
Great crowds came to Him, bringing with them lame, blind, maimed, mute, and many others. They laid them at His feet, and He healed them,
  Matthew 11:5 — thematic parallel: The crowd's bringing blind and lame people for healing corresponds to Jesus' earlier account of the blind receiving sight and the lame walking. The other conditions in the two lists differ, and the report does not describe this same gathering.
    Matthew 11:5 the blind receive sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, the dead are raised and the poor have good news proclaimed to them.

Matthew 15:31
so that the crowd marveled when they saw mute people speaking, maimed people healthy, lame people walking, and blind people seeing. They glorified the God of Israel.
  Isaiah 35:5–35:6 — allusion: Matthew's restored sight, walking, and speech recall Isaiah's promised opening of blind eyes, the lame leaping, and clear speech. This compares the restoration imagery without claiming that every detail, including Isaiah's wilderness water, is narrated here.
    Isaiah 35:5 Then blind people’s eyes will open, and deaf people’s ears will hear.
    Isaiah 35:6 Then the lame person will leap like a deer, and the tongue of those with difficulty speaking will be clear, for water burst out in the wilderness and a ravine in a thirsty land,

Matthew 15:32
Jesus summoned His disciples and said, “I am moved with compassion for the crowd, because they have already stayed with Me three days and have nothing to eat. I am unwilling to dismiss them fasting, lest they faint on the road.”
  Mark 8:1–8:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' compassion for a crowd that has remained three days without food and His concern that dismissing them fasting would cause collapse on the road.
    Mark 8:1 In those days, when there was again a large crowd and they had nothing to eat, He called the disciples to Him and said to them,
    Mark 8:2 “I have compassion on the crowd, because they have already remained with Me for three days and have nothing to eat.
    Mark 8:3 If I send them home fasting, they will collapse on the way; and some of them have come from far away.”

Matthew 15:33
The disciples said to Him, “Where can we get so many loaves in a deserted place, enough to fill so great a crowd?”
  Mark 8:4 — narrative parallel: Both give the disciples' question about obtaining enough bread in a deserted place to satisfy the crowd.
    Mark 8:4 His disciples answered Him, “Where can anyone get enough bread to satisfy these people here in a wilderness?”

Matthew 15:34
Jesus said to them, “How many loaves do you have?” They said, “Seven, and a few small fish.”
  Mark 8:5 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus asking how many loaves are available and the answer seven; Matthew also mentions a few small fish, which Mark reports later.
    Mark 8:5 He asked them, “How many loaves do you have?” They said, “Seven.”

Matthew 15:35
He instructed the crowd to recline on the ground,
  Mark 8:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus ordering the crowd to recline on the ground before distributing the food.
    Mark 8:6 He instructed the crowd to recline on the ground. Taking the seven loaves, He gave thanks, broke them, and kept giving them to His disciples to set before the people; they set them before the crowd.

Matthew 15:36
took the seven loaves and the fish, gave thanks, broke them, and kept giving them to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds.
  Mark 8:6–8:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe seven loaves being blessed or given thanks over, broken, and distributed through the disciples, with small fish also provided; Mark describes the fish blessing separately.
    Mark 8:6 He instructed the crowd to recline on the ground. Taking the seven loaves, He gave thanks, broke them, and kept giving them to His disciples to set before the people; they set them before the crowd.
    Mark 8:7 They also had a few small fish. He blessed them and said to set these before them as well.

Matthew 15:37
They all ate and were filled, and they took up the surplus fragments: seven large baskets full.
  Mark 8:8 — narrative parallel: Both report everyone eating to satisfaction and seven large baskets or hampers of leftover pieces being collected.
    Mark 8:8 They ate and were satisfied. They took up seven hampers of leftover pieces.

Matthew 15:38
Those eating were four thousand men, besides women and children.
  Mark 8:9 — narrative parallel: Both count about four thousand eaters; Matthew specifies men besides women and children, while Mark calls them people.
    Mark 8:9 There were about four thousand people. He dismissed them.

Matthew 15:39
After dismissing the crowds, He entered the boat and came to the territory of Magadan.
  Mark 8:10 — narrative parallel: Both follow the crowd's dismissal with Jesus' boat journey. Matthew names the territory of Magadan and Mark the district of Dalmanutha; the names are preserved without asserting that the saved records establish their geographical identity.
    Mark 8:10 Immediately He boarded the boat with His disciples and came to the district of Dalmanutha.

Matthew 16:1
The Pharisees and Sadducees came and, testing Him, asked Him to show them a sign from heaven.
  Mark 8:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe opponents testing Jesus by requesting a sign from heaven. Matthew names Pharisees and Sadducees; Mark names Pharisees.
    Mark 8:11 The Pharisees came out and began disputing with Him, seeking from Him a sign from heaven, to test Him.

Matthew 16:2
He answered them, “When evening comes, you say, ‘Fair weather, for the sky is red.’
  Luke 12:54–12:56 — teaching parallel: Both teachings contrast successful weather assessment with failure to assess the present time. Matthew uses a red evening sky, whereas Luke uses a western cloud and south wind; these are different weather examples.
    Luke 12:54 He also said to the crowds, “When you see a cloud rising in the west, you say at once, ‘A rainstorm is coming,’ and so it happens.
    Luke 12:55 And when you see a south wind blowing, you say, ‘There will be scorching heat,’ and it happens.
    Luke 12:56 Hypocrites! You know how to assess the appearance of the earth and the sky, but how is it that you do not know how to assess this time?

Matthew 16:3
And in the morning, ‘Today a storm, for the sky is red and gloomy.’ You know how to distinguish the face of the sky, but can you not distinguish the signs of the times?
  Luke 12:56 — teaching parallel: Both rebuke those who can assess the sky's appearance but fail to discern the significance of the present time.
    Luke 12:56 Hypocrites! You know how to assess the appearance of the earth and the sky, but how is it that you do not know how to assess this time?

Matthew 16:4
An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, but no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah.” He left them and went away.
  Matthew 12:39 — teaching parallel: Both responses describe an evil and adulterous generation seeking a sign and allow only the sign of Jonah; this is a repeated saying in different settings within Matthew.
    Matthew 12:39 He answered them, “An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, but no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah the prophet.

Matthew 16:5
When the disciples came to the other side, they had forgotten to take bread.
  Mark 8:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples forgetting to bring bread for the boat journey. Mark additionally says there was one loaf in the boat.
    Mark 8:14 They had forgotten to take bread, and had only one loaf with them in the boat.

Matthew 16:6
Jesus said to them, “Look out and beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees.”
  Mark 8:15 — narrative parallel: Both warn the disciples against the leaven of the Pharisees. Matthew additionally names the Sadducees; Mark instead names Herod, so the additional referents are not equated.
    Mark 8:15 He warned them, saying, “Watch out! Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and the leaven of Herod.”

Matthew 16:7
They reasoned among themselves, saying, “We did not take bread.”
  Mark 8:16 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples interpreting Jesus' warning through their failure to bring bread.
    Mark 8:16 They reasoned with one another that they had no bread.

Matthew 16:8
Knowing this, Jesus said, “Why do you reason among yourselves, people of little faith, because you have no bread?
  Mark 8:17 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus recognizing their discussion about lacking bread and questioning their failure to understand; Matthew calls them people of little faith, whereas Mark asks about a hardened heart.
    Mark 8:17 Aware of this, He said to them, “Why are you reasoning that you have no bread? Do you not yet grasp or understand? Is your heart hardened?

Matthew 16:9
Do you not yet understand or remember the five loaves for the five thousand, and how many baskets you took up?
  Mark 8:19 — narrative parallel: Both recall the five loaves for the five thousand and the baskets of fragments collected afterward; Mark supplies the disciples' answer twelve.
    Mark 8:19 when I broke the five loaves for the five thousand, how many baskets full of pieces you took up?” They said to Him, “Twelve.”

Matthew 16:10
Or the seven loaves for the four thousand, and how many large baskets you took up?
  Mark 8:20 — narrative parallel: Both recall the seven loaves for the four thousand and the large baskets collected; Mark supplies the disciples' answer seven.
    Mark 8:20 “And when I broke the seven for the four thousand, how many hampers full of pieces did you take up?” They said to Him, “Seven.”

Matthew 16:11
How do you not understand that I did not speak to you about bread? But beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees.”
  Mark 8:15–8:17 — narrative parallel: Both narratives juxtapose the leaven warning with the disciples' mistaken discussion of missing bread and Jesus' challenge to their understanding. Matthew names Pharisees and Sadducees, while Mark names Pharisees and Herod.
    Mark 8:15 He warned them, saying, “Watch out! Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and the leaven of Herod.”
    Mark 8:16 They reasoned with one another that they had no bread.
    Mark 8:17 Aware of this, He said to them, “Why are you reasoning that you have no bread? Do you not yet grasp or understand? Is your heart hardened?

Matthew 16:12
Then they understood that He did not tell them to beware of the leaven of bread, but of the teaching of the Pharisees and Sadducees.
  Luke 12:1 — teaching parallel: Both explain Pharisaic leaven as something beyond literal bread. Matthew identifies the teaching of Pharisees and Sadducees; Luke identifies the Pharisees' hypocrisy, so the interpretations are compared rather than made identical.
    Luke 12:1 Meanwhile, as the crowd gathered in its tens of thousands, so that they were trampling one another, He began to speak first to His disciples: “Guard yourselves against the leaven of the Pharisees, which is hypocrisy.

Matthew 16:13
When Jesus came into the regions of Caesarea Philippi, He asked His disciples, “Who do people say the Son of Man is?”
  Mark 8:27 — narrative parallel: Both locate Jesus' question about public judgments of His identity near Caesarea Philippi; Matthew phrases it as the identity of the Son of Man and Mark as His own identity.
    Mark 8:27 Jesus and His disciples went out to the villages of Caesarea Philippi. On the way He questioned His disciples, saying to them, “Who do people say that I am?”

Matthew 16:14
They said, “Some John the Baptist; others Elijah; still others Jeremiah or one of the prophets.”
  Mark 8:28 — narrative parallel: Both report identifications of Jesus as John the Baptist, Elijah, or a prophet. Matthew additionally names Jeremiah.
    Mark 8:28 They answered Him, saying, “John the Baptist; others, Elijah; and still others, one of the prophets.”

Matthew 16:15
He said to them, “But you, who do you say I am?”
  Mark 8:29 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus turning the identity question directly to the disciples: who do you say I am?
    Mark 8:29 He asked them, “But you—who do you say that I am?” Peter answered Him, “Thou art the Christ.”

Matthew 16:16
Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”
  Mark 8:29 — narrative parallel: Both record Peter confessing Jesus as the Christ. Matthew's confession additionally calls Him the Son of the living God.
    Mark 8:29 He asked them, “But you—who do you say that I am?” Peter answered Him, “Thou art the Christ.”

Matthew 16:17
Jesus answered him, “Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, because flesh and blood did not reveal this to you, but My Father in the heavens.
  Matthew 11:25–11:27 — thematic parallel: Jesus attributes Peter's recognition to the heavenly Father's revelation rather than flesh and blood; the earlier thanksgiving likewise attributes understanding to the Father's revealing action and speaks of knowledge of the Son. These are different sayings about revelation.
    Matthew 11:25 At that time Jesus answered, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants.
    Matthew 11:26 Yes, Father, because this was pleasing before Thee.
    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

Matthew 16:18
And I tell you that you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build My Church, and the gates of Hades shall not prevail against it.
  John 1:42 — identity parallel: Both connect Simon with the name Peter: Matthew addresses him as Peter while making the Church promise, and John records Jesus naming Simon Cephas and explicitly translating that name as Peter. John does not repeat the rock, Church, or Hades promises.
    John 1:42 He brought him to Jesus. Jesus looked at him and said, “You are Simon, the son of John; you shall be called Cephas”—which is translated Peter.

Matthew 16:19
I will give you the keys of the kingdom of the heavens. Whatever you bind upon earth will have been bound in the heavens, and whatever you loose upon earth will have been loosed in the heavens.”
  Matthew 18:18 — teaching parallel: Both sayings connect earthly binding and loosing with heavenly binding and loosing. Here Jesus addresses Peter and also promises kingdom keys; Matthew 18:18 addresses a plural audience and does not mention keys.
    Matthew 18:18 Truly I tell you, whatever you bind upon earth will have been bound in heaven, and whatever you loose upon earth will have been loosed in heaven.

Matthew 16:20
Then He instructed the disciples to tell no one that He was the Christ.
  Mark 8:30 — narrative parallel: Both follow Peter's confession with Jesus directing the disciples not to tell others about Him; Matthew expressly identifies the concealed confession as His being the Christ.
    Mark 8:30 He strictly warned them to tell no one about Him.

Matthew 16:21
From then on Jesus began to show His disciples that He must go to Jerusalem, suffer many things from the elders, chief priests, and scribes, be killed, and on the third day be raised.
  Mark 8:31 — narrative parallel: Both predict rejection or suffering involving elders, chief priests, and scribes, followed by death and resurrection. Matthew says on the third day and Mark after three days; that wording distinction is retained.
    Mark 8:31 He began teaching them that the Son of Man must suffer many things, be rejected by the elders, the chief priests, and the scribes, be killed, and rise after three days.

Matthew 16:22
Peter took Him aside and began to rebuke Him, saying, “May mercy be Thine, Lord! This shall certainly never happen to Thee.”
  Mark 8:32 — narrative parallel: Both describe Peter taking Jesus aside and rebuking Him after the suffering prediction. Matthew supplies Peter's protest; Mark does not reproduce its words.
    Mark 8:32 He spoke this word openly. Peter took Him aside and began rebuking Him.

Matthew 16:23
But He turned and said to Peter, “Get behind Me, Satan! You are a stumbling block to Me, because you do not set your mind on the things of God, but on the things of human beings.”
  Mark 8:33 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus addressing Peter as Satan and contrasting human concerns with God's concerns. Matthew additionally calls Peter a stumbling block.
    Mark 8:33 But He turned, saw His disciples, and rebuked Peter, saying, “Go behind Me, Satan! For you are not setting your mind on God’s things, but on human things.”

Matthew 16:24
Then Jesus said to His disciples, “If anyone wishes to come after Me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow Me.
  Mark 8:34 — teaching parallel: Both require a would-be follower to deny himself, take up his cross, and follow Jesus. Matthew addresses the disciples; Mark includes the crowd with the disciples.
    Mark 8:34 Calling the crowd to Him with His disciples, He said to them, “If anyone wants to follow behind Me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow Me.

Matthew 16:25
For whoever wishes to save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life because of Me will find it.
  Mark 8:35 — teaching parallel: Both contrast losing life through seeking to save it with finding or saving life through losing it for Jesus. Mark additionally mentions the gospel.
    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

Matthew 16:26
For what will a person gain if he gains the whole world but forfeits his life? Or what will a person give in exchange for his life?
  Mark 8:36–8:37 — teaching parallel: Both ask what benefit there is in gaining the world while forfeiting life and what a person can give in exchange for life.
    Mark 8:36 For what does it benefit a person to gain the whole world and forfeit his life?
    Mark 8:37 For what can a person give in exchange for his life?

Matthew 16:27
For the Son of Man is to come in the glory of His Father with His angels, and then He will repay each person according to his conduct.
  Psalms 61:13 — allusion: Matthew's promise to repay each person according to conduct recalls the psalm's address to the Lord that He will repay each according to works. Matthew applies the repayment to the Son of Man's coming with angels; those additional details are not in this psalm verse.
    Psalms 61:13 that power belongs to God, and mercy unto Thee, Lord, for Thou wilt repay each one according to his works.

Matthew 16:28
Truly I tell you that some standing here will certainly not taste death until they see the Son of Man coming in His kingdom.”
  Mark 9:1 — teaching parallel: Both promise that some present will not taste death before seeing the kingdom's arrival or manifestation. Matthew speaks of the Son of Man coming in His kingdom, and Mark of God's kingdom having come in power; the link does not assign a date or a single interpretive fulfillment.
    Mark 9:1 He said to them, “Truly I tell you, some standing here will certainly not taste death until they see that the kingdom of God has come in power.”

Matthew 17:1
After six days Jesus took Peter, James, and John his brother, and led them up a high mountain privately.
  Mark 9:2 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' private ascent of a high mountain with Peter, James, and John after six days.
    Mark 9:2 After six days, Jesus took Peter, James, and John with Him and brought them up a high mountain privately, alone. He was transfigured before them,

Matthew 17:2
He was transfigured before them. His face shone like the sun, and His garments became white like the light.
  Mark 9:2–9:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' transfiguration and brilliantly white clothing; Matthew additionally describes His face shining like the sun, while Mark compares the clothing with what no earthly bleacher could whiten.
    Mark 9:2 After six days, Jesus took Peter, James, and John with Him and brought them up a high mountain privately, alone. He was transfigured before them,
    Mark 9:3 and His clothes became gleaming, exceedingly white, as no cloth bleacher on earth could whiten them.

Matthew 17:3
And behold, Moses and Elijah appeared to them, conversing with Him.
  Mark 9:4 — narrative parallel: Both report Moses and Elijah appearing and conversing with Jesus; the named order differs.
    Mark 9:4 Elijah appeared to them with Moses, and they were talking with Jesus.

Matthew 17:4
Peter answered Jesus, “Lord, it is good for us to be here. If Thou wishest, I will make three tents here: one for Thee, one for Moses, and one for Elijah.”
  Mark 9:5 — narrative parallel: Both record Peter saying it is good to be there and proposing three tents, one each for Jesus, Moses, and Elijah. Matthew addresses Jesus as Lord and Mark as Rabbi.
    Mark 9:5 Peter answered Jesus, “Rabbi, it is good for us to be here. Let us make three tents: one for Thee, one for Moses, and one for Elijah.”

Matthew 17:5
While he was still speaking, behold, a bright cloud overshadowed them. And behold, a voice from the cloud said, “This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased. Hear Him.”
  Mark 9:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe the overshadowing cloud and the voice identifying Jesus as the beloved Son and commanding the hearers to listen to Him. Matthew additionally describes a bright cloud and divine pleasure in Him.
    Mark 9:7 A cloud came, overshadowing them, and a voice came from the cloud: “This is My beloved Son; listen to Him.”

Matthew 17:6
When the disciples heard, they fell on their faces and were exceedingly afraid.
  Mark 9:6 — narrative parallel: Both transfiguration accounts describe the disciples' fear. Matthew places their falling on their faces in response to the voice; Mark mentions terror while explaining Peter's proposal, so the precise placement is not harmonized.
    Mark 9:6 For he did not know what to answer, because they had become terrified.

Matthew 17:7
Jesus came, touched them, and said, “Rise, and do not fear.”
  Daniel 10:10–10:12 — thematic parallel: Jesus' touch, instruction to rise, and reassurance not to fear after the vision resemble Daniel's being raised by a hand and told to stand and not fear. The actors and visions differ; the link does not identify Daniel's messenger as Jesus.
    Daniel 10:10 He brought a hand toward me and raised me upon my knees, upon the soles of my feet.
    Daniel 10:11 He said to me, “Daniel, you are a man worthy of pity. Understand the commands I speak to you, and stand in your place, for I have just been sent to you.” As he spoke this command with me, I stood trembling.
    Daniel 10:12 He said to me, “Do not fear, Daniel. From the first day you set your face to understand and humble yourself before the Lord your God, your word was heard, and I entered because of your word.

Matthew 17:8
Lifting their eyes, they saw no one except Jesus Himself alone.
  Mark 9:8 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the appearance of Moses and Elijah with the disciples looking and seeing Jesus alone.
    Mark 9:8 Suddenly, looking around, they no longer saw anyone with them except Jesus alone.

Matthew 17:9
As they came down from the mountain, Jesus commanded them, “Tell the vision to no one until the Son of Man is raised from the dead.”
  Mark 9:9 — narrative parallel: Both describe the descent from the mountain and Jesus' prohibition on reporting what they saw until the Son of Man's resurrection.
    Mark 9:9 As they came down from the mountain, He ordered them to tell no one what they had seen until the Son of Man had risen from the dead.

Matthew 17:10
The disciples asked Him, “Why, then, do the scribes say that Elijah must come first?”
  Mark 9:11 — narrative parallel: Both record the disciples asking why the scribes say Elijah must come first.
    Mark 9:11 They questioned Him, saying, “Why do the scribes say that Elijah must come first?”

Matthew 17:11
He answered, “Elijah indeed comes and will restore all things.
  Mark 9:12 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus affirming Elijah's coming and restoration of all things; Mark joins this to a question about the Son of Man's suffering and contempt.
    Mark 9:12 He said to them, “Elijah indeed comes first and restores all things. Yet how is it written of the Son of Man that He should suffer many things and be treated with contempt?

Matthew 17:12
But I tell you that Elijah has already come, and they did not recognize him, but did to him whatever they wished. In the same way the Son of Man is also to suffer at their hands.”
  Mark 9:13 — narrative parallel: Both say Elijah has already come and was subjected to what others wished. Matthew additionally states that he was not recognized and explicitly parallels his treatment with the Son of Man's coming suffering.
    Mark 9:13 But I tell you that Elijah has also come, and they did to him whatever they wanted, just as it is written of him.”

Matthew 17:13
Then the disciples understood that He spoke to them about John the Baptist.
  Matthew 11:14 — identity parallel: The disciples' recognition that Jesus spoke about John the Baptist corresponds to Jesus' earlier identification of John as the Elijah who is to come. This connects the supplied narrative's own identification, without treating John and the historical Elijah as the same ordinary human individual.
    Matthew 11:14 And if you are willing to receive it, he is Elijah who is to come.

Matthew 17:14
When they came to the crowd, a man approached Him, kneeling before Him
  Luke 9:38 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the afflicted boy's father approaching from the crowd and petitioning Jesus; Matthew describes his kneeling and Luke his calling out about his only son.
    Luke 9:38 And behold, a man from the crowd cried out, “Teacher, I beg Thee to look upon my son, because he is my only child.

Matthew 17:15
and saying, “Lord, have mercy on my son, because he suffers seizures and is badly afflicted; for he often falls into fire and often into water.
  Mark 9:22 — narrative parallel: Both fathers describe the boy's repeated danger from fire and water and appeal for compassionate help. Matthew calls his condition seizures; Mark's surrounding narrative attributes the attacks to the spirit.
    Mark 9:22 Often it has thrown him both into fire and into water, to destroy him. But if Thou canst do anything, have compassion on us and help us.”

Matthew 17:16
I brought him to Thy disciples, but they could not heal him.”
  Mark 9:18 — narrative parallel: Both report that the father brought the boy or his request to the disciples, who were unable to cast out the spirit or heal him.
    Mark 9:18 Wherever it seizes him, it dashes him down; he foams, grinds his teeth, and becomes rigid. I told Thy disciples to cast it out, but they did not have the strength.”

Matthew 17:17
Jesus answered, “O faithless and perverted generation, how long will I be with you? How long will I bear with you? Bring him here to Me.”
  Mark 9:19 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus lamenting the unbelieving generation, asking how long He must remain and bear with them, and commanding that the boy be brought to Him.
    Mark 9:19 He answered them, “O unbelieving generation, how long shall I be with you? How long shall I bear with you? Bring him to Me.”

Matthew 17:18
Jesus rebuked him, and the demon came out of him; the boy was healed from that hour.
  Luke 9:42 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus rebuking the demon or unclean spirit and healing the boy; Luke also narrates the convulsion during his approach and his return to his father.
    Luke 9:42 While he was still approaching, the demon dashed him down and convulsed him. Jesus rebuked the unclean spirit, healed the boy, and gave him back to his father.

Matthew 17:19
Then the disciples came to Jesus privately and said, “Why could we not cast it out?”
  Mark 9:28 — narrative parallel: Both record the disciples privately asking Jesus why they could not cast out the spirit.
    Mark 9:28 When He entered a house, His disciples asked Him privately, “Why could we not cast it out?”

Matthew 17:20
