He said to them, “Because of your little faith. For truly I tell you, if you have faith like a mustard seed, you will say to this mountain, ‘Move from here to there,’ and it will move; and nothing will be impossible for you.”
  Luke 17:6 — teaching parallel: Both use faith like a mustard seed to illustrate an apparently impossible command becoming effective. Matthew names a mountain moving from here to there; Luke names a mulberry tree uprooted and planted in the sea. The images and settings differ.
    Luke 17:6 And the Lord said, “If you have faith like a mustard seed, you would say to this mulberry tree, ‘Be uprooted and planted in the sea,’ and it would obey you.

Matthew 17:22
While they gathered in Galilee, Jesus said to them, “The Son of Man is about to be handed over into human hands.
  Mark 9:30–9:31 — narrative parallel: Both place a prediction of the Son of Man being handed over into human hands in a Galilean setting; Mark describes passing through Galilee and continues into the death and resurrection prediction.
    Mark 9:30 They left there and passed through Galilee, and He did not want anyone to know.
    Mark 9:31 For He was teaching His disciples and saying to them, “The Son of Man is being handed over into human hands, and they will kill Him. After He is killed, He will rise after three days.”

Matthew 17:23
They will kill Him, and on the third day He will be raised.” They were exceedingly grieved.
  Mark 9:31–9:32 — narrative parallel: Both predict killing and resurrection after the betrayal saying. Matthew says on the third day and describes grief; Mark says after three days and describes incomprehension and fear of asking.
    Mark 9:31 For He was teaching His disciples and saying to them, “The Son of Man is being handed over into human hands, and they will kill Him. After He is killed, He will rise after three days.”
    Mark 9:32 But they did not understand the saying, and were afraid to ask Him.

Matthew 17:24
And when they came to Capernaum, those who collected the didrachmas came to Peter and said, “Does not your Teacher pay the didrachmas?”
  Matthew 17:25 — internal question and explicit response: The collectors’ question about the Teacher and the didrachmas is followed by Peter’s recorded Yes at the start of17:25. This identifies the question being answered,without independently establishing a payment history,another tax amount or the force of the later royal-tax analogy.
    Matthew 17:25 He says, “Yes.” And when he entered the house, Jesus spoke first to him, saying, “What do you think, Simon? From whom do the kings of the earth receive toll or tribute: from their sons, or from strangers?”

Matthew 17:25
He says, “Yes.” And when he entered the house, Jesus spoke first to him, saying, “What do you think, Simon? From whom do the kings of the earth receive toll or tribute: from their sons, or from strangers?”
  Matthew 17:26 — internal question and explicit response: Jesus asks whether earthly kings collect toll or tribute from sons or strangers. The answer From strangers and the conclusion Then the sons are free supply the precise response and inference of this analogy,without reconstructing an administrative exemption or importing a different contribution amount.
    Matthew 17:26 And when he said, “From strangers,” Jesus said to him, “Then the sons are free.

Matthew 17:26
And when he said, “From strangers,” Jesus said to him, “Then the sons are free.
  Matthew 17:25 — internal argument and stated premise: The sons-free conclusion follows the answer From strangers to the preceding question about kings collecting from sons or strangers. The link identifies the expressed premise of the inference,without treating the analogy as an independently established temple-tax rule.
    Matthew 17:25 He says, “Yes.” And when he entered the house, Jesus spoke first to him, saying, “What do you think, Simon? From whom do the kings of the earth receive toll or tribute: from their sons, or from strangers?”

Matthew 17:27
But lest we cause them to stumble, go to the sea and cast a hook, and take the first fish that comes up; and when you open its mouth, you will find a stater. Take it and give it to them for Me and you.”
  1 Corinthians 9:12 — thematic parallel: Jesus arranges payment to avoid causing others to stumble, while Paul describes forgoing a right to avoid hindering the gospel. The shared principle is voluntary restraint for others' sake; Paul's passage does not parallel the fish or coin miracle.
    1 Corinthians 9:12 If others share in this right over you, do we not all the more? But we did not use this right; instead, we bear everything so that we may put no hindrance in the way of the good news of Christ.

Matthew 18:1
At that hour the disciples came to Jesus, saying, “Who, then, is greatest in the kingdom of the heavens?”
  Luke 9:46 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the disciples' question or dispute about which of them is greatest. Matthew names greatness in the kingdom of the heavens.
    Luke 9:46 A discussion arose among them about which of them might be greatest.

Matthew 18:2
He called a child and stood the child in their midst,
  Mark 9:36 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus place a child among the disciples as part of His answer about greatness. Mark also describes Him embracing the child.
    Mark 9:36 He took a child, stood the child among them, and, embracing the child, said to them,

Matthew 18:3
and said, “Truly I tell you, unless you turn and become like children, you will certainly not enter the kingdom of the heavens.
  Mark 10:15 — teaching parallel: Both make becoming or receiving like a child necessary for entering God's kingdom. Matthew says to turn and become like children; Mark speaks of receiving the kingdom like a child.
    Mark 10:15 Truly I tell you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child will certainly not enter it.”

Matthew 18:4
Therefore whoever humbles himself like this child is greatest in the kingdom of the heavens.
  Luke 9:48 — teaching parallel: Matthew identifies the person humbling himself like the child as greatest; Luke concludes the child-reception teaching by calling the least among the disciples great.
    Luke 9:48 and said to them, “Whoever receives this child in My name receives Me; whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me. For the one who is least among you all is great.”

Matthew 18:5
And whoever receives one such child in My name receives Me.
  Mark 9:37 — teaching parallel: Both say that receiving such a child in Jesus' name is receiving Jesus Himself. Mark extends the chain of reception to the One who sent Him.
    Mark 9:37 “Whoever receives one such child in My name receives Me; and whoever receives Me does not receive Me but the One who sent Me.”

Matthew 18:6
But whoever causes one of these little ones who believe in Me to stumble, it is better for him that a donkey-driven millstone be hung around his neck and he be sunk in the open sea.
  Mark 9:42 — teaching parallel: Both warn against causing a believing little one to stumble and compare the offender's fate unfavorably with being thrown into the sea wearing a millstone.
    Mark 9:42 Whoever causes one of these little ones who believe in Me to stumble—it would be better for him if a donkey-driven millstone were placed around his neck and he were thrown into the sea.

Matthew 18:7
Woe to the world because of stumbling blocks! For stumbling blocks must come, but woe to the person through whom the stumbling block comes!
  Luke 17:1 — teaching parallel: Both acknowledge the unavoidable coming of stumbling blocks while pronouncing woe on the person through whom they come. Necessity does not remove that person's responsibility.
    Luke 17:1 And He said to His disciples, “It is impossible for stumbling blocks not to come, but woe to the one through whom they come!

Matthew 18:8
If your hand or foot causes you to stumble, cut it off and throw it away from you. It is better for you to enter life maimed or lame than, having two hands or two feet, to be cast into the eternal fire.
  Mark 9:43–9:45 — teaching parallel: Both use cutting off a stumbling hand or foot to contrast entering life maimed with retaining the members and entering fire. Mark's original consecutive units here are 43 and 45; absent verse 44 is not invented.
    Mark 9:43 If your hand causes you to stumble, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life maimed than to have two hands and go away into Gehenna, into the unquenchable fire.
    Mark 9:45 If your foot causes you to stumble, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life lame than to have two feet and be thrown into Gehenna.

Matthew 18:9
And if your eye causes you to stumble, tear it out and throw it away from you. It is better for you to enter life with one eye than, having two eyes, to be cast into the Gehenna of fire.
  Mark 9:47–9:48 — teaching parallel: Both use removing a stumbling eye to contrast one-eyed life with retaining two eyes and entering Gehenna. Mark continues with undying worm and unextinguished fire.
    Mark 9:47 If your eye causes you to stumble, cast it out. It is better for you to enter the kingdom of God with one eye than to have two eyes and be thrown into Gehenna,
    Mark 9:48 where their worm does not die and the fire is not extinguished.

Matthew 18:10
See that you do not despise one of these little ones, for I tell you that their angels in the heavens continually see the face of My Father in the heavens.
  Hebrews 1:14 — thematic parallel: Matthew grounds protection of little ones in their angels' access to the Father; Hebrews describes angels serving heirs of salvation. Both associate angels with care for God's people; Hebrews does not repeat the face-of-the-Father claim.
    Hebrews 1:14 Are they not all ministering spirits, sent out for service for the sake of those who are to inherit salvation?

Matthew 18:12
What do you think? If someone has a hundred sheep and one of them strays, will he not leave the ninety-nine on the mountains and go seeking the one that strays?
  Luke 15:4 — teaching parallel: Both tell of a shepherd with a hundred sheep who leaves ninety-nine to seek the missing one. Matthew locates the ninety-nine on mountains; Luke places them in the wilderness.
    Luke 15:4 “What man among you, having a hundred sheep and losing one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness and go after the lost one until he finds it?

Matthew 18:13
If he succeeds in finding it, truly I tell you that he rejoices over it more than over the ninety-nine that have not strayed.
  Luke 15:5–15:7 — teaching parallel: Both emphasize exceptional joy when the lost sheep is found and compare it with the ninety-nine. Luke develops the image into heavenly joy over a repentant sinner.
    Luke 15:5 And when he finds it, he lays it on his shoulders, rejoicing.
    Luke 15:6 When he comes home, he calls his friends and neighbors together and says to them, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found my sheep that was lost!’
    Luke 15:7 I tell you, in the same way there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who have no need of repentance.

Matthew 18:14
In the same way, it is not the will of your Father in the heavens that one of these little ones perish.
  Luke 15:7 — teaching parallel: Matthew applies the lost-sheep story to the Father's will that no little one perish; Luke applies it to heavenly joy over one repentant sinner. Both emphasize the recovery of the one.
    Luke 15:7 I tell you, in the same way there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who have no need of repentance.

Matthew 18:15
If your brother sins against you, go and reprove him between you and him alone. If he hears you, you have gained your brother.
  Luke 17:3 — teaching parallel: Both require rebuking a sinning brother and seeking restoration. Matthew specifies private correction and gaining the brother; Luke explicitly connects repentance and forgiveness.
    Luke 17:3 Watch yourselves. If your brother sins, rebuke him; and if he repents, forgive him.

Matthew 18:16
But if he does not hear, take one or two more with you, so that every matter may be established upon the mouth of two or three witnesses.
  Deuteronomy 19:15 — quotation: The requirement to establish every matter upon two or three witnesses closely repeats Deuteronomy's rule. Matthew applies it after unsuccessful private correction.
    Deuteronomy 19:15 One witness shall not suffice to testify against a man concerning any injustice, or any trespass, or any sin which he sins. At the mouth of two witnesses, and at the mouth of three witnesses, shall every matter be established.

Matthew 18:17
If he refuses to hear them, tell the Church. And if he refuses to hear even the Church, let him be to you like the Gentile and the tax collector.
  1 Corinthians 5:11–5:13 — thematic parallel: Both describe limiting fellowship with someone inside the community who persists in wrongdoing. Paul specifies offenses and exclusion from association; Matthew describes refusal to hear and treatment as an outsider.
    1 Corinthians 5:11 But now I have written to you not to associate with anyone who is called a brother if he is sexually immoral or greedy or an idolater or abusive or a drunkard or grasping—not even to eat with such a person.
    1 Corinthians 5:12 For what have I to do with judging those outside? Do you not judge those inside?
    1 Corinthians 5:13 But God will judge those outside. Remove the wicked person from among yourselves.

Matthew 18:18
Truly I tell you, whatever you bind upon earth will have been bound in heaven, and whatever you loose upon earth will have been loosed in heaven.
  Matthew 16:19 — teaching parallel: The earlier saying to Peter uses the same earthly binding and loosing with heavenly correspondence. Matthew 18 addresses the disciples amid communal discipline rather than repeating the promise of keys.
    Matthew 16:19 I will give you the keys of the kingdom of the heavens. Whatever you bind upon earth will have been bound in the heavens, and whatever you loose upon earth will have been loosed in the heavens.”

Matthew 18:19
Again, truly I tell you that if two of you agree upon earth concerning any matter they ask, it will be done for them by My Father in the heavens.
  Matthew 7:7–7:11 — teaching parallel: Both connect requests with the Father's giving. Matthew 18 specifies two disciples agreeing on a request; the Sermon supplies the broader assurance of good gifts to those who ask.
    Matthew 7:7 Ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.
    Matthew 7:8 For everyone who asks receives, the one who seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.
    Matthew 7:9 Or who among you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone?
    Matthew 7:10 Or if he asks for a fish, will give him a snake?
    Matthew 7:11 If you, then, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father in the heavens give good things to those who ask Him!

Matthew 18:20
For where two or three are gathered into My name, there I am in their midst.”
  Matthew 28:20 — thematic parallel: Both promise Jesus' continuing presence with disciples. Matthew 18 specifies gathering in His name; the conclusion promises His presence throughout the days until the age's completion.
    Matthew 28:20 teaching them to keep all that I commanded you; and behold, I am with you all the days, until the consummation of the age.”

Matthew 18:21
Then Peter came and said to Him, “Lord, how often will my brother sin against me and I forgive him? Up to seven times?”
  Luke 17:3–17:4 — teaching parallel: Peter asks about forgiving a brother repeatedly, up to seven times; Luke describes seven sins in a day with repeated repentance and forgiveness.
    Luke 17:3 Watch yourselves. If your brother sins, rebuke him; and if he repents, forgive him.
    Luke 17:4 And if he sins against you seven times in the day and turns back to you seven times, saying, ‘I repent,’ you shall forgive him.”

Matthew 18:22
Jesus says to him, “I tell you, not up to seven times, but up to seventy times seven.
  Luke 17:4 — teaching parallel: Both reject repeated sin as a reason to stop forgiveness. Matthew answers seventy times seven; Luke describes seven occurrences in a day and explicitly specifies repentance.
    Luke 17:4 And if he sins against you seven times in the day and turns back to you seven times, saying, ‘I repent,’ you shall forgive him.”

Matthew 18:23
Therefore the kingdom of the heavens was likened to a king who wished to settle accounts with his slaves.
  Luke 19:15 — thematic parallel: Both parables introduce a ruler calling slaves to an accounting. Matthew's king settles debts; Luke's returning king asks what business entrusted money produced. They are different parables.
    Luke 19:15 And it happened that when he returned, having received the kingdom, he ordered those slaves to whom he had given the money to be called to him, so that he might know what business they had done.

Matthew 18:24
And when he began to settle them, one was brought to him who owed ten thousand talents.
  Luke 7:41–7:42 — thematic parallel: Both parables use indebtedness and inability to repay to introduce forgiveness. Matthew specifies ten thousand talents; Luke contrasts five-hundred-denarius and fifty-denarius debts.
    Luke 7:41 “A certain lender had two debtors. One owed five hundred denarii and the other fifty.
    Luke 7:42 Since they had nothing with which to repay, he graciously forgave them both. Which of them, then, will love him more?”

Matthew 18:25
And since he did not have the means to repay, his master commanded that he be sold, with his wife and children and all that he had, and that payment be made.
  2 Kings 4:1 — thematic parallel: Both depict an unpaid debt threatening enslavement of the debtor's children. Matthew includes the debtor and wife in the threatened sale; the widow in Kings reports a lender coming for her sons.
    2 Kings 4:1 A woman from among the sons of the prophets cried to Elisha, saying, ‘Your slave, my husband, has died. You know that he was a slave who feared the Lord. Now the lender has come to take my two sons as his slaves.’

Matthew 18:26
The slave therefore fell down and did him homage, saying, ‘Have patience with me, and I will repay you everything.’
  Matthew 18:29 — teaching parallel: This explicitly same-chapter comparison repeats the request for patience and promise to repay. The first slave's plea becomes his fellow slave's plea to him, exposing his inconsistency.
    Matthew 18:29 So his fellow slave fell down and pleaded with him, saying, ‘Be patient with me, and I will repay you.’

Matthew 18:27
And the master of that slave, moved with compassion, released him and forgave him the loan.
  Luke 7:42 — thematic parallel: Both describe a creditor canceling debt for someone unable to repay. Matthew attributes the release to compassion; Luke's cancellation introduces a question about grateful love.
    Luke 7:42 Since they had nothing with which to repay, he graciously forgave them both. Which of them, then, will love him more?”

Matthew 18:28
But that slave went out and found one of his fellow slaves who owed him a hundred denarii. He seized him and began choking him, saying, ‘Repay whatever you owe.’
  Matthew 18:24 — thematic parallel: The same parable contrasts this violently demanded hundred-denarius debt with the ten-thousand-talent debt previously owed by the demanding slave himself. The differing amounts expose the reversal of mercy.
    Matthew 18:24 And when he began to settle them, one was brought to him who owed ten thousand talents.

Matthew 18:29
So his fellow slave fell down and pleaded with him, saying, ‘Be patient with me, and I will repay you.’
  Matthew 18:26 — teaching parallel: This same-chapter plea mirrors the slave's earlier request for patience and promise to repay. He now hears essentially the request he made to his master.
    Matthew 18:26 The slave therefore fell down and did him homage, saying, ‘Have patience with me, and I will repay you everything.’

Matthew 18:30
But he was unwilling. Instead, he went and threw him into prison until he repaid what was owed.
  Matthew 18:27 — thematic parallel: The same chapter contrasts the master's compassionate release with the forgiven slave's refusal of patience and imprisonment of his fellow debtor.
    Matthew 18:27 And the master of that slave, moved with compassion, released him and forgave him the loan.

Matthew 18:32
Then his master summoned him and said, ‘Evil slave! I forgave you all that debt because you pleaded with me.
  Matthew 18:27 — narrative parallel: The master explicitly recalls his earlier cancellation of the slave's debt. This same-chapter callback identifies the mercy already received that makes the slave's behavior culpable.
    Matthew 18:27 And the master of that slave, moved with compassion, released him and forgave him the loan.

Matthew 18:33
Should you not also have had mercy on your fellow slave, as I had mercy on you?’
  Luke 6:36 — teaching parallel: The master demands mercy patterned on his own mercy; Luke calls disciples to be merciful as their Father is merciful. Luke states the principle directly instead of through this debt parable.
    Luke 6:36 Be merciful, just as your Father is merciful.

Matthew 18:34
His master became angry and handed him over to the torturers until he repaid everything owed.
  James 2:13 — thematic parallel: The merciless slave faces judgment for withholding mercy, corresponding to James's warning of judgment without mercy for someone showing none. James does not repeat the torture or debt imagery.
    James 2:13 For judgment is without mercy to the one who has shown no mercy; mercy triumphs over judgment.

Matthew 18:35
So also My heavenly Father will do to you, unless each of you forgives his brother from your hearts.”
  Matthew 6:14–6:15 — teaching parallel: Both connect the Father's treatment with the person's forgiveness of others. Matthew 18 adds from the heart and applies the principle through the unforgiving-slave parable.
    Matthew 6:14 For if you forgive people their trespasses, your heavenly Father will also forgive you.
    Matthew 6:15 But if you do not forgive people, neither will your Father forgive your trespasses.

Matthew 19:1
When Jesus finished these words, He departed from Galilee and came into the territories of Judea beyond the Jordan.
  Mark 10:1 — narrative parallel: Both locate Jesus' movement in Judea and beyond the Jordan after leaving the prior setting. Matthew explicitly says He departed from Galilee.
    Mark 10:1 He rose from there and came into the territory of Judea and beyond the Jordan. Crowds gathered to Him again, and as was His custom, He again taught them.

Matthew 19:2
Great crowds followed Him, and He healed them there.
  Matthew 15:30 — narrative parallel: Both describe great crowds coming to Jesus and His healing them. This is a recurring crowd-healing pattern in different narrative settings, not a claim that the occasions are one event.
    Matthew 15:30 Great crowds came to Him, bringing with them lame, blind, maimed, mute, and many others. They laid them at His feet, and He healed them,

Matthew 19:3
Pharisees came to Him, testing Him and saying, “Is it lawful for a man to divorce his wife for any cause?”
  Mark 10:2 — narrative parallel: Both have Pharisees test Jesus by questioning the lawfulness of divorcing a wife. Matthew explicitly adds for any cause.
    Mark 10:2 Pharisees came to Him and asked whether it was lawful for a man to divorce his wife, testing Him.

Matthew 19:4
He answered, “Have you not read that the Creator from the beginning made them male and female,
  Genesis 1:27 — quotation: The appeal to the Creator making them male and female recalls Genesis's creation of humanity as male and female in God's image, here invoked in the divorce dispute.
    Genesis 1:27 And God made man; in the image of God He made him; male and female He made them.

Matthew 19:5
and said, ‘For this reason a man will leave father and mother and be joined to his wife, and the two will become one flesh’?
  Genesis 2:24 — quotation: Matthew quotes leaving father and mother, union with the wife, and the two becoming one flesh. The source wording remains distinct, including cleave versus be joined.
    Genesis 2:24 For this reason a man shall leave his father and his mother and cleave to his wife, and the two shall become one flesh.

Matthew 19:6
So they are no longer two, but one flesh. Therefore what God has yoked together, let no human being separate.”
  Mark 10:8–10:9 — teaching parallel: Both conclude that the married pair are no longer two but one flesh and prohibit humans from separating what God has yoked together.
    Mark 10:8 and the two will become one flesh.’ So they are no longer two, but one flesh.
    Mark 10:9 Therefore, what God has yoked together, let no human separate.”

Matthew 19:7
They said to Him, “Why then did Moses command giving a certificate of divorce and divorcing her?”
  Mark 10:4 — narrative parallel: Both opponents appeal to Moses and a divorce certificate. Matthew calls their objection a command; Mark's speakers call it permission to write a certificate and dismiss the wife.
    Mark 10:4 They said, “Moses permitted a man to write a certificate of divorce and dismiss her.”

Matthew 19:8
He said to them, “Because of your hardness of heart Moses permitted you to divorce your wives, but from the beginning it has not been so.
  Mark 10:5–10:6 — teaching parallel: Both explain the Mosaic divorce provision by hardness of heart and contrast it with creation's beginning. Matthew explicitly calls it permission and denies that it was originally so.
    Mark 10:5 Jesus said to them, “Because of your hardness of heart he wrote you this commandment.
    Mark 10:6 But from the beginning of creation, ‘He made them male and female.’

Matthew 19:9
I tell you that whoever divorces his wife, except on account of sexual immorality, and marries another commits adultery.”
  Mark 10:11–10:12 — teaching parallel: Both associate divorce followed by remarriage with adultery. Matthew includes a sexual-immorality exception absent here from Mark, who additionally discusses a wife divorcing her husband.
    Mark 10:11 He said to them, “Whoever divorces his wife and marries another commits adultery against her.
    Mark 10:12 And if she divorces her husband and marries another, she commits adultery.”

Matthew 19:10
His disciples said to Him, “If this is the situation of the man with the wife, it is better not to marry.”
  1 Corinthians 7:8–7:9 — thematic parallel: The disciples react to the marriage ruling by saying it is better not to marry. Paul advises unmarried people and widows about remaining as he is, while qualifying the advice where self-control is lacking. This compares particular assessments of unmarried life without converting the disciples' reaction into Jesus' command or erasing Paul's qualifications.
    1 Corinthians 7:8 Now I say to the unmarried and the widows: it is good for them if they remain as I am.
    1 Corinthians 7:9 But if they do not exercise self-control, let them marry, for it is better to marry than to burn.

Matthew 19:11
He said to them, “Not everyone can receive this saying, but only those to whom it has been given.
  1 Corinthians 7:7 — teaching parallel: Jesus says the saying can be received only by those to whom it is given; Paul acknowledges differing gifts from God while discussing marriage and remaining unmarried.
    1 Corinthians 7:7 I wish that all people were as I myself am. But each has his own gift from God, one in this way and another in that.

Matthew 19:12
For there are eunuchs who were born so from their mother’s womb, there are eunuchs who were made eunuchs by human beings, and there are eunuchs who made themselves eunuchs because of the kingdom of the heavens. Let the one able to receive it receive it.”
  1 Corinthians 7:32–7:35 — thematic parallel: Matthew includes eunuchs for the kingdom among distinct categories; Paul emphasizes unmarried people's undistracted devotion to the Lord. The comparison concerns kingdom-oriented renunciation, without making Paul repeat Matthew's three categories.
    1 Corinthians 7:32 I want you to be free from anxiety. The unmarried man is concerned about the Lord’s affairs, how he may please the Lord.
    1 Corinthians 7:33 But the married man is concerned about the world’s affairs, how he may please his wife,
    1 Corinthians 7:34 and he is divided. Both the unmarried woman and the virgin are concerned about the Lord’s affairs, so that each may be holy in both body and spirit. But the married woman is concerned about the world’s affairs, how she may please her husband.
    1 Corinthians 7:35 I say this for your own benefit, not to throw a noose over you, but for good order and undistracted devotion to the Lord.

Matthew 19:13
Then children were brought to Him so that He might lay His hands upon them and pray. But the disciples rebuked them.
  Mark 10:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe children being brought for Jesus' touch or blessing and the disciples rebuking them. Matthew mentions laying on hands and prayer.
    Mark 10:13 People brought children to Him so that He might touch them, but the disciples rebuked them.

Matthew 19:14
Jesus said, “Let the children come, and do not hinder them from coming to Me, for the kingdom of the heavens belongs to such as these.”
  Mark 10:14 — teaching parallel: Both forbid hindering the children and say the kingdom belongs to such as these. Mark also records Jesus' indignation at the disciples' interference.
    Mark 10:14 When Jesus saw it, He was indignant and said to them, “Let the children come to Me; do not stop them, for the kingdom of God belongs to such as these.

Matthew 19:15
After laying His hands upon them, He went on from there.
  Mark 10:16 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus laying His hands on the children. Mark adds embracing and blessing; Matthew then narrates His departure.
    Mark 10:16 He embraced them and blessed them, laying His hands on them.

Matthew 19:16
And behold, someone came and said to Him, “Teacher, what good thing shall I do to have eternal life?”
  Mark 10:17 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a man's question about obtaining or inheriting eternal life. Mark's man addresses Good Teacher; Matthew's man asks what good thing to do.
    Mark 10:17 As He was setting out on the road, a man ran up, knelt before Him, and asked Him, “Good Teacher, what should I do to inherit eternal life?”

Matthew 19:17
He said to him, “Why do you ask Me about what is good? One is good. But if you wish to enter life, keep the commandments.”
  Mark 10:18–10:19 — teaching parallel: Both address goodness and the commandments. Matthew asks why the man asks about what is good; Mark asks why he calls Jesus good and names God as the One who is good.
    Mark 10:18 Jesus said to him, “Why do you call Me good? No one is good except One—God.
    Mark 10:19 You know the commandments: ‘Do not murder; do not commit adultery; do not steal; do not bear false witness; do not defraud; honor your father and mother.’”

Matthew 19:18
He said to Him, “Which ones?” Jesus said, “‘You shall not murder; you shall not commit adultery; you shall not steal; you shall not bear false witness;
  Mark 10:19 — teaching parallel: Both list prohibitions of murder, adultery, theft, and false witness in the reply about eternal life. Mark also includes do not defraud.
    Mark 10:19 You know the commandments: ‘Do not murder; do not commit adultery; do not steal; do not bear false witness; do not defraud; honor your father and mother.’”

Matthew 19:19
honor father and mother’; and ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’”
  Mark 10:19 — teaching parallel: Both include honoring father and mother in the list. Matthew additionally gives love of neighbor here, a clause absent from this verse of Mark's list.
    Mark 10:19 You know the commandments: ‘Do not murder; do not commit adultery; do not steal; do not bear false witness; do not defraud; honor your father and mother.’”

Matthew 19:20
The young man said to Him, “I have kept all these. What do I still lack?”
  Mark 10:20 — narrative parallel: Both have the questioner assert that he has kept the listed commandments. Matthew adds what remains lacking; Mark says he has kept them from youth.
    Mark 10:20 He said to Him, “Teacher, I have kept all these from my youth.”

Matthew 19:21
Jesus said to him, “If you wish to be perfect, go, sell your belongings, and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in the heavens. Then come, follow Me.”
  Mark 10:21 — teaching parallel: Both require selling possessions, giving to the poor, and following Jesus, promising heavenly treasure. Matthew frames it as wishing to be perfect; Mark says the man lacks one thing.
    Mark 10:21 Jesus looked at him, loved him, and said to him, “You lack one thing. Go, sell what you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven. Then come, follow Me.”

Matthew 19:22
When the young man heard the saying, he went away grieved, for he had many properties.
  Mark 10:22 — narrative parallel: Both say the man left grieved because he possessed much. Matthew describes many properties; Mark adds his fallen expression at the saying.
    Mark 10:22 But his face fell at the word, and he went away grieved, for he had many possessions.

Matthew 19:23
Jesus said to His disciples, “Truly I tell you that a rich person will enter the kingdom of the heavens with difficulty.
  Mark 10:23 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus address the disciples about a wealthy person's difficulty entering God's kingdom after the man's departure.
    Mark 10:23 Jesus looked around and said to His disciples, “How hard it will be for those who have wealth to enter the kingdom of God!”

Matthew 19:24
Again I tell you, it is easier for a camel to enter through the hole of a needle than for a rich person to enter the kingdom of God.”
  Mark 10:25 — teaching parallel: Both compare a camel passing through a needle's opening with a rich person's entrance into God's kingdom. Neither supplied verse identifies a city gate.
    Mark 10:25 It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich person to enter the kingdom of God.”

Matthew 19:25
When the disciples heard this, they were exceedingly astonished, saying, “Who then can be saved?”
  Mark 10:26 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples' extreme astonishment and question about who can be saved after the teaching concerning wealth.
    Mark 10:26 They were exceedingly astonished and said to one another, “Then who can be saved?”

Matthew 19:26
Looking at them, Jesus said, “With human beings this is impossible, but with God all things are possible.”
  Mark 10:27 — teaching parallel: Both answer the salvation question by contrasting human impossibility with God's ability and explicitly say all things are possible with God.
    Mark 10:27 Jesus looked at them and said, “With humans it is impossible, but not with God; for all things are possible with God.”

Matthew 19:27
Then Peter answered Him, “Behold, we have left everything and followed Thee. What then will there be for us?”
  Mark 10:28 — narrative parallel: Both have Peter say the disciples left everything and followed Jesus. Matthew also asks what then will be theirs.
    Mark 10:28 Peter began saying to Him, “Look, we have left everything and followed Thee.”

Matthew 19:28
Jesus said to them, “Truly I tell you that you who have followed Me, in the regeneration, when the Son of Man sits upon the throne of His glory, shall also sit upon twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel.
  Luke 22:28–22:30 — teaching parallel: Both promise faithful followers thrones and a role judging Israel's twelve tribes. Matthew specifies twelve thrones and the regeneration; Luke connects the promise with Jesus' kingdom table.
    Luke 22:28 And you are those who have remained with Me in My trials.
    Luke 22:29 And I assign to you a kingdom, just as My Father assigned one to Me,
    Luke 22:30 so that you may eat and drink at My table in My kingdom, and you will sit on thrones judging the twelve tribes of Israel.

Matthew 19:29
Everyone who has left houses, brothers, sisters, father, mother, children, or fields because of My name will receive a hundredfold and inherit eternal life.
  Mark 10:29–10:30 — teaching parallel: Both promise a hundredfold and eternal life to those leaving family or property for Jesus. Mark distinguishes present reception with persecutions from eternal life in the coming age.
    Mark 10:29 Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, there is no one who has left house, brothers, sisters, mother, father, children, or fields for My sake and for the sake of the gospel,
    Mark 10:30 who will not receive a hundredfold now, in this time—houses, brothers, sisters, mothers, children, and fields, with persecutions—and in the coming age, eternal life.

Matthew 19:30
But many who are first will be last, and the last first.
  Mark 10:31 — teaching parallel: Both close the teaching on possessions and discipleship with the reversal that many first will be last and the last first.
    Mark 10:31 But many who are first will be last, and the last first.”

Matthew 20:1
For the kingdom of the heavens is like a householder who went out at dawn to hire labourers for his vineyard.
  Matthew 19:30 — thematic parallel: The kingdom parable follows the first-and-last saying and repeats it at 20:16. This same-book framing link identifies the parable's context, not an independent account of the vineyard hiring.
    Matthew 19:30 But many who are first will be last, and the last first.

Matthew 20:2
And having agreed with the labourers for a denarius for the day, he sent them into his vineyard.
  Deuteronomy 24:14–24:15 — thematic parallel: The parable begins with an agreed day's wage. Deuteronomy supplies the legal background of paid labor and wages due that day, but does not prescribe the one-denarius amount.
    Deuteronomy 24:14 You shall not withhold the wages of the poor and needy, whether of your brethren or of the sojourners within your cities.
    Deuteronomy 24:15 On the same day you shall pay his wages; the sun shall not go down upon them, for he is poor and sets his hope upon them; lest he cry against you unto the Lord, and there be sin upon you.

Matthew 20:3
And going out about the third hour, he saw others standing idle in the marketplace;
  Matthew 20:6 — internal structural parallel: repeated hiring stages: The owner goes out and finds idle men in both hiring rounds. The third-hour scene names the marketplace; the eleventh-hour scene adds his question about standing idle all day. This is a repeated hiring-stage comparison, preserving the actual different hours and details rather than independent attestation or a claim that the men are lazy.
    Matthew 20:6 About the eleventh hour he went out and found others standing, and said to them, ‘Why have you stood here idle the whole day?’

Matthew 20:4
and to them he said, ‘You also go into the vineyard, and whatever is just I will give you.’
  Colossians 4:1 — thematic parallel: The owner promises hired workers whatever is just; Colossians requires authorities over labor to provide what is just and equitable. Colossians addresses masters and slaves rather than daily hired workers.
    Colossians 4:1 Masters, provide your slaves with what is just and equitable, knowing that you too have a Master in heaven.

Matthew 20:5
They went. Again he went out about the sixth and ninth hours and did the same.
  Matthew 20:3–20:4 — internal structural parallel: repeated hiring stages: The sixth- and ninth-hour notice says the owner did the same; the earlier third-hour round supplies the described going out, finding workers and inviting them with a just-payment promise. This is a specific internal antecedent for the repeated action. The hours stay different, and the link does not supply an unstated verbatim speech or establish an additional historical occurrence.
    Matthew 20:3 And going out about the third hour, he saw others standing idle in the marketplace;
    Matthew 20:4 and to them he said, ‘You also go into the vineyard, and whatever is just I will give you.’

Matthew 20:6
About the eleventh hour he went out and found others standing, and said to them, ‘Why have you stood here idle the whole day?’
  Matthew 20:3 — internal structural parallel: repeated hiring stages: Both rounds show the owner going out and finding others standing idle. The last round adds the eleventh hour and the all-day question, whereas the earlier round gives the third hour and marketplace. The repeated hiring pattern is explicit, without making their times identical or inferring the workers' motives before their reply.
    Matthew 20:3 And going out about the third hour, he saw others standing idle in the marketplace;

Matthew 20:7
They said to him, ‘Because no one hired us.’ He said to them, ‘You also go into the vineyard.’
  Matthew 20:4 — internal structural parallel: repeated hiring stages: The owner's You also go into the vineyard invitation recurs in the earlier hiring round. The final workers first explain that nobody hired them; that reply is absent from the earlier verse, which instead states a just-payment promise. This is a repeated invitation within the parable, not independent testimony or an equation of every spoken detail.
    Matthew 20:4 and to them he said, ‘You also go into the vineyard, and whatever is just I will give you.’

Matthew 20:8
When evening came, the master of the vineyard said to his steward, ‘Call the workers and pay them the wage, beginning with the last up to the first.’
  Leviticus 19:13 — thematic parallel: The owner orders payment at evening. Leviticus forbids keeping a hired worker's wages overnight; the shared point is paying rather than detaining the worker's wages until morning.
    Leviticus 19:13 You shall not wrong your neighbour, neither shall you rob; and the wages of the hired worker shall in no wise remain with you overnight until morning.

Matthew 20:9
Those hired about the eleventh hour came and received a denarius each.
  Matthew 20:2 — narrative parallel: This same-chapter comparison shows even the last-hired workers receiving the denarius agreed with the first workers for the full day. The repeated amount establishes the surprising equality of payment.
    Matthew 20:2 And having agreed with the labourers for a denarius for the day, he sent them into his vineyard.

Matthew 20:10
When the first came, they supposed they would receive more, but they too received a denarius each.
  Matthew 20:9 — narrative parallel: The same chapter gives the eleventh-hour workers and the first workers a denarius each. The repeated payment contrasts the first workers' expectation of more with the equal amount actually distributed.
    Matthew 20:9 Those hired about the eleventh hour came and received a denarius each.

Matthew 20:11
On receiving it, they grumbled against the householder,
  Luke 15:28–15:30 — thematic parallel: The early workers resent generous treatment of latecomers, comparable to the older son's anger at his father's welcome of the returning brother. These are different parables about resentment of another's benefit.
    Luke 15:28 But he became angry and was unwilling to enter. His father came out and pleaded with him.
    Luke 15:29 But he answered his father, ‘Behold, all these years I have served you as a slave, and I never disobeyed your command; yet you never gave me a young goat so that I might be glad with my friends.
    Luke 15:30 But when this son of yours came, who devoured your livelihood with prostitutes, you slaughtered the fattened calf for him!’

Matthew 20:12
saying, ‘These last worked one hour, and you made them equal to us who bore the day’s burden and scorching heat.’
  Luke 15:29–15:30 — thematic parallel: Both complaints contrast long service with favorable treatment of someone considered less deserving. The workers cite the day's heat; the older son cites years of obedience before the brother's feast.
    Luke 15:29 But he answered his father, ‘Behold, all these years I have served you as a slave, and I never disobeyed your command; yet you never gave me a young goat so that I might be glad with my friends.
    Luke 15:30 But when this son of yours came, who devoured your livelihood with prostitutes, you slaughtered the fattened calf for him!’

Matthew 20:13
He answered one of them, ‘Friend, I am not wronging you. Did you not agree with me for a denarius?
  Matthew 20:2 — narrative parallel: The householder explicitly recalls the same chapter's original denarius agreement. That earlier contract supplies the stated basis for denying that the first worker has been wronged.
    Matthew 20:2 And having agreed with the labourers for a denarius for the day, he sent them into his vineyard.

Matthew 20:14
Take what is yours and go. I wish to give to this last one the same as to you.
  Luke 15:31–15:32 — thematic parallel: Both householder and father defend generosity toward the late or returning recipient while answering a resentful earlier claimant. The father cites the brother's restoration rather than a wage contract.
    Luke 15:31 And he said to him, ‘Child, you are always with me, and everything of mine is yours.
    Luke 15:32 But we had to be glad and rejoice, because this brother of yours was dead and came to life; he was lost and was found.’”

Matthew 20:15
Am I not permitted to do what I wish with what is mine? Or is your eye evil because I am good?’
  Deuteronomy 15:9 — thematic parallel: Both use an evil eye in a monetary setting to expose begrudging another's benefit. Deuteronomy warns against withholding help from a poor brother; Matthew rebukes envy of the owner's generosity, a different financial situation.
    Deuteronomy 15:9 Take heed unto yourself, lest there be a hidden, lawless thought in your heart, saying: The seventh year, the year of release, draws near; and your eye be evil toward your needy brother, and you give him nothing, and he cry against you unto the Lord, and there be great sin upon you.

Matthew 20:16
So the last will be first, and the first last.”
  Mark 10:31 — teaching parallel: Both state the first-and-last reversal. Matthew concludes the worker parable; Mark places the saying at the end of the preceding teaching on discipleship and possessions.
    Mark 10:31 But many who are first will be last, and the last first.”

Matthew 20:17
As Jesus went up to Jerusalem, He took the twelve disciples privately, and on the road He said to them,
  Mark 10:32 — narrative parallel: Both set the passion prediction on the road up to Jerusalem and have Jesus take the Twelve aside. Mark adds the followers' astonishment and fear.
    Mark 10:32 They were on the road going up to Jerusalem. Jesus was going ahead of them, and they were astounded, while those following were afraid. Taking the Twelve aside again, He began telling them what was going to happen to Him:

Matthew 20:18
“Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and the Son of Man will be handed over to the chief priests and scribes. They will condemn Him to death
  Mark 10:33 — teaching parallel: Both predict betrayal of the Son of Man to chief priests and scribes, who will condemn Him to death, during the Jerusalem journey.
    Mark 10:33 “Look, we are going up to Jerusalem, and the Son of Man will be handed over to the chief priests and the scribes. They will condemn Him to death and hand Him over to the Gentiles.

Matthew 20:19
and hand Him over to the Gentiles to mock, flog, and crucify Him; and on the third day He will be raised.”
  Mark 10:34 — teaching parallel: Both predict Gentile abuse, flogging, death, and resurrection. Matthew names crucifixion and on the third day; Mark says they will kill Him and He will rise after three days.
    Mark 10:34 They will mock Him, spit on Him, flog Him, and kill Him, and after three days He will rise.”

Matthew 20:20
Then the mother of Zebedee’s sons came to Him with her sons, bowing before Him and asking something from Him.
  Mark 10:35 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a request associated with Zebedee's sons. Matthew has their mother approach with them and ask; Mark reports James and John themselves making the request.
    Mark 10:35 James and John, the sons of Zebedee, approached Him and said to Him, “Teacher, we want Thee to do for us whatever we ask Thee.”

Matthew 20:21
He said to her, “What do you wish?” She said to Him, “Command that these two sons of mine sit, one at Thy right and one at Thy left, in Thy kingdom.”
  Mark 10:36–10:37 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus ask what is wanted and hear a request for seats at His right and left. Matthew names the kingdom and the mother speaks; Mark names glory and the sons speak.
    Mark 10:36 He said to them, “What do you want Me to do for you?”
    Mark 10:37 They said to Him, “Grant us to sit, one at Thy right and one at Thy left, in Thy glory.”

Matthew 20:22
Jesus answered, “You do not know what you ask. Are you able to drink the cup that I am about to drink?” They said to Him, “We are able.”
  Mark 10:38–10:39 — teaching parallel: Both challenge the requesters with drinking Jesus' cup and record their claim that they can. Mark also includes a baptism image absent from Matthew's supplied verse.
    Mark 10:38 Jesus said to them, “You do not know what you are asking. Can you drink the cup that I drink, or be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized?”
    Mark 10:39 They said to Him, “We can.” Jesus said to them, “You will drink the cup that I drink, and you will be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized.

Matthew 20:23
He said to them, “You will indeed drink My cup, but to sit at My right and left is not Mine to give, except to those for whom it has been prepared by My Father.”
  Mark 10:39–10:40 — teaching parallel: Both affirm sharing Jesus' cup but assign the right and left seats to those for whom they are prepared. Matthew explicitly names the Father as their preparer.
    Mark 10:39 They said to Him, “We can.” Jesus said to them, “You will drink the cup that I drink, and you will be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized.
    Mark 10:40 But to sit at My right or at My left is not Mine to give; it is for those for whom it has been prepared.”

Matthew 20:24
When the ten heard, they became indignant about the two brothers.
  Mark 10:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe the ten becoming indignant with James and John after hearing the request for privileged places. Matthew calls them the two brothers.
    Mark 10:41 When the ten heard, they began to be indignant with James and John.

Matthew 20:25
Jesus summoned them and said, “You know that the rulers of the nations lord it over them, and their great ones exercise authority over them.
  Mark 10:42 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the disciples' community with rulers of nations lording it over others and great ones exercising authority over them.
    Mark 10:42 Jesus called them to Him and said to them, “You know that those regarded as rulers of the nations lord it over them, and their great ones exercise authority over them.

Matthew 20:26
It shall not be so among you. Instead, whoever wishes to become great among you will be your servant,
  Mark 10:43 — teaching parallel: Both reject domination among disciples and define becoming great in their community through becoming a servant.
    Mark 10:43 But it is not so among you. Whoever wants to become great among you shall be your servant,

Matthew 20:27
and whoever wishes to be first among you will be your slave,
  Mark 10:44 — teaching parallel: Both define the desire to be first through slavery or service to others. Mark says slave of all; Matthew says your slave.
    Mark 10:44 and whoever wants to be first among you shall be slave of all.

Matthew 20:28
just as the Son of Man did not come to be served, but to serve and to give His life as a ransom in place of many.”
  Mark 10:45 — teaching parallel: Both ground servant leadership in the Son of Man coming to serve rather than be served and giving His life as a ransom for many.
    Mark 10:45 For even the Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve, and to give His life as a ransom in exchange for many.”

Matthew 20:29
As they went out from Jericho, a great crowd followed Him.
  Mark 10:46 — narrative parallel: Both place the blind-healing narrative as Jesus leaves Jericho with a considerable crowd. Mark also names the blind beggar Bartimaeus.
    Mark 10:46 They came to Jericho. As He was leaving Jericho with His disciples and a considerable crowd, Bartimaeus, the son of Timaeus, a blind beggar, was sitting beside the road.

Matthew 20:30
And behold, two blind men sitting beside the road heard that Jesus was passing by and cried out, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”
  Mark 10:47 — narrative parallel: Both have roadside blind petitioners invoke Jesus as Son of David and ask mercy. Matthew depicts two blind men; Mark centers on the named Bartimaeus.
    Mark 10:47 Hearing that it was Jesus the Nazarene, he began crying out, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Matthew 20:31
The crowd rebuked them to make them silent, but they cried out all the more, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”
  Mark 10:48 — narrative parallel: Both have crowds attempt to silence blind petitioners, who then cry for the Son of David's mercy more loudly. Matthew has two men; Mark narrates one.
    Mark 10:48 Many rebuked him so that he would be silent, but he cried out much more, “Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Matthew 20:32
Jesus stopped, called them, and said, “What do you wish Me to do for you?”
  Mark 10:49–10:51 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus stop, call the blind petitioner or petitioners, and ask what they want Him to do. Mark elaborates Bartimaeus's calling and approach; Matthew addresses two men.
    Mark 10:49 Jesus stopped and said, “Call him.” They called the blind man, saying to him, “Take courage! Get up; He is calling you.”
    Mark 10:50 He threw off his garment, sprang up, and came to Jesus.
    Mark 10:51 Jesus answered him, “What do you want Me to do for you?” The blind man said to Him, “Rabbouni, that I may see again.”

Matthew 20:33
They said to Him, “Lord, that our eyes may be opened.”
  Mark 10:51 — narrative parallel: Both requests ask for restored sight. Matthew's two men ask that their eyes be opened; Mark's blind man asks to see again.
    Mark 10:51 Jesus answered him, “What do you want Me to do for you?” The blind man said to Him, “Rabbouni, that I may see again.”

Matthew 20:34
Moved with compassion, Jesus touched their eyes. Immediately they received sight and followed Him.
  Mark 10:52 — narrative parallel: Both conclude with immediate sight and following Jesus. Matthew includes compassion and touching two men's eyes; Mark emphasizes one man's faith and does not mention that touch.
    Mark 10:52 Jesus said to him, “Go; your faith has saved you.” Immediately he saw again and followed Him on the road.

Matthew 21:1
When they drew near Jerusalem and came to Bethphage, to the Mount of Olives, Jesus sent two disciples,
  Mark 11:1 — narrative parallel: Both place the approach to Jerusalem near Bethphage and the Mount of Olives and have Jesus send two disciples. Mark also names Bethany.
    Mark 11:1 As they drew near Jerusalem, to Bethphage and Bethany at the Mount of Olives, He sent two of His disciples

Matthew 21:2
saying to them, “Go into the village opposite you, and immediately you will find a donkey tied up and a colt with her. Untie them and bring them to Me.
  Mark 11:2 — narrative parallel: Both send disciples into the opposite village to untie and bring a mount. Matthew describes a donkey and her colt; Mark describes one tied colt on which nobody has sat.
    Mark 11:2 and said to them, “Go into the village opposite you. Immediately on entering it, you will find a colt tied, on which no human has ever sat. Untie it and bring it.

Matthew 21:3
If anyone says anything to you, say, ‘The Lord has need of them,’ and immediately he will send them.”
  Mark 11:3 — narrative parallel: Both supply the Lord's need as the answer to a challenge about taking the mount. Matthew uses the plural; Mark concerns one colt and mentions sending it back.
    Mark 11:3 If anyone says to you, ‘Why are you doing this?’ say, ‘The Lord has need of it, and He will send it back here immediately.’”

Matthew 21:4
This happened so that what was spoken through the prophet might be fulfilled:
  Zechariah 9:9 — promise and fulfillment: The fulfillment introduction leads into the following Zion-and-king quotation. Zechariah supplies the royal-mount oracle; the link reads Matthew's introduction together with 21:5.
    Zechariah 9:9 Rejoice greatly, daughter Zion; proclaim, daughter Jerusalem. Behold, your king comes to you, righteous and saving, gentle, and mounted upon a beast of burden and a young colt.

Matthew 21:5
“Tell the daughter of Zion: ‘Behold, your King comes to you, meek and mounted upon a donkey, and upon a colt, the foal of a beast of burden.’”
  Zechariah 9:9 — quotation: Both present Zion's king as gentle and mounted on a beast of burden and colt. Matthew's introductory wording and animal description remain distinct rather than every clause being treated as verbatim repetition.
    Zechariah 9:9 Rejoice greatly, daughter Zion; proclaim, daughter Jerusalem. Behold, your king comes to you, righteous and saving, gentle, and mounted upon a beast of burden and a young colt.

Matthew 21:6
The disciples went and did as Jesus directed them.
  Mark 11:4–11:6 — narrative parallel: Matthew summarizes disciples obeying Jesus' directions; Mark narrates finding and untying the colt and answering as instructed. The accounts differ in the number of animals.
    Mark 11:4 They went away and found a colt tied at a door outside on the street, and untied it.
    Mark 11:5 Some of those standing there said to them, “What are you doing, untying the colt?”
    Mark 11:6 They answered them just as Jesus had said, and the people let them go.

Matthew 21:7
They brought the donkey and the colt, placed the garments upon them, and He sat upon them.
  Mark 11:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe bringing the mount, placing garments upon it, and Jesus sitting upon it. Matthew includes donkey and colt; Mark narrates only the colt.
    Mark 11:7 They brought the colt to Jesus, threw their garments on it, and He sat on it.

Matthew 21:8
Most of the crowd spread their garments on the road, while others cut branches from the trees and spread them on the road.
  Mark 11:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe garments on the road and cut vegetation laid out for Jesus' arrival. Matthew specifies tree branches; Mark speaks of leafy branches cut from fields.
    Mark 11:8 Many spread their garments on the road; others spread leafy branches they had cut from the fields.

Matthew 21:9
The crowds going before Him and those following cried out, “Hosanna to the Son of David! Blessed is the One coming in the name of the Lord! Hosanna in the highest!”
  Mark 11:9–11:10 — narrative parallel: Both crowds cry Hosanna, bless the One coming in the Lord's name, and invoke Davidic kingship. Matthew names the Son of David; Mark names David's coming kingdom.
    Mark 11:9 Those going ahead and those following cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord!
    Mark 11:10 Blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David! Hosanna in the highest!”

Matthew 21:10
When He entered Jerusalem, the whole city was shaken, saying, “Who is this?”
  Matthew 2:3 — thematic parallel: Both associate Jesus' kingship with a citywide Jerusalem reaction. The birth account describes Herod and Jerusalem troubled; the entry account describes the city shaken and asking His identity.
    Matthew 2:3 When King Herod heard this, he was troubled, and all Jerusalem with him.

Matthew 21:11
The crowds said, “This is the prophet Jesus, from Nazareth of Galilee.”
  John 1:45 — thematic parallel: Both identify Jesus with Nazareth. Philip names Jesus son of Joseph from Nazareth; Matthew's crowd calls Him the prophet from Nazareth of Galilee. The speakers and occasions differ.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”

Matthew 21:12
Jesus entered the temple and cast out all those selling and buying in the temple. He overturned the tables of the money changers and the seats of those selling doves.
  Mark 11:15 — narrative parallel: Both recount Jesus driving out temple buyers and sellers and overturning the money changers' tables and dove sellers' seats.
    Mark 11:15 They came to Jerusalem. He entered the temple and began driving out those selling and those buying in the temple. He overturned the tables of the money changers and the seats of those selling doves,

Matthew 21:13
He said to them, “It is written, ‘My house will be called a house of prayer,’ but you make it a robbers’ cave.”
  Mark 11:17 — teaching parallel: Both combine the house-of-prayer saying with the accusation of a robbers' den or cave. Mark explicitly includes for all the nations in the prayer-house clause.
    Mark 11:17 He taught and said to them, “Is it not written, ‘My house shall be called a house of prayer for all the nations’? But you have made it a den of robbers.”

Matthew 21:14
Blind and lame people came to Him in the temple, and He healed them.
  Isaiah 35:5–35:6 — thematic parallel: Matthew narrates healing blind and lame people; Isaiah's restoration imagery promises opened blind eyes and a lame person leaping. This is a healing correspondence, not a claim Matthew explicitly quotes Isaiah here.
    Isaiah 35:5 Then blind people’s eyes will open, and deaf people’s ears will hear.
    Isaiah 35:6 Then the lame person will leap like a deer, and the tongue of those with difficulty speaking will be clear, for water burst out in the wilderness and a ravine in a thirsty land,

Matthew 21:15
But when the chief priests and scribes saw the wonders He did and the children crying in the temple, “Hosanna to the Son of David,” they became indignant
  Luke 19:39–19:40 — thematic parallel: Both passages portray religious opponents objecting to praise of Jesus. Matthew describes chief priests and scribes indignant at children in the temple; Luke has Pharisees ask Jesus to rebuke disciples during the entry. This compares separate praise controversies without identifying them as the same exchange.
    Luke 19:39 And some of the Pharisees from the crowd said to Him, “Teacher, rebuke Thy disciples.”
    Luke 19:40 And He answered, “I tell you, if these fall silent, the stones will cry out.”

Matthew 21:16
and said to Him, “Dost Thou hear what these are saying?” Jesus said to them, “Yes. Have you never read, ‘Out of the mouth of infants and nursing children Thou didst prepare praise’?”
  Psalms 8:3 — quotation: The words about prepared praise from infants and nursing children repeat distinctive wording of this edition's Greek-numbered Psalm 8:3. Jesus uses it to answer objections to the children's praise.
    Psalms 8:3 From the mouth of infants and nursing children Thou didst prepare praise, because of Thine enemies, to bring down the enemy and the avenger.

Matthew 21:17
Leaving them, He went outside the city to Bethany and spent the night there.
  Mark 11:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus leaving Jerusalem for Bethany. Matthew places this departure after the temple cleansing and temple discussion, while Mark places his departure before his narrated cleansing. Matthew mentions staying the night; Mark mentions the Twelve. The shared travel details do not establish an identical immediate sequence.
    Mark 11:11 He entered Jerusalem, into the temple. After looking around at everything, since the hour was already late, He went out to Bethany with the Twelve.

Matthew 21:18
In the morning, as He returned to the city, He became hungry.
  Mark 11:12 — narrative parallel: Both begin the fig-tree episode with Jesus hungry as He travels from Bethany toward Jerusalem on the following morning or day.
    Mark 11:12 On the next day, as they came out from Bethany, He became hungry.

Matthew 21:19
Seeing a fig tree beside the road, He came to it and found nothing on it except leaves alone. He said to it, “Let no fruit ever come from you again.” Immediately the fig tree withered.
  Mark 11:13–11:14 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus find a leafy fig tree without fruit and pronounce no future fruit. Matthew reports immediate withering; Mark's discovery of withering occurs later.
    Mark 11:13 Seeing from far away a fig tree with leaves, He came to see whether He might find anything on it. When He reached it, He found nothing but leaves, for it was not the season for figs.
    Mark 11:14 He addressed it, “May no one ever eat fruit from you again.” His disciples heard it.

Matthew 21:20
When the disciples saw it, they marveled, saying, “How did the fig tree wither immediately?”
  Mark 11:20–11:21 — narrative parallel: Both have disciples notice the cursed tree's withering. Matthew asks about its immediacy; Mark has Peter recall the curse when they pass the tree in the morning.
    Mark 11:20 Passing by in the morning, they saw the fig tree withered from the roots.
    Mark 11:21 Peter remembered and said to Him, “Rabbi, look! The fig tree that Thou didst curse has withered.”

Matthew 21:21
Jesus answered them, “Truly I tell you, if you have faith and do not doubt, you will do not only what was done to the fig tree; even if you say to this mountain, ‘Be lifted up and cast into the sea,’ it will happen.
  Mark 11:22–11:23 — teaching parallel: Both connect undoubting faith with commanding a mountain to be cast into the sea. Matthew explicitly connects the assurance with the fig tree.
    Mark 11:22 Jesus answered them, “Have faith in God.
    Mark 11:23 Truly I tell you, whoever says to this mountain, ‘Be lifted up and thrown into the sea,’ and does not doubt in his heart, but believes that what he says is happening, it will be his.

Matthew 21:22
Everything you ask in prayer, believing, you will receive.”
  Mark 11:24 — teaching parallel: Both associate believing prayer with receiving requests. Mark formulates it as believing that the requested things have already been received.
    Mark 11:24 Therefore I tell you, everything you pray and ask for—believe that you have received it, and it will be yours.

Matthew 21:23
When He entered the temple, the chief priests and elders of the people approached Him as He taught, saying, “By what authority dost Thou do these things? And who gave Thee this authority?”
  Mark 11:27–11:28 — narrative parallel: Both have religious authorities confront Jesus in the temple about His authority. Matthew mentions chief priests and elders; Mark also includes scribes.
    Mark 11:27 They came again to Jerusalem. As He walked in the temple, the chief priests, the scribes, and the elders came to Him
    Mark 11:28 and said to Him, “By what authority art Thou doing these things? Or who gave Thee this authority to do them?”

Matthew 21:24
Jesus answered them, “I too will ask you one thing. If you tell Me, I too will tell you by what authority I do these things.
  Mark 11:29 — teaching parallel: Both answer the authority challenge by asking a prior question and making Jesus' answer conditional on their answering that question.
    Mark 11:29 Jesus said to them, “I will ask you one question. Answer Me, and I will tell you by what authority I do these things.

Matthew 21:25
John’s baptism—from where was it? From heaven or from human beings?” They reasoned among themselves, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say to us, ‘Why, then, did you not believe him?’
  Mark 11:30–11:31 — narrative parallel: Both ask whether John's baptism came from heaven or humans and describe the authorities' reasoning that affirming heaven would invite a challenge about their failure to believe John.
    Mark 11:30 Was John’s baptism from heaven or from humans? Answer Me.”
    Mark 11:31 They reasoned with one another, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why then did you not believe him?’

Matthew 21:26
But if we say, ‘From human beings,’ we fear the crowd, for all regard John as a prophet.”
  Mark 11:32 — narrative parallel: Both describe fear of the crowd if the authorities call John's baptism human, because the people regard John as a prophet.
    Mark 11:32 But should we say, ‘From humans’?” They feared the crowd, for everyone held that John truly was a prophet.

Matthew 21:27
They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” He also said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.
  Mark 11:33 — narrative parallel: Both conclude with the authorities saying they do not know and Jesus consequently refusing to disclose the source of His own authority.
    Mark 11:33 They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”

Matthew 21:28
But what do you think? A man had two children. He came to the first and said, ‘Child, go work in the vineyard today.’
  Luke 15:11 — thematic parallel: Both introduce a father and two children as a parable's framework. The plots differ: Matthew tests responses to a work command; Luke develops the younger son's departure and older son's response.
    Luke 15:11 And He said, “A certain man had two sons.

Matthew 21:29
He answered, ‘I do not want to,’ but afterward he changed his mind and went.
  Luke 15:18–15:20 — thematic parallel: Both have a child's changed intention become actual movement. Luke's son resolves to return and rises and comes to his father; Matthew's first child reverses refusal and goes to work.
    Luke 15:18 I will rise and go to my father and say to him, “Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you.
    Luke 15:19 I am no longer worthy to be called your son. Make me like one of your hired workers.”’
    Luke 15:20 And he rose and came to his own father. But while he was still far away, his father saw him and was moved with compassion. He ran, fell upon his neck, and kissed him tenderly.

Matthew 21:30
He came to the second and said the same. He answered, ‘I will, sir,’ but did not go.
  Luke 6:46 — teaching parallel: The second child gives respectful assent without acting; Luke challenges calling Jesus Lord while not doing what He says. Both distinguish verbal profession from action.
    Luke 6:46 Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?

Matthew 21:31
Which of the two did the father’s will?” They said, “The first.” Jesus said to them, “Truly I tell you that tax collectors and prostitutes go ahead of you into the kingdom of God.
  Luke 7:29–7:30 — thematic parallel: Matthew contrasts tax collectors entering ahead of the opponents with the obedient child. Luke likewise contrasts tax collectors responding to John's baptism with Pharisees and law experts rejecting God's purpose.
    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.
    Luke 7:30 But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.

Matthew 21:32
For John came to you in the way of righteousness and you did not believe him, but tax collectors and prostitutes believed him. Even after seeing this, you did not afterward change your minds and believe him.
  Luke 7:29–7:30 — narrative parallel: Both explicitly contrast tax collectors' positive response to John with the opponents' refusal. Matthew stresses belief and failure to change one's mind; Luke describes accepting or refusing baptism.
    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.
    Luke 7:30 But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.

Matthew 21:33
Hear another parable. A householder planted a vineyard, put a fence around it, dug a winepress in it, and built a tower. He leased it to farmers and traveled abroad.
  Mark 12:1 — teaching parallel: Both open the tenant parable with planting a vineyard, fencing it, digging a winepress, building a tower, leasing it, and traveling abroad.
    Mark 12:1 He began speaking to them in parables: “A man planted a vineyard, put a fence around it, dug a winepress vat, and built a tower. He leased it to tenant farmers and went abroad.

Matthew 21:34
When the season of fruits drew near, he sent his slaves to the farmers to receive his fruits.
  Mark 12:2 — teaching parallel: Both have the owner send servants at fruit season to collect produce. Matthew's first mission is plural; Mark initially describes one slave.
    Mark 12:2 At the proper season he sent a slave to the farmers, to receive from them some of the vineyard’s fruit.

Matthew 21:35
The farmers seized his slaves: one they beat, another they killed, another they stoned.
  Mark 12:3–12:5 — teaching parallel: Both describe tenants attacking the owner's messengers through beatings and killings. Matthew includes stoning and groups the initial slaves; Mark narrates successive individual attacks.
    Mark 12:3 They seized him, beat him, and sent him away empty-handed.
    Mark 12:4 Again he sent another slave to them; they struck that one on the head and dishonored him.
    Mark 12:5 He sent another, and they killed that one; and many others—some they beat, and others they killed.

Matthew 21:36
Again he sent other slaves, more than the first, and they did the same to them.
  Mark 12:4–12:5 — teaching parallel: Both have further dispatched messengers meet continued violence. Matthew summarizes a larger second group; Mark describes individuals and many others.
    Mark 12:4 Again he sent another slave to them; they struck that one on the head and dishonored him.
    Mark 12:5 He sent another, and they killed that one; and many others—some they beat, and others they killed.

Matthew 21:37
Last he sent his son to them, saying, ‘They will respect my son.’
  Mark 12:6 — teaching parallel: Both make the owner's son the final messenger and give his hope that the tenants will respect him. Mark calls the son beloved.
    Mark 12:6 He still had one, a beloved son. He sent him to them last, saying, ‘They will respect my son.’

Matthew 21:38
But when the farmers saw the son, they said among themselves, ‘This is the heir. Come, let us kill him and take his inheritance.’
  Mark 12:7 — teaching parallel: Both have tenants identify the son as heir and conspire to kill him to obtain the inheritance.
    Mark 12:7 But those farmers said to one another, ‘This is the heir. Come, let us kill him, and the inheritance will be ours.’

Matthew 21:39
They seized him, cast him outside the vineyard, and killed him.
  Mark 12:8 — teaching parallel: Both narrate the son's seizure, death, and exclusion from the vineyard. Matthew places casting him outside before killing him; Mark places killing before throwing him outside.
    Mark 12:8 They seized him, killed him, and threw him outside the vineyard.

Matthew 21:40
So when the master of the vineyard comes, what will he do to those farmers?”
  Mark 12:9 — teaching parallel: Both ask what the owner will do after his son's death. Mark immediately supplies the answer in Jesus' telling; Matthew asks the listeners first.
    Mark 12:9 What, then, will the owner of the vineyard do? He will come and destroy the farmers and give the vineyard to others.

Matthew 21:41
They said to Him, “He will miserably destroy those miserable men, and lease the vineyard to other farmers who will give him the fruits in their seasons.”
  Mark 12:9 — teaching parallel: Both answer that the owner destroys the tenants and gives the vineyard to others. Matthew specifies that the replacements will produce fruit in season.
    Mark 12:9 What, then, will the owner of the vineyard do? He will come and destroy the farmers and give the vineyard to others.

Matthew 21:42
Jesus said to them, “Have you never read in the Scriptures: ‘The stone that the builders rejected became the head of the corner; this came from the Lord, and it is marvelous in our eyes’?
  Mark 12:10–12:11 — quotation: Both quote the builders' rejected stone becoming the cornerstone and the Lord's marvelous action. Matthew gives the full quotation in one verse; Mark divides it into two.
    Mark 12:10 Have you not even read this Scripture: ‘The stone that the builders rejected has become the head of the corner.
    Mark 12:11 This came from the Lord, and it is marvelous in our eyes’?”

Matthew 21:43
Therefore I tell you that the kingdom of God will be taken from you and given to a people producing its fruits.
  Luke 20:16 — thematic parallel: Luke's vineyard parable describes transfer to others; Matthew explicitly applies transfer to the kingdom and a fruit-producing people. Luke's concrete wording does not itself name the kingdom or specify replacement ethnic groups.
    Luke 20:16 He will come and destroy those farmers and give the vineyard to others.” When they heard this, they said, “May it never happen!”

Matthew 21:44
And the one falling upon this stone will be shattered; but on whomever it falls, it will crush him.”
  Luke 20:18 — teaching parallel: Both say falling upon the stone shatters a person and the stone falling on someone crushes him. The two directions of the image correspond closely.
    Luke 20:18 Everyone who falls upon that stone will be shattered; but whoever it falls upon, it will crush him.”

Matthew 21:45
When the chief priests and Pharisees heard His parables, they knew He was speaking about them.
  Mark 12:12 — narrative parallel: Both report the religious opponents recognizing that Jesus directed the parable against them. Mark connects that recognition with their wish to seize Him and fear of the crowd.
    Mark 12:12 They sought to seize Him, but feared the crowd, for they knew that He had spoken the parable against them. They left Him and went away.

Matthew 21:46
Although they sought to seize Him, they feared the crowds, because they regarded Him as a prophet.
  Mark 12:12 — narrative parallel: Both have opponents wanting to seize Jesus but restrained by fear of the crowd. Matthew additionally identifies the crowd's regard for Him as a prophet.
    Mark 12:12 They sought to seize Him, but feared the crowd, for they knew that He had spoken the parable against them. They left Him and went away.

Matthew 22:1
Jesus again answered them in parables, saying,
