  Luke 14:15–14:16 — thematic parallel: Both introduce a banquet parable connected with God's kingdom. Matthew again addresses opponents; Luke tells the story at a meal after a guest's kingdom remark.
    Luke 14:15 When one of those reclining at table heard these things, he said to Him, “Blessed is whoever shall eat bread in the kingdom of God!”
    Luke 14:16 But He said to him, “A certain man was giving a great supper, and he invited many.

Matthew 22:2
“The kingdom of the heavens was likened to a king who made a wedding feast for his son.
  Luke 14:16 — teaching parallel: Both picture a host preparing a great meal and inviting guests. Matthew specifies a king's wedding feast for his son; Luke names a man giving a great supper.
    Luke 14:16 But He said to him, “A certain man was giving a great supper, and he invited many.

Matthew 22:3
He sent his slaves to call those invited to the wedding feast, but they were unwilling to come.
  Luke 14:17–14:18 — teaching parallel: Both have a host summon previously invited people through a servant or servants, meeting refusal or excuses. Matthew says they were unwilling; Luke starts listing excuses.
    Luke 14:17 At the hour of the supper he sent his slave to tell those who had been invited, ‘Come, because things are now ready.’
    Luke 14:18 And they all alike began to excuse themselves. The first said to him, ‘I bought a field, and I need to go out and see it. I ask you to excuse me.’

Matthew 22:4
Again he sent other slaves, saying, ‘Tell those invited: Behold, I have prepared my meal. My bulls and fattened animals have been slaughtered, and everything is ready. Come to the wedding feast.’
  Luke 14:17 — teaching parallel: Both invitations announce the meal's readiness and urge the invitees to come. Matthew includes slaughtered cattle and a renewed dispatch of slaves.
    Luke 14:17 At the hour of the supper he sent his slave to tell those who had been invited, ‘Come, because things are now ready.’

Matthew 22:5
But they disregarded it and went away, one to his own field, another to his business.
  Luke 14:18–14:20 — teaching parallel: Both depict invited people preferring ordinary pursuits to the feast. Matthew gives field and business; Luke gives a field, oxen, and a new marriage.
    Luke 14:18 And they all alike began to excuse themselves. The first said to him, ‘I bought a field, and I need to go out and see it. I ask you to excuse me.’
    Luke 14:19 And another said, ‘I bought five pairs of oxen, and I am going to test them. I ask you to excuse me.’
    Luke 14:20 And another said, ‘I married a wife, and for this reason I cannot come.’

Matthew 22:6
The rest seized his slaves, mistreated them, and killed them.
  Matthew 21:35 — thematic parallel: The neighboring vineyard parable likewise has the master's messengers seized, mistreated, and killed. This same-book comparison concerns different parables, not the same group of historical victims.
    Matthew 21:35 The farmers seized his slaves: one they beat, another they killed, another they stoned.

Matthew 22:7
The king became angry. He sent his troops, destroyed those murderers, and burned their city.
  Luke 19:27 — thematic parallel: Both parables depict rulers violently judging rejection of their rule or messengers. Matthew specifies troops and a burned city; Luke specifies execution before the ruler, without city-burning.
    Luke 19:27 But those enemies of mine who did not want me to reign over them—bring them here and slaughter them before me.’”

Matthew 22:8
Then he said to his slaves, ‘The wedding feast is ready, but those invited were not worthy.
  Luke 14:24 — teaching parallel: Both banquet stories exclude original invitees after their refusal. Matthew calls them unworthy; Luke says none of those invited men will taste the supper.
    Luke 14:24 For I tell you, none of those men who were invited will taste my supper.’”

Matthew 22:9
So go to the outlets of the roads and invite to the wedding feast as many as you find.’
  Luke 14:21–14:23 — teaching parallel: Both replace the original invitees by sending servants into streets or roads for new guests. Luke includes the poor and disabled and extends the search until the house is filled.
    Luke 14:21 The slave returned and reported these things to his master. Then the master of the house became angry and said to his slave, ‘Go out quickly into the streets and lanes of the city, and bring the poor, the disabled, the blind, and the lame in here.’
    Luke 14:22 And the slave said, ‘Master, what you commanded has been done, and there is still room.’
    Luke 14:23 And the master said to the slave, ‘Go out into the roads and along the fences, and compel people to come in, so that my house may be filled.

Matthew 22:10
Those slaves went out into the roads and gathered everyone they found, both evil and good, and the wedding feast was filled with people reclining.
  Luke 14:21–14:23 — teaching parallel: Both describe widened invitations intended to fill the banquet. Matthew explicitly gathers evil and good; Luke names marginalized people and people along roads without repeating those moral categories.
    Luke 14:21 The slave returned and reported these things to his master. Then the master of the house became angry and said to his slave, ‘Go out quickly into the streets and lanes of the city, and bring the poor, the disabled, the blind, and the lame in here.’
    Luke 14:22 And the slave said, ‘Master, what you commanded has been done, and there is still room.’
    Luke 14:23 And the master said to the slave, ‘Go out into the roads and along the fences, and compel people to come in, so that my house may be filled.

Matthew 22:11
The king entered to see those reclining and saw there a man not clothed in a wedding garment.
  Matthew 22:12 — internal observation and question response: The king's observation of a man lacking a wedding garment is followed by his question repeating the missing garment and the guest's speechlessness. This is an internal observation/question response, not an independent garment account, material description or allegorical definition.
    Matthew 22:12 He said to him, ‘Friend, how did you enter here without a wedding garment?’ He was speechless.

Matthew 22:12
He said to him, ‘Friend, how did you enter here without a wedding garment?’ He was speechless.
  Matthew 22:11 — explicit internal referent connection: The preceding observation identifies the king, the guest and the missing wedding garment to which the question refers. This is an explicit internal referent connection. It supplies the question's scene without explaining the man's unspoken thoughts, the reason for his silence or an allegory for the garment.
    Matthew 22:11 The king entered to see those reclining and saw there a man not clothed in a wedding garment.

Matthew 22:13
Then the king said to the attendants, ‘Bind his feet and hands and cast him into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.’
  Matthew 8:12 — teaching parallel: Both use exclusion into outer darkness with weeping and grinding of teeth as judgment imagery. Matthew 22 additionally describes binding the rejected guest's hands and feet.
    Matthew 8:12 but the sons of the kingdom will be cast into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.”

Matthew 22:14
For many are called, but few chosen.”
  Luke 13:23–13:28 — thematic parallel: Matthew closes the feast account with many called and few chosen. Luke records a question about few being saved, followed by Jesus' warning that many will seek entry and not be able. Both confront restricted admission despite approach or invitation; Luke is not made a quotation of called/chosen terminology or a statistical salvation rate.
    Luke 13:23 Someone said to Him, “Lord, are those who are being saved few?” And He said to them,
    Luke 13:24 “Strive to enter through the narrow door, because many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able,
    Luke 13:25 once the master of the house rises and shuts the door. Then you will begin to stand outside and knock at the door, saying, ‘Lord, open to us!’ And he will answer you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from.’
    Luke 13:26 Then you will begin to say, ‘We ate and drank in your presence, and you taught in our streets.’
    Luke 13:27 And he will say to you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from. Depart from me, all you workers of unrighteousness!’
    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

Matthew 22:15
Then the Pharisees went and took counsel how to trap Him in a statement.
  Mark 12:13 — narrative parallel: Both introduce an attempt to trap Jesus in His speech. Matthew records Pharisees consulting; Mark records sending Pharisees and Herodians.
    Mark 12:13 They sent some of the Pharisees and the Herodians to Him, to catch Him in a word.

Matthew 22:16
They sent Him their disciples with the Herodians, saying, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost teach God’s way in truth. Thou art not swayed by anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward status.
  Mark 12:14 — narrative parallel: Both emissaries flatter Jesus as truthful, impartial, and a teacher of God's way. Matthew identifies Pharisaic disciples with Herodians as speakers.
    Mark 12:14 They came and said to Him, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost not defer to anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward standing, but dost teach God’s way in truth. Is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar or not? Should we give it or not?”

Matthew 22:17
Tell us, then, what Thou thinkest: is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”
  Mark 12:14 — narrative parallel: Both ask whether giving tribute to Caesar is lawful. Mark continues with the explicit question whether they should give it or not.
    Mark 12:14 They came and said to Him, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost not defer to anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward standing, but dost teach God’s way in truth. Is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar or not? Should we give it or not?”

Matthew 22:18
Knowing their evil intent, Jesus said, “Why do you test Me, hypocrites?
  Mark 12:15 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus recognize hostile or hypocritical intent and challenge why they test Him. Matthew calls them hypocrites; Mark describes His knowledge of their hypocrisy.
    Mark 12:15 Knowing their hypocrisy, He said to them, “Why are you testing Me? Bring Me a denarius so that I may see it.”

Matthew 22:19
Show Me the coin of the tribute.” And they brought Him a denarius.
  Mark 12:15–12:16 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus request a denarius and the questioners produce one. Matthew names the tribute coin; Mark asks for a denarius to inspect.
    Mark 12:15 Knowing their hypocrisy, He said to them, “Why are you testing Me? Bring Me a denarius so that I may see it.”
    Mark 12:16 They brought one. He said to them, “Whose image and inscription is this?” They said to Him, “Caesar’s.”

Matthew 22:20
And He says to them, “Whose image is this, and whose inscription?”
  Mark 12:16 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus ask whose image and inscription appear on the coin. Mark includes the reply Caesar's in the same verse.
    Mark 12:16 They brought one. He said to them, “Whose image and inscription is this?” They said to Him, “Caesar’s.”

Matthew 22:21
They say to Him, “Caesar’s.” Then He says to them, “Render therefore to Caesar the things of Caesar, and to God the things of God.”
  Mark 12:16–12:17 — teaching parallel: Both identify the image as Caesar's and give the paired directive to return Caesar's things to Caesar and God's things to God.
    Mark 12:16 They brought one. He said to them, “Whose image and inscription is this?” They said to Him, “Caesar’s.”
    Mark 12:17 Jesus said to them, “Give back Caesar’s things to Caesar, and God’s things to God.” They marveled greatly at Him.

Matthew 22:22
When they heard, they marveled, left Him, and went away.
  Mark 12:17 — narrative parallel: Both report marveling at Jesus' tribute answer. Matthew adds that they leave Him and go away.
    Mark 12:17 Jesus said to them, “Give back Caesar’s things to Caesar, and God’s things to God.” They marveled greatly at Him.

Matthew 22:23
On that day Sadducees, who say there is no resurrection, came to Him and questioned Him,
  Mark 12:18 — narrative parallel: Both identify the Sadducees as denying resurrection and introduce their questioning. The comparison preserves the named group and stated doctrinal issue.
    Mark 12:18 Sadducees, who say there is no resurrection, came to Him and questioned Him, saying,

Matthew 22:24
saying, “Teacher, Moses said, ‘If someone dies without children, his brother shall marry his wife and raise up offspring for his brother.’
  Mark 12:19 — quotation: Both cite the Mosaic levirate premise: a childless man's brother should marry the widow and raise offspring for him.
    Mark 12:19 “Teacher, Moses wrote for us that if someone’s brother dies and leaves a wife but no child, his brother should take the wife and raise up offspring for his brother.

Matthew 22:25
There were seven brothers among us. The first married and died, and having no offspring, left his wife to his brother.
  Mark 12:20 — narrative parallel: Both begin with seven brothers, the first marrying and dying without offspring. Matthew explicitly says he left his wife to his brother.
    Mark 12:20 There were seven brothers. The first took a wife, and died leaving no offspring.

Matthew 22:26
Likewise the second and the third, up to the seventh.
  Mark 12:21–12:22 — narrative parallel: Both extend the childless marriages through the other brothers, the second and third and eventually all seven. Mark repeats that none left offspring.
    Mark 12:21 The second took her and died leaving no offspring, and the third likewise.
    Mark 12:22 The seven left no offspring. Last of all, the woman also died.

Matthew 22:27
Last of all, the woman died.
  Mark 12:22 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the hypothetical case with the woman dying after the seven brothers. Mark includes the absence of offspring in the same verse.
    Mark 12:22 The seven left no offspring. Last of all, the woman also died.

Matthew 22:28
In the resurrection, then, whose wife of the seven will she be? For all had her.”
  Mark 12:23 — narrative parallel: Both ask whose wife the woman will be at the resurrection because all seven brothers had married her.
    Mark 12:23 In the resurrection, when they rise, whose wife will she be? For the seven had her as wife.”

Matthew 22:29
Jesus answered them, “You are mistaken, because you know neither the Scriptures nor the power of God.
  Mark 12:24 — teaching parallel: Both diagnose the Sadducees' error as ignorance of the Scriptures and God's power. Mark phrases it as a question.
    Mark 12:24 Jesus said to them, “Is this not why you are mistaken: you know neither the Scriptures nor God’s power?

Matthew 22:30
For in the resurrection they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in heaven.
  Mark 12:25 — teaching parallel: Both say resurrected people neither marry nor are given in marriage and are like angels in heaven. Neither text says humans become angels.
    Mark 12:25 For when they rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in the heavens.

Matthew 22:31
But concerning the resurrection of the dead, have you not read what was spoken to you by God:
  Mark 12:26 — teaching parallel: Both introduce God's speech in Moses as evidence about resurrection. Mark identifies the burning-bush passage before quoting the patriarchal names.
    Mark 12:26 As for the dead being raised, have you not read in the book of Moses, in the passage about the bush, how God spoke to him, saying, ‘I am the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob’?

Matthew 22:32
‘I am the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob’? He is not God of the dead, but of the living.”
  Mark 12:26–12:27 — teaching parallel: Both cite God's identification with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob and conclude that He is God of the living rather than the dead.
    Mark 12:26 As for the dead being raised, have you not read in the book of Moses, in the passage about the bush, how God spoke to him, saying, ‘I am the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob’?
    Mark 12:27 He is not God of the dead, but of the living. You are greatly mistaken.”

Matthew 22:33
When the crowds heard, they were astonished at His teaching.
  Mark 12:28 — narrative parallel: Matthew records the crowd's astonishment at Jesus' answer; Mark has a listening scribe recognize His good answer. These are different responses and speakers following the resurrection dispute.
    Mark 12:28 One of the scribes approached, having heard them disputing and seen that He had answered them well. He asked Him, “Which commandment is first of all?”

Matthew 22:34
Hearing that He had silenced the Sadducees, the Pharisees gathered together.
  Mark 12:28 — narrative parallel: Both move from the resurrection dispute to the foremost-commandment question. Matthew has Pharisees gather after the Sadducees' defeat; Mark focuses on a scribe impressed by Jesus' answer.
    Mark 12:28 One of the scribes approached, having heard them disputing and seen that He had answered them well. He asked Him, “Which commandment is first of all?”

Matthew 22:35
One of them, an expert in the Law, questioned Him to test Him:
  Mark 12:28 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a law-trained questioner. Matthew explicitly describes testing Jesus; Mark portrays a scribe recognizing His good answer without stating that testing motive.
    Mark 12:28 One of the scribes approached, having heard them disputing and seen that He had answered them well. He asked Him, “Which commandment is first of all?”

Matthew 22:36
“Teacher, which commandment is great in the Law?”
  Mark 12:28 — teaching parallel: Both ask which commandment ranks greatest or first, initiating the answer about love for God and neighbor.
    Mark 12:28 One of the scribes approached, having heard them disputing and seen that He had answered them well. He asked Him, “Which commandment is first of all?”

Matthew 22:37
He said to him, “‘You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your mind.’
  Mark 12:30 — quotation: Both require love of the Lord God with heart, soul, and mind. Mark also includes strength, absent from Matthew's supplied verse here.
    Mark 12:30 You shall love the Lord your God with your whole heart, with your whole soul, with your whole mind, and with your whole strength.’

Matthew 22:38
This is the great and first commandment.
  Mark 12:29–12:30 — teaching parallel: Both identify love for God as the first or great commandment. Mark precedes it with the confession that the Lord is one.
    Mark 12:29 Jesus answered, “The first is, ‘Hear, Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one.
    Mark 12:30 You shall love the Lord your God with your whole heart, with your whole soul, with your whole mind, and with your whole strength.’

Matthew 22:39
A second is like it: ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’
  Mark 12:31 — quotation: Both identify loving one's neighbor as oneself as the second commandment. Mark immediately says none is greater than these.
    Mark 12:31 The second is this: ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’ There is no other commandment greater than these.”

Matthew 22:40
On these two commandments hang the whole Law and the Prophets.”
  Mark 12:29–12:31 — teaching parallel: Both give love of God and neighbor a governing rank. Matthew says the whole Law and Prophets hang on them; Mark says no commandment is greater.
    Mark 12:29 Jesus answered, “The first is, ‘Hear, Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one.
    Mark 12:30 You shall love the Lord your God with your whole heart, with your whole soul, with your whole mind, and with your whole strength.’
    Mark 12:31 The second is this: ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’ There is no other commandment greater than these.”

Matthew 22:41
While the Pharisees were gathered, Jesus questioned them,
  Mark 12:35 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus' own Christ-and-David question after the commandment discussion. Matthew specifies gathered Pharisees; Mark gives a temple teaching setting without naming those listeners here.
    Mark 12:35 Jesus responded as He taught in the temple, “How do the scribes say that the Christ is David’s son?

Matthew 22:42
saying, “What do you think about the Christ? Whose son is He?” They said to Him, “David’s.”
  Mark 12:35 — teaching parallel: Both raise the claim that the Christ is David's son. Matthew asks whose son and records the answer; Mark asks how the scribes say He is David's son.
    Mark 12:35 Jesus responded as He taught in the temple, “How do the scribes say that the Christ is David’s son?

Matthew 22:43
He said to them, “How then does David in the Spirit call Him Lord, saying,
  Mark 12:36 — teaching parallel: Both introduce David speaking in the Spirit and calling the addressed figure Lord before citing the enthronement psalm. Mark explicitly says Holy Spirit.
    Mark 12:36 David himself said in the Holy Spirit, ‘The Lord said to my Lord: Sit at My right until I put Thy enemies beneath Thy feet.’

Matthew 22:44
‘The Lord said to my Lord: Sit at My right until I put Thy enemies beneath Thy feet’?
  Psalms 109:1 — quotation: Matthew quotes the Lord commanding my lord to sit at His right until the enemies are beneath His feet. This edition numbers the psalm 109:1 and explicitly uses footstool imagery.
    Psalms 109:1 Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.

Matthew 22:45
If David calls Him Lord, how is He his son?”
  Mark 12:37 — teaching parallel: Both ask how the Christ can be David's son when David himself calls Him Lord. The question presses beyond simply stating Davidic descent.
    Mark 12:37 David himself calls Him Lord; how then is He his son?” The large crowd listened to Him gladly.

Matthew 22:46
No one could answer Him a word, nor from that day did anyone dare question Him any longer.
  Mark 12:34 — narrative parallel: Both say people no longer dared question Jesus. Matthew places the closure after the David-and-Lord question; Mark puts it before that teaching, preserving a difference in ordering.
    Mark 12:34 Seeing that he answered thoughtfully, Jesus said to him, “You are not far from the kingdom of God.” No one dared to question Him any further.

Matthew 23:1
Then Jesus spoke to the crowds and His disciples,
  Luke 20:45 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus' warning in the hearing of the people while addressing His disciples. Matthew names crowds and disciples; Luke says all the people were listening.
    Luke 20:45 While all the people were listening, He said to His disciples,

Matthew 23:2
saying, “The scribes and Pharisees have seated themselves in Moses’ chair.
  Exodus 18:13–18:16 — Mosaic judging-seat background: Exodus portrays Moses sitting to judge the people and explain God's ordinances. This supplies a concrete Mosaic authority-and-seating background for Moses' chair, without identifying the scribes' chair as Moses' surviving physical seat or reconstructing an unstated succession of institutions.
    Exodus 18:13 After the next day Moses sat to judge the people, and all the people stood before Moses from morning until evening.
    Exodus 18:14 And when Jethro saw all that he did to the people, he said, “What is this that you do to the people? Why do you sit alone, while all the people stand before you from morning until evening?”
    Exodus 18:15 Moses said to his father-in-law, “The people come to me to seek judgment from God.
    Exodus 18:16 When they have a dispute and come to me, I judge each case and instruct them in God’s ordinances and His law.”

Matthew 23:3
Therefore do and observe everything they tell you, but do not act according to their works, for they say and do not do.
  Romans 2:21–2:23 — thematic parallel: Both expose a contradiction between teaching the law and failing to practice it. Matthew tells hearers not to imitate the teachers' works; Paul challenges the teacher with examples of transgression. Romans does not repeat Matthew's instruction to observe what these teachers say.
    Romans 2:21 you then who teach another, do you not teach yourself? You who proclaim that one must not steal, do you steal?
    Romans 2:22 You who say that one must not commit adultery, do you commit adultery? You who abhor idols, do you rob temples?
    Romans 2:23 You who boast in law, do you dishonor God through transgression of the law?

Matthew 23:4
They bind heavy burdens, hard to carry, and lay them upon people’s shoulders, but they themselves are unwilling to move them with their finger.
  Luke 11:46 — teaching parallel: Both accuse legal teachers of placing burdens hard to carry on others while refusing to touch those burdens with a finger. Matthew addresses scribes and Pharisees; Luke addresses experts in the Law.
    Luke 11:46 But He said, “Woe also to you experts in the Law, because you load people with burdens hard to carry, and you yourselves do not touch the burdens with one of your fingers!

Matthew 23:5
They do all their works to be seen by people. They broaden their phylacteries and enlarge their fringes.
  Matthew 6:1 — thematic parallel: This same-book comparison connects the criticism of works performed to be seen by people with Jesus' earlier warning against practicing righteousness for that purpose. Matthew 6:1 does not mention phylacteries or garment fringes.
    Matthew 6:1 Take care not to practice your righteousness before people in order to be seen by them; otherwise you have no reward with your Father in the heavens.

Matthew 23:6
They love the foremost place at dinners and the foremost seats in the synagogues,
  Mark 12:39 — narrative parallel: Both warnings describe love of the foremost synagogue seats and foremost places at dinners, with the order of the two locations reversed.
    Mark 12:39 the foremost seats in the synagogues, and the foremost reclining places at dinners.

Matthew 23:7
greetings in the marketplaces, and being called ‘Rabbi’ by people.
  Mark 12:38 — narrative parallel: Both criticize the desire for greetings in the marketplaces. Matthew additionally mentions being called Rabbi; Mark's cited verse instead mentions long robes and does not include the title.
    Mark 12:38 In His teaching He said, “Beware of the scribes who like to walk about in long robes and receive greetings in the marketplaces,

Matthew 23:8
But you shall not be called ‘Rabbi,’ for One is your Teacher, and you are all brothers.
  John 13:13 — thematic parallel: Matthew grounds the instruction about the title Rabbi in the disciples' one Teacher; John records Jesus affirming that they rightly call Him the Teacher and Lord. The shared point is Jesus' teaching authority; John does not repeat Matthew's title restriction or all-brothers statement.
    John 13:13 You call Me the Teacher and the Lord, and you speak well, for I am.

Matthew 23:9
And call no one upon earth your father, for One is your Father, the heavenly One.
  Malachi 2:10 — thematic parallel: Both appeal to one Father shared by the people addressed. Matthew specifies the heavenly Father and forbids calling someone on earth father; Malachi places the one-Father appeal beside the one Creator and a rebuke of betrayal between brothers. Malachi does not itself contain Matthew's naming prohibition.
    Malachi 2:10 Did not one God create you? Is there not one Father of you all? Why have you each abandoned your brother, so as to profane the covenant of your fathers?

Matthew 23:10
Nor shall you be called guides, because One is your Guide, the Christ.
  John 13:13–13:15 — Jesus' authority and disciples' conduct comparison: Matthew reserves the Guide designation for the Christ; John has Jesus acknowledge Teacher and Lord and direct His followers to the example of His service. The passages place Jesus' authority over His disciples' conduct. Guide, Teacher and Lord remain distinct titles, and John's acknowledgment is not rewritten as Matthew's title prohibition.
    John 13:13 You call Me the Teacher and the Lord, and you speak well, for I am.
    John 13:14 So if I, the Lord and the Teacher, washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another’s feet.
    John 13:15 For I have given you an example, so that you also may do just as I did to you.

Matthew 23:11
But the greatest among you will be your servant.
  Mark 9:35 — teaching parallel: Both redefine precedence through service. Matthew says the greatest will be the servant; Mark says one wishing to be first must be last and servant of all.
    Mark 9:35 He sat down, called the Twelve, and said to them, “If anyone wants to be first, he shall be last of all and servant of all.”

Matthew 23:12
Whoever exalts himself will be humbled, and whoever humbles himself will be exalted.
  Luke 14:11 — teaching parallel: Both give the reversal that the self-exalting person will be humbled and the self-humbling person exalted. Luke places it after a teaching about seating at a banquet.
    Luke 14:11 For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”

Matthew 23:13
Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you shut the kingdom of the heavens before people! For you do not enter, nor do you permit those entering to enter.
  Luke 11:52 — teaching parallel: Both charge religious teachers with failing to enter and hindering others who would enter. Matthew speaks of shutting the kingdom of the heavens; Luke speaks of taking away the key of knowledge. The two objects are related in the warnings but are not identical wording.
    Luke 11:52 Woe to you experts in the Law, because you have taken away the key of knowledge! You did not enter yourselves, and you hindered those who were entering.”

Matthew 23:16
Woe to you, blind guides, who say, ‘Whoever swears by the sanctuary, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gold of the sanctuary is bound.’
  Matthew 5:33–5:35 — thematic parallel: Jesus' earlier teaching rejects oath-taking by sacred or divine-associated objects rather than establishing classes of supposedly binding and nonbinding oaths. This same-book comparison gives the broader oath-teaching background; the earlier passage does not specifically discuss sanctuary gold.
    Matthew 5:33 Again, you have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not swear falsely, but shall fulfil your oaths to the Lord.’
    Matthew 5:34 But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,
    Matthew 5:35 nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.

Matthew 23:17
Fools and blind men! For which is greater, the gold or the sanctuary that sanctified the gold?
  Matthew 23:19 — internal argument, referent or explicit response: Jesus uses parallel greater-than questions: sanctuary and sanctified gold here, altar and sanctified gift there. The second example develops the same sanctifying hierarchy with different objects, without inferring a hierarchy merely from material value.
    Matthew 23:19 Blind men! For which is greater, the gift or the altar that sanctifies the gift?

Matthew 23:18
And, ‘Whoever swears by the altar, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gift upon it is bound.’
  Matthew 5:34–5:35 — thematic parallel: The earlier same-book instruction not to swear at all bears on the disputed distinction between swearing by an altar and swearing by its gift. Its examples are heaven, earth and Jerusalem, not this verse's altar and gift, so it supplies a related principle rather than an exact restatement.
    Matthew 5:34 But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,
    Matthew 5:35 nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.

Matthew 23:19
Blind men! For which is greater, the gift or the altar that sanctifies the gift?
  Exodus 29:37 — allusion: Both attribute sanctifying holiness to the altar. Exodus calls the altar most holy and says everyone touching it will be sanctified, while Matthew specifically speaks of the altar sanctifying a gift. The persons-and-gift difference and the absence of an oath rule in Exodus are retained.
    Exodus 29:37 For seven days you shall purify the altar and consecrate it; and the altar shall be most holy; everyone who touches the altar shall be sanctified.

Matthew 23:20
Therefore the one swearing by the altar swears by it and by everything upon it.
  Matthew 23:18–23:19 — internal argument, referent or explicit response: The conclusion about an altar-oath embracing its contents answers the earlier claim that only a gift-oath binds and the question about the altar sanctifying the gift. This is the stated argument's own basis.
    Matthew 23:18 And, ‘Whoever swears by the altar, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gift upon it is bound.’
    Matthew 23:19 Blind men! For which is greater, the gift or the altar that sanctifies the gift?

Matthew 23:21
The one swearing by the sanctuary swears by it and by the One dwelling in it.
  Psalms 10:4 — thematic parallel: Matthew relates the sanctuary to the One dwelling in it; the psalm says the Lord is in His holy temple and immediately places His throne in heaven. The comparison concerns sacred dwelling and divine presence, without claiming the psalm identifies the same physical sanctuary.
    Psalms 10:4 The Lord is in His holy temple; the Lord’s throne is in heaven. His eyes look toward the needy; His eyelids examine the sons of men.

Matthew 23:22
The one swearing by heaven swears by God’s throne and by the One seated upon it.
  Isaiah 66:1 — allusion: Matthew's identification of heaven as God's throne recalls the Lord's statement that heaven is His throne. Matthew applies that imagery to swearing by heaven; Isaiah is not itself an oath prohibition.
    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

Matthew 23:23
Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you tithe mint, dill, and cumin, but have left aside the weightier matters of the Law: justice, mercy, and faithfulness! These you ought to have done without leaving those aside.
  Luke 11:42 — teaching parallel: Both rebuke meticulous tithing of herbs while neglecting weightier duties, and say the latter should be done without omitting the former. Matthew names mint, dill and cumin with justice, mercy and faithfulness; Luke names mint, rue and every herb with justice and love of God.
    Luke 11:42 But woe to you Pharisees, because you tithe mint and rue and every garden herb, and pass by justice and the love of God! You ought to have done these things without leaving those undone.

Matthew 23:24
Blind guides, straining out the gnat but swallowing the camel!
  Matthew 7:3–7:5 — internal teaching analogy: disproportion and impaired moral sight: Both rebukes use an absurd contrast between a small object and a much larger one, with defective sight framing the moral failure. Matthew23:24 calls the guides blind while contrasting a strained gnat and swallowed camel; Matthew7:3–5 contrasts the noticed splinter and unnoticed beam and calls the critic a hypocrite. The surrounding criticism of neglected weightier matters and the criticism of judging another are distinct; no fixed allegorical identity is assigned to the objects.
    Matthew 7:3 Why do you see the splinter in your brother’s eye, but do not notice the beam in your own eye?
    Matthew 7:4 Or how will you say to your brother, ‘Let me remove the splinter from your eye,’ when behold, the beam is in your own eye?
    Matthew 7:5 Hypocrite! First remove the beam from your own eye, and then you will see clearly to remove the splinter from your brother’s eye.

Matthew 23:25
Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you cleanse the outside of the cup and dish, but inside they are full of plunder and self-indulgence!
  Luke 11:39 — teaching parallel: Both address Pharisees who clean the outside of cup and dish while the inside is full of plunder. Matthew adds self-indulgence; Luke adds wickedness.
    Luke 11:39 And the Lord said to him, “Now you Pharisees cleanse the outside of the cup and the dish, but your inside is full of plunder and wickedness.

Matthew 23:26
Blind Pharisee, first cleanse the inside of the cup, so that its outside may also become clean.
  Luke 11:39–11:41 — teaching parallel: Both direct attention from the cleaned exterior to the morally disordered interior. Matthew commands cleansing the inside first so the outside becomes clean; Luke connects inner and outer creation and instructs giving what is within as alms. The remedies are compared without being made identical.
    Luke 11:39 And the Lord said to him, “Now you Pharisees cleanse the outside of the cup and the dish, but your inside is full of plunder and wickedness.
    Luke 11:40 Fools! Did not the One who made the outside also make the inside?
    Luke 11:41 But give what is within as alms, and behold, everything is clean for you.

Matthew 23:27
Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!
  Luke 11:44 — teaching parallel: Both use tombs to criticize the hearers through concealed uncleanness. Matthew describes whitewashed tombs beautiful outside but full of bones and uncleanness; Luke describes unmarked tombs crossed unknowingly. The distinct tomb images remain visible.
    Luke 11:44 Woe to you, because you are like unmarked tombs, and the people who walk over them do not know it!”

Matthew 23:28
So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.
  Luke 16:15 — teaching parallel: Both contrast favorable appearance before people with an inward condition exposed by God or Jesus. Matthew names hypocrisy and lawlessness within an outwardly righteous appearance; Luke says God knows the hearts of those justifying themselves before people.
    Luke 16:15 And He said to them, “You are those who justify yourselves before people, but God knows your hearts, because what is exalted among people is an abomination before God.

Matthew 23:29
Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you build the prophets’ tombs and adorn the monuments of the righteous,
  Luke 11:47 — teaching parallel: Both criticize building the prophets' tombs in the context of the fathers' killing the prophets. Matthew additionally names decorating the righteous people's monuments.
    Luke 11:47 Woe to you, because you build the tombs of the prophets, but your fathers killed them!

Matthew 23:30
and say, ‘If we had lived in our fathers’ days, we would not have shared with them in the blood of the prophets.’
  Luke 11:47–11:48 — teaching parallel: Both discuss the present generation's relation to the fathers who killed the prophets while the descendants build their tombs. Matthew quotes a denial of complicity; Luke records Jesus' charge that their building witnesses to approval. The quoted defense is not attributed to Luke.
    Luke 11:47 Woe to you, because you build the tombs of the prophets, but your fathers killed them!
    Luke 11:48 So you are witnesses and approve the deeds of your fathers: they killed them, and you build.

Matthew 23:31
So you testify concerning yourselves that you are sons of those who murdered the prophets.
  Luke 11:48 — teaching parallel: Both characterize the hearers as witnesses concerning their fathers' deeds against the prophets. Matthew stresses their identity as sons of the murderers; Luke explicitly charges approval of the deeds. These are related accusations with different formulations.
    Luke 11:48 So you are witnesses and approve the deeds of your fathers: they killed them, and you build.

Matthew 23:32
You, then, fill up the measure of your fathers!
  Genesis 15:16 — allusion: Matthew's command to fill the fathers' measure can be compared with Genesis' description of the Amorites' sins not yet being filled up. Both use a completion image for accumulated wrongdoing; the peoples and historical situations differ, and Genesis does not name Matthew's hearers.
    Genesis 15:16 And in the fourth generation they shall return here; for the sins of the Amorites are not yet filled up.”

Matthew 23:33
Snakes, brood of vipers! How will you flee from the judgment of Gehenna?
  Matthew 3:7 — teaching parallel: This same-book comparison repeats the address brood of vipers and the question about escaping coming judgment. John asks about fleeing coming wrath in Matthew 3; Jesus here asks about fleeing the judgment of Gehenna.
    Matthew 3:7 When he saw many of the Pharisees and Sadducees coming to his baptism, he said to them, “Brood of vipers! Who warned you to flee from the coming wrath?

Matthew 23:34
Therefore behold, I send you prophets, wise people, and scribes. Some of them you will kill and crucify, and some you will flog in your synagogues and persecute from city to city,
  Luke 11:49 — teaching parallel: Both predict the sending of messengers, some of whom will be killed and persecuted. Matthew has Jesus say I send and names prophets, wise people and scribes; Luke attributes the saying to God's wisdom and names prophets and apostles. Matthew's crucifixion, synagogue flogging and city-to-city details are additional.
    Luke 11:49 For this reason the wisdom of God also said, ‘I will send them prophets and apostles, and some of these they will kill and persecute,’

Matthew 23:35
so that upon you may come all righteous blood poured out upon earth, from the blood of righteous Abel to the blood of Zechariah son of Berechiah, whom you murdered between the sanctuary and the altar.
  Luke 11:50–11:51 — teaching parallel: Both describe accountability for bloodshed stretching from Abel to Zechariah, killed between altar and sanctuary or house. Matthew calls Zechariah son of Berechiah; Luke gives no father. This link does not resolve the identity by substituting the different patronymic in a separate Chronicles account.
    Luke 11:50 so that the blood of all the prophets poured out since the foundation of the world may be required of this generation,
    Luke 11:51 from the blood of Abel to the blood of Zechariah, who perished between the altar and the house. Yes, I tell you, it will be required of this generation.

Matthew 23:36
Truly I tell you, all these things will come upon this generation.
  Luke 11:51 — teaching parallel: Both assign accountability for the preceding bloodshed to this generation. Matthew says all these things will come upon it; Luke says the blood will be required of it.
    Luke 11:51 from the blood of Abel to the blood of Zechariah, who perished between the altar and the house. Yes, I tell you, it will be required of this generation.

Matthew 23:37
Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wished to gather your children as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were unwilling!
  Luke 13:34 — teaching parallel: Both lament Jerusalem's killing of prophets and stoning of the sent, then compare Jesus' wish to gather its unwilling children with a bird gathering a brood beneath its wings. Matthew specifically says hen and chicks; Luke says bird and brood.
    Luke 13:34 Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wanted to gather your children as a bird gathers her own brood under her wings, and you were not willing!

Matthew 23:38
Behold, your house is left to you desolate.
  Luke 13:35 — teaching parallel: Both announce the house being left to the hearers. Matthew explicitly calls it desolate; that adjective is absent from the supplied Luke verse, which continues with the future greeting.
    Luke 13:35 Behold, your house is left to you. And I tell you, you will certainly not see Me until the time comes when you say, ‘Blessed is He who comes in the name of the Lord!’”

Matthew 23:39
For I tell you, you will certainly not see Me from now until you say, ‘Blessed is the One coming in the name of the Lord.’”
  Psalms 117:26 — quotation: Jesus quotes the blessing upon the One coming in the Lord's name. The Saints psalm supplies that clause and continues with blessing from the Lord's house; Matthew uses the clause in His statement about seeing Him again. The target is Greek-numbered Psalms 117:26.
    Psalms 117:26 Blessed is the one who comes in the name of the Lord. We have blessed you from the house of the Lord.

Matthew 24:1
Jesus went out from the temple and was going away, when His disciples came to show Him the temple buildings.
  Mark 13:1 — narrative parallel: Both begin the temple-destruction discourse with Jesus leaving the temple and a disciple or disciples drawing attention to its buildings. Mark adds admiration of the stones and buildings; Matthew gives the disciples' approach without quoting it.
    Mark 13:1 As He was leaving the temple, one of His disciples said to Him, “Teacher, look! What stones and what buildings!”

Matthew 24:2
He answered them, “Do you not see all these things? Truly I tell you, not one stone will be left here upon another that will not be thrown down.”
  Mark 13:2 — narrative parallel: Both predict that the impressive temple buildings will be thrown down with no stone left upon another. Matthew introduces the prediction with Truly I tell you.
    Mark 13:2 Jesus said to him, “Do you see these great buildings? Not one stone will be left here on another that will not be torn down.”

Matthew 24:3
As He sat on the Mount of Olives, the disciples came to Him privately, saying, “Tell us, when will these things happen, and what is the sign of Thy coming and the consummation of the age?”
  Mark 13:3–13:4 — narrative parallel: Both place a private question on the Mount of Olives about when the predicted events will happen and their sign. Mark names Peter, James, John and Andrew and the temple opposite them; Matthew explicitly asks about Jesus' coming and the age's consummation, which Mark's question does not formulate that way.
    Mark 13:3 As He sat on the Mount of Olives opposite the temple, Peter, James, John, and Andrew questioned Him privately:
    Mark 13:4 “Tell us, when will these things be, and what will be the sign when all these things are about to be fulfilled?”

Matthew 24:4
Jesus answered them, “See that no one leads you astray.
  Mark 13:5 — teaching parallel: Both open Jesus' reply with the warning to see that no one leads the hearers astray.
    Mark 13:5 Jesus began saying to them, “Watch out that no one leads you astray.

Matthew 24:5
For many will come in My name, saying, ‘I am the Christ,’ and will lead many astray.
  Mark 13:6 — teaching parallel: Both warn that many will come in Jesus' name and deceive many. Matthew gives their claim as I am the Christ, while Mark's supplied wording is I am he; the explicit Christ title is not silently inserted into Mark.
    Mark 13:6 Many will come in My name, saying, ‘I am he,’ and will lead many astray.

Matthew 24:6
You will hear wars and reports of wars. See that you are not alarmed, for these things must happen, but the end is not yet.
  Mark 13:7 — teaching parallel: Both tell hearers not to be alarmed by wars and reports of wars because these must happen but the end is not yet.
    Mark 13:7 When you hear of wars and reports of wars, do not be alarmed. These must happen, but the end is not yet.

Matthew 24:7
For nation will rise against nation and kingdom against kingdom, and there will be famines and earthquakes in various places.
  Mark 13:8 — teaching parallel: Both predict nation against nation and kingdom against kingdom, with earthquakes in various places and famines. The order of earthquakes and famines differs.
    Mark 13:8 For nation will rise against nation, and kingdom against kingdom. There will be earthquakes in various places; there will be famines. These things are the beginning of birth pains.

Matthew 24:8
All these things are the beginning of birth pains.
  Mark 13:8 — teaching parallel: Both describe the preceding upheavals as the beginning of birth pains. Mark includes the saying in the same verse as the earthquakes and famines.
    Mark 13:8 For nation will rise against nation, and kingdom against kingdom. There will be earthquakes in various places; there will be famines. These things are the beginning of birth pains.

Matthew 24:9
Then they will hand you over to affliction and kill you, and you will be hated by all nations because of My name.
  Mark 13:9–13:13 — teaching parallel: Both warn of handing over, death and hatred because of Jesus. Mark specifies councils, synagogue beatings, rulers and betrayal within families; Matthew here describes affliction and hatred by all nations. The scope and sequence of the warnings are not treated as identical.
    Mark 13:9 But watch yourselves. They will hand you over to councils; you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for My sake, as a testimony to them.
    Mark 13:10 The gospel must first be proclaimed to all the nations.
    Mark 13:11 When they lead you away to hand you over, do not worry beforehand about what to say. Whatever is given you in that hour, say that; for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.
    Mark 13:12 Brother will hand brother over to death, and a father his child. Children will rise against parents and have them put to death.
    Mark 13:13 You will be hated by all because of My name. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

Matthew 24:10
Then many will stumble, hand one another over, and hate one another.
  Mark 13:12 — teaching parallel: Both predict betrayal of people close to one another. Matthew describes many stumbling, betraying and hating each other; Mark specifically lists brother against brother, father against child and children against parents, without repeating Matthew's whole description.
    Mark 13:12 Brother will hand brother over to death, and a father his child. Children will rise against parents and have them put to death.

Matthew 24:11
Many false prophets will arise and lead many astray.
  Mark 13:22 — teaching parallel: Both warn of false prophets leading people astray. Mark additionally names false christs, signs and wonders and the attempted deception of the chosen; Matthew develops those features later in this discourse.
    Mark 13:22 For false christs and false prophets will arise and give signs and wonders to lead astray, if possible, the chosen.

Matthew 24:12
Because lawlessness increases, the love of many will grow cold.
  2 Timothy 3:1–3:5 — thematic parallel: Both warnings associate a coming moral deterioration with damaged love. Matthew explicitly makes increasing lawlessness the cause of love growing cold; Second Timothy describes the last days through wrongdoing and disordered or absent love. The specific causal clause belongs to Matthew and is not inserted into Timothy, nor are the settings assigned one calculated date.
    2 Timothy 3:1 But know this: in the last days difficult times will come.
    2 Timothy 3:2 For people will be lovers of themselves, lovers of money, boastful, arrogant, blasphemous, disobedient to parents, ungrateful, unholy,
    2 Timothy 3:3 without natural affection, irreconcilable, slanderers, without self-control, savage, not loving the good,
    2 Timothy 3:4 traitors, reckless, conceited, lovers of pleasure rather than lovers of God,
    2 Timothy 3:5 having a form of godliness but denying its power. Turn away from these also.

Matthew 24:13
But the one who endures to the end will be saved.
  Mark 13:13 — teaching parallel: Both promise salvation to the one who endures to the end. Mark attaches it to hatred on account of Jesus' name, while Matthew places it after the preceding warnings of betrayal and lawlessness.
    Mark 13:13 You will be hated by all because of My name. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

Matthew 24:14
This good news of the kingdom will be proclaimed in the whole inhabited world as a testimony to all nations, and then the end will come.
  Mark 13:10 — teaching parallel: Both say the good news must reach all nations. Matthew explicitly makes worldwide proclamation a testimony and says the end then comes; Mark says the proclamation to all nations must happen first without repeating the whole formulation.
    Mark 13:10 The gospel must first be proclaimed to all the nations.

Matthew 24:15
So when you see the abomination of desolation spoken of through Daniel the prophet standing in a holy place—let the reader understand—
  Mark 13:14 — teaching parallel: Both warn of the abomination of desolation standing in an improper or holy location and include the reader's instruction to understand. Matthew explicitly attributes it to Daniel and calls the place holy; Mark says where it ought not and proceeds directly to flight from Judea.
    Mark 13:14 When you see the abomination of desolation standing where it must not—let the reader understand—then let those in Judea flee to the mountains.
  Daniel 9:27 — quotation: Daniel's supplied text places an abomination of desolations upon the temple, the distinctive image Jesus identifies as spoken through Daniel. The wording, long time-period description and desolation context are retained as saved; this link does not select a historical identification or recalculate the periods.
    Daniel 9:27 The covenant will prevail for many. It will return again and be rebuilt in breadth and length. At the completion of times, after seven and seventy periods and sixty-two years, until the time of the completion of war, desolation will be removed when the covenant grows strong for many weeks. At the end of the week the sacrifice and libation will be removed. Upon the temple will be an abomination of desolations until completion, and completion will be given upon the desolation.”

Matthew 24:16
then let those in Judea flee to the mountains.
  Mark 13:14 — teaching parallel: Both direct those in Judea to flee to the mountains when the preceding sign appears.
    Mark 13:14 When you see the abomination of desolation standing where it must not—let the reader understand—then let those in Judea flee to the mountains.

Matthew 24:17
Let the one on the housetop not go down to take things from his house,
  Mark 13:15 — teaching parallel: Both prohibit someone on the housetop from going down into the house to collect possessions during the flight.
    Mark 13:15 Let the one on the housetop not go down or enter to take anything from his house,

Matthew 24:18
and let the one in the field not turn back to take his cloak.
  Mark 13:16 — teaching parallel: Both prohibit someone in the field from turning back to take a cloak.
    Mark 13:16 and let the one in the field not turn back to take his garment.

Matthew 24:19
Woe to those with child and those nursing infants in those days!
  Mark 13:17 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce woe on pregnant women and those nursing infants in those days.
    Mark 13:17 Woe to those who are pregnant and those nursing infants in those days!

Matthew 24:20
Pray that your flight may not occur in winter or on a Sabbath.
  Mark 13:18 — teaching parallel: Both direct prayer that the crisis or flight not happen in winter. Matthew additionally mentions the Sabbath; that additional qualification is absent from Mark's supplied verse.
    Mark 13:18 Pray that it does not happen in winter.

Matthew 24:21
For then there will be great affliction such as has not happened from the beginning of the world until now, and certainly will never happen again.
  Mark 13:19 — teaching parallel: Both describe an affliction unparalleled from the world's or creation's beginning until now and never to be repeated. Mark explicitly speaks of the creation God created.
    Mark 13:19 For those days will be distress such as has not occurred from the beginning of the creation that God created until now, and certainly will not occur again.

Matthew 24:22
If those days had not been shortened, no flesh would have been saved; but because of the chosen, those days will be shortened.
  Mark 13:20 — teaching parallel: Both say that without shortening the days no flesh would be saved and that the shortening is for the chosen. Mark explicitly names the Lord as shortening the days and calls the chosen those He chose.
    Mark 13:20 If the Lord had not shortened the days, no flesh would have been saved. But for the sake of the chosen, whom He chose, He shortened the days.

Matthew 24:23
Then if anyone tells you, ‘Behold, here is the Christ!’ or ‘Here!’ do not believe it.
  Mark 13:21 — teaching parallel: Both forbid believing reports that the Christ is here or there.
    Mark 13:21 Then if anyone says to you, ‘Look, here is the Christ!’ or, ‘Look, there!’ do not believe it.

Matthew 24:24
For false christs and false prophets will arise and give great signs and wonders, so as to lead astray, if possible, even the chosen.
  Mark 13:22 — teaching parallel: Both predict false christs and false prophets performing signs and wonders to deceive, if possible, the chosen. Matthew calls the signs great.
    Mark 13:22 For false christs and false prophets will arise and give signs and wonders to lead astray, if possible, the chosen.

Matthew 24:25
Behold, I have told you beforehand.
  Mark 13:23 — teaching parallel: Both remind the disciples that Jesus has told them beforehand. Mark also commands vigilance and says He has told all things beforehand.
    Mark 13:23 But you, watch out! I have told you everything beforehand.

Matthew 24:26
So if they tell you, ‘Behold, He is in the wilderness,’ do not go out; or ‘Behold, in the inner rooms,’ do not believe it.
  Luke 17:23 — teaching parallel: Both reject directions that locate the awaited figure here or there and tell hearers not to go after such reports. Matthew specifies wilderness and inner rooms; Luke does not name those places.
    Luke 17:23 And they will say to you, ‘Behold, there!’ or ‘Behold, here!’ Do not go away or pursue them.

Matthew 24:27
For just as lightning comes from the east and shines to the west, so will be the coming of the Son of Man.
  Luke 17:24 — teaching parallel: Both compare the Son of Man's manifestation with lightning lighting the sky. Matthew says from east to west and speaks of His coming; Luke says from one part of the sky to another and speaks of His day.
    Luke 17:24 For just as lightning flashes and shines from one region under heaven to another under heaven, so will the Son of Man be in His day.

Matthew 24:28
Wherever the corpse is, there the eagles will gather.
  Luke 17:37 — teaching parallel: Both give the image of eagles gathering where the body or corpse is. Matthew says corpse, while Luke says body in answer to a question about where; the comparison does not resolve the saying's full eschatological interpretation.
    Luke 17:37 And they answered Him, “Where, Lord?” He said to them, “Where the body is, there the eagles will also gather.”

Matthew 24:29
Immediately after the affliction of those days, the sun will be darkened, the moon will not give its brightness, the stars will fall from heaven, and the powers of the heavens will be shaken.
  Mark 13:24–13:25 — teaching parallel: Both place darkness of sun and moon, falling stars and shaken heavenly powers after the affliction of those days. Matthew explicitly says immediately after.
    Mark 13:24 But in those days, after that distress, the sun will be darkened and the moon will not give its light.
    Mark 13:25 The stars will be falling from heaven, and the powers in the heavens will be shaken.

Matthew 24:30
Then the sign of the Son of Man will appear in heaven, and then all the tribes of the earth will mourn; they will see the Son of Man coming upon the clouds of heaven with power and great glory.
  Mark 13:26 — teaching parallel: Both describe the Son of Man coming on clouds with power and great glory. Matthew also includes His heavenly sign and the mourning of all earthly tribes; those additional elements are not supplied by the cited Mark verse.
    Mark 13:26 Then they will see the Son of Man coming in clouds with great power and glory.
  Daniel 7:13 — allusion: Matthew's Son of Man coming upon heaven's clouds recalls Daniel's vision of one like a son of man coming upon the clouds of heaven. The supplied Saints Daniel wording and visionary context are retained; this is a comparison of the distinctive cloud imagery rather than a proposed date or a harmonization of every figure in the vision.
    Daniel 7:13 I watched in the night vision, and there, upon the clouds of heaven, one like a son of man was coming. He was present as an Ancient of Days, and those standing by were present with him.

Matthew 24:31
He will send His angels with a great trumpet, and they will gather His chosen from the four winds, from one end of the heavens to the other.
  Mark 13:27 — teaching parallel: Both describe angels gathering the chosen from the four winds and the farthest extents. Matthew adds a great trumpet and speaks of one end of heaven to the other; Mark speaks of earth's end to heaven's end.
    Mark 13:27 Then He will send the angels and gather His chosen from the four winds, from the end of the earth to the end of heaven.

Matthew 24:32
Learn the parable from the fig tree: when its branch has already become tender and puts out leaves, you know that summer is near.
  Mark 13:28 — teaching parallel: Both direct learning from the fig tree, whose tender branch and emerging leaves indicate that summer is near.
    Mark 13:28 Learn the parable from the fig tree: when its branch has already become tender and puts out leaves, you know that summer is near.

Matthew 24:33
So also you, when you see all these things, know that it is near, at the doors.
  Mark 13:29 — teaching parallel: Both tell hearers that seeing these events indicates nearness, at the doors. The comparison retains the source's wording without deciding which proposed referent of it is intended.
    Mark 13:29 So also, when you see these things happening, know that it is near, at the doors.

Matthew 24:34
Truly I tell you that this generation will certainly not pass away until all these things happen.
  Mark 13:30 — teaching parallel: Both say this generation will not pass away until all these things happen. The link records the shared saying without assigning an interpretive date.
    Mark 13:30 Truly I tell you, this generation will certainly not pass away until all these things happen.

Matthew 24:35
Heaven and earth will pass away, but My words will certainly not pass away.
  Mark 13:31 — teaching parallel: Both contrast passing heaven and earth with Jesus' words that will not pass away.
    Mark 13:31 Heaven and earth will pass away, but My words will certainly not pass away.

Matthew 24:36
Concerning that day and hour, no one knows, not even the angels of the heavens, nor the Son, but the Father alone.
  Mark 13:32 — teaching parallel: Both deny knowledge of the day or hour to anyone, including the angels and the Son, and reserve it to the Father. Matthew specifies angels of the heavens and the Father alone.
    Mark 13:32 But concerning that day or hour, no one knows—not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son—except the Father.

Matthew 24:37
For just as the days of Noah were, so will be the coming of the Son of Man.
  Luke 17:26 — teaching parallel: Both compare the days of Noah with the coming or days of the Son of Man. The phrasing differs between Matthew's coming and Luke's days.
    Luke 17:26 And just as it happened in the days of Noah, so it will also be in the days of the Son of Man.

Matthew 24:38
For in those days before the flood they were eating and drinking, marrying and giving in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark.
  Luke 17:27 — teaching parallel: Both describe ordinary eating, drinking and marriage continuing until the day Noah entered the ark. Luke includes the ensuing flood in the same verse.
    Luke 17:27 They were eating, drinking, marrying, and being given in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark, and the flood came and destroyed them all.

Matthew 24:39
They did not know until the flood came and took them all away. So also will be the coming of the Son of Man.
  Luke 17:27 — teaching parallel: Both portray the flood bringing a decisive end to the people outside the ark, as part of the Son of Man comparison. Matthew explicitly says they did not know until it came; Luke says the flood destroyed them all without that separate statement of unawareness.
    Luke 17:27 They were eating, drinking, marrying, and being given in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark, and the flood came and destroyed them all.

Matthew 24:40
Then two men will be in the field: one is taken and one is left.
  Luke 17:34 — teaching parallel: Both picture two people together with one taken and the other left. Matthew locates the pair in a field, while Luke places two in one bed at night; these differing settings are not harmonized or replaced by an absent Luke field verse.
    Luke 17:34 I tell you, on that night two will be on one bed: one will be taken and the other left.

Matthew 24:41
Two women will be grinding at the mill: one is taken and one is left.
  Luke 17:35 — teaching parallel: Both describe two women grinding together with one taken and one left.
    Luke 17:35 Two women will be grinding together: one will be taken and the other left.”

Matthew 24:42
So keep watch, because you do not know on what day your Lord comes.
  Mark 13:35 — teaching parallel: Both require watchfulness because the master's or Lord's coming is not known. Matthew asks about the day of your Lord's coming; Mark uses the household master's return and lists watches of the night.
    Mark 13:35 Therefore keep watch, for you do not know when the master of the house is coming: in the evening, at midnight, at cockcrow, or in the morning.

Matthew 24:43
But know this: if the householder had known in what watch the thief was coming, he would have kept watch and would not have allowed his house to be broken into.
  Luke 12:39 — teaching parallel: Both use a householder who would have prevented a break-in if he had known when the thief was coming. Matthew explicitly adds keeping watch and specifies the watch of the night; Luke uses hour.
    Luke 12:39 But know this: if the master of the house had known at what hour the thief was coming, he would not have allowed his house to be broken into.

Matthew 24:44
Therefore you too must be ready, because the Son of Man comes at an hour you do not expect.
  Luke 12:40 — teaching parallel: Both command readiness because the Son of Man comes at an unexpected hour.
    Luke 12:40 You also must be ready, because the Son of Man comes at an hour you do not expect.”

Matthew 24:45
Who, then, is the faithful and prudent slave whom the master appointed over his household to give them food at the proper time?
  Luke 12:42 — teaching parallel: Both ask who is the faithful and prudent servant appointed by the master to distribute household food at the proper time. Luke calls him a steward and specifies the food allowance.
    Luke 12:42 And the Lord said, “Who, then, is the faithful steward, the prudent one, whom his master will appoint over his household staff to give out their ration of grain at the proper time?

Matthew 24:46
Blessed is that slave whom his master, when he comes, will find doing so.
  Luke 12:43 — teaching parallel: Both bless the servant whose returning master finds him carrying out that responsibility.
    Luke 12:43 Blessed is that slave whom his master finds doing so when he comes.

Matthew 24:47
Truly I tell you that he will appoint him over all his belongings.
  Luke 12:44 — teaching parallel: Both promise that the faithful servant will be appointed over all the master's belongings.
    Luke 12:44 Truly, I tell you, he will appoint him over all his possessions.

Matthew 24:48
But if that evil slave says in his heart, ‘My master delays,’
  Luke 12:45 — teaching parallel: Both introduce the servant's inward claim that his master is delaying. Luke continues directly into the abusive conduct described in Matthew's next verse.
    Luke 12:45 But if that slave says in his heart, ‘My master is taking a long time to come,’ and begins to beat the male and female servants, and to eat and drink and become drunk,

Matthew 24:49
and begins to beat his fellow slaves and eats and drinks with drunkards,
  Luke 12:45 — teaching parallel: Both describe the servant beating fellow servants and indulging in eating and drinking or drunkenness during the presumed delay. Luke specifically names male and female servants; Matthew mentions dining with drunkards.
    Luke 12:45 But if that slave says in his heart, ‘My master is taking a long time to come,’ and begins to beat the male and female servants, and to eat and drink and become drunk,

Matthew 24:50
the master of that slave will come on a day he does not expect and at an hour he does not know.
  Luke 12:46 — teaching parallel: Both warn that the master will return on a day the servant does not expect and at an hour he does not know.
    Luke 12:46 the master of that slave will come on a day he does not expect and at an hour he does not know, and will cut him in two and assign his portion with the unfaithful.

Matthew 24:51
He will cut him in two and assign his portion with the hypocrites. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.
  Luke 12:46 — teaching parallel: Both describe the master cutting the unfaithful servant in two and assigning his portion with an excluded group. Matthew names hypocrites and adds weeping and grinding of teeth; Luke names the unfaithful or unbelievers and does not include that final formula.
    Luke 12:46 the master of that slave will come on a day he does not expect and at an hour he does not know, and will cut him in two and assign his portion with the unfaithful.

Matthew 25:1
Then the kingdom of the heavens will be likened to ten virgins who took their lamps and went out to meet the bridegroom.
  Luke 12:35–12:36 — teaching parallel: Both teachings combine lamps, a wedding setting, and readiness for an arriving master or bridegroom. Luke has servants waiting for their master to return from a wedding; Matthew has virgins going to meet the bridegroom, so the roles and journey differ.
    Luke 12:35 Let your loins be girded and your lamps burning,
    Luke 12:36 and be like people waiting for their own master to return from the wedding feast, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him at once.

Matthew 25:2
Five of them were foolish and five prudent.
  Matthew 25:3–25:4 — internal purpose, contrast, referent or explanation: The following for-clause and contrasting prudent group's preparation explain the foolish/prudent distinction through their oil provisions. This is the parable's own explanation, not a second numerical counterpart or a population estimate.
    Matthew 25:3 For the foolish took their lamps but did not take oil with them.
    Matthew 25:4 The prudent took oil in containers along with their lamps.

Matthew 25:3
For the foolish took their lamps but did not take oil with them.
  Matthew 25:4 — internal contrast: The same parable deliberately contrasts the foolish virgins' failure to take oil with the prudent virgins' provision of oil in containers. This links the two opposed preparations within the story, without claiming an independent account.
    Matthew 25:4 The prudent took oil in containers along with their lamps.

Matthew 25:4
The prudent took oil in containers along with their lamps.
  Matthew 25:3 — internal contrast: The prudent virgins' containers of oil directly contrast with the foolish virgins' taking lamps without oil in the preceding description. The comparison concerns the parable's two preparations and supplies no separate allegorical identity for the oil.
    Matthew 25:3 For the foolish took their lamps but did not take oil with them.

Matthew 25:5
As the bridegroom delayed, they all became drowsy and slept.
  Luke 12:45 — teaching parallel: Both parables include the master's or bridegroom's delay. Matthew says all the virgins grew sleepy; Luke's slave interprets delay as permission to mistreat others and indulge himself. The differing responses are not equated, and Matthew's sleep is not labelled Luke's misconduct.
    Luke 12:45 But if that slave says in his heart, ‘My master is taking a long time to come,’ and begins to beat the male and female servants, and to eat and drink and become drunk,

Matthew 25:6
At midnight a cry arose: ‘Behold, the bridegroom! Come out to meet him.’
  Luke 12:36 — teaching parallel: Both wedding-related readiness images turn on the awaited figure's arrival and an immediate response by those waiting. Matthew gives a midnight cry to meet the bridegroom; Luke gives the returning master's knock and immediate opening, without the midnight cry.
    Luke 12:36 and be like people waiting for their own master to return from the wedding feast, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him at once.

Matthew 25:8
The foolish said to the prudent, ‘Give us some of your oil, because our lamps are going out.’
  Matthew 25:9 — internal purpose, contrast, referent or explanation: The prudent group's answer responds to the actual request for oil by citing possible insufficient supply and directing a purchase. It supplies the particular reply without identifying the oil allegorically or creating a universal rule about sharing.
    Matthew 25:9 The prudent answered, ‘There may not be enough for us and you. Go instead to those selling and buy for yourselves.’

Matthew 25:9
The prudent answered, ‘There may not be enough for us and you. Go instead to those selling and buy for yourselves.’
  Matthew 25:8 — internal purpose, contrast, referent or explanation: The reply's concern about enough for us and you answers the foolish group's request to share oil for failing lamps. The preceding request identifies the resource and speakers, not an independent transaction or a general prohibition of generosity.
    Matthew 25:8 The foolish said to the prudent, ‘Give us some of your oil, because our lamps are going out.’

Matthew 25:10
While they went away to buy, the bridegroom came. Those who were ready entered with him into the wedding feast, and the door was shut.
  Luke 13:25 — teaching parallel: Both images include a door being shut and late people left outside. Matthew describes the ready virgins entering the wedding feast; Luke describes the householder rising and shutting the door, without this wedding-party scene.
    Luke 13:25 once the master of the house rises and shuts the door. Then you will begin to stand outside and knock at the door, saying, ‘Lord, open to us!’ And he will answer you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from.’

Matthew 25:11
Afterward the other virgins also came, saying, ‘Lord, lord, open to us.’
  Luke 13:25 — teaching parallel: Both depict those left outside appealing to the lord to open the door. Matthew names the late virgins; Luke speaks of hearers standing outside and knocking.
    Luke 13:25 once the master of the house rises and shuts the door. Then you will begin to stand outside and knock at the door, saying, ‘Lord, open to us!’ And he will answer you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from.’

Matthew 25:12
He answered, ‘Truly I tell you, I do not know you.’
  Matthew 7:23 — teaching parallel: Both warnings express exclusion through denial of recognition. The parable's bridegroom says he does not know the late arrivals; Jesus' earlier judgment warning says He never knew those practicing lawlessness. The different wording and setting remain distinct.
    Matthew 7:23 Then I will declare to them, ‘I never knew you. Depart from Me, you who practice lawlessness.’

Matthew 25:13
Therefore keep watch, because you know neither the day nor the hour.
  Matthew 24:42 — teaching parallel: Both commands require keeping watch because the day of the Lord's arrival is unknown; Matthew 25 also names the unknown hour.
    Matthew 24:42 So keep watch, because you do not know on what day your Lord comes.

Matthew 25:14
For it is like a man traveling abroad, who called his own slaves and handed over his belongings to them.
  Luke 19:12–19:13 — teaching parallel: Both money parables begin with a departing master or nobleman entrusting resources to slaves. Luke adds the journey to receive a kingdom and a command to do business; the surrounding plots are not made identical.
    Luke 19:12 So He said, “A certain nobleman went to a distant country to receive a kingdom for himself and return.
    Luke 19:13 He called ten of his own slaves, gave them ten minas, and said to them, ‘Do business until I come.’

Matthew 25:15
To one he gave five talents, to another two, to another one, each according to his own ability, and traveled abroad. Immediately
  Luke 19:13 — teaching parallel: Both parables describe money being entrusted to slaves before the master's absence. Matthew assigns unequal talents according to ability; Luke names ten minas distributed among ten slaves, with no corresponding ability-based allocation stated in this verse.
    Luke 19:13 He called ten of his own slaves, gave them ten minas, and said to them, ‘Do business until I come.’

Matthew 25:16
the one who received the five talents went and traded with them and gained another five.
  Luke 19:16 — teaching parallel: Both describe a first slave producing a gain from entrusted money. Matthew's five talents yield five more, while Luke's reported mina yields ten minas; the amounts and rates of increase differ.
    Luke 19:16 The first came, saying, ‘Master, your mina has gained ten minas.’

Matthew 25:17
Likewise the one with two gained another two.
  Luke 19:18 — teaching parallel: Both portray a second slave reporting or achieving a profitable use of entrusted money. Matthew describes two talents gaining two more; Luke's second slave reports a mina making five minas.
    Luke 19:18 The second came, saying, ‘Your mina, master, has made five minas.’

Matthew 25:18
But the one who received one went away, dug in the ground, and hid his master’s silver.
  Luke 19:20 — teaching parallel: Both contrast productive slaves with one who merely preserves the master's money. Matthew's slave buries the silver in the ground; Luke's slave keeps the mina in a cloth.
