    Luke 19:20 And the other came, saying, ‘Master, behold your mina, which I kept laid away in a cloth.

Matthew 25:19
After a long time, the master of those slaves came and settled accounts with them.
  Luke 19:15 — teaching parallel: Both money parables bring the returning master to an accounting with the slaves. Matthew emphasizes a long interval; Luke specifies that the nobleman has received the kingdom and asks about business done.
    Luke 19:15 And it happened that when he returned, having received the kingdom, he ordered those slaves to whom he had given the money to be called to him, so that he might know what business they had done.

Matthew 25:20
The one who received five talents came and brought another five talents, saying, ‘Master, you handed over five talents to me. Look, I gained another five talents.’
  Luke 19:16 — teaching parallel: Both first slaves report the gain on the master's entrusted funds. Matthew's report doubles five talents; Luke's report describes one mina gaining ten minas, so the figures are preserved separately.
    Luke 19:16 The first came, saying, ‘Master, your mina has gained ten minas.’

Matthew 25:21
His master said to him, ‘Well done, good and faithful slave. You were faithful over a few things; I will appoint you over many. Enter into your master’s joy.’
  Luke 19:17 — teaching parallel: Both masters praise the good slave and reward faithfulness in a small trust with greater responsibility. Matthew appoints him over many things and welcomes him into joy; Luke assigns authority over ten cities.
    Luke 19:17 And he said to him, ‘Well done, good slave! Because you have been faithful in a very small thing, have authority over ten cities.’

Matthew 25:22
The one with two talents also came and said, ‘Master, you handed over two talents to me. Look, I gained another two talents.’
  Luke 19:18 — teaching parallel: Both accounts include a second profitable slave's report. Matthew reports two talents becoming two more; Luke reports the master's mina making five minas.
    Luke 19:18 The second came, saying, ‘Your mina, master, has made five minas.’

Matthew 25:23
His master said to him, ‘Well done, good and faithful slave. You were faithful over a few things; I will appoint you over many. Enter into your master’s joy.’
  Luke 19:17 — teaching parallel: Matthew's second slave receives praise for faithfulness over few things and greater responsibility, corresponding to the principle in Luke's praise of the first slave. The slave's position and the reward wording differ; Luke does not reproduce this second-slave speech.
    Luke 19:17 And he said to him, ‘Well done, good slave! Because you have been faithful in a very small thing, have authority over ten cities.’

Matthew 25:24
The one who received one talent also came and said, ‘Master, I knew you to be a hard man, reaping where you did not sow and gathering from where you did not scatter.
  Luke 19:21 — teaching parallel: Both unproductive slaves characterize their master as severe and reaping where he did not sow. Matthew also says gathering where he did not scatter; Luke says taking up what he did not lay down.
    Luke 19:21 For I was afraid of you, because you are a severe man: you take up what you did not lay down and reap what you did not sow.’

Matthew 25:25
I was afraid, so I went away and hid your talent in the ground. Look, you have what is yours.’
  Luke 19:20–19:21 — teaching parallel: Both slaves explain their nonproductive preservation of the master's funds through fear of him. Matthew specifies burial in the ground and returning the talent; Luke specifies keeping the mina in a cloth and calls the master severe.
    Luke 19:20 And the other came, saying, ‘Master, behold your mina, which I kept laid away in a cloth.
    Luke 19:21 For I was afraid of you, because you are a severe man: you take up what you did not lay down and reap what you did not sow.’

Matthew 25:26
His master answered him, ‘Evil and idle slave! You knew that I reap where I did not sow and gather from where I did not scatter?
  Luke 19:22 — teaching parallel: Both masters confront the evil slave with his own description of a severe master who reaps where he did not sow. Luke expressly says judgment comes from the slave's own mouth; Matthew additionally calls him idle.
    Luke 19:22 He said to him, ‘From your own mouth I will judge you, evil slave. You knew that I am a severe man, taking up what I did not lay down and reaping what I did not sow?

Matthew 25:27
Then you ought to have deposited my silver with the bankers, and when I came I would have recovered what is mine with interest.
  Luke 19:23 — teaching parallel: Both masters ask why the money was not placed on deposit so it could be recovered with interest at the return.
    Luke 19:23 Then why did you not put my money on deposit? And when I came, I would have collected it with interest.’

Matthew 25:28
So take the talent from him and give it to the one who has the ten talents.
  Luke 19:24 — teaching parallel: Both direct that the unproductive slave's entrusted money be taken and given to the one who has ten. Matthew uses talents and Luke minas; the denominations are not equated.
    Luke 19:24 And he said to those standing by, ‘Take the mina from him and give it to the one who has the ten minas.’

Matthew 25:29
For to everyone who has, more will be given and he will have abundance; but from the one who does not have, even what he has will be taken.
  Luke 19:26 — teaching parallel: Both conclude the money-distribution judgment with more being given to the one who has and even the existing possession taken from the one who does not have. Matthew additionally mentions abundance.
    Luke 19:26 ‘I tell you that to everyone who has, more will be given; but from the one who does not have, even what he has will be taken away.

Matthew 25:30
Cast the useless slave into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.’
  Matthew 8:12 — teaching parallel: Both warnings describe exclusion into outer darkness accompanied by weeping and grinding of teeth. Matthew 25 applies it to the useless slave in the money parable; Matthew 8 uses it in the warning about sons of the kingdom.
    Matthew 8:12 but the sons of the kingdom will be cast into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.”

Matthew 25:31
When the Son of Man comes in His glory, and all the angels with Him, then He will sit upon the throne of His glory.
  Matthew 16:27 — teaching parallel: Both describe the Son of Man coming in glory with angels for judgment. Matthew 25 adds His sitting on the throne of glory; Matthew 16 explicitly says He will repay each person according to conduct.
    Matthew 16:27 For the Son of Man is to come in the glory of His Father with His angels, and then He will repay each person according to his conduct.

Matthew 25:32
All the nations will be gathered before Him, and He will separate them from one another as the shepherd separates the sheep from the goats.
  Ezekiel 34:17 — allusion: The Son of Man's sheep-and-goats separation recalls the Lord's promised distinction among sheep, rams, and male goats in Ezekiel. Matthew gathers all nations, while Ezekiel addresses the flock; the groups and scope are not silently equated.
    Ezekiel 34:17 And you, sheep, thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I will distinguish between sheep and sheep, rams and male goats.

Matthew 25:33
He will stand the sheep at His right and the young goats at His left.
  Matthew 25:34–25:41 — thematic parallel: The following addresses identify the meaning of the parable's right/left placement: those at the right are invited to inherit the kingdom, while those at the left are told to depart. This is the same discourse's explicit interpretation of its allocated sides, not an independent sheep-and-goat event or a new meaning for every animal detail.
    Matthew 25:34 Then the King will say to those at His right, ‘Come, you blessed by My Father; inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world.
    Matthew 25:35 For I was hungry and you gave Me food, I was thirsty and you gave Me drink, I was a stranger and you welcomed Me,
    Matthew 25:36 naked and you clothed Me, I was sick and you visited Me, I was in prison and you came to Me.’
    Matthew 25:37 Then the righteous will answer Him, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry and feed Thee, or thirsty and give Thee drink?
    Matthew 25:38 When did we see Thee a stranger and welcome Thee, or naked and clothe Thee?
    Matthew 25:39 When did we see Thee sick or in prison and come to Thee?’
    Matthew 25:40 The King will answer them, ‘Truly I tell you, insofar as you did it to one of the least of these My brothers, you did it to Me.’
    Matthew 25:41 Then He will also say to those at His left, ‘Depart from Me, you cursed, into the eternal fire prepared for the devil and his angels.

Matthew 25:34
Then the King will say to those at His right, ‘Come, you blessed by My Father; inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world.
  Luke 12:32 — teaching parallel: Both associate the kingdom's gift with the Father. Matthew's King invites the blessed to inherit the kingdom prepared from the world's foundation; Luke assures the little flock that the Father was pleased to give them the kingdom.
    Luke 12:32 Do not fear, little flock, because your Father was pleased to give you the kingdom.

Matthew 25:35
For I was hungry and you gave Me food, I was thirsty and you gave Me drink, I was a stranger and you welcomed Me,
  Isaiah 58:7 — thematic parallel: Both commend feeding hungry people and welcoming people in need. Isaiah commands sharing bread and bringing homeless poor people into one's house; Matthew describes feeding the hungry King and welcoming Him as a stranger. Isaiah's verse does not include Matthew's act of giving drink.
    Isaiah 58:7 Break your bread for the hungry and bring homeless poor people into your house. If you see someone naked, clothe him; do not overlook those belonging to your own family.

Matthew 25:36
naked and you clothed Me, I was sick and you visited Me, I was in prison and you came to Me.’
  Hebrews 13:3 — thematic parallel: Matthew commends coming to the imprisoned and caring for the afflicted; Hebrews commands remembering prisoners as fellow prisoners and those being mistreated. The link concerns embodied solidarity with prisoners, without making Hebrews repeat all the clothing and sickness details.
    Hebrews 13:3 Remember the prisoners as fellow prisoners, and those being mistreated, as you yourselves also are in a body.

Matthew 25:37
Then the righteous will answer Him, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry and feed Thee, or thirsty and give Thee drink?
  Matthew 25:44 — internal contrast: the two groups' questions to the King: Both groups ask the Lord when they saw Him hungry or thirsty. The righteous ask when they fed Him or gave Him drink; the other group asks about those and further needs when they did not serve Him. This compares the scene's paired rhetorical questions and their opposing conduct, rather than claiming independent attestation of either speech.
    Matthew 25:44 Then they too will answer, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry or thirsty or a stranger or naked or sick or in prison, and did not serve Thee?’

Matthew 25:38
When did we see Thee a stranger and welcome Thee, or naked and clothe Thee?
  Hebrews 13:2 — thematic comparison: hospitality and unrecognized recipients: Matthew's stranger-and-hospitality question concerns the righteous not having recognized their welcome as service to the King. Hebrews says that through hospitality some unknowingly entertained angels. Both connect hospitality with unrecognized significance, while the participants and settings differ; this does not identify Matthew's brothers with Hebrews' angels or assert the same event.
    Hebrews 13:2 Do not forget hospitality, for through it some unknowingly entertained angels.

Matthew 25:39
When did we see Thee sick or in prison and come to Thee?’
  Matthew 25:44 — internal contrast: sick or imprisoned and the unrecognized King: The righteous ask when they saw the King sick or in prison and came to Him; the other group includes sickness and imprisonment in its question about not serving Him. The repeated conditions and Lord-directed question form a concrete contrast within the same judgment scene. Visiting and failing to serve remain different responses, not independent accounts.
    Matthew 25:44 Then they too will answer, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry or thirsty or a stranger or naked or sick or in prison, and did not serve Thee?’

Matthew 25:40
The King will answer them, ‘Truly I tell you, insofar as you did it to one of the least of these My brothers, you did it to Me.’
  Proverbs 19:17 — thematic parallel: The King identifies kindness toward the least of His brothers with kindness toward Himself; Proverbs describes mercy to the poor as lending to God. Both relate aid to other people to a divine recipient or repayment, without assuming the two passages define their human beneficiaries identically.
    Proverbs 19:17 One who has mercy on the poor lends to God, and He will repay him according to his gift.

Matthew 25:41
Then He will also say to those at His left, ‘Depart from Me, you cursed, into the eternal fire prepared for the devil and his angels.
  Matthew 13:42 — teaching parallel: Both judgment scenes describe the condemned being consigned to fire. Matthew 25 calls it eternal fire prepared for the devil and his angels; Matthew 13 describes a furnace of fire with weeping and grinding of teeth, without that statement about its preparation.
    Matthew 13:42 They will cast them into the furnace of fire. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.

Matthew 25:42
For I was hungry and you did not give Me food, I was thirsty and you did not give Me drink,
  James 2:15–2:16 — thematic parallel: Both expose failure to supply food to a person in bodily need. James contrasts well-wishing with withholding what the body requires; Matthew's judgment accusation specifies hunger and thirst, while James's example names food and clothing rather than drink.
    James 2:15 If a brother or sister is without clothing and lacks daily food,
    James 2:16 and one of you says to them, “Go in peace; be warmed and filled,” but you do not give them what the body needs, what good is it?

Matthew 25:43
I was a stranger and you did not welcome Me, naked and you did not clothe Me, sick and in prison and you did not visit Me.’
  Isaiah 58:7 — thematic parallel: Matthew condemns withholding welcome from a stranger and clothing from the naked; Isaiah commands bringing homeless poor people into one's house and clothing the naked. This compares corresponding acts and omissions without treating all of Matthew's sickness and prison details as present in Isaiah.
    Isaiah 58:7 Break your bread for the hungry and bring homeless poor people into your house. If you see someone naked, clothe him; do not overlook those belonging to your own family.

Matthew 25:44
Then they too will answer, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry or thirsty or a stranger or naked or sick or in prison, and did not serve Thee?’
  Matthew 25:37–25:39 — internal contrast: corresponding need lists and questions: The negative group's single question gathers hunger, thirst, strangerhood, nakedness, sickness and imprisonment; the righteous ask about those same six needs across three verses. The positive questions describe feeding, giving drink, welcoming, clothing and visiting, while the negative question summarizes failure to serve. This marks an explicit literary counterpart within the same scene, without turning repetition into independent testimony.
    Matthew 25:37 Then the righteous will answer Him, ‘Lord, when did we see Thee hungry and feed Thee, or thirsty and give Thee drink?
    Matthew 25:38 When did we see Thee a stranger and welcome Thee, or naked and clothe Thee?
    Matthew 25:39 When did we see Thee sick or in prison and come to Thee?’

Matthew 25:45
Then He will answer them, ‘Truly I tell you, insofar as you did not do it to one of the least of these, neither did you do it to Me.’
  Matthew 25:40 — internal contrast: The King repeats the same scene's identification of treatment of the least with treatment of Himself, now in the negative. The earlier positive verdict supplies the deliberate contrast between service and failure to serve; this is an internal literary comparison.
    Matthew 25:40 The King will answer them, ‘Truly I tell you, insofar as you did it to one of the least of these My brothers, you did it to Me.’

Matthew 25:46
And these shall depart into eternal punishment, but the righteous into eternal life.”
  Daniel 12:2 — allusion: Both contrast enduring favorable and unfavorable outcomes: Matthew names eternal life and eternal punishment, while this supplied Daniel text names everlasting life, reproach, dispersion, and everlasting shame. Daniel explicitly frames the contrast through sleepers arising; Matthew's verse concludes the nations' judgment.
    Daniel 12:2 Many of those sleeping in the breadth of the earth shall arise: some unto everlasting life, others unto reproach, and others unto dispersion and everlasting shame.

Matthew 26:1
When Jesus finished all these words, He said to His disciples,
  Matthew 7:28 — same-book structural parallel: completed-discourse formula: Both transitions begin by saying that Jesus finished these words. Matthew 7 moves to the crowds' reaction to His teaching; Matthew 26 moves to His address to the disciples before the Passion prediction. The connection is an explicit repeated discourse-closing formula within Matthew, not the same occasion or independent narrative testimony.
    Matthew 7:28 When Jesus finished these words, the crowds were astonished at His teaching,

Matthew 26:2
“You know that after two days the Passover takes place, and the Son of Man is handed over to be crucified.”
  Mark 14:1 — narrative parallel: Both place the Passover two days away as the passion narrative begins. Matthew gives Jesus' prediction of being handed over for crucifixion; Mark states the date while describing the authorities' plan to seize and kill Him.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.

Matthew 26:3
Then the chief priests and elders of the people gathered in the courtyard of the high priest called Caiaphas.
  Mark 14:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce chief-priestly opposition planning Jesus' arrest and death near Passover. Matthew specifies the elders' meeting in Caiaphas's courtyard; Mark names scribes and does not supply the courtyard or high priest's name in this verse.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.

Matthew 26:4
They took counsel to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him.
  Mark 14:1 — narrative parallel: Both describe the authorities seeking to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.

Matthew 26:5
But they said, “Not during the feast, lest an uproar occur among the people.”
  Mark 14:2 — narrative parallel: Both record the decision to avoid the feast because of the risk of a disturbance among the people.
    Mark 14:2 For they said, “Not during the feast, lest there be a disturbance among the people.”

Matthew 26:6
While Jesus was in Bethany, in the house of Simon the leper,
  Mark 14:3 — narrative parallel: Both place the anointing meal in Bethany at the house of Simon the leper; Mark includes the anointing itself in the same verse.
    Mark 14:3 While He was in Bethany, reclining in the house of Simon the leper, a woman came with an alabaster jar of very costly ointment of pure nard. She broke the alabaster jar and poured it over His head.

Matthew 26:7
a woman came to Him with an alabaster flask of very costly perfume and poured it upon His head as He reclined at table.
  Mark 14:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe a woman pouring expensive perfume from an alabaster container onto Jesus' head while He reclines. Mark additionally names pure nard and says the jar was broken.
    Mark 14:3 While He was in Bethany, reclining in the house of Simon the leper, a woman came with an alabaster jar of very costly ointment of pure nard. She broke the alabaster jar and poured it over His head.

Matthew 26:8
Seeing it, the disciples became indignant, saying, “Why this waste?
  Mark 14:4 — narrative parallel: Both record indignation and the question why the perfume was wasted. Matthew identifies the disciples, while Mark describes some people being indignant without naming them in this verse.
    Mark 14:4 Some were indignant among themselves: “Why has this ointment been wasted?

Matthew 26:9
For this could have been sold for much and given to the poor.”
  Mark 14:5 — narrative parallel: Both argue that the perfume could have been sold and the money given to the poor. Mark specifies more than three hundred denarii and also describes harsh scolding; Matthew states only a large sale value.
    Mark 14:5 For this ointment could have been sold for more than three hundred denarii and given to the poor.” They scolded her harshly.

Matthew 26:10
Knowing this, Jesus said to them, “Why do you trouble the woman? For she has done a good work toward Me.
  Mark 14:6 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus challenge the mistreatment of the woman and call her action toward Him a good work.
    Mark 14:6 Jesus said, “Leave her alone. Why are you troubling her? She has done a good work for Me.

Matthew 26:11
For you always have the poor with you, but you do not always have Me.
  Mark 14:7 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the continuing presence of poor people with Jesus' limited presence among the hearers. Mark additionally says they can do good to the poor whenever they wish.
    Mark 14:7 For you always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good to them; but you do not always have Me.

Matthew 26:12
By pouring this perfume upon My body, she did it to prepare Me for burial.
  Mark 14:8 — narrative parallel: Both interpret the woman's anointing of Jesus' body as preparation for burial; Mark also says she did what she could.
    Mark 14:8 She did what she could. She has anointed My body beforehand for burial.

Matthew 26:13
Truly I tell you, wherever this good news is proclaimed in the whole world, what she did will also be told in remembrance of her.”
  Mark 14:9 — teaching parallel: Both promise that wherever the gospel is proclaimed throughout the world the woman's deed will be recounted in remembrance of her.
    Mark 14:9 Truly I tell you, wherever the gospel is proclaimed in the whole world, what she has done will also be told in remembrance of her.”

Matthew 26:14
Then one of the twelve, called Judas Iscariot, went to the chief priests
  Mark 14:10 — narrative parallel: Both identify Judas Iscariot as one of the Twelve and describe his approach to the chief priests to hand Jesus over.
    Mark 14:10 Judas Iscariot, one of the Twelve, went away to the chief priests to hand Him over to them.

Matthew 26:15
and said, “What are you willing to give me, and I will hand Him over to you?” They weighed out for him thirty silver coins.
  Mark 14:11 — narrative parallel: Both connect Judas's betrayal arrangement with payment in silver. Matthew supplies his question and thirty silver coins; Mark records the priests' pleasure and promise of silver money without specifying thirty.
    Mark 14:11 When they heard, they rejoiced and promised to give him silver money. He sought how to hand Him over at a suitable time.

Matthew 26:16
From then on he sought a suitable opportunity to hand Him over.
  Mark 14:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe Judas seeking a suitable opportunity to hand Jesus over after the payment arrangement.
    Mark 14:11 When they heard, they rejoiced and promised to give him silver money. He sought how to hand Him over at a suitable time.

Matthew 26:17
On the first day of Unleavened Bread, the disciples came to Jesus, saying, “Where dost Thou wish us to prepare for Thee to eat the Passover?”
  Mark 14:12 — narrative parallel: Both place the disciples' question about preparing Jesus' Passover meal on the first day of Unleavened Bread. Mark additionally explains it as the day of sacrificing the Passover.
    Mark 14:12 On the first day of Unleavened Bread, when they sacrificed the Passover, His disciples said to Him, “Where dost Thou want us to go and prepare the Passover for Thee to eat?”

Matthew 26:18
He said, “Go into the city to a certain man and tell him, ‘The Teacher says: My appointed time is near. I keep the Passover at your house with My disciples.’”
  Mark 14:13–14:15 — narrative parallel: Both direct disciples into the city to arrange a place for Jesus to eat the Passover with them, using a message from the Teacher. Mark supplies the water-jar bearer and furnished upper room; Matthew supplies the appointed time's nearness and addresses a certain man without those additional directions.
    Mark 14:13 He sent two of His disciples and said to them, “Go into the city, and a man carrying an earthenware jar of water will meet you. Follow him.
    Mark 14:14 Wherever he enters, say to the master of the house, ‘The Teacher says: Where is My guest room, where I may eat the Passover with My disciples?’
    Mark 14:15 He will show you a large upper room, furnished and ready. Prepare for us there.”

Matthew 26:19
The disciples did as Jesus directed them, and prepared the Passover.
  Mark 14:16 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples following Jesus' directions and preparing the Passover; Mark also states that they found things as He had said.
    Mark 14:16 The disciples went out, came to the city, and found it just as He had told them. They prepared the Passover.

Matthew 26:20
When evening came, He reclined at table with the twelve.
  Mark 14:17–14:18 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus with the Twelve in the evening meal scene; Mark's two verses describe His arrival and the ensuing reclining and eating.
    Mark 14:17 When evening came, He arrived with the Twelve.
    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”

Matthew 26:21
As they ate, He said, “Truly I tell you that one of you will hand Me over.”
  Mark 14:18 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus' announcement during the meal that one of His companions will hand Him over. Mark additionally emphasizes that the betrayer is eating with Him.
    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”

Matthew 26:22
Exceedingly grieved, each began saying to Him, “Surely it is not I, Lord?”
  Mark 14:19 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples' grief and their individual questions whether they are the betrayer. Matthew gives their address Lord; Mark's cited wording does not include that address.
    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”

Matthew 26:23
He answered, “The one who dipped his hand with Me in the dish will hand Me over.
  Mark 14:20 — narrative parallel: Both identify the betrayer through sharing the dipping dish with Jesus. Matthew specifies the hand; Mark additionally calls him one of the Twelve.
    Mark 14:20 He said to them, “One of the Twelve, the one dipping with Me into the dish.

Matthew 26:24
The Son of Man indeed goes as it is written concerning Him, but woe to that man through whom the Son of Man is handed over! It would have been good for him if that man had not been born.”
  Mark 14:21 — teaching parallel: Both juxtapose the Son of Man's scriptural course with a woe on His betrayer and the statement that it would have been good for that man not to have been born.
    Mark 14:21 For the Son of Man goes just as it is written about Him, but woe to that man through whom the Son of Man is handed over! It would have been good for that man if he had not been born.”

Matthew 26:25
Judas, who was handing Him over, answered, “Surely it is not I, Rabbi?” He said to him, “You have said it.”
  Mark 14:19 — narrative parallel: Matthew singles out Judas asking whether he is the betrayer, corresponding to Mark's report of the companions asking that question one by one. Matthew gives Judas's address Rabbi and Jesus' reply You have said it; Mark's cited verse does not single out Judas or give that reply.
    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”

Matthew 26:26
And as they were eating, Jesus took bread, and having blessed, He broke it; and giving it to the disciples, He said, “Take, eat; this is My body.”
  Mark 14:22 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus taking, blessing, breaking, and giving bread while identifying it as His body. Matthew additionally includes the command eat.
    Mark 14:22 As they ate, He took bread, pronounced a blessing, broke it, and gave it to them, saying, “Take; this is My body.”

Matthew 26:27
And He took a cup, and having given thanks, He gave it to them, saying, “Drink from it, all of you;
  Mark 14:23 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus taking a cup, giving thanks, and giving it to the disciples. Matthew gives the instruction that all should drink; Mark reports that they all drank.
    Mark 14:23 He took a cup, gave thanks, and gave it to them, and they all drank from it.

Matthew 26:28
for this is My blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many for forgiveness of sins.
  Mark 14:24 — teaching parallel: Both identify the cup with Jesus' blood of the covenant poured out for many. Matthew adds for forgiveness of sins, a clause absent from this supplied verse in Mark.
    Mark 14:24 He said to them, “This is My blood of the covenant, poured out for many.

Matthew 26:29
And I tell you: from this time I shall by no means drink of this fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new with you in the kingdom of My Father.”
  Mark 14:25 — teaching parallel: Both promise no further drinking of the fruit of the vine until drinking it new in the kingdom. Matthew says with you in My Father's kingdom; Mark says in God's kingdom.
    Mark 14:25 Truly I tell you, I will certainly no longer drink of the fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new in the kingdom of God.”

Matthew 26:30
After singing a hymn, they went out to the Mount of Olives.
  Mark 14:26 — narrative parallel: Both describe singing a hymn before going out to the Mount of Olives.
    Mark 14:26 After singing a hymn, they went out to the Mount of Olives.

Matthew 26:31
Then Jesus said to them, “All of you will stumble because of Me this night, for it is written: ‘I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock will be scattered.’
  Mark 14:27 — quotation: Both predict the disciples' stumbling and cite the shepherd being struck and the sheep scattered. Matthew additionally specifies this night and the sheep of the flock.
    Mark 14:27 Jesus said to them, “You will all stumble, because it is written, ‘I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep will be scattered.’

Matthew 26:32
But after I am raised, I will go ahead of you to Galilee.”
  Mark 14:28 — teaching parallel: Both promise that after being raised Jesus will go ahead of the disciples to Galilee.
    Mark 14:28 But after I am raised, I will go ahead of you into Galilee.”

Matthew 26:33
Peter answered Him, “Even if everyone stumbles because of Thee, I will never stumble.”
  Mark 14:29 — narrative parallel: Both record Peter claiming that he will not stumble even if all the others do.
    Mark 14:29 Peter said to Him, “Even if all stumble, I will not.”

Matthew 26:34
Jesus said to him, “Truly I tell you that this night, before a rooster crows, you will deny Me three times.”
  Mark 14:30 — narrative parallel: Both predict Peter's three denials that night before the rooster's crowing. Mark explicitly says before a rooster crows twice, whereas Matthew does not include twice.
    Mark 14:30 Jesus said to him, “Truly I tell you, today, this very night, before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.”

Matthew 26:35
Peter said to Him, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” All the disciples said likewise.
  Mark 14:31 — narrative parallel: Both record Peter insisting he will not deny Jesus even if he must die with Him, and both say the other disciples make the same claim.
    Mark 14:31 He spoke all the more insistently, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” They all said the same.

Matthew 26:36
Then Jesus came with them to a place called Gethsemane, and said to the disciples, “Sit here while I go over there and pray.”
  Mark 14:32 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus and the disciples at Gethsemane and give His instruction to sit while He prays.
    Mark 14:32 They came to a place whose name was Gethsemane. He said to His disciples, “Sit here while I pray.”

Matthew 26:37
Taking Peter and the two sons of Zebedee, He began to be grieved and distressed.
  Mark 14:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus taking Peter with the two companions and becoming distressed. Matthew identifies the others as Zebedee's two sons; Mark names James and John.
    Mark 14:33 He took Peter, James, and John with Him, and began to be deeply alarmed and distressed.

Matthew 26:38
Then He said to them, “My soul is deeply grieved, even to death. Remain here and keep watch with Me.”
  Mark 14:34 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' soul as deeply grieved to death and His request that the companions remain and keep watch.
    Mark 14:34 He said to them, “My soul is deeply grieved, to the point of death. Remain here and keep watch.”

Matthew 26:39
Going forward a little, He fell upon His face and prayed, saying, “My Father, if it is possible, let this cup pass from Me. Nevertheless, not as I wish, but as Thou wishest.”
  Mark 14:35–14:36 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus moving away, falling to the ground, and praying that the cup or hour pass while submitting His will to the Father's. Matthew specifies falling on His face; Mark records the address Abba and the Father's ability to do all things.
    Mark 14:35 Going forward a little, He fell on the ground and prayed that, if possible, the hour might pass from Him.
    Mark 14:36 He said, “Abba, Father, all things are possible for Thee. Take this cup away from Me. Yet not what I will, but what Thou wilt.”

Matthew 26:40
He came to the disciples and found them sleeping, and said to Peter, “So, could you not keep watch with Me for one hour?
  Mark 14:37 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus return to sleeping disciples and challenge Peter about failing to keep watch for one hour. Mark addresses him as Simon.
    Mark 14:37 He came and found them sleeping, and said to Peter, “Simon, are you sleeping? Could you not keep watch for one hour?

Matthew 26:41
Keep watch and pray, so that you may not enter temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak.”
  Mark 14:38 — teaching parallel: Both require watchfulness and prayer against entering temptation and contrast the willing spirit with weak flesh.
    Mark 14:38 Keep watch and pray, so that you may not enter into temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak.”

Matthew 26:42
Again He went away a second time and prayed, saying, “My Father, if this cannot pass unless I drink it, let Thy will be done.”
  Mark 14:39 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus going away to pray again. Matthew supplies the second prayer's wording about drinking the cup and the Father's will; Mark says He repeated the same word without reproducing that second prayer's wording.
    Mark 14:39 Again He went away and prayed, saying the same word.

Matthew 26:43
He came and again found them sleeping, for their eyes were heavy.
  Mark 14:40 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus again finding the disciples asleep because their eyes were heavy; Mark adds that they did not know how to answer Him.
    Mark 14:40 Again He came and found them sleeping, for their eyes were weighed down, and they did not know what to answer Him.

Matthew 26:44
He left them and went away again, praying a third time and saying the same words again.
  Mark 14:39–14:41 — narrative parallel: Both narratives describe repeated prayer and repeated visits to the sleeping disciples. Matthew expressly states a third prayer with the same words; Mark records repeated prayer and then a third return, without explicitly narrating a distinct third prayer in this cited passage.
    Mark 14:39 Again He went away and prayed, saying the same word.
    Mark 14:40 Again He came and found them sleeping, for their eyes were weighed down, and they did not know what to answer Him.
    Mark 14:41 He came the third time and said to them, “Sleep on now and rest. It is enough. The hour has come. Look, the Son of Man is being handed over into the hands of sinners.

Matthew 26:45
Then He came to the disciples and said to them, “Sleep on now and rest. Behold, the hour has drawn near, and the Son of Man is handed over into sinners’ hands.
  Mark 14:41 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus tell the disciples to sleep and rest as the hour arrives and the Son of Man is handed over into sinners' hands.
    Mark 14:41 He came the third time and said to them, “Sleep on now and rest. It is enough. The hour has come. Look, the Son of Man is being handed over into the hands of sinners.

Matthew 26:46
Rise, let us go. Behold, the one handing Me over has drawn near.”
  Mark 14:42 — narrative parallel: Both record Jesus' summons to rise and go because the betrayer has drawn near.
    Mark 14:42 Rise; let us go. Look, the one handing Me over has drawn near.”

Matthew 26:47
While He was still speaking, behold, Judas, one of the twelve, came with a great crowd carrying swords and clubs, from the chief priests and elders of the people.
  Mark 14:43 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Judas as one of the Twelve arriving while Jesus still speaks, accompanied by an armed crowd sent by the religious authorities. Mark additionally names scribes among those authorities.
    Mark 14:43 Immediately, while He was still speaking, Judas, one of the Twelve, arrived, and with him a crowd with swords and clubs from the chief priests, the scribes, and the elders.

Matthew 26:48
The one handing Him over had given them a sign, saying, “The One I kiss is He; seize Him.”
  Mark 14:44 — narrative parallel: Both describe Judas arranging a kiss as the sign identifying whom the armed party should seize; Mark also directs them to lead Him away securely.
    Mark 14:44 The one handing Him over had given them an agreed sign, saying, “The One I kiss is He. Seize Him and lead Him away securely.”

Matthew 26:49
Immediately he came to Jesus and said, “Greetings, Rabbi,” and kissed Him.
  Mark 14:45 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Judas approaching, addressing Jesus as Rabbi, and kissing Him. Matthew additionally gives the greeting.
    Mark 14:45 He came and immediately approached Him, saying, “Rabbi!” Then he kissed Him.

Matthew 26:50
Jesus said to him, “Friend, do what you have come for.” Then they came forward, laid their hands upon Jesus, and seized Him.
  Mark 14:46 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the party laying hands on Jesus and seizing Him. Matthew additionally records Jesus' address to Judas as friend and His instruction concerning what Judas came for; Mark's cited verse gives only the arrest.
    Mark 14:46 They laid their hands on Him and seized Him.

Matthew 26:51
And behold, one of those with Jesus stretched out his hand, drew his sword, struck the high priest’s slave, and cut off his ear.
  Mark 14:47 — narrative parallel: Both describe someone near Jesus drawing a sword and cutting off the high priest's slave's ear. Neither of these two supplied verses names the swordsman.
    Mark 14:47 One of those standing nearby drew his sword, struck the high priest’s slave, and cut off his ear.

Matthew 26:52
Then Jesus said to him, “Return your sword to its place, for all who take a sword will perish by a sword.
  John 18:11 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus command that the sword be put away during the arrest. Matthew adds the warning about sword-users perishing by the sword; John addresses Peter and gives the Father's cup as the reason for submission.
    John 18:11 So Jesus said to Peter, “Put the sword into the sheath. The cup that the Father has given Me—shall I not drink it?”

Matthew 26:53
Or do you think I cannot plead with My Father, and He will provide Me at once more than twelve legions of angels?
  2 Kings 6:16–6:17 — thematic parallel: Jesus speaks of help His Father could provide in overwhelming numbers; Elisha assures his attendant of more numerous help and prays for his eyes to see the fiery horses and chariot around him. The comparison concerns divine protective resources; Kings does not name those visible figures angels or number them as twelve legions, and Matthew does not say Jesus summoned them.
    2 Kings 6:16 Elisha said, ‘Do not be afraid, for those with us are more numerous than those with them.’
    2 Kings 6:17 Elisha prayed and said, ‘Lord, open the attendant’s eyes so that he may see.’ The Lord opened his eyes, and he saw: behold, the mountain was full of horses, and a chariot of fire was all around Elisha.

Matthew 26:54
How, then, would the Scriptures be fulfilled, that it must happen this way?”
  Luke 24:26–24:27 — thematic parallel: Jesus' arrest saying asks how the Scriptures could otherwise be fulfilled; His later explanation in Luke describes the Christ's necessary suffering and interprets the Scriptures about Himself. The settings are before the passion and after the resurrection respectively.
    Luke 24:26 Was it not necessary for the Christ to suffer these things and enter into His glory?”
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

Matthew 26:55
At that hour Jesus said to the crowds, “Have you come out as against a robber, with swords and clubs, to arrest Me? Daily I sat teaching in the temple, and you did not seize Me.
  Mark 14:48–14:49 — narrative parallel: Both contrast the armed arrest as though against a robber with Jesus' prior public teaching in the temple when He was not seized.
    Mark 14:48 Jesus answered them, “Have you come out with swords and clubs to arrest Me as though against a robber?
    Mark 14:49 Day after day I was with you in the temple, teaching, and you did not seize Me. But this is so that the Scriptures may be fulfilled.”

Matthew 26:56
But all this has happened so that the Scriptures of the prophets might be fulfilled.” Then all the disciples left Him and fled.
  Mark 14:49–14:50 — narrative parallel: Both connect the arrest with fulfillment of Scripture and then describe all the disciples leaving and fleeing. Matthew expressly calls them the Scriptures of the prophets.
    Mark 14:49 Day after day I was with you in the temple, teaching, and you did not seize Me. But this is so that the Scriptures may be fulfilled.”
    Mark 14:50 They all left Him and fled.

Matthew 26:57
Those who seized Jesus led Him away to Caiaphas the high priest, where the scribes and elders had gathered.
  Mark 14:53 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus being taken to the high priest, where the scribes and elders assemble. Matthew names Caiaphas; Mark's cited verse leaves the high priest unnamed and also lists chief priests.
    Mark 14:53 They led Jesus away to the high priest, and all the chief priests, the elders, and the scribes assembled.

Matthew 26:58
Peter followed Him at a distance as far as the high priest’s courtyard. Going inside, he sat with the officers to see the outcome.
  Mark 14:54 — narrative parallel: Both have Peter follow at a distance into the high priest's courtyard and sit with the attendants. Matthew says he wanted to see the outcome; Mark describes warming at the firelight.
    Mark 14:54 Peter followed Him from a distance, as far as inside the high priest’s courtyard. He sat with the attendants and warmed himself at the firelight.

Matthew 26:59
The chief priests and the whole council sought false testimony against Jesus to put Him to death,
  Mark 14:55 — narrative parallel: Both describe the chief priests and council seeking testimony to put Jesus to death. Matthew calls the sought testimony false; Mark's next verse explains the false witnesses and their disagreement.
    Mark 14:55 The chief priests and the whole council sought testimony against Jesus to put Him to death, but found none.

Matthew 26:60
but found none, although many false witnesses came forward. Finally two came forward
  Mark 14:56–14:57 — narrative parallel: Both describe unsuccessful false testimony followed by accusers standing forward. Matthew specifically says two finally came; Mark describes some standing up without assigning that number here.
    Mark 14:56 For many bore false witness against Him, but their testimonies did not agree.
    Mark 14:57 Some stood up and bore false witness against Him, saying,

Matthew 26:61
and said, “This Man said, ‘I can destroy God’s sanctuary and build it in three days.’”
  Mark 14:58 — narrative parallel: Both witnesses allege that Jesus spoke of destroying the sanctuary and rebuilding in three days. Matthew's accusation says I can destroy God's sanctuary; Mark's says I will destroy the hand-made sanctuary and build another not made by hands. The accusation wordings are preserved separately.
    Mark 14:58 “We heard Him say, ‘I will tear down this sanctuary made by hands, and in three days I will build another not made by hands.’”

Matthew 26:62
The high priest rose and said to Him, “Dost Thou answer nothing? What is this that they testify against Thee?”
  Mark 14:60 — narrative parallel: Both describe the high priest rising and challenging Jesus to answer the testimony against Him.
    Mark 14:60 The high priest stood up in the midst and questioned Jesus, saying, “Dost Thou answer nothing? What is it these men testify against Thee?”

Matthew 26:63
But Jesus remained silent. The high priest said to Him, “I adjure Thee by the living God to tell us whether Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.”
  Mark 14:61 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' silence followed by the high priest's question whether He is the Christ and divine Son. Matthew adjures Him by the living God and says Son of God; Mark says Son of the Blessed One.
    Mark 14:61 He remained silent and answered nothing. Again the high priest questioned Him and said to Him, “Art Thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed One?”

Matthew 26:64
Jesus said to him, “You have said it. Nevertheless I tell you, from now on you will see the Son of Man seated at the right of the Power and coming upon the clouds of heaven.”
  Mark 14:62 — narrative parallel: Both answer with the Son of Man seated at the right of the Power and coming with heaven's clouds. Matthew begins You have said it; Mark begins I am, so the opening responses are not harmonized.
    Mark 14:62 Jesus said, “I am. And you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right of the Power and coming with the clouds of heaven.”

Matthew 26:65
Then the high priest tore his garments, saying, “He has blasphemed! What further need do we have of witnesses? Look, now you heard the blasphemy.
  Mark 14:63–14:64 — narrative parallel: Both describe the high priest tearing his garments, dismissing the need for further witnesses, and declaring that the hearers have heard blasphemy.
    Mark 14:63 The high priest tore his tunics and said, “What further need have we of witnesses?
    Mark 14:64 You heard the blasphemy. What is your judgment?” They all condemned Him as deserving death.

Matthew 26:66
What do you think?” They answered, “He is liable to death.”
  Mark 14:64 — narrative parallel: Both give the council's resulting judgment that Jesus deserves death.
    Mark 14:64 You heard the blasphemy. What is your judgment?” They all condemned Him as deserving death.

Matthew 26:67
Then they spat into His face and struck Him with their fists; others slapped Him,
  Mark 14:65 — narrative parallel: Both describe spitting, fist blows, and slaps during the abuse. Mark additionally describes covering His face; that detail is not supplied in Matthew's cited verse.
    Mark 14:65 Some began spitting on Him, covering His face, striking Him with their fists, and saying to Him, “Prophesy!” The attendants received Him with slaps.

Matthew 26:68
saying, “Prophesy to us, Christ! Who is the one who struck Thee?”
  Luke 22:64 — narrative parallel: Both mock Jesus with the command to prophesy who struck Him. Luke explicitly describes blindfolding, a detail not given in Matthew's supplied verse.
    Luke 22:64 They blindfolded Him and questioned Him, saying, “Prophesy! Who is it that struck Thee?”

Matthew 26:69
Peter was sitting outside in the courtyard. One servant girl approached him, saying, “You too were with Jesus the Galilean.”
  Mark 14:66–14:67 — narrative parallel: Both place Peter in the courtyard and describe a female servant identifying him with Jesus. Matthew calls Jesus the Galilean; Mark calls Him the Nazarene and mentions Peter warming himself.
    Mark 14:66 While Peter was below in the courtyard, one of the high priest’s female slaves came.
    Mark 14:67 Seeing Peter warming himself, she looked at him and said, “You too were with Jesus the Nazarene.”

Matthew 26:70
But he denied it before everyone, saying, “I do not know what you are saying.”
  Mark 14:68 — narrative parallel: Both give Peter's denial through claiming not to know or understand what the woman says. Mark additionally describes his movement outside and an initial rooster crow.
    Mark 14:68 He denied it, saying, “I neither know nor understand what you are saying.” He went outside into the forecourt, and a rooster crowed.

Matthew 26:71
When he went out to the gateway, another girl saw him and said to those there, “This man was with Jesus the Nazarene.”
  Mark 14:69 — narrative parallel: Both have a female servant identify Peter to people standing nearby after his movement outward. Matthew calls her another girl and refers to Jesus the Nazarene; Mark refers back to the female slave without introducing another one here.
    Mark 14:69 The female slave saw him and again began saying to those standing nearby, “This man is one of them.”

Matthew 26:72
Again he denied it with an oath: “I do not know the Man.”
  Mark 14:70 — narrative parallel: Both report Peter denying the identification again. Matthew explicitly adds an oath and denial of knowing the Man; Mark's cited verse gives the renewed denial without that oath.
    Mark 14:70 Again he denied it. After a little while, those standing nearby again said to Peter, “Truly you are one of them, for you are a Galilean.”

Matthew 26:73
After a little while, those standing there came and said to Peter, “Truly you too are one of them, for even your speech makes you recognizable.”
  Mark 14:70 — narrative parallel: Both have the bystanders renew the identification of Peter through his Galilean association. Matthew specifies recognition through his speech; Mark explicitly calls him Galilean without mentioning an accent here.
    Mark 14:70 Again he denied it. After a little while, those standing nearby again said to Peter, “Truly you are one of them, for you are a Galilean.”

Matthew 26:74
Then he began to invoke curses and swear, “I do not know the Man.” Immediately a rooster crowed.
  Mark 14:71–14:72 — narrative parallel: Both describe Peter invoking curses and swearing he does not know the Man, followed by a rooster crow. Mark calls this the second crow, while Matthew does not record an earlier crow in this scene.
    Mark 14:71 He began invoking a curse and swearing, “I do not know this Man you are speaking of.”
    Mark 14:72 Immediately a rooster crowed a second time. Peter remembered the word Jesus had spoken to him: “Before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.” He broke down and wept.

Matthew 26:75
Peter remembered Jesus’ word: “Before a rooster crows, you will deny Me three times.” He went outside and wept bitterly.
  Mark 14:72 — narrative parallel: Both have the rooster remind Peter of Jesus' prediction and lead to weeping. Matthew's remembered prediction lacks Mark's twice for the crow, and Matthew describes going outside to weep bitterly.
    Mark 14:72 Immediately a rooster crowed a second time. Peter remembered the word Jesus had spoken to him: “Before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.” He broke down and wept.

Matthew 27:1
When morning came, all the chief priests and elders of the people took counsel against Jesus to put Him to death.
  Mark 15:1 — narrative parallel: Both describe a morning consultation by chief priests and elders before Jesus is handed to Pilate. Matthew explicitly states their purpose of putting Him to death; Mark also names scribes and the whole council.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.

Matthew 27:2
They bound Him, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate the governor.
  Mark 15:1 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus being bound, led away, and handed to Pilate after the leaders' consultation. Matthew explicitly identifies Pilate as governor.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.

Matthew 27:3
Then Judas, who had handed Him over, saw that He had been condemned. Filled with regret, he returned the thirty silver coins to the chief priests and elders,
  Matthew 26:14–26:16 — narrative parallel: The earlier same-book account gives Judas's agreement with the chief priests for thirty silver coins. His return of those coins after the condemnation reverses that transaction; the earlier passage does not describe his regret.
    Matthew 26:14 Then one of the twelve, called Judas Iscariot, went to the chief priests
    Matthew 26:15 and said, “What are you willing to give me, and I will hand Him over to you?” They weighed out for him thirty silver coins.
    Matthew 26:16 From then on he sought a suitable opportunity to hand Him over.

Matthew 27:4
saying, “I sinned by handing over innocent blood.” They said, “What is that to us? See to it yourself.”
  Luke 23:47 — thematic parallel: Judas calls the betrayed blood innocent; Luke's centurion calls Jesus righteous after His death. Both are affirmations of Jesus' innocence in the passion narratives, from different speakers at different moments.
    Luke 23:47 When the centurion saw what had happened, he glorified God, saying, “Truly, this man was righteous.”

Matthew 27:5
He threw the silver coins into the sanctuary and withdrew; then he went away and hanged himself.
  Acts 1:18 — narrative parallel: Both accounts relate Judas's death in connection with betrayal money, but the details differ: Matthew says he returned the coins and hanged himself; Acts describes a field acquired from wrongdoing wages and a headlong fall with bodily rupture. No combined death sequence is invented.
    Acts 1:18 Now this man acquired a field from the wages of wrongdoing, and falling headlong, he burst open in the middle, and all his entrails poured out.

Matthew 27:6
The chief priests took the silver coins and said, “It is not lawful to put them into the treasury, because they are the price of blood.”
  Matthew 26:15 — narrative parallel: The same-book negotiation identifies the silver as payment to hand Jesus over. That earlier use supplies the narrative reason the priests now call it blood-price, without independently establishing the legal rule they invoke.
    Matthew 26:15 and said, “What are you willing to give me, and I will hand Him over to you?” They weighed out for him thirty silver coins.

Matthew 27:7
After taking counsel, they bought the potter’s field with them as a burial place for strangers.
  Acts 1:18–1:19 — narrative parallel: Both link a field to Judas's wrongdoing and blood. Matthew says the priests buy a potter's field with returned coins for strangers' burial; Acts says Judas acquired a field from wrongdoing wages. The purchaser, stated use, and sequence remain distinct.
    Acts 1:18 Now this man acquired a field from the wages of wrongdoing, and falling headlong, he burst open in the middle, and all his entrails poured out.
    Acts 1:19 This became known to all who lived in Jerusalem, so that the field was called in their own language Hakeldamach—that is, Field of Blood.

Matthew 27:8
Therefore that field has been called Field of Blood until today.
  Acts 1:19 — narrative parallel: Both explain the name Field of Blood as a publicly known result of the Judas episode. Acts additionally gives Hakeldamach in the local language; this shared name does not resolve the different purchase and death details.
    Acts 1:19 This became known to all who lived in Jerusalem, so that the field was called in their own language Hakeldamach—that is, Field of Blood.

Matthew 27:9
Then what was spoken through Jeremiah the prophet was fulfilled: “They took the thirty silver coins, the price of the One priced, whom some of the sons of Israel priced,
  Zechariah 11:12–11:13 — allusion: The thirty silver coins and valuation language have a close comparison in Zechariah's thirty-piece wages. Matthew expressly attributes its fulfillment saying to Jeremiah; the supplied Zechariah ends with a smelting furnace in the Lord's house, not a potter's field. The attribution and wording differences remain unresolved.
    Zechariah 11:12 I will say to them, “If it is good before you, weigh out and give my wages, or refuse.” They weighed out my wages: thirty silver pieces.
    Zechariah 11:13 The Lord said to me, “Put them into the smelting furnace and examine whether it is approved, as I was tested on their behalf.” I took the thirty silver pieces and threw them into the Lord’s house, into the smelting furnace.

Matthew 27:10
and gave them for the potter’s field, as the Lord directed me.”
  Matthew 27:7 — narrative parallel: Within this same chapter, the fulfillment saying's coins given for a potter's field correspond to the priests' narrated purchase of that field. This internal link identifies the narrative application, not an independent Old Testament source or a resolution of the Jeremiah attribution.
    Matthew 27:7 After taking counsel, they bought the potter’s field with them as a burial place for strangers.

Matthew 27:11
Jesus stood before the governor, and the governor questioned Him, saying, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” Jesus said, “You say it.”
  Mark 15:2 — narrative parallel: Both have Pilate ask whether Jesus is King of the Jews and Jesus reply You say it or You say so. Matthew explicitly locates Him standing before the governor.
    Mark 15:2 Pilate questioned Him, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” He answered him, “You say so.”

Matthew 27:12
While He was accused by the chief priests and elders, He answered nothing.
  Mark 15:3–15:5 — narrative parallel: Both present accusations by the chief priests and Jesus' refusal to answer further. Matthew also names elders; Mark places the explicit silence after Pilate's follow-up question.
    Mark 15:3 The chief priests accused Him of many things.
    Mark 15:4 Pilate questioned Him again, saying, “Dost Thou answer nothing? Look how many accusations they bring against Thee.”
    Mark 15:5 But Jesus gave no further answer, so that Pilate marveled.

Matthew 27:13
Then Pilate said to Him, “Dost Thou not hear how many things they testify against Thee?”
  Mark 15:4 — narrative parallel: Both have Pilate ask why Jesus does not answer the many accusations brought against Him. The questions draw attention to His silence rather than supplying a defense.
    Mark 15:4 Pilate questioned Him again, saying, “Dost Thou answer nothing? Look how many accusations they bring against Thee.”

Matthew 27:14
He did not answer him even one word, so that the governor marveled greatly.
  Mark 15:5 — narrative parallel: Both connect Jesus' continued refusal to answer with Pilate's marveling. Matthew emphasizes that He did not answer even one word and that the governor marveled greatly.
    Mark 15:5 But Jesus gave no further answer, so that Pilate marveled.

Matthew 27:15
At the feast the governor customarily released to the crowd one prisoner whom they wanted.
  Mark 15:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe the customary release at the feast of one prisoner requested by the crowd. Matthew specifies the governor as the one following the custom.
    Mark 15:6 At the feast he used to release for them one prisoner whom they requested.

Matthew 27:16
At that time they had a notorious prisoner called Jesus Barabbas.
  Mark 15:7 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the imprisoned Barabbas. Matthew calls him notorious and preserves the name Jesus Barabbas; Mark calls him Barabbas and associates him with rebels who committed murder during an uprising.
    Mark 15:7 The man called Barabbas was imprisoned with the rebels who had committed murder in the uprising.

Matthew 27:17
So when they gathered, Pilate said to them, “Whom do you want me to release to you: Jesus Barabbas, or Jesus called Christ?”
  Mark 15:9–15:11 — narrative parallel: Both have Pilate offer Jesus for release and the crowd directed toward Barabbas instead. Matthew explicitly presents a choice between Jesus Barabbas and Jesus called Christ; Mark's question names the King of the Jews.
    Mark 15:9 Pilate answered them, “Do you want me to release for you the King of the Jews?”
    Mark 15:10 For he knew that the chief priests had handed Him over out of envy.
    Mark 15:11 But the chief priests stirred up the crowd to have him release Barabbas for them instead.

Matthew 27:18
For he knew that they had handed Him over because of envy.
  Mark 15:10 — narrative parallel: Both explain Pilate's perception that the chief priests handed Jesus over from envy. Matthew's pronoun refers back to the accusers named in the narrative.
    Mark 15:10 For he knew that the chief priests had handed Him over out of envy.

Matthew 27:19
While he sat on the judgment seat, his wife sent to him, saying, “Have nothing to do with that righteous Man, for I suffered many things today in a dream because of Him.”
  Luke 23:47 — thematic parallel: Pilate's wife calls Jesus righteous in her warning, while Luke's centurion calls Him righteous after seeing His death. The shared innocence testimony does not make Luke attest the wife's dream or message.
    Luke 23:47 When the centurion saw what had happened, he glorified God, saying, “Truly, this man was righteous.”

Matthew 27:20
But the chief priests and elders persuaded the crowds to ask for Barabbas and destroy Jesus.
  Mark 15:11 — narrative parallel: Both have chief priests influence the crowd to request Barabbas's release rather than Jesus. Matthew also names elders and explicitly states the aim of destroying Jesus.
    Mark 15:11 But the chief priests stirred up the crowd to have him release Barabbas for them instead.

Matthew 27:21
The governor answered them, “Which of the two do you want me to release to you?” They said, “Barabbas.”
  Mark 15:9–15:11 — narrative parallel: Both accounts present the crowd's preference for Barabbas in the release dispute. Matthew states the governor's question as a choice of two and gives the direct answer; Mark describes the priests stirring the crowd toward that request.
    Mark 15:9 Pilate answered them, “Do you want me to release for you the King of the Jews?”
    Mark 15:10 For he knew that the chief priests had handed Him over out of envy.
    Mark 15:11 But the chief priests stirred up the crowd to have him release Barabbas for them instead.

Matthew 27:22
Pilate said to them, “What then shall I do with Jesus called Christ?” They all said, “Let Him be crucified!”
  Mark 15:12–15:13 — narrative parallel: Both have Pilate ask what to do with Jesus and receive the crowd's demand for crucifixion. Matthew calls Him Jesus called Christ; Mark uses the One you call the King of the Jews.
    Mark 15:12 Pilate answered them again, “Then what do you want me to do with the One you call the King of the Jews?”
    Mark 15:13 They cried out again, “Crucify Him!”

Matthew 27:23
He said, “Why, what evil did He do?” But they cried out all the more, “Let Him be crucified!”
  Mark 15:14 — narrative parallel: Both have Pilate question what evil Jesus has done, followed by a louder demand for crucifixion. Neither cited passage supplies an answer identifying a crime.
    Mark 15:14 Pilate said to them, “Why, what evil has He done?” They cried out all the more, “Crucify Him!”

Matthew 27:24
When Pilate saw that he was accomplishing nothing, but rather an uproar was arising, he took water and washed his hands before the crowd, saying, “I am innocent of this blood. See to it yourselves.”
  Deuteronomy 21:6–21:9 — thematic parallel: Both combine washing hands with disclaiming innocent blood. Deuteronomy concerns elders responding to an unsolved killing and seeking atonement; Pilate publicly disclaims responsibility before authorizing crucifixion. This comparison neither identifies the procedures as identical nor validates Pilate's claim of innocence.
    Deuteronomy 21:6 And all the elders of that city nearest unto the slain man shall wash their hands over the head of the heifer whose sinews were cut in the ravine,
    Deuteronomy 21:7 and shall answer and say: Our hands have not shed this blood, neither have our eyes seen it.
    Deuteronomy 21:8 Be merciful unto Thy people Israel, whom Thou hast redeemed, O Lord, out of the land of Egypt, that there be no guilt of innocent blood among Thy people Israel. And atonement shall be made for the blood on their behalf.
    Deuteronomy 21:9 And you shall remove the guilt of innocent blood from among yourselves, if you do that which is good and pleasing before the Lord your God.

Matthew 27:25
All the people answered, “His blood be upon us and upon our children.”
  Acts 5:28 — thematic parallel: Both use blood coming upon people as language of responsibility for Jesus' death. Matthew reports this crowd accepting it; Acts has leaders accuse the apostles of intending to bring His blood upon them. The comparison does not extend responsibility to all Jews or later generations.
    Acts 5:28 saying, “Did we not strictly command you not to teach in this name? Yet look—you have filled Jerusalem with your teaching and intend to bring this man’s blood upon us.”

Matthew 27:26
Then he released Barabbas to them; after flogging Jesus, he handed Him over to be crucified.
  Mark 15:15 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Barabbas being released and Jesus flogged and handed over for crucifixion. Mark explicitly relates Pilate's action to satisfying the crowd.
    Mark 15:15 Pilate, wanting to satisfy the crowd, released Barabbas for them. After having Jesus scourged, he handed Him over to be crucified.

Matthew 27:27
Then the governor’s soldiers took Jesus into the praetorium and gathered the whole cohort around Him.
  Mark 15:16 — narrative parallel: Both have soldiers lead Jesus into the praetorium and assemble the whole cohort. Matthew identifies them as the governor's soldiers; Mark describes the courtyard as the praetorium.
    Mark 15:16 The soldiers led Him inside the courtyard—that is, the praetorium—and called together the whole cohort.

Matthew 27:28
They stripped Him and put a scarlet cloak around Him.
  Mark 15:17 — narrative parallel: Both describe soldiers putting a royal-colored garment on Jesus for mockery. Matthew calls it a scarlet cloak after stripping Him; Mark calls it purple. The color descriptions are retained rather than harmonized.
    Mark 15:17 They clothed Him in purple and, after weaving a crown of thorns, put it on Him.

Matthew 27:29
They wove a crown from thorns, placed it upon His head, and put a reed in His right hand. Kneeling before Him, they mocked Him, saying, “Greetings, King of the Jews!”
  Mark 15:17–15:19 — narrative parallel: Both narrate a thorn crown, a mocking royal salute, and kneeling before Jesus. Matthew additionally places a reed in His right hand; Mark's reed appears as the instrument used to strike His head.
    Mark 15:17 They clothed Him in purple and, after weaving a crown of thorns, put it on Him.
    Mark 15:18 They began saluting Him, “Hail, King of the Jews!”
    Mark 15:19 They kept striking His head with a reed and spitting on Him, and knelt down to bow before Him.

Matthew 27:30
They spat on Him, took the reed, and kept striking Him upon the head.
  Mark 15:19 — narrative parallel: Both describe soldiers spitting on Jesus and repeatedly striking His head with a reed. Mark also includes kneeling to bow before Him in the same verse.
    Mark 15:19 They kept striking His head with a reed and spitting on Him, and knelt down to bow before Him.

Matthew 27:31
After mocking Him, they stripped off the cloak, dressed Him in His own garments, and led Him away to crucify Him.
  Mark 15:20 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the soldiers' mockery by removing its garment, putting Jesus' own clothes back on Him, and leading Him out for crucifixion. Matthew's cloak and Mark's purple remain differently described.
    Mark 15:20 After they had mocked Him, they stripped off the purple and put His own garments on Him. They led Him out to crucify Him.

Matthew 27:32
As they went out, they found a Cyrenian man named Simon. They compelled him to carry His cross.
  Mark 15:21 — narrative parallel: Both identify Simon of Cyrene as the man compelled to carry Jesus' cross. Mark additionally calls him a passerby from the country and names his sons Alexander and Rufus.
    Mark 15:21 They compelled a passerby, Simon of Cyrene, coming from the country, the father of Alexander and Rufus, to carry His cross.

Matthew 27:33
They came to a place called Golgotha, which is called Place of a Skull,
  Mark 15:22 — narrative parallel: Both identify Golgotha and give the meaning Place of a Skull. Matthew says they came there; Mark says they brought Jesus there.
    Mark 15:22 They brought Him to the place Golgotha, which translated means Place of a Skull.

Matthew 27:34
and gave Him wine mixed with gall to drink. After tasting it, He was unwilling to drink.
  Mark 15:23 — narrative parallel: Both describe offered wine that Jesus does not drink. Matthew specifies gall and says He tasted it before refusing; Mark specifies myrrh and says He did not take it. The mixtures and narrated actions are not made identical.
    Mark 15:23 They offered Him wine mixed with myrrh, but He did not take it.

Matthew 27:35
After crucifying Him, they divided His garments by casting a lot,
  Mark 15:24 — narrative parallel: Both narrate crucifixion and the division of Jesus' garments by casting a lot. Mark explicitly states that the lot determines who takes what.
    Mark 15:24 They crucified Him and divided His garments, casting a lot over them to determine who would take what.

Matthew 27:36
and sat there keeping watch over Him.
  Matthew 27:54 — narrative parallel: The later account returns to the centurion and those with him guarding Jesus, describing their response to the earthquake and events. This traces the watchers introduced sitting here into their later narrated testimony. It does not independently repeat the sitting posture or identify each guard personally.
    Matthew 27:54 The centurion and those with him guarding Jesus saw the earthquake and what had happened, and were exceedingly afraid, saying, “Truly this was God’s Son.”

Matthew 27:37
Above His head they placed the written charge against Him: “This is Jesus, the King of the Jews.”
  Mark 15:26 — narrative parallel: Both identify the written crucifixion charge as King of the Jews. Matthew's inscription includes This is Jesus; Mark's cited wording does not, so the inscription texts remain distinct.
    Mark 15:26 The inscription of the charge against Him was written: “The King of the Jews.”

Matthew 27:38
Then two robbers were crucified with Him, one at His right and one at His left.
  Mark 15:27 — narrative parallel: Both describe two robbers crucified beside Jesus, one at His right and one at His left. The shared detail does not depend on adding Mark's absent verse 28.
    Mark 15:27 They crucified two robbers with Him, one at His right and one at His left.

Matthew 27:39
Those passing by reviled Him, shaking their heads
  Mark 15:29 — narrative parallel: Both describe passersby reviling Jesus and shaking their heads. Mark begins their spoken taunt in the same verse; Matthew continues it in the next.
    Mark 15:29 Those passing by reviled Him, shaking their heads and saying, “Aha! Thou who dost tear down the sanctuary and build it in three days,

Matthew 27:40
and saying, “Thou who destroyest the sanctuary and buildest it in three days, save Thyself! If Thou art God’s Son, come down from the cross.”
  Mark 15:29–15:30 — narrative parallel: Both taunts invoke destroying and rebuilding the sanctuary in three days and demand self-rescue from the cross. Matthew adds If Thou art God's Son, which is absent from Mark's cited taunt.
    Mark 15:29 Those passing by reviled Him, shaking their heads and saying, “Aha! Thou who dost tear down the sanctuary and build it in three days,
    Mark 15:30 save Thyself by coming down from the cross!”

Matthew 27:41
Likewise the chief priests, mocking with the scribes and elders, said,
  Mark 15:31 — narrative parallel: Both introduce mocking by chief priests together with scribes. Matthew additionally names elders; Mark says they mock among themselves and begins their speech in the same verse.
    Mark 15:31 Likewise the chief priests, mocking Him among themselves with the scribes, said, “He saved others; He cannot save Himself.

Matthew 27:42
“He saved others; Himself He cannot save. He is King of Israel! Let Him come down from the cross now, and we will believe in Him.
  Mark 15:31–15:32 — narrative parallel: Both mock Jesus for saving others but not Himself and demand that Israel's king descend from the cross to elicit belief. Mark also calls Him the Christ and mentions seeing before believing.
    Mark 15:31 Likewise the chief priests, mocking Him among themselves with the scribes, said, “He saved others; He cannot save Himself.
    Mark 15:32 Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross, so that we may see and believe.” Those crucified with Him also reproached Him.

Matthew 27:43
He trusts in God; let Him rescue Him now, if He desires Him, for He said, ‘I am God’s Son.’”
  Psalms 21:9 — allusion: The taunt about trusting God and asking God to rescue the one He desires closely recalls this edition's Greek-numbered Psalm 21:9. Matthew additionally cites Jesus' claim to be God's Son, which the psalm verse does not state.
    Psalms 21:9 “He hoped in the Lord; let Him rescue him; let Him save him, since He desires him.”

Matthew 27:44
The robbers crucified with Him also reproached Him in the same way.
  Mark 15:32 — narrative parallel: Both say those crucified with Jesus reproached Him. This agreement is retained without importing a different account's distinction between the two condemned men.
    Mark 15:32 Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross, so that we may see and believe.” Those crucified with Him also reproached Him.

Matthew 27:45
From the sixth hour darkness came upon all the land until the ninth hour.
  Mark 15:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe darkness over the whole land from the sixth until the ninth hour. The shared time span is preserved without converting it into an unstated modern timetable.
    Mark 15:33 When the sixth hour came, darkness came over the whole land until the ninth hour.

Matthew 27:46
And about the ninth hour Jesus cried with a loud voice, saying, “Eli, Eli, lema sabachthani?” That is, “My God, My God, why hast Thou forsaken Me?”
  Mark 15:34 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' loud forsakenness cry at about the ninth hour and translate it as My God, My God, why hast Thou forsaken Me. Matthew preserves Eli, Eli; Mark preserves Eloi, Eloi.
    Mark 15:34 At the ninth hour, Jesus cried out with a loud voice, “Eloi, Eloi, lema sabachthani?” which translated means, “My God, My God, why hast Thou forsaken Me?”

Matthew 27:47
Some standing there heard and said, “This Man is calling Elijah.”
  Mark 15:35 — narrative parallel: Both report nearby hearers interpreting the cry as a call to Elijah. Matthew says some standing there; Mark similarly names some standing nearby.
    Mark 15:35 Some of those standing nearby heard and said, “Look, He is calling Elijah.”

Matthew 27:48
Immediately one of them ran, took a sponge, filled it with sour wine, placed it on a reed, and gave Him a drink.
  Mark 15:36 — narrative parallel: Both describe someone running to fill a sponge with sour wine, placing it on a reed, and offering Jesus a drink. Mark continues with the Elijah remark in the same verse.
    Mark 15:36 Someone ran, filled a sponge with sour wine, placed it on a reed, and gave Him a drink, saying, “Leave Him; let us see whether Elijah comes to take Him down.”

Matthew 27:49
But the rest said, “Leave it; let us see whether Elijah comes to save Him.”
  Mark 15:36 — narrative parallel: Both contain a proposal to wait and see whether Elijah intervenes. Matthew attributes it to the rest, while Mark attaches it to the person offering the drink; save and take Him down are also distinct wordings.
    Mark 15:36 Someone ran, filled a sponge with sour wine, placed it on a reed, and gave Him a drink, saying, “Leave Him; let us see whether Elijah comes to take Him down.”

Matthew 27:50
Jesus again cried out with a loud voice and released His spirit.
  Mark 15:37 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus dying after a loud cry. Matthew says He released His spirit; Mark says He breathed His last, preserving the different descriptions.
    Mark 15:37 Jesus let out a loud cry and breathed His last.

Matthew 27:51
And behold, the veil of the sanctuary was torn in two from top to bottom. The earth shook, and the rocks split.
  Mark 15:38 — narrative parallel: Both describe the sanctuary veil torn in two from top to bottom. Matthew additionally records an earthquake and splitting rocks, details not supplied by this Mark verse.
