    Mark 15:38 The curtain of the sanctuary was torn in two from top to bottom.

Matthew 27:52
The tombs were opened, and many bodies of the holy ones who had fallen asleep were raised.
  Ezekiel 37:11–37:14 — thematic parallel: Both associate opened tombs with restored life. Ezekiel explicitly addresses Israel's house in a restoration oracle; Matthew narrates holy people's bodies raised in the passion account. Ezekiel is an imagery comparison, not independent testimony to Matthew's particular event.
    Ezekiel 37:11 The Lord spoke to me, saying, ‘Son of man, these bones are all Israel’s house, and they say, Our bones have become dry, our hope has perished; we are utterly spent.
    Ezekiel 37:12 Therefore prophesy and say, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am opening your tombs and will bring you up from your tombs and into Israel’s land,
    Ezekiel 37:13 and you will know that I am the Lord when I open your graves to bring My people up from the graves.
    Ezekiel 37:14 I will put My Spirit into you, and you will live. I will place you upon your land, and you will know that I, the Lord, have spoken and will act, says the Lord.’

Matthew 27:53
Coming out of the tombs after His rising, they entered the holy city and appeared to many.
  Ezekiel 37:12–37:14 — grave-opening and restoration imagery: Matthew narrates holy people coming out of tombs and entering the holy city after Jesus' rising; Ezekiel promises God's people opened graves, emergence and return to Israel's land. The grave-and-return imagery corresponds, while the audiences, destinations and forms of restoration differ. No named identities or exhaustive fulfillment claim is supplied.
    Ezekiel 37:12 Therefore prophesy and say, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am opening your tombs and will bring you up from your tombs and into Israel’s land,
    Ezekiel 37:13 and you will know that I am the Lord when I open your graves to bring My people up from the graves.
    Ezekiel 37:14 I will put My Spirit into you, and you will live. I will place you upon your land, and you will know that I, the Lord, have spoken and will act, says the Lord.’

Matthew 27:54
The centurion and those with him guarding Jesus saw the earthquake and what had happened, and were exceedingly afraid, saying, “Truly this was God’s Son.”
  Mark 15:39 — narrative parallel: Both have a centurion identify Jesus as God's Son. Matthew includes fellow guards and fear at the earthquake and events; Mark describes the centurion's response to the way Jesus died.
    Mark 15:39 When the centurion standing opposite Him saw that He breathed His last in this way, he said, “Truly this Man was God’s Son.”

Matthew 27:55
Many women were there, watching from a distance. They had followed Jesus from Galilee, serving Him.
  Mark 15:40–15:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe women watching from a distance who followed and served Jesus in Galilee. Mark additionally mentions many other women coming with Him to Jerusalem.
    Mark 15:40 There were also women watching from a distance, among them Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James the Little and Joses, and Salome.
    Mark 15:41 When He was in Galilee, they had followed Him and served Him; there were also many other women who had come up with Him to Jerusalem.

Matthew 27:56
Among them were Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James and Joseph, and the mother of Zebedee’s sons.
  Mark 15:40 — narrative parallel: Both name Mary Magdalene and a Mary associated with James among the witnesses. Matthew says James and Joseph and identifies Zebedee's sons' mother; Mark says James the Little and Joses and names Salome. No unproved identity of the third designation is imposed.
    Mark 15:40 There were also women watching from a distance, among them Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James the Little and Joses, and Salome.

Matthew 27:57
When evening came, a rich man from Arimathea named Joseph came, who himself had also become Jesus’ disciple.
  Mark 15:42–15:43 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Joseph of Arimathea approaching in the evening for the burial. Matthew calls him rich and a disciple; Mark calls him a respected council member awaiting God's kingdom and specifies Preparation.
    Mark 15:42 Evening had already come, and since it was Preparation—that is, the day before the Sabbath—
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.

Matthew 27:58
He went to Pilate and asked for Jesus’ body. Then Pilate ordered it to be given.
  Mark 15:43–15:45 — narrative parallel: Both have Joseph ask Pilate for Jesus' body and Pilate grant it. Mark includes Pilate checking the death with the centurion; Matthew abbreviates the exchange to the request and order.
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.
    Mark 15:44 Pilate marveled that He was already dead. Calling the centurion to him, he asked whether He had died some time ago.
    Mark 15:45 When he learned from the centurion, he granted the corpse to Joseph.

Matthew 27:59
Joseph took the body and wrapped it in a clean linen cloth.
  Mark 15:46 — narrative parallel: Both describe Joseph wrapping Jesus' body in linen. Matthew specifies clean linen; Mark says Joseph bought the cloth and took the body down.
    Mark 15:46 Joseph bought a linen cloth, took Him down, wrapped Him in the cloth, and laid Him in a tomb cut out of rock. He rolled a stone against the entrance of the tomb.

Matthew 27:60
He laid it in his own new tomb, which he had cut in the rock. Rolling a great stone to the tomb’s entrance, he went away.
  Mark 15:46 — narrative parallel: Both describe burial in a rock-cut tomb with a stone rolled to its entrance. Matthew explicitly calls it Joseph's own new tomb; those ownership and newness details are absent from this Mark verse.
    Mark 15:46 Joseph bought a linen cloth, took Him down, wrapped Him in the cloth, and laid Him in a tomb cut out of rock. He rolled a stone against the entrance of the tomb.

Matthew 27:61
Mary Magdalene and the other Mary were there, sitting opposite the tomb.
  Mark 15:47 — narrative parallel: Both identify Mary Magdalene and another Mary as watching the burial place. Matthew calls the second woman the other Mary and says they sit opposite; Mark calls her Mary the mother of Joses.
    Mark 15:47 Mary Magdalene and Mary the mother of Joses watched where He was laid.

Matthew 27:63
saying, “Sir, we remembered that while He was still alive, that deceiver said, ‘After three days I am raised.’
  Matthew 20:19 — narrative parallel: The opponents recall Jesus' prediction of rising after three days; the earlier same-book prediction explicitly promises resurrection on the third day. The reported after-three-days and on-the-third-day formulations remain distinct.
    Matthew 20:19 and hand Him over to the Gentiles to mock, flog, and crucify Him; and on the third day He will be raised.”

Matthew 27:64
So command that the tomb be made secure until the third day, lest His disciples come, steal Him, and tell the people, ‘He has been raised from the dead,’ and the last deception will be worse than the first.”
  Matthew 28:13 — narrative parallel: The feared explanation here is disciples stealing the body and claiming resurrection; the later same-book episode explicitly instructs guards to spread the stolen-body story. This traces an internal narrative development, not proof that the reported theft occurred.
    Matthew 28:13 saying, “Say, ‘His disciples came by night and stole Him while we slept.’

Matthew 27:65
Pilate said to them, “You have a guard. Go and make it secure as you know how.”
  Matthew 27:66 — internal instruction and narrated performance: Pilate directs the party to go and make the tomb secure; the narrative then has them go,secure it,and seal the stone with the guard. This is the particular instruction’s narrated performance,without deciding whether You have a guard assigns new personnel or refers to an already available guard.
    Matthew 27:66 They went and secured the tomb by sealing the stone, with the guard.

Matthew 27:66
They went and secured the tomb by sealing the stone, with the guard.
  Matthew 28:2–28:4 — narrative parallel: The sealed stone and guard established here are confronted in the following scene by the rolled stone and guards overcome with fear. This same-book contrast concerns the narrative's security and its reversal, not an independent account of the sealing.
    Matthew 28:2 And behold, a great earthquake occurred, for an angel of the Lord descended from heaven, came, rolled away the stone, and sat upon it.
    Matthew 28:3 His appearance was like lightning, and his clothing white as snow.
    Matthew 28:4 From fear of him the guards shook and became like dead men.

Matthew 28:1
After the Sabbath, as it dawned toward the first day of the week, Mary Magdalene and the other Mary came to view the tomb.
  Mark 16:1–16:2 — narrative parallel: Both place women including Mary Magdalene at the tomb after the Sabbath on the first day. Matthew names the other Mary and the dawn; Mark names Mary mother of James and Salome, spices, and the sun having risen.
    Mark 16:1 When the Sabbath had passed, Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome bought spices so that they might come and anoint Him.
    Mark 16:2 Very early on the first day of the week, they came to the tomb when the sun had risen.

Matthew 28:2
And behold, a great earthquake occurred, for an angel of the Lord descended from heaven, came, rolled away the stone, and sat upon it.
  Mark 16:4 — narrative parallel: Both describe the tomb's stone rolled away. Matthew narrates an earthquake and an angel moving it; Mark has the women discover it already rolled away and does not describe the earthquake or the rolling action.
    Mark 16:4 Looking up, they saw that the stone had been rolled away, for it was very large.

Matthew 28:3
His appearance was like lightning, and his clothing white as snow.
  Mark 16:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe the tomb messenger in white clothing. Matthew calls him an angel with lightning-like appearance outside on the stone; Mark describes a young man in white sitting inside on the right. The locations and descriptions remain distinct.
    Mark 16:5 They entered the tomb and saw a young man sitting on the right, clothed in a white robe, and they were greatly alarmed.

Matthew 28:4
From fear of him the guards shook and became like dead men.
  Daniel 10:5–10:9 — narrative parallel: Both scenes associate an overwhelming reaction with an extraordinary appearing figure. Matthew says the guards shook and became like dead men; Daniel describes frightened companions and his own loss of strength and collapse. The participants, their perception and their exact reactions remain distinct; Matthew does not say the guards literally died.
    Daniel 10:5 and I lifted my eyes and saw a man clothed in fine linen, with fine linen wrapped around his waist, and light coming from his middle.
    Daniel 10:6 His body was like a tarshish stone, his face like a flash of lightning, his eyes like flaming torches, his arms and feet like flashing bronze, and the sound of his speech like the sound of an uproar.
    Daniel 10:7 I, Daniel, saw this great vision, but the men with me did not see it. Strong fear fell upon them, and they fled in haste.
    Daniel 10:8 I was left alone and saw this great vision. No strength remained in me; a spirit turned upon me for destruction, and I had no strength.
    Daniel 10:9 I did not hear the sound of his speech; I had fallen face down upon the earth.

Matthew 28:5
The angel answered the women, “Do not be afraid, for I know that you seek Jesus who was crucified.
  Mark 16:6 — narrative parallel: Both tell the women not to fear or be alarmed and identify the one they seek as the crucified Jesus. Mark additionally calls Him the Nazarene.
    Mark 16:6 He said to them, “Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus the Nazarene, who was crucified. He has been raised; He is not here. Look, the place where they laid Him.

Matthew 28:6
He is not here, for He was raised, as He said. Come, see the place where He lay.
  Mark 16:6 — narrative parallel: Both say Jesus is raised and not there and direct attention to where His body had lain. Matthew adds as He said; neither passage supplies a body remaining in that place.
    Mark 16:6 He said to them, “Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus the Nazarene, who was crucified. He has been raised; He is not here. Look, the place where they laid Him.

Matthew 28:7
Go quickly and tell His disciples that He was raised from the dead; and behold, He goes ahead of you to Galilee. There you will see Him. Behold, I have told you.”
  Mark 16:7 — narrative parallel: Both send the women to tell disciples that Jesus goes ahead to Galilee and will be seen there. Mark explicitly includes Peter and recalls what Jesus told them.
    Mark 16:7 But go, tell His disciples and Peter, ‘He is going ahead of you into Galilee. There you will see Him, just as He told you.’”

Matthew 28:8
They departed quickly from the tomb with fear and great joy and ran to report to His disciples.
  Mark 16:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe departure from the tomb with fear. Matthew adds great joy and running to report; Mark's verse 8 describes trembling, astonishment, and saying nothing to anyone. These immediate responses are not forced into identical wording or sequence.
    Mark 16:8 They went out and fled from the tomb, for trembling and astonishment held them. They said nothing to anyone, for they were afraid.

Matthew 28:9
And behold, Jesus met them, saying, “Greetings!” They came, grasped His feet, and worshiped Him.
  John 20:16–20:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe an encounter with risen Jesus followed by a message to disciples. Matthew has women grasp His feet and worship; John centers on Mary Magdalene, her recognition, and His instruction not to cling. The participants and actions are not silently harmonized.
    John 20:16 Jesus says to her, “Mary.” Turning, she says to Him in Hebrew, “Rabbouni!”—which means Teacher.
    John 20:17 Jesus says to her, “Do not cling to Me, for I have not yet ascended to the Father. But go to My brothers and tell them, ‘I am ascending to My Father and your Father, and My God and your God.’”
    John 20:18 Mary Magdalene comes, announcing to the disciples, “I have seen the Lord,” and that He said these things to her.

Matthew 28:10
Then Jesus said to them, “Do not fear. Go and report to My brothers that they should go to Galilee, and there they will see Me.”
  John 20:17 — narrative parallel: Both have risen Jesus send a message through a woman or women to My brothers. Matthew directs them to Galilee to see Him; John's message concerns His ascending to the Father, not a Galilee rendezvous.
    John 20:17 Jesus says to her, “Do not cling to Me, for I have not yet ascended to the Father. But go to My brothers and tell them, ‘I am ascending to My Father and your Father, and My God and your God.’”

Matthew 28:11
As they went, behold, some of the guard came into the city and reported to the chief priests everything that had happened.
  Matthew 27:66 — narrative parallel: The guards reporting the events are linked to the preceding same-book account of the guarded, sealed tomb. This internal continuity identifies the witnesses in the narrative rather than supplying independent confirmation of their assignment.
    Matthew 27:66 They went and secured the tomb by sealing the stone, with the guard.

Matthew 28:12
They gathered with the elders and took counsel, then gave a considerable amount of silver to the soldiers,
  Matthew 26:14–26:16 — thematic parallel: Both show the chief priests using silver to secure action against Jesus: earlier Judas's betrayal, here soldiers' cooperation with the proposed account. The recipients, sums, and purposes differ; the transactions are not identified as one.
    Matthew 26:14 Then one of the twelve, called Judas Iscariot, went to the chief priests
    Matthew 26:15 and said, “What are you willing to give me, and I will hand Him over to you?” They weighed out for him thirty silver coins.
    Matthew 26:16 From then on he sought a suitable opportunity to hand Him over.

Matthew 28:13
saying, “Say, ‘His disciples came by night and stole Him while we slept.’
  Matthew 27:64 — narrative parallel: The stolen-body explanation that opponents feared earlier becomes the story they now instruct the soldiers to tell. The same-book recurrence exposes the narrative's movement from an anticipated allegation to an instructed claim.
    Matthew 27:64 So command that the tomb be made secure until the third day, lest His disciples come, steal Him, and tell the people, ‘He has been raised from the dead,’ and the last deception will be worse than the first.”

Matthew 28:14
If this is heard before the governor, we will persuade him and make you free from worry.”
  Acts 12:18–12:19 — thematic parallel: Matthew records a promise to protect the guards from anxiety if their account reaches the governor. Acts reports Herod searching for Peter, examining the guards and ordering them led away for execution. This is a concrete comparison of guard accountability after a missing charge, without importing Herod's sentence into Matthew or claiming a universal Roman custody law.
    Acts 12:18 When day came, there was no small disturbance among the soldiers over what had become of Peter.
    Acts 12:19 Herod searched for him and did not find him. After examining the guards, he ordered them to be led away for execution. Then he went down from Judea to Caesarea and stayed there.

Matthew 28:15
They took the silver and did as they were instructed. This account was spread among Jews until this very day.
  Matthew 28:12–28:13 — narrative parallel: This same-chapter callback records the carrying out of the paid instruction and dissemination of the specified account. The earlier verses supply the payment and invented stolen-body story; the link does not endorse that story as a true theft report.
    Matthew 28:12 They gathered with the elders and took counsel, then gave a considerable amount of silver to the soldiers,
    Matthew 28:13 saying, “Say, ‘His disciples came by night and stole Him while we slept.’

Matthew 28:16
The eleven disciples went to Galilee, to the mountain where Jesus had directed them.
  Matthew 26:32 — narrative parallel: The disciples' journey to Galilee corresponds to Jesus' earlier promise that after being raised He would go ahead there. The earlier verse does not specify the mountain or independently narrate this meeting.
    Matthew 26:32 But after I am raised, I will go ahead of you to Galilee.”

Matthew 28:17
When they saw Him, they worshiped, but some doubted.
  Luke 24:36–24:40 — narrative parallel: Both portray uncertainty among disciples seeing risen Jesus. Matthew combines worship with some doubting; Luke describes fear of a spirit, questions about doubts, and bodily reassurance. The scenes and reactions are compared without being declared identical.
    Luke 24:36 While they were speaking these things, He Himself stood among them and said to them, “Peace be with you.”
    Luke 24:37 But they were startled and became afraid, thinking they were seeing a spirit.
    Luke 24:38 And He said to them, “Why are you troubled, and why do doubts arise in your heart?
    Luke 24:39 See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself. Feel Me and see, because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have.”
    Luke 24:40 And having said this, He showed them His hands and feet.

Matthew 28:18
And Jesus came and spoke to them, saying, “All authority in heaven and upon earth has been given to Me.
  Daniel 7:14 — thematic parallel: Both speak of authority being given with universal scope. Daniel describes nations worshiping the visionary figure and an everlasting authority and kingdom; Matthew has Jesus declare all authority in heaven and earth. This is a royal-authority comparison, not a verbatim quotation.
    Daniel 7:14 Authority was given him, and all the nations of the earth according to their families, and all glory, worshipped him. His authority is everlasting authority that will never be taken away, and his kingdom will never be destroyed.

Matthew 28:19
Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them into the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,
  Acts 1:8 — teaching parallel: Both commissions extend the disciples' mission universally. Matthew says make disciples of all nations and gives a triadic baptismal formula; Acts says witness to the earth's end with the Spirit's power and does not repeat that formula.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

Matthew 28:20
teaching them to keep all that I commanded you; and behold, I am with you all the days, until the consummation of the age.”
  Matthew 1:23 — thematic parallel: Matthew's closing assurance I am with you corresponds to its opening explanation of Emmanuel as God with us. This same-book literary connection preserves the different settings of birth-prophecy interpretation and the continuing discipleship mission.
    Matthew 1:23 “Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and they shall call His name Emmanuel”; which means “God with us.”

Reviewed units still unfinished
Matthew 1:14: The actual Azor, Zadok, Achim and Eliud succession is retained between its neighboring generations. An isolated Zadok or other common name does not substantiate the whole ancestry segment or a second matching line.
Matthew 1:15: Eliud, Eleazar, Matthan and Jacob form the printed sequence toward Joseph. Neighboring continuation and a separately named Eleazar do not establish a distinct parallel pedigree; no missing links are supplied.
Matthew 15:23: Jesus silence and the disciples dismissal request have different speakers from the ensuing lost-sheep reply. The text does not state an inner reason for the silence, and another supplicants experience is not equated with this one.
Matthew 18:31: The fellow slaves are grieved by the imprisonment and report what happened before the masters summons. No external reporting episode or every observers inner motive is established; I retain no separate comparison for the reporting notice in this review.
Matthew 23:15: The accusation combines sea-and-land effort, one convert and the twice-as-much Gehenna outcome. Other mission or convert notices do not establish those stated particulars or justify generalizing the accusation to every mission.
Matthew 25:7: The virgins rise and arrange lamps in response to the midnight cry. I retain no merely verbal waking or lamp parallel and do not identify their equipment with sanctuary lamps.
Matthew 27:62: The chief priests and Pharisees gather before Pilate on the day after Preparation. Earlier trial gatherings have different times and purposes; this notice alone does not support a matching summons or an imported calendar calculation.
